# The Unification of Italy and Germany - Live Learning Session

> **What this session teaches:** It begins with a settlement that refused a question - the Vienna order of 1815, which left Italy divided under Austrian domination and Germany a confederation of thirty-nine states under Austrian influence - and it ends with two new states that answered that question in opposite ways and destabilised the continent by doing so. Between those two points it teaches what a nation-state is and why the word "unification" hides two different operations; the chronology that may be asserted and the chronology that may not; the Risorgimento and its competing programmes, with Mazzini and Young Italy of 1831 as the first national organisation of its kind; the Zollverein of 1834 and the German case in which an economic union preceded a political one; the failure of 1848 in both lands, the Frankfurt Parliament's refused crown and the exact lesson the next generation drew from that failure; Cavour and the conversion of Piedmont-Sardinia into an instrument, through the Crimean intervention of 1855 and the Congress of Paris of 1856; Plombieres, the war of 1859, Villafranca, and the price paid in Nice and Savoy; the central duchies and the plebiscite instrument; Garibaldi, the Thousand, Marsala, Sicily, Naples, and the meeting at Teano that converted an insurrection into an annexation; the proclamation of 17 March 1861, Venetia in 1866 and Rome on 20 September 1870; the Roman question and the limits of Italian integration - the south, brigandage, trasformismo and the unredeemed lands; then the German sequence in its own logic - the Confederation's structure, Prussian dualism with Austria, the army-budget conflict of 1862 that made Bismarck necessary, Realpolitik as a method rather than a slogan, and the three wars of 1864, 1866 and 1870-71 with the North German Confederation built between the second and the third; the proclamation at Versailles on 18 January 1871 and the constitution that followed; who was made to belong and who was not, through the Kulturkampf, the anti-socialist legislation and the treatment of Poles, Danes and Alsatians; the comparison of the two paths, made as an argument about agency rather than a pair of narratives; what the two unifications did to the European states system; and finally what historians actually disagree about, including the counterfactuals a careful answer may and may not entertain. Nationalism as a doctrine belongs to the overview topic; the Vienna settlement itself belongs to the topic before this one; the alliance system and the outbreak of 1914 belong to the topic on the world in 1914; the Indian reception of Mazzini and Garibaldi belongs to the Modern Indian History owner. Here you learn how two nations were made into states, by whom, at what cost, and with what left undone.

## How to read this session

Work through the lessons in order. The order is not the order of a textbook, because a textbook narrates Italy and then narrates Germany, and that is exactly the structure that fails in the examination hall. Here the two cases are separated where their internal logic is separate and joined where a single mechanism runs through both. Lesson 4 comes before Lesson 5 because you cannot understand why Cavour worked through diplomacy until you know what happened to the people who tried the other method in 1848. Lesson 11 returns to Germany only after the whole Italian sequence is complete, because the German case is best read as a controlled comparison rather than as a parallel story. Inside a lesson, take the visual first, then the plain explanation, then the exact evidence, then the objection and the reply, and only then the examination use.

Two labels run through every lesson, and keeping them apart is the single habit that separates a confident answer from an over-confident one.

| Label | Meaning | Use in an answer |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ **Fact** | Directly supported by a verified source - the two standard accounts held for this topic, the subject's master chronology, revision chart, syllabus mapping and folder README, the one core world-history book read for this session, a verified official question paper, or the single verification pass recorded in the source ledger | Assert it. Dates, names, treaties and recorded sequences can be stated flatly |
| ⚠️ **Analytical inference** | Interpretation, weighting, comparison or synthesis built on those facts | Argue it, signal it, and expect a counter-position |

Three further disciplines are specific to this topic, and each exists because this is the part of the syllabus where a fluent but wrong answer is easiest to write.

**The symmetry control.** The topic's title joins two words with "and", and the examiner almost always wants the *difference* between them. This session therefore refuses the parallel-narrative structure wherever the comparison is the demand. Where the two cases genuinely share a mechanism - the exclusion of Austria, the use of a core state, the conversion of nationalist legitimacy into state power - the shared mechanism is taught once and then differentiated. Where they diverge - popular military conquest in Italy with no German equivalent, an external great-power ally for Italy and deliberate isolation of enemies by Prussia - the divergence is taught as an argument with a cause, not as a pair of contrasting adjectives.

**The quotation and attribution control.** This topic attracts two famous sentences, one for each country: a phrase about iron and blood associated with Bismarck in 1862, and a phrase about having made Italy and now needing to make Italians. The standard account for this topic records an explicit control against reproducing Bismarck verbatim and carries the point with the word Realpolitik instead; and the second phrase turns out, on verification, to have a traditional but disputed attribution and no secure verbatim basis in the writings of the statesman to whom it is usually assigned. This session therefore teaches what each phrase is *for*, names the attribution problem openly, and does not supply either as a quotation you may reproduce as evidence. Similarly, the historiographical debates of Lesson 19 are taught as positions rather than as named schools, because this evidence base records the positions without attaching them to historians.

**The boundary control.** Four neighbouring topics own material that this one constantly touches. The Vienna settlement of 1814-15 - its principles, its personalities and its machinery of congresses - belongs to the topic before this one; this session uses only the settlement's *output*, which is the starting map, and the recorded finding that Vienna postponed rather than resolved the national question. Nationalism as a doctrine, alongside liberalism, conservatism and socialism, belongs to the overview topic that owns the political-philosophies clause; this session uses nationalism as a historical force and does not re-derive the doctrine. The alliance system, the armed peace and the outbreak of 1914 belong to the topic on the world in 1914; this session carries the consequences of 1871 up to the point where that topic begins and stops there. And the Indian reception of the Risorgimento - the nationalist leaders who wrote about Mazzini and Garibaldi and the use they made of the European example - belongs to the Modern Indian History owner; this session records the boundary where it arises and hands over rather than annexing.

*Read the two columns as separate machines built for the same purpose. The horizontal joins are the examinable content: both cases begin from the same settlement, both required Austria to be defeated, both used a core state, and both converted a movement's legitimacy into a dynasty's territory. The vertical differences are where the marks are: Italy needed an external ally and a popular army, Germany needed neither; Italy's completion was staggered across eleven years and depended twice on other powers' wars, Germany's was concentrated into seven years of wars it planned.*

**Scope of this session.** The Vienna settlement is used here only as the starting condition; its negotiation, principles and enforcement machinery belong to the topic that owns it. Nationalism, liberalism and conservatism as doctrines belong to the overview topic; this session uses them as historical forces. The pre-1914 alliance system, the arms race and the July crisis belong to the topic on the world in 1914; this session delivers the structural consequences of 1871 to that boundary and stops. The Anschluss question of 1938 and Italy's interwar collapse belong to their own owners; each is named once as a consequence line and is not narrated. Where a point is owned elsewhere, the lesson says so rather than re-narrating it.

## Lesson 1 - What "unification" actually names, and the two operations hidden inside one word

Progress: 1/20 | Stage: Foundation | Subtopic: nation, state and nation-state; consolidation against liberation as two distinct operations; the four levels at which "unified" is claimed; the Italian and German starting conditions compared

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the standard account's record that the Vienna settlement left Italy divided among Austrian-controlled or Austrian-influenced states, the Papal States and Sardinia-Piedmont, and Germany a loose confederation of thirty-nine states under Austrian influence; the subject revision chart's row treating national unification as nation-states built through leadership and war
CA search: not sought - this topic carries exactly one current linkage, held in Lesson 10
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: one word, two operations

```text
   THE WORD                        WHAT IT ACTUALLY DESCRIBES
   "unification"
        |
        +---> OPERATION ONE: CONSOLIDATION
        |     many sovereignties  ->  one sovereignty
        |     Italy: several separate states, each with its own ruler,
        |            army, tariff schedule, currency and legal code
        |     Germany: thirty-nine members of a confederation, each
        |            sovereign, meeting in a diet that could compel none
        |     THE OBSTACLE HERE IS INTERNAL: princes who will not abdicate
        |
        +---> OPERATION TWO: LIBERATION
              foreign rule and foreign veto  ->  self-rule
              Italy: Austria holds Lombardy and Venetia OUTRIGHT and shapes
                     the rest of the peninsula; France garrisons Rome
              Germany: no German land is foreign-occupied, but Austria
                     presides over the Confederation and blocks reorganisation
              THE OBSTACLE HERE IS EXTERNAL: a great power with an interest
              in keeping the map fragmented

   ------------------------------------------------------------------
   WHY THE DISTINCTION IS THE WHOLE TOPIC

   Italy must perform BOTH operations, and the external one first:
        defeat Austria  ->  then absorb the princes
        therefore Italy needs an ARMY IT DOES NOT HAVE
        therefore Italy needs an ALLY

   Germany must perform ONE operation in a peculiar form:
        Austria is not an occupier but a PRESIDING RIVAL
        therefore Germany's problem is not expulsion but EXCLUSION
        therefore the question becomes: which German state leads?
        therefore Prussia's own army is the instrument, and no ally is needed
   ------------------------------------------------------------------
```

*The diagram is the reason the two stories cannot be told in the same shape. An answer that says "both countries were divided and both were unified by war" has described the surface. The examinable content is that Italy faced an occupier and needed borrowed force, while Germany faced a competitor and needed only to win a family quarrel with the rest of Europe standing aside.*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

In 1815 a traveller crossing the Italian peninsula from Turin to Naples changed sovereign several times, paid customs duties at each border, carried different coins and argued in courts working under different codes. The same traveller crossing from the Rhine to the eastern marches passed through a dozen jurisdictions inside something that called itself a German Confederation but was not a country. Both populations shared a language, a literature and a sense of common descent. Neither had a state that corresponded to it.

That gap - between a people who feel like a nation and the political map they actually live under - is what nineteenth-century nationalism proposed to close. The proposal was simple to state and extraordinarily hard to execute, because closing the gap required somebody to lose something. Princes had to surrender sovereignty, a great power had to surrender influence, and a church had to surrender territory. None of them would do so because an argument had been made. Each of them had to be defeated, bought off, or outmanoeuvred.

So the real question of this topic is not "why did Italians and Germans want to be united?" - by the 1830s many of them plainly did - but "by what means could that want be made effective against people who had armies?" Every date in this session is an answer to that second question.

### 2. Three words that must not be used loosely

✅ **Fact:** the standard account for this topic records that after 1815 Italy remained divided among Austrian-controlled or Austrian-influenced states, the Papal States and Sardinia-Piedmont, and that Germany remained a loose confederation of thirty-nine states under Austrian influence.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** three terms must be separated before that sentence can be used.

| Term | Working definition | The test to apply | Where this topic uses it |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Nation** | A population that believes it shares descent, language, culture or history, and that this belief entitles it to govern itself | Ask whether the claim to common identity is being *made politically*, not whether it is objectively true | Italians and Germans are nations in this sense well before 1861 or 1871 |
| **State** | An organisation holding sovereign authority over a territory, with an army, a treasury and a legal order | Ask who may lawfully use force in that territory | Piedmont-Sardinia and Prussia are states; "Italy" and "Germany" are not, until they are made so |
| **Nation-state** | A state whose boundaries are justified by, and roughly coincide with, one nation's claim | Ask what the state says it is *for* | This is the output of 1861-71, and the reason the 1815 settlement could not survive |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the examination value of the distinction is that it lets you explain the sequence instead of asserting it. A nation without a state has enthusiasm and no army. A state without a national justification has an army and no claim on its neighbours' populations. Unification is what happens when one state decides to borrow the other's claim - and that transaction, rather than any single battle, is the mechanism this topic tests.

### 3. Consolidation and liberation, stated exactly

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** every unification movement in nineteenth-century Europe had to carry out one or both of two operations.

**Consolidation** is the merging of several sovereignties into one. Its obstacle is internal: rulers who will not give up their thrones, elites attached to local privilege, and populations who identify with a city or a region before a nation.

**Liberation** is the removal of foreign rule or foreign veto. Its obstacle is external: a great power whose interests are served by the existing fragmentation.

✅ **Fact:** in Italy, Austria dominated Lombardy-Venetia and shaped the peninsula after Vienna, and the historiographical companion records that this foreign rule sharpened nationalism.

✅ **Fact:** in Germany, the thirty-nine-state Confederation was dominated by Austria, and the historiographical companion treats that structure as the explanation for the scale of the German problem.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** Italy therefore had to do both operations, and had to do the external one first. You cannot absorb a central duchy while an Austrian field army stands in Lombardy able to march south. Germany had to do the internal operation in an unusual form: no German territory was under foreign occupation, but the state presiding over the Confederation, Austria, was itself only partly German and had every reason to prevent a consolidation it could not lead. Germany's problem was therefore not to expel a foreigner but to *exclude a rival* - which is why the decisive German war of the sequence is fought against Austria rather than against any outside power.

> 🔑 **Answer line:** Italian unification was a war of liberation that became a consolidation; German unification was a consolidation that required a rival to be excluded. Both needed Austria defeated, and only one of them needed a foreign ally to do it.

### 4. What "unified" is actually claiming - the four levels

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the word is used at four different levels, and a large share of weak answers slides between them without noticing.

| Level | What it means | When Italy reaches it | When Germany reaches it |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Territorial** | The claimed national territory lies inside one state's borders | Staggered across 1859, 1860, 1861, 1866 and 1870 - and still incomplete in the unredeemed lands | Concentrated in 1866-71, and deliberately incomplete because Austria's Germans are excluded |
| **Constitutional** | One legal order, one citizenship, one set of institutions | Piedmontese institutions extended outward from 1861 | A federal constitution written for the purpose in 1867 and 1871 |
| **Economic** | One market, one currency, one tariff frontier | Follows political union | *Precedes* political union, through the customs union of 1834 |
| **Social and cultural** | The population identifies primarily with the nation the state claims to embody | Contested for decades; the phrase about "making Italians" exists precisely because this level lagged | Contested too - the new Empire treated several of its own populations as suspect |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the German column contains the single most useful asymmetry in the topic. Economic union came *before* political union in Germany and *after* it in Italy. That one row explains why Prussian leadership was already a fact of German life before any war was fought, and why the Italian state had to build a national market after 1861 in a peninsula whose regions had been trading in different directions for centuries.

### 5. Why the two cases are not interchangeable, stated as four differences

✅ **Fact:** the standard account for this topic sets out the comparison directly - the main obstacle in Italy was Austrian domination plus political fragmentation and in Germany Austrian rivalry plus fragmentation; the key state was Piedmont-Sardinia in one case and Prussia in the other; Italy had a famous mass leader in Garibaldi while Germany had no equivalent mass military hero of the same kind; the key strategists were Cavour and Bismarck; and the character of the two processes was mixed - diplomacy plus mass campaign - in Italy and heavily state-driven and military in Germany.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** these are not four facts to be listed. They are one argument with four expressions, and the argument is about *who supplied the decisive force*.

1. **The obstacle differs in kind.** Domination must be defeated militarily; rivalry can also be defeated politically, by isolating the rival and making intervention on its behalf unattractive.
2. **The core state differs in capacity.** Prussia possessed a first-rank army and an existing economic leadership of the German states. Piedmont-Sardinia possessed neither on that scale, which is why its statesman had to purchase a French army with territory.
3. **The popular element differs in necessity.** The Italian south was conquered by a volunteer force, because no Piedmontese army could have been sent there openly without provoking a European crisis. No comparable German operation was needed, because Prussia could march in its own name.
4. **The resulting state differs in strength.** ⚠️ The standard account's own summary is that Italy emerged a middling power with weak state capacity and Germany the strongest continental military-economic power - and that this asymmetry feeds directly into the crisis of 1914 owned by a later topic.

### 6. What this lesson licenses you to say, and what it does not

| You may assert | You may not assert |
|---|---|
| That both unifications began from a settlement that had refused the national question | That Vienna "caused" unification; it created conditions and postponed a demand |
| That Austria had to be defeated in both cases | That the two wars against Austria were the same kind of war - one expelled an occupier, the other excluded a presiding rival |
| That nationalism supplied legitimacy and states supplied means | That nationalism by itself unified anything; Lesson 4 is the proof that it could not |
| That Italy's process was mixed and Germany's state-driven | That Italy was democratic and Germany authoritarian - both ended as monarchies, and the Italian republic was defeated inside the Italian movement |
| That the two processes produced very different states | That either outcome was inevitable; Lesson 19 handles the counterfactuals |

### Strongest objection, reply and residual

**Objection:** The distinction between consolidation and liberation is an analytical convenience. Contemporaries did not think in those terms; they thought in terms of independence, constitutions and dynastic ambition. Imposing a two-operation scheme on them is the kind of retrospective tidiness that makes examination answers neat and history false.

**Reply:** The objection is right about vocabulary and wrong about substance. Nobody in 1848 announced that they were performing operation two first. But the *constraint* the scheme describes was experienced directly and decisively: the Italian revolutions of 1848-49 were defeated by an Austrian field army, and the German assembly of the same years failed because it had no army at all. The scheme is a compression of what actually stopped them. Its test is predictive - it tells you in advance why the successful Italian route required a foreign ally and the successful German route did not - and it passes that test in both cases.

**Residual:** What the scheme does not settle is how much of the eventual outcome was driven by national feeling at all, as against dynastic aggrandisement wearing a national costume. The evidence base for this session records the national movements and the state actions but supplies no measurement of popular national sentiment in either country - no electoral series, no membership figures, no readership data. Lesson 19 therefore states that debate as unresolved rather than deciding it.

### UPSC application

This lesson supplies the opening move of every answer in the topic and the vocabulary that keeps the rest of the answer disciplined. The mark-earning move is to refuse the narrative opening - "Italy was divided into several states while Germany was a confederation of thirty-nine" - and substitute the analytical one: both nations faced a settlement that had denied them a state, but Italy faced an occupier and Germany a rival, which is why one needed a borrowed army and the other did not. For a "compare" directive this lesson supplies your axes; for a "from above or from below" directive it establishes that the question can receive different answers in the two cases, which is itself worth marks. Keep it to three or four sentences in a 250-word answer: this is framing, and the evidence lies in Lessons 5 to 16.

### Examiner traps in this lesson

- Writing "Italy and Germany were both divided, so both needed unification" - a description that contains no mechanism and therefore earns no analytical marks.
- Using "nation" and "state" interchangeably, which makes the whole transaction between movement and monarchy invisible.
- Treating Austria's role as identical in both cases, when it was a dominating power in one and a presiding rival in the other.
- Claiming that either country was "unified" at a single level, when the four levels were reached at different times and one of them was never fully reached in Italy.
- Beginning the answer with Cavour or Bismarck, which converts a structural question into biography.
- Assuming German economic union followed political union, when the recorded sequence in Germany is the reverse.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ After 1815 Italy was divided among Austrian-controlled or Austrian-influenced states, the Papal States and Sardinia-Piedmont.
2. ✅ After 1815 Germany was a loose confederation of thirty-nine states under Austrian influence.
3. ⚠️ Nation = a population claiming the right to self-government; state = the organisation holding sovereign authority; nation-state = a state justified by one nation's claim.
4. ⚠️ Unification has two operations: consolidation, whose obstacle is internal princes, and liberation, whose obstacle is an external power.
5. ⚠️ Italy needed both operations and the external one first; therefore it needed an ally.
6. ⚠️ Germany needed exclusion rather than expulsion; therefore Prussia's own army sufficed.
7. ⚠️ "Unified" operates at four levels - territorial, constitutional, economic and social - reached at different times.
8. ⚠️ Germany reached economic union before political union; Italy reached it after.
9. ✅ Main obstacle: Austrian domination plus fragmentation in Italy; Austrian rivalry plus fragmentation in Germany.
10. ✅ Key state: Piedmont-Sardinia and Prussia. Key strategist: Cavour and Bismarck. Mass leader: Garibaldi, with no German equivalent of the same kind.
11. ✅ Character: mixed diplomacy and mass campaign in Italy; heavily state-driven and military in Germany.
12. ⚠️ Outcome asymmetry: a middling power with weak state capacity against the strongest continental military-economic power.
13. ⚠️ Sentence to memorise: nationalism supplied the legitimacy, states supplied the means, and neither could have succeeded alone.

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate opens an answer with: "Both Italy and Germany were politically divided after 1815, and both were unified by strong leaders who used war." Identify what is analytically missing, and rewrite the opening so that it sets up a comparison rather than a narrative.

**Model answer:** Three things are missing. First, the *kind* of obstacle is not stated: Austria dominated Lombardy-Venetia and shaped the rest of the peninsula, so Italy faced a foreign occupying power, whereas in Germany Austria presided over a confederation of thirty-nine states and had to be excluded rather than expelled. Second, the consequence of that difference is not drawn: a dominating occupier must be defeated in the field, which is why Piedmont-Sardinia needed an external great-power ally, while Prussia, already leading the German states economically and possessing a first-rank army, needed to isolate Austria diplomatically rather than borrow anyone's force. Third, "strong leaders who used war" erases the popular element that is present in one case and absent in the other, since Garibaldi's volunteers conquered Sicily and Naples while Germany had no comparable mass military campaign. A rewritten opening runs: the Vienna settlement left Italy under Austrian domination and fragmentation and Germany in a thirty-nine-state confederation under Austrian influence, so that both nations required Austria to be defeated; but Italy had to combine an external alliance with a popular military campaign to achieve a staggered unification, whereas Prussia used its own army and its own diplomacy to exclude Austria and concentrate unification into seven years - and that difference in agency, not in enthusiasm, explains the very different states that emerged.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that because both processes ended in monarchies created by war, they were essentially the same process under different names. The obstacle differed in kind, the force used was borrowed in one case and native in the other, and the popular element was decisive in one and marginal in the other; that is precisely why the examiner sets the comparison.

**Bridge to Lesson 2:** If the starting condition was a settlement, the settlement's exact output is the first thing to get right - which territories, which rulers, which reinforced states - and after it the chronology this session is permitted to assert. The next lesson sets out the map of 1815 as it bears on this topic, the dated spine from 1831 to 1871, and the dating and attribution controls that prevent the four commonest factual errors in the topic.

## Lesson 2 - The map of 1815 and the chronology this session may assert

Progress: 2/20 | Stage: Foundation | Subtopic: the Vienna output as starting condition; the two nuclei created by the settlement they overthrew; the dated spine from 1815 to 1871; the two chronological spans; the dating, attribution and quotation controls

### Visual: the spine, with each marker's function attached

```text
   1815 ─── THE STARTING CONDITION ──────────────────────────────────────
        Italy: Austria holds Lombardy and Venetia and influences the rest;
               Papal States across the centre; the south under its own crown;
               SARDINIA-PIEDMONT STRENGTHENED BY GENOA
        Germany: CONFEDERATION OF 39 STATES under Austrian influence;
               PRUSSIA STRENGTHENED BY RHINELAND, WESTPHALIA, PART OF SAXONY
        FUNCTION: creates the problem AND, unintentionally, both instruments
                  that will later solve it
        |
   1831 ─── MAZZINI LAUNCHES YOUNG ITALY
        FUNCTION: republican nationalism acquires an organisation and a programme
        |
   1834 ─── ZOLLVEREIN, the Prussian-led German customs union
        FUNCTION: economic unification precedes political unification in Germany
        |
   1848 ─── REVOLUTIONS ACROSS EUROPE; liberal-national attempts fail
        FUNCTION: proves that national feeling without an army cannot unify;
                  redirects the movement toward statecraft, diplomacy and war
        |
   1859 ─── PIEDMONT-SARDINIA AND FRANCE DEFEAT AUSTRIA; LOMBARDY GAINED
        FUNCTION: unification moves from ideas to state action
        |
   1860 ─── GARIBALDI'S RED SHIRTS CONQUER SICILY AND NAPLES
        FUNCTION: the south enters the process by popular military action
        |
   1861 ─── KINGDOM OF ITALY PROCLAIMED UNDER VICTOR EMMANUEL II
        FUNCTION: political unification begins formally - note the word "begins"
        |
   1864 ─── DENMARK DEFEATED BY PRUSSIA AND AUSTRIA
        FUNCTION: first of the three wars; creates the dispute Prussia will use
        |
   1866 ─── PRUSSIA DEFEATS AUSTRIA (SEVEN WEEKS' WAR); VENETIA TO ITALY
        FUNCTION: Austria excluded from German affairs; Italy advances without
                  winning its own campaign
        |
   1870 ─── ROME JOINS ITALY AFTER FRENCH WITHDRAWAL
        FUNCTION: Italian unification completed territorially
        |
   Jan 1871 ─ GERMAN EMPIRE PROCLAIMED AT VERSAILLES
        FUNCTION: German unification completed under Prussian leadership
   ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
```

*Ten markers, each with a job. Two features are visible here that a bare list of dates hides: the Italian and German sequences interlock in 1866, when a Prussian war delivers Venetia to Italy; and Rome arrives in 1870 only because a French garrison leaves, which means the last act of Italian unification is a by-product of the war that completes German unification.*

### 1. Why the settlement is taught as an instrument and not only as an obstacle

✅ **Fact:** the standard account for this topic records that the Vienna settlement of 1815 left Italy divided and Germany in a thirty-nine-state confederation, so that nationalism remained politically unresolved.

✅ **Fact:** the standard account also records, in its comparison bank, that Piedmont-Sardinia was strengthened at Vienna by the addition of Genoa and that Prussia was strengthened by the Rhineland, Westphalia and Saxon lands - and it draws the conclusion that both nuclei were created by the settlement they later overthrew.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** that single observation is worth more in an answer than any number of narrative dates, and it is worth stating slowly. The congress was building a barrier against France. To do that it needed strong states on France's flanks. On the Italian flank it enlarged Piedmont-Sardinia with a major port. On the German flank it pushed Prussia westward into the Rhineland and Westphalia, which happened to be the coal and iron districts on which the next half-century of industrial and military power would rest. The men who made the settlement produced, as a by-product of anti-French containment, the two states that dismantled their design within sixty years.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the shape here is irony with a mechanism rather than irony as decoration. The mechanism is that a balance-of-power system rewards *capable* states, and capable states can use their capacity for purposes the system never intended. Use the point in a "how far did Vienna postpone the national question?" answer, and route the rest of that question to the topic that owns the settlement.

### 2. The dated markers, stated exactly

✅ **Fact:** the standard account's timeline for this topic records the following ten markers with these significances.

| Date | Event as recorded | Recorded significance |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ 1815 | The Vienna settlement leaves Italy divided and Germany in a thirty-nine-state confederation | Nationalism remains politically unresolved |
| ✅ 1831 | Mazzini launches Young Italy | Republican nationalism gains a clear voice |
| ✅ 1848 | Revolutions in Europe | Liberal-national attempts fail, but the issue survives |
| ✅ 1859 | Piedmont-Sardinia and France defeat Austria; Lombardy gained | Italian unification moves from ideas to state action |
| ✅ 1860 | Garibaldi's Red Shirts conquer Sicily and Naples | Southern Italy joins the unification process |
| ✅ 1861 | The Kingdom of Italy is proclaimed under Victor Emmanuel II | Political unification begins formally |
| ✅ 1864 | Denmark is defeated by Prussia and Austria | First of Bismarck's three wars |
| ✅ 1866 | Prussia defeats Austria in the Seven Weeks' War; Venetia goes to Italy | Austria is excluded from German affairs; Italy advances |
| ✅ 1870 | Rome joins Italy after French withdrawal | Italian unification is completed territorially |
| ✅ January 1871 | The German Empire is proclaimed at Versailles | German unification is completed under Prussian leadership |

✅ **Fact:** the subject's master chronology dates Italian unification to 1859-1871 with a core phase of 1859-61, recording that Cavour's diplomacy and Garibaldi's expedition unified the Italian states, with the Kingdom of Italy proclaimed in 1861 and Rome annexed in 1870; and it dates German unification to 1864-1871 through Bismarck's three wars - Danish, Austro-Prussian and Franco-Prussian - with the German Empire proclaimed at Versailles in 1871.

✅ **Fact:** the subject revision chart gives the same two spines as 1859-70 for Italy and 1864-71 for Germany and compresses them into two lines: "Mazzini-idea; Cavour-diplomacy; Garibaldi-action; Rome 1870" and "Bismarck; Denmark 1864; Austria 1866; France 1870-71".

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the conventions differ by a year at the Italian end - 1870 for territorial completion, 1871 for the transfer of the capital and the attempt to settle relations with the papacy - and either may be used provided you say which completion you mean. The safe formulation is "completed territorially in 1870, with its consequences settled over the following year".

### 3. The connecting detail this session verified, and why it is worth carrying

⚠️ **Why these extra markers are needed:** the two standard accounts held for this topic supply the ten-marker spine above and little more. Several standard connecting events carry no dates in them at all - the alliance that produced the war of 1859, the price paid for it, the manner in which the central duchies joined, the instrument that turned Garibaldi's conquest into an annexation, the intermediate confederation between the second and third German wars, and the treaty that ended the last war. The standard essentials below fill exactly those gaps. None of them overturns a standard account; each supplies the exact term where the teaching would otherwise have to be vague.

| Verified marker | Date | Why the teaching needs it |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ Piedmontese troops sent to the Crimean War | 1855 | Explains how a small kingdom bought a seat among the great powers |
| ✅ Congress of Paris, where the Italian question was raised | 1856 | Converts a military gesture into diplomatic standing |
| ✅ Plombieres meeting between Cavour and Napoleon III | 21 July 1858 | The alliance is planned before the war, which is the core of the "diplomacy first" argument |
| ✅ Armistice of Villafranca | 11 July 1859 | The war ends early and incompletely, which is the origin of the Venetia problem |
| ✅ Treaty of Turin ceding Nice and Savoy to France | 24 March 1860 | Names the price of the alliance |
| ✅ Plebiscites in Tuscany, Parma, Modena and Romagna | March 1860 | The instrument by which the central states joined |
| ✅ Garibaldi's Thousand land at Marsala | 11 May 1860 | The southern campaign acquires a start date |
| ✅ Garibaldi meets Victor Emmanuel II at Teano | 26 October 1860 | The exact moment the revolution transfers its conquest |
| ✅ Kingdom of Italy proclaimed | 17 March 1861 | The standard account's "1861", given precisely |
| ✅ Italian troops enter Rome through the Porta Pia | 20 September 1870 | The standard account's "1870", given precisely |
| ✅ Law of Guarantees regulating the papacy's position | 1871 | The Italian answer to the Roman question, and the reason the papacy rejected it |
| ✅ Zollverein, the Prussian-led German customs union | 1834 | Economic union precedes political union in Germany |
| ✅ Frankfurt National Assembly offers the imperial crown to Frederick William IV | April 1849 | The exact form of the German failure of 1848 |
| ✅ Punctation of Olmutz | November 1850 | An earlier Prussian bid fails and Austrian primacy is reaffirmed |
| ✅ Bismarck appointed minister-president of Prussia | September 1862 | The standard account's "1862", placed inside the budget crisis |
| ✅ Convention of Gastein dividing the administration of Schleswig and Holstein | August 1865 | The device that converts a joint victory into a usable quarrel |
| ✅ Battle of Koniggratz | 3 July 1866 | The decisive engagement of the Austro-Prussian war |
| ✅ Treaty of Prague | 23 August 1866 | Austria's exclusion put into a treaty |
| ✅ Peace of Vienna transferring Venetia for Italy | 3 October 1866 | Venetia is ceded by Austria for transfer, not won by Italy |
| ✅ North German Confederation constitution in force | 1867 | The state that exists between the second and third wars |
| ✅ Ems telegram published | 14 July 1870 | The provocation that produced the French declaration |
| ✅ France declares war on Prussia | 19 July 1870 | The war has a declarer, and it is not Prussia |
| ✅ Battle of Sedan and French surrender | 1-2 September 1870 | The military decision of the war |
| ✅ William I proclaimed German emperor at Versailles | 18 January 1871 | The standard account's "January 1871", given precisely |
| ✅ Treaty of Frankfurt | 10 May 1871 | Alsace and part of Lorraine annexed, with an indemnity of five billion francs |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** carry about a dozen of these into the hall rather than all twenty-five. The ones that do argumentative work are 1834, April 1849, 1856, 1858, 1859, the two markers of 1860, 1861, September 1862, 1864, 1866, 1867, the two markers of 1870 and January 1871. The rest are supporting precision to be used when space allows.

### 4. The four controls that prevent the standard errors

✅ **Fact:** the standard account for this topic states four factual-risk controls explicitly, and each corresponds to an error that appears in scripts every year.

| Control | The error it prevents | The correct formulation |
|---|---|---|
| **The Rome control** | Dating Rome's accession to 1861 | ✅ The Kingdom of Italy was proclaimed in 1861; Venetia joined in 1866; Rome joined in 1870 - three separate stages |
| **The Mazzini control** | Writing that Mazzini completed unification | ✅ He inspired it; Cavour, Garibaldi and Victor Emmanuel II completed it |
| **The Austria control** | Writing that Austria led German unification | ✅ Prussia led it, after defeating Austria in 1866 |
| **The assembly control** | Writing that Germany was unified by popular assemblies | ✅ Prussian power and war were decisive |

✅ **Fact:** the standard account adds two controls of a different kind. Bismarck's dates are to be given only as minister-president from 1862 and the empire proclaimed in January 1871 at Versailles; and Bismarck is not to be quoted verbatim, the point being carried instead by the term Realpolitik.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the first of those controls exists to bar *unsupported* dates, not correct ones. The dates this session supports for Bismarck are his appointment as minister-president in **September 1862**, the North German chancellorship from **1 July 1867**, the proclamation of the empire on **18 January 1871**, the imperial chancellorship from **21 March 1871** and the dismissal of **March 1890**. Assert nothing outside that set.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the quotation control deserves a word, because candidates resist it. The famous formulation about the great questions of the day being settled by iron and blood is genuinely associated with Bismarck's address to the Prussian budget committee in September 1862, and Lesson 12 explains what that speech was *doing*. But a quotation used as evidence must be exact, and this evidence base holds no authenticated text. The disciplined move is to name the occasion, explain the argument it made, and describe the method as Realpolitik - which is what the examiner is testing anyway. A paraphrase that explains outscores a half-remembered quotation that impresses nobody.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the same discipline applies to the Italian counterpart phrase about having made Italy and now needing to make Italians. The attribution to the statesman usually credited with it is traditional but disputed, the exact wording is not securely located in his principal memoir, and the phrase spread later and was put to political uses of its own. Teach the *problem* it names - a state ahead of its society, which is Lesson 10's central claim - and do not offer the sentence as a sourced quotation.

### 5. What must never be dated, and what must never be numbered

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** four categories are deliberately left without figures in this session, because no source read for it supplies them.

| Category | Why it is left empty |
|---|---|
| Populations, electorates, literacy and income in either country | No demographic, electoral or economic series is held here; the north-south comparison is therefore qualitative throughout, as the standard account's own caution requires |
| Army sizes, casualty totals and orders of battle | The standard accounts record which wars were fought and who won; they hold no force figures. None is asserted here for the Italian wars or for 1864 and 1866. For the war of 1870-71 alone a small number of exact figures is available, and they appear in Lesson 15 and nowhere else |
| Plebiscite percentages | The plebiscites are recorded as having occurred; no vote share is treated as reliable here, so the instrument is analysed and the numbers refused |
| Membership of national and secret societies | No membership figure is held; the organisations are taught by function |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the quantitative items this session does carry - the five-billion-franc indemnity of 1871, the fact that Piedmont sent a contingent to the Crimea, and the small set of 1870-71 force and casualty figures used in Lesson 15 - are used sparingly. Where the argument requires a magnitude no source supplies, this session teaches the direction of change and refuses the number. That is a limit on assertion, not on understanding.

### Strongest objection, reply and residual

**Objection:** A session that spends an entire lesson on dates is teaching trivia. Unification is about forces - nationalism, industrialisation, the balance of power - and an answer built on a date list reads as a chronicle, which is exactly what examiners penalise.

**Reply:** The objection is right about date *lists* and wrong about dates. A date in this topic is a compressed argument. "1834" argues that German economic integration preceded political integration, and therefore that Prussian leadership existed before any war. "April 1849" argues that the liberal route was tried, completed a constitution, and was refused - so its failure was not a failure of effort. "1858 before 1859" argues that the war was the execution of a diplomatic plan rather than an outbreak of national enthusiasm. Used this way, five dates carry five arguments. The chronicle problem arises when dates are recited without their functions, which is precisely what the annotated spine above prevents.

**Residual:** What this evidence base cannot settle is the internal chronology of popular national feeling - when, and among which social groups, the national idea actually became widespread in either country. The standard accounts record organisations and events, not the diffusion of sentiment. That gap is why Lesson 19 treats the "how national was it?" question as open, and why no claim about mass national consciousness at any date appears in this session.

### UPSC application

This lesson is the answer's skeleton and its insurance policy. The skeleton is the annotated spine: any 250-word answer on either unification can be built by selecting four or five markers and stating each one's function. The insurance is the control table, which prevents the four errors that cost marks mechanically rather than analytically - Rome in 1861, Mazzini as completer, Austria as leader, assemblies as instrument. A useful habit is to write 1859 - 1860 - 1861 - 1866 - 1870 for Italy and 1862 - 1864 - 1866 - 1867 - 1871 for Germany in the margin before starting, then delete whichever markers the specific directive does not need.

### Examiner traps in this lesson

- Dating Rome's accession to 1861, the single most penalised error in the topic.
- Collapsing 1861, 1866 and 1870 into one "unification of Italy" date.
- Giving Bismarck dates outside the supported set of September 1862, 1 July 1867, 18 January 1871, 21 March 1871 and March 1890.
- Offering the iron-and-blood formulation as a verbatim quotation.
- Offering the "make Italians" phrase as a sourced quotation from a named statesman.
- Supplying plebiscite percentages, army sizes or population figures that no source here holds.
- Treating 1866 as an Italian military victory, when Venetia arrived through a Prussian war and a transfer.
- Forgetting that Rome became available only because a French garrison was withdrawn during the Franco-German war.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ 1815: Italy divided; Germany a thirty-nine-state confederation; nationalism unresolved.
2. ✅ Vienna strengthened Sardinia-Piedmont with Genoa and Prussia with the Rhineland, Westphalia and Saxon lands.
3. ⚠️ Both nuclei were created by the settlement they overthrew - an irony with a mechanism, not a decoration.
4. ✅ 1831 Young Italy; 1848 revolutions fail; 1859 Lombardy; 1860 Sicily and Naples; 1861 Kingdom of Italy; 1864 Denmark; 1866 Austria and Venetia; 1870 Rome; January 1871 the German Empire.
5. ✅ Master chronology spans: Italy 1859-1871 with a core phase of 1859-61; Germany 1864-1871.
6. ✅ Verified connecting markers: 1834 Zollverein; April 1849 the refused crown; November 1850 Olmutz; 1855 Crimea; 1856 Congress of Paris; 21 July 1858 Plombieres; 11 July 1859 Villafranca; 24 March 1860 Nice and Savoy; March 1860 the duchies' plebiscites; 11 May 1860 Marsala; 26 October 1860 Teano; 17 March 1861 the proclamation; August 1865 Gastein; 3 July 1866 Koniggratz; 23 August 1866 Prague; 3 October 1866 Vienna and Venetia; 1867 the North German Confederation; 14 July 1870 the Ems telegram; 19 July 1870 the French declaration; 1-2 September 1870 Sedan; 20 September 1870 Rome; 18 January 1871 the proclamation; 10 May 1871 the Treaty of Frankfurt.
7. ✅ Four controls: Rome 1870 not 1861; Mazzini inspired and did not complete; Prussia led, not Austria; power and war decided, not assemblies.
8. ✅ Bismarck's supported dates: minister-president from September 1862; North German chancellor from 1 July 1867; empire proclaimed 18 January 1871 at Versailles; imperial chancellor from 21 March 1871; dismissed March 1890.
9. ⚠️ Do not quote Bismarck; use Realpolitik. Do not quote the "make Italians" phrase; teach the problem it names.
10. ⚠️ No population, electoral, income, casualty, army-size or plebiscite-percentage figure is available here; use direction of change instead.
11. ⚠️ The two sequences interlock at 1866 and 1870 - a Prussian war delivers Venetia, and a Prussian war frees Rome.
12. ⚠️ A date earns its place by doing argumentative work; five functional markers beat a dozen recited ones.

### Concept check

**Question:** Using only four dates on each side, construct two separate arguments: one showing that Italian unification was staggered and dependent on external events, and one showing that German unification was concentrated and self-directed. State which dating conventions you are entitled to assert.

**Model answer:** The Italian argument uses 1859, 1861, 1866 and 1870. Lombardy was gained in 1859 only because a French army fought alongside Piedmont-Sardinia; the Kingdom of Italy was proclaimed in 1861 without Venetia or Rome; Venetia arrived in 1866 as a consequence of Prussia's victory over Austria rather than of an Italian battlefield success, being ceded by Austria for transfer rather than surrendered to Italy directly; and Rome arrived in 1870 only when the French garrison protecting the papacy was withdrawn during the Franco-German war. Four dates therefore show a completion spread over eleven years, with the two final acquisitions produced by other powers' wars. The German argument uses 1862, 1864, 1866 and January 1871. Bismarck became minister-president in September 1862 in the middle of a domestic constitutional conflict over the army budget; the Danish war of 1864 created the dispute that could be turned against Austria; the Seven Weeks' War of 1866 excluded Austria from German affairs and was followed by a new confederation under Prussian leadership in 1867; and the empire was proclaimed at Versailles in January 1871. Four dates therefore show a sequence executed in nine years by one state, each war creating the condition for the next. The conventions that may be asserted are the master chronology's spans - 1859-1871 for Italy with a core phase of 1859-61, and 1864-1871 for Germany - together with the standard account's ten-marker timeline; the distinction between territorial completion in 1870 and the settlement of its consequences in 1871 must be stated rather than blurred, and Bismarck's dates confined to 1862 and January 1871.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating 1866 as a joint Italian-Prussian victory in which Italy won Venetia by arms. The transfer followed Prussia's victory and was executed through a cession for transfer to Italy; Italian forces did not defeat Austria, and an answer implying that they did misstates exactly the dependence that makes the Italian case distinctive.

**Bridge to Lesson 3:** The spine shows what happened but not what the national movements actually had to work with before the successful phase began. The next lesson examines the materials available before 1848 - the Risorgimento and its competing programmes, Mazzini's organisation of 1831, the societies that preceded him, and the German peculiarity of a customs union that unified the market a generation before anyone unified the state.

### Visual: three Italian programmes and one German short cut

```text
   ITALY BEFORE 1848 - THREE ANSWERS TO "HOW?"

   +------------------------+------------------------+------------------------+
   | REPUBLICAN-UNITARY     | FEDERAL-PAPAL          | MODERATE-MONARCHICAL   |
   | Mazzini, Young Italy   | a league of existing   | enlarge and reform the |
   | from 1831              | princes with the Pope  | one credible state and |
   |                        | as its presiding head  | let it lead            |
   |------------------------|------------------------|------------------------|
   | WHO ACTS?              | WHO ACTS?              | WHO ACTS?              |
   | the people, by         | the existing rulers,   | Piedmont-Sardinia,     |
   | insurrection           | by agreement           | by war and diplomacy   |
   |------------------------|------------------------|------------------------|
   | END-STATE              | END-STATE              | END-STATE              |
   | one republic, indivi-  | Italy united without   | a kingdom formed by    |
   | sible, no monarchy     | anyone losing a throne | annexation to Piedmont |
   |------------------------|------------------------|------------------------|
   | FATAL FLAW             | FATAL FLAW             | FATAL FLAW             |
   | no army; risings are   | requires the Pope to   | requires a great-power |
   | crushed by regular     | fight Catholic Austria | ally, and the ally     |
   | troops                 | - which he will not do | charges for the favour |
   +------------------------+------------------------+------------------------+
                 |                     |                       |
        1848-49 TESTS ALL THREE AT ONCE - AND ALL THREE FAIL
                 |                     |                       |
                 +----------> only the third is REPAIRABLE <----+
                              (find the ally: Lesson 6)

   ------------------------------------------------------------------
   GERMANY BEFORE 1848 - A DIFFERENT KIND OF PREPARATION

   1818  Prussia abolishes its internal customs dues and offers free
         trade to neighbours
   1828  a southern union (Bavaria, Wurttemberg) and a central German
         union form separately
   1834  ZOLLVEREIN: 18 states join a single customs area led by Prussia
   1854  Hanover and Oldenburg join
   1867  the Mecklenburgs, Schleswig-Holstein, Lauenburg and Lubeck join
   1888  Hamburg and Bremen adhere, 17 years after the Empire

   RESULT BY THE 1860s: all Germany outside AUSTRIA is inside one market
   led by Prussia. The economic map of "little Germany" exists before the
   political one, and Austria is already outside it.
   ------------------------------------------------------------------
```

*The two halves of the diagram are not parallel and are not meant to be. Italy's pre-1848 preparation was ideological and organisational; Germany's was, in addition, institutional and economic. That asymmetry is the reason the German answer to "which state leads?" was partly settled before a shot was fired, while the Italian answer had to be extracted from a defeat.*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

Suppose you are convinced in 1830 that Italy should be one country. What do you actually do on Monday morning? You cannot vote, because there is no national assembly to vote in. You cannot campaign openly, because the police of several states will arrest you. You cannot raise an army, because you have no state and no revenue. The gap between conviction and capability is total.

The nineteenth-century answers to that practical question fall into three families, and the whole of the Italian story before 1859 is the sequential testing of those three families. You can conspire in secret and rise when the moment comes. You can persuade the existing rulers to federate voluntarily. Or you can pick the one Italian state that has an army and a treasury and attach your movement to it.

Germany faced the same question with one extra resource. Germany's states were sovereign and quarrelsome, but their economies were adjacent and their merchants were tired of paying tolls. So a fourth answer was available there that was not available in Italy: unify the *market* first, and let political unification follow the customs frontier. That answer was being executed from 1834 onward, quietly, by officials rather than revolutionaries, and it turned out to matter enormously.

### 2. Risorgimento: what the word names

✅ **Fact:** the historiographical companion records that **Risorgimento** refers to the movement for Italian unification.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the word means a re-arising or resurgence, and the choice of word is itself an argument. A movement that calls itself a *resurgence* is claiming that the nation already exists, has merely been submerged, and is returning to a condition it once held. That claim did political work: it converted unification from an innovation, which conservatives could attack, into a restoration, which they found harder to attack. Every national movement in Europe made some version of this move, and noticing it is worth a sentence in any answer about how nationalism justified itself.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the Risorgimento is not a single organisation, a single programme or a single class. It is a label covering everything from poetry and opera to conspiracies, pamphlets, exile networks, diplomatic memoranda and finally armies. When an examiner asks about "the role of the Risorgimento", the marks lie in distinguishing its currents rather than praising its spirit.

### 3. The three programmes, and why each was incomplete

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** three distinct answers to the "how" question circulated before 1848, and they were rivals, not allies.

**The republican-unitary programme.** ✅ **Fact:** the standard account for this topic records that Mazzini founded Young Italy and argued for a united *republican* Italy, and it classifies his role as the ideological inspiration of the movement; the historiographical companion adds that Young Italy spread a republican and national message and that this ideological groundwork mattered. The programme's content was that Italy should be a single indivisible republic, created by the Italian people themselves through insurrection, without foreign help and without a king.

**The federal-papal programme.** ⚠️ **Analytical inference:** a second current proposed that Italy be united as a confederation of its existing states under the moral presidency of the papacy, which would preserve every throne and avoid revolution altogether. ⚠️ This session's evidence base holds no dedicated source for that current's authors or texts, so it is taught here as a *position* rather than attached to a name; what matters for an answer is its structural flaw, not its bibliography. The flaw was fatal and simple: a federation led by the Pope would have had to expel Austria from Lombardy-Venetia, and the Pope was the head of a church whose most powerful Catholic protector was Austria. The programme required its own leader to make war on his principal ally.

**The moderate-monarchical programme.** ⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the third current proposed that unification be led by the one Italian state with a functioning army, treasury and diplomatic service - Piedmont-Sardinia - and that liberals should work through its institutions rather than against them. ✅ Its eventual dominance is recorded in the standard account's judgement that Piedmont-Sardinia became the *nucleus* of unification and in the historiographical companion's statement that unification required a functioning state.

| Programme | Agent of change | What it needed that it did not have | Verdict after 1849 |
|---|---|---|---|
| Republican-unitary | The people, in arms | Regular troops able to defeat an imperial field army | Defeated militarily, but its legitimacy survived and was later borrowed |
| Federal-papal | The existing princes, led by the papacy | A papacy willing to fight Catholic Austria | Destroyed by its own internal contradiction |
| Moderate-monarchical | Piedmont-Sardinia | A great-power ally, and a reason for that ally to act | The only one that could be *repaired*, and it was repaired in 1858 |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** notice the verdict column, because it is the argument. The three programmes did not simply lose to one another on merit; two of them had structural flaws that no amount of enthusiasm could fix, while the third had a *missing input* that could in principle be obtained. Cavour's achievement, taught in Lessons 5 and 6, is precisely the obtaining of that missing input.

### 4. Secret societies and Young Italy: what an organisation adds to an idea

✅ **Fact:** the historiographical companion records that Mazzini, secret societies and Garibaldi mobilised nationalism in Italy - that is, it treats the secret societies as one of the three named channels of mobilisation from below.

⚠️ **Analytical reference:** the standard name for the best-known network of conspiratorial societies in the Italian states of the 1820s is the Carbonari, and their risings of the 1820s and early 1830s are part of the ordinary account of the period. ⚠️ This session's evidence base holds no dedicated source for that organisation, so the name is offered here as an analytical reference in the standard account's own style rather than as a sourced fact of this folder, and no date, membership or programme is attached to it.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** what matters analytically is the *difference* between a secret society and what Mazzini built in 1831, because that difference is a genuine step in political technique.

| Feature | Conspiratorial society of the 1820s | Young Italy, from 1831 |
|---|---|---|
| **Aim** | Usually a constitution in one state, or the removal of one ruler | A single Italian republic - a national aim from the outset |
| **Method** | Secret cells, oaths, a rising timed to a favourable moment | Conspiracy plus open propaganda, journalism, exile networks and education of opinion |
| **Membership logic** | Recruit notables and sympathetic officers | Recruit the young, deliberately, on the argument that a generation had to be formed |
| **Relation to the future state** | Vague; the rising would decide | Specified in advance: republican, unitary, and made by Italians without foreign help |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** Young Italy therefore did three things no secret society had done. It fixed the *end-state* in advance, so that the movement had a programme rather than a grievance. It treated propaganda as a weapon equal to conspiracy, which created a national public where none had existed. And it defined membership by generation rather than by rank, which is why the organisation's name is an age group. ✅ The standard account's classification of Mazzini as the movement's ideological inspiration is an accurate compression of exactly these three innovations.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the limitation must be stated with equal clarity, and it is the hinge of Lesson 4. An organisation that can produce opinion and volunteers but cannot produce artillery will lose to a state that has artillery. Mazzini's programme forbade foreign help on principle, which meant that his movement was structurally incapable of acquiring the one input it lacked. That is not a personal failure; it is a design consequence.

### 5. The German preparation: culture, and then a customs frontier

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** German nationalism before 1848 had a cultural base of a kind Italy also possessed - a shared literary language, a scholarly interest in national antiquity, student associations, gymnastic and choral societies - and the Vienna-era order suppressed the political expression of that base, which is the material owned by the topic on the Vienna settlement and the Concert of Europe. This session takes that suppression as given and turns to what was distinctive.

✅ **Fact:** the distinctive German development was the **Zollverein**, a German customs union established in **1834 under Prussian leadership**, which created a free-trade area across much of Germany and is standardly treated as an important step toward German unification.

✅ **Fact:** its build-up ran as follows. In **1818** Prussia enacted a tariff law abolishing all internal customs dues and announced its willingness to establish free trade with neighbouring states. A decade later it signed the first such pact with Hesse-Darmstadt. In **1828** a customs union was set up in southern Germany by Bavaria and Wurttemberg, joined by the Palatinate in 1829, while the central German states formed a similar union including Saxony, the Thuringian states, electoral Hesse and Nassau. In **1834** these were among the **eighteen states** that joined in the Zollverein. Hanover and Oldenburg joined in **1854**; the two Mecklenburgs, Schleswig-Holstein, Lauenburg and Lubeck in **1867**; and thereby **all Germany outside Austria** was included except Hamburg and Bremen, which adhered in **1888**, seventeen years after the German Empire was established.

✅ **Fact:** the movement to create a German free-trade zone received great impetus from economists, of whom the most active advocate in early nineteenth-century Germany was Friedrich List.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** four consequences follow, and they are the reason this lesson exists.

1. **A "little Germany" existed economically before it existed politically.** The customs area covered Germany *minus Austria*. Every year the union operated, the habit of treating Prussia as the natural organising centre and Austria as an outsider became more entrenched - not by argument, but by invoicing.
2. **Prussia acquired a peacetime instrument of leadership.** A customs union is negotiated, administered and adjusted continuously. The state that sets its terms acquires a routine and legitimate authority over the others' commercial life without firing a shot.
3. **Commercial interests were converted into national interests.** Merchants and manufacturers who had wanted cheaper internal trade found that they had acquired a stake in a larger German political frame. ⚠️ This is the mechanism by which liberal and business opinion in Germany came to accept Prussian leadership long before 1866 - and it is the best single explanation of why German liberalism eventually settled for unity without liberty, which is Lesson 14's argument.
4. **The 1867 and 1888 accessions show the sequence continuing after politics.** ✅ The northern coastal states joined in the same year the North German Confederation's constitution came into force, and the two great free ports adhered only in 1888. ⚠️ Economic unification was therefore not a single act completed before politics; it was a long process that politics accelerated and completed.

> 🔑 **Answer line:** In Germany the customs frontier drew the map that the army later enforced; in Italy no comparable instrument existed, so the map had to be drawn by war and plebiscite from the start.

### 6. Why the comparison here is an argument, not a contrast

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** it is tempting to convert this lesson into a neat opposition - Italy had ideas, Germany had economics. That is too clean, and an examiner will puncture it. Three qualifications keep the comparison honest.

- Italy also had material change: Piedmont's reforms, railways and budget policy under Cavour are exactly an attempt to build the economic sinews the peninsula lacked, and Lesson 5 records them. The difference is that Piedmont built *its own* capacity, whereas the Zollverein built a *shared* institution that others joined.
- Germany also had ideas: the cultural nationalism of the pre-1848 period is real, and the Frankfurt assembly of 1848 is its political expression. The difference is that the German idea acquired an institutional ally that the Italian idea did not.
- ⚠️ The customs union did not by itself unify Germany, and saying so is the standard overstatement in this area. It made Prussian leadership plausible and Austrian participation awkward. The decision still had to be taken by war in 1866, and the choice of war was a political act, not an economic inevitability.

### Strongest objection, reply and residual

**Objection:** Treating the Zollverein as a step toward unification is reading the outcome backwards. It was a fiscal arrangement made by finance officials for revenue and convenience. Merchants wanted cheaper trade, not a nation-state; Prussia wanted revenue and influence, not German unity; and several members of the union fought *against* Prussia in 1866. The claim that it prepared political unification is a teleological story fitted to a known ending.

**Reply:** The objection is correct on motives and wrong on effects, and the distinction is the reply. Nobody needs to have intended unification for the union to have produced conditions favourable to it. Three effects are independent of intention: the union defined a German economic space that excluded Austria, so that when the political question was finally posed as "with Austria or without?", the economic answer had been given for thirty years; it gave Prussia continuous administrative authority over the others' commercial life, which is a form of leadership that requires no battle; and it attached propertied interests to a larger German frame. The 1866 point actually strengthens the reply rather than weakening it: several Zollverein members did fight against Prussia, which proves that the union did not determine political allegiance - and yet the union survived the war and was extended in 1867, which shows how deeply the economic frame had been established.

**Residual:** What the evidence base does not settle is the *weight* of the economic factor against the military-diplomatic one in explaining German unification. No output, trade, revenue or membership series is held here, so this session cannot quantify the union's effect or show how much of Prussian primacy it produced. Lesson 19 therefore treats "economic base or military-diplomatic decision?" as an open debate, and Lesson 17 teaches how to write a ranked answer on it without inventing figures.

### UPSC application

This lesson supplies two things examiners reward. The first is the three-programme typology, which turns "the role of the Risorgimento" from a paragraph of praise into an analysis of competing methods with structural flaws. The second is the 1834 date, which is the single most under-used fact in the topic: any answer on why Prussia rather than Austria led Germany that does not mention that all Germany outside Austria was inside a Prussian-led customs union has left out the cheapest available mark. For "ideas or state power?" directives, this lesson gives you the honest middle position - ideas supplied the programme and the personnel, institutions supplied the leadership, and neither produced a state until a war was fought.

### Examiner traps in this lesson

- Treating the Risorgimento as one movement with one aim, when it contained three rival programmes with incompatible end-states.
- Presenting Mazzini's republicanism as a detail; his refusal of monarchy and of foreign help is precisely what made his route unworkable and the moderate route repairable.
- Writing that the Zollverein "caused" German unification - it made Prussian leadership plausible and Austrian membership awkward; the political decision still came by war.
- Dating the customs union loosely; the year is 1834 and the membership at foundation was eighteen states.
- Forgetting that Austria was outside the union, which is the whole point of mentioning it in a unification answer.
- Attaching sourced-fact status to organisations, authors or texts that this evidence base records only as positions.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Risorgimento = the movement for Italian unification; ⚠️ the word claims a resurgence, converting innovation into restoration.
2. ✅ Mazzini founded Young Italy and argued for a united republican Italy; the standard account classifies him as the ideological inspiration.
3. ✅ Young Italy was founded in the early 1830s, and the standard account's timeline dates its launch to 1831.
4. ✅ The historiographical companion names Mazzini, secret societies and Garibaldi as the channels of mobilisation from below.
5. ⚠️ Three programmes: republican-unitary, federal-papal, moderate-monarchical - with insurrection, princely agreement and Piedmontese leadership as their respective agents.
6. ⚠️ Republican flaw: no army. Federal-papal flaw: the Pope would have had to fight Catholic Austria. Moderate flaw: needs a great-power ally - the only *repairable* flaw.
7. ⚠️ Young Italy's three innovations: a fixed end-state, propaganda as a weapon equal to conspiracy, and recruitment by generation.
8. ✅ Zollverein: German customs union established 1834 under Prussian leadership; eighteen states at foundation.
9. ✅ Build-up: Prussian tariff law of 1818 abolishing internal dues; southern and central German unions in 1828; accessions in 1854, 1867 and 1888.
10. ✅ All Germany outside Austria was eventually included; Hamburg and Bremen adhered in 1888, seventeen years after the Empire.
11. ✅ Friedrich List was the most active advocate of a German free-trade zone.
12. ⚠️ Four effects: a little-German economic map; a peacetime instrument of Prussian leadership; commercial interests converted into national ones; and an economic sequence that politics later completed.
13. ⚠️ The honest limit: the union made Prussian leadership plausible; it did not decide the question, which was decided by war in 1866.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why the customs union of 1834 is relevant to a question about *political* unification, and then state the strongest qualification a careful answer must add.

**Model answer:** Under Prussian leadership the union of 1834 created a single market covering much of Germany, and by the 1860s it included all Germany outside Austria; its political relevance is threefold. It drew, in commercial terms, precisely the boundary that the settlement of 1866-71 would later draw, so the little-German solution excluding Austria had been operating economically for a generation before it was imposed politically. It gave Prussia continuous, routine and legitimate authority over the commercial life of the other German states, which is a form of leadership acquired without war and without the resentment a conquest produces. And it attached merchants, manufacturers and officials to a larger German frame in which Prussia was the organising centre, which helps explain why propertied and liberal opinion later accepted Prussian primacy. The necessary qualification is that none of this determined the outcome: several members of the union fought on Austria's side in 1866, which shows that economic membership did not dictate political allegiance; the decisive act was a war chosen for reasons of state; and this evidence base holds no trade or revenue series by which the economic factor's weight could be measured against the military-diplomatic one. The defensible formulation is that the customs frontier drew the map and the army enforced it.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that because economic unification preceded political unification in Germany, economics caused the unification. Sequence is not causation. The union removed obstacles and shaped expectations; the choice to settle the question by three wars was a political decision taken by a government that could have chosen otherwise, and Lesson 19 treats the relative weighting as genuinely open.

**Bridge to Lesson 4:** Three Italian programmes and one German assembly were all about to be tested simultaneously. The next lesson takes the year in which every method available to the national movements was tried at once and every one of them failed - and extracts from that failure the specific lesson that produced the successful strategies of the 1850s and 1860s.

## Lesson 4 - 1848: every method tried at once, and what the failure actually taught

Progress: 4/20 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the Italian revolutions of 1848-49 and their defeat; the Frankfurt Parliament, the little-German and great-German options and the refused crown of April 1849; Olmutz in 1850; the four structural lessons the failure delivered; why the survivors changed method rather than aim
