### Visual: two revolutions, one structural verdict

```text
   ITALY 1848-49                          GERMANY 1848-49
   ------------                           ---------------
   Jan 1848  revolt at Palermo;           Mar 1848  risings force German
             constitutions granted                  princes to concede
             across several states                  and to permit elections
        |                                      |
   Mar 1848  Piedmont's king grants a     May 1848  NATIONAL ASSEMBLY meets
             constitution (which will               at Frankfurt - elected,
             outlive the revolution) and            deliberative, respectable
             DECLARES WAR ON AUSTRIA               |
        |                                      |
   Jul 1848  DEFEAT AT CUSTOZA;           Mar 1849  a constitution is completed
             Austria recovers Lombardy-        |
             Venetia                       Apr 1849  THE CROWN IS OFFERED TO
        |                                            THE KING OF PRUSSIA
   Feb 1849  ROMAN REPUBLIC proclaimed;                  |
             Mazzini in its triumvirate;         AND IS REFUSED
             Garibaldi organises defence              |
        |                                      the assembly disperses;
   Mar 1849  DEFEAT AT NOVARA; the king    1850  OLMUTZ: Prussia abandons its
             abdicates; VICTOR EMMANUEL          own union scheme; Austrian
             II succeeds                         primacy reaffirmed
        |
   Jul 1849  French troops crush the
             Roman Republic and restore
             the Pope
        |
   Aug 1849  Venice surrenders to Austria

   ================ THE COMMON VERDICT ================
   ITALY: an army existed (Piedmont's) but was too weak alone,
          and the popular republics had no army at all
   GERMANY: an assembly existed, wrote a constitution, and had
          no army, no treasury and no power to compel a king
   IN BOTH CASES: LEGITIMACY WITHOUT FORCE WAS DEFEATED BY
   FORCE WITHOUT LEGITIMACY. The next generation reversed the
   problem: take a state that has force, and lend it legitimacy.
   ====================================================
```

*The diagram's two columns fail in different ways for the same reason. Italy's revolutions had soldiers but not enough of them, and no ally. Germany's revolution had a parliament, a drafted constitution and a formal offer of a crown, and no means whatever of enforcing any of it. The bottom panel is the sentence to carry into the hall.*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

1848 is the year in which almost every political demand in Europe was raised at once - constitutions, national unity, independence from empires, wider suffrage, relief for the poor - and in which almost all of them were defeated within eighteen months. For this topic, the year matters not as a narrative but as an experiment. Every available method for making a nation was tried in public and under pressure, and the results came back quickly.

The republicans tried insurrection: defeated by regular troops. The liberals tried a parliament: it drafted, debated and offered, and a king declined. The moderates tried a princely war of liberation: it won at first and then lost decisively. The federal-papal current discovered that the papacy would not lead a national war against a Catholic empire. By August 1849 every route had been tested, and the old order was back in place almost everywhere.

The interesting question is therefore not why 1848 failed - the answers are visible in the diagram - but what the *survivors concluded*. And the answer to that question is the reason this lesson sits where it does, immediately before the lessons on Cavour and Bismarck.

### 2. Italy in 1848-49, stated exactly

✅ **Fact:** the standard account for this topic records the 1848 entry as revolutions in Europe in which liberal-national attempts failed while the issue survived.

✅ **Fact:** the verified sequence runs as follows. A revolt broke out at **Palermo in January 1848**, liberal and separatist in character, and the king of the Two Sicilies was forced to grant a constitution, which set off a wave of constitutional concessions elsewhere in Italy. In **March 1848** Charles Albert, king of Piedmont-Sardinia, granted the liberal **Statuto Albertino**, which remained the basis of Italy's constitution into the twentieth century, and then declared war on Austria, beginning the first war of Italian independence; Lombardy and Venetia briefly expelled Austrian forces and sought union with Piedmont. In **July 1848** the Piedmontese were decisively defeated by the Austrian field marshal Radetzky at **Custoza**, and Austria regained most of Lombardy-Venetia. In **February 1849** Rome declared itself a republic, led by a triumvirate that included **Mazzini**, with **Garibaldi** organising its military defence. In **March 1849** Charles Albert renewed the war and was defeated at **Novara**, after which he abdicated in favour of his son, **Victor Emmanuel II**. France, seeking to support **Pope Pius IX**, besieged Rome and crushed the Roman Republic by **July 1849**, restoring papal authority. **Venice**, which had declared itself a republic under Daniele Manin, surrendered to Austria in **August 1849**. The outcome was that Austrian dominance was restored in the north and former rulers returned in the centre and south, while the Statuto survived in Piedmont.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** three features of that sequence do the analytical work.

1. **The war of 1848 was fought by a *state*, and still lost.** This is decisive for what follows. The failure was not merely that revolutionaries were disorganised; it was that the most capable Italian state, fighting alone against Austria, was beaten twice. The conclusion is not "Italy needs a better revolution" but "Piedmont needs an ally".
2. **The republic was destroyed by a foreign Catholic power acting for the papacy.** That single fact makes the Roman question permanent: the papacy's temporal position in central Italy was not merely an Italian matter but a European one, protected by French arms. ⚠️ Twenty-one years later, Rome becomes available to Italy at exactly the moment that French protection is withdrawn, which is Lesson 9's point.
3. **One institution survived the defeat: the Piedmontese constitution.** ⚠️ A single surviving constitutional state gave Italian liberals somewhere to go - a parliament to sit in, a press to write for, an army to serve in. The Risorgimento after 1849 is, in large part, the story of talent migrating to the one place where it could act legally.

### 3. Germany in 1848-49: the assembly that had everything except power

✅ **Fact:** the account of the Vienna settlement records the failed **Frankfurt** attempt at German unification and draws the general lesson that liberal nationalism had no instrument of force.

✅ **Fact:** the historiographical companion records that in Germany the 1848 liberals tried to make a nation, and that unification was ultimately achieved instead by Prussia and Bismarck through war and diplomacy.

✅ **Fact:** the verified detail records that the **Frankfurt National Assembly**, the first all-German parliament, met from **May 1848** at St Paul's Church in Frankfurt am Main; that it drafted a constitution **adopted on 28 March 1849** envisaging a unified, parliamentary, constitutional German empire with universal male suffrage, a hereditary emperor and a national parliament; that in **April 1849** it offered the imperial crown to **Frederick William IV**, king of Prussia; and that he **refused** it, on the ground that a crown offered by a popular assembly was not a crown he could accept.

✅ **Fact:** by the **Punctation of Olmutz** of **November 1850**, Prussia abandoned its own attempt to organise a smaller German union without Austria, and Austrian leadership of the German Confederation was reaffirmed.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the refusal of April 1849 is the most instructive single event in the German half of this topic, and it repays being unpacked into its three components.

| Component of the refusal | What it revealed | Consequence for the later route |
|---|---|---|
| **The source of the offer** | A crown conferred by an elected assembly implied that sovereignty lay with the nation and was merely delegated to a monarch. A king who accepted it would be admitting that his title came from below | Unification would have to be granted from above, or it would not be granted at all |
| **The rival it would offend** | Accepting would have meant assuming leadership of Germany against Austria's claim - a step Prussia was not yet willing or able to take | The Austro-Prussian question had to be settled *first*, and by force; the assembly had tried to settle it by vote |
| **The absence of compulsion** | The assembly had no army, no revenue and no administration; when the king said no, nothing followed | Any future unification would be executed by an institution that already possessed force |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the two options the assembly had debated are worth naming because they define the entire subsequent German settlement. A **little-German** solution meant a Germany under Prussian leadership *excluding* Austria; a **great-German** solution meant including Austria's German lands, which would have required the Habsburg monarchy to dismember its own multinational empire or to bring its non-German subjects into Germany. The assembly's constitutional draft pointed toward the little-German answer, and ✅ the eventual settlement of 1866-71 was explicitly of that form - the standard account for this topic records that Austria was excluded in 1866 so that unification was "small German" in form.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** Olmutz in 1850 completes the demonstration. When Prussia attempted its own version of a union of German states without Austria, it was compelled to abandon the attempt - which established that the little-German solution was not merely a constitutional preference but a claim that Austria would resist, and that Prussia could not yet make good. Everything Prussia does between 1862 and 1866 is preparation to make that claim good.

### 4. The four lessons the failure delivered

✅ **Fact:** the standard account for this topic states the central conclusion directly - in both cases the failure of 1848 pushed unification away from liberal idealism alone and toward statecraft, diplomacy and war - and the historiographical companion adds that idealist nationalism survived while power passed to monarchies and ministers.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** that conclusion can be decomposed into four operational lessons, and a strong answer states them as lessons rather than as consequences.

1. **Legitimacy does not move troops.** An assembly that represents a nation still cannot compel a king, and a republic that embodies a people's will can still be dissolved by a foreign expeditionary force. Force had to be acquired, not deserved.
2. **The national question is a European question.** Austria intervened in Italy because Italy was in its sphere; France intervened in Rome because the papacy was a French interest. A domestic settlement of a national question therefore required the consent, neutrality or defeat of the relevant great powers - which makes diplomacy a precondition of unification rather than an accompaniment to it.
3. **A single state with an army is worth more than a coalition of movements.** Piedmont lost in 1848-49 but survived as a constitutional state with an army; the republics did not survive at all. ⚠️ The asset that mattered was continuity of institutions, not intensity of commitment.
4. **Aims survive; methods change.** ✅ The standard account's timeline records precisely this - the attempts failed but the issue survived. The generation of the 1850s did not abandon unification; it abandoned insurrection and parliamentary persuasion as the *means*.

> 🔑 **Answer line:** 1848 did not discredit the national idea; it discredited the national idea's instruments. After 1849 the question was no longer whether Italians and Germans should have states, but which existing state would build them, and on whose terms.

### 5. Continuity and rupture across 1849: what actually carried over

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** answers frequently treat 1848 as a clean break, after which "realists" replaced "idealists". That is too simple in three specific ways, and noticing them is what converts a good answer into a very good one.

| Apparently broken | Actually carried over | Why it matters |
|---|---|---|
| The republican programme | Its *personnel* and its *legitimacy*. Garibaldi, who organised the Roman Republic's defence in 1849, commands the decisive popular campaign of 1860 in a monarchy's name | The monarchy's victory was achieved partly by employing the republic's most effective soldier |
| The liberal constitutional programme | The Piedmontese Statuto of 1848 survived the defeat and became the constitutional frame of the future kingdom | Italy's eventual constitution is a survival of 1848, not a creation of 1861 |
| The Frankfurt programme | Universal male suffrage for a national parliament reappears in the constitutions of 1867 and 1871 | ⚠️ The later Prussian-led settlement adopted a democratic-looking form from the liberal programme while withholding parliamentary control of the executive - a borrowing that is central to Lesson 16 |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the pattern in all three rows is the same and is worth naming: the successful unifiers did not defeat the movements of 1848; they *absorbed* selected parts of them - a soldier, a constitution, a suffrage - while discarding the part that threatened them, which was popular sovereignty. That is the precise content of the phrase "revolution from above", and it is why Lesson 17 insists that the from-above and from-below alternatives are not exclusive.

### Strongest objection, reply and residual

**Objection:** Calling 1848 a failure is a verdict written by the winners. The revolutions ended serfdom in parts of central Europe, forced constitutions that in some cases survived, created a generation of politically experienced men, and made the national question permanent. On any reckoning other than territorial, 1848 partly succeeded - and the standard "failure" narrative exists mainly to make Cavour and Bismarck look necessary.

**Reply:** The objection is right that the year had durable effects and wrong that this rescues the *method*. Distinguish outcomes from instruments. The outcomes were mixed: the Piedmontese constitution survived and became Italy's, the Frankfurt draft's suffrage principle reappeared in 1867 and 1871, and the personnel of 1848-49 staffed the later movements. But the instruments - the barricade, the elected assembly without an army, the appeal to a sovereign's conscience - were tested against regular troops and lost every time within eighteen months. The failure is a failure of means, precisely stated, and that is exactly why the successor strategies were built around borrowed or native military force. ⚠️ The suspicion that the failure narrative flatters the later statesmen is fair as a caution, and Lesson 19 takes it seriously; but the two defeats at Custoza and Novara and the refusal of April 1849 are not narrative constructions.

**Residual:** What the evidence base cannot settle is how close any of these attempts came to succeeding - whether a different Piedmontese command in 1848, an earlier French neutrality, or a Prussian king willing to accept the crown would have changed the outcome. Counterfactuals of that kind require evidence about capabilities and intentions that this session does not hold, and Lesson 19 states explicitly which counterfactuals may be entertained in an answer and which may not.

### UPSC application

This lesson is the pivot of the whole topic and is therefore the paragraph that most often separates a mid-range answer from a high one. Any question containing "from above or from below", "ideas or state power", or "role of nationalism" is really asking you to explain the transition that 1848 produced. The mark-earning structure is: state what was attempted, state what defeated it, and then state the *lesson drawn* rather than the emotion felt. Two sentences will do it - the revolutions of 1848 demonstrated that national legitimacy could not command force, and the generation that followed therefore attached the national claim to states that already possessed it. For a question specifically on German unification, the refusal of April 1849 is the single most efficient piece of evidence available, because it simultaneously proves the weakness of the liberal route and identifies the little-German solution that the wars later imposed.

### Examiner traps in this lesson

- Writing that 1848 "failed everywhere" without specifying what failed - the aims survived, the instruments did not.
- Omitting the Frankfurt Parliament from a German unification answer, which loses the proof that the liberal route was tried and refused.
- Saying the Frankfurt assembly was dissolved by force, when the decisive event was a refusal that it had no power to contest.
- Treating the little-German and great-German options as trivia; they define the shape of the state actually created in 1871.
- Forgetting Olmutz, and therefore treating 1866 as Prussia's first attempt to exclude Austria rather than its second.
- Presenting 1848 in Italy as purely republican, when the decisive war was declared by a monarch and the surviving institution was his constitution.
- Claiming a clean break between idealists and realists, when the later settlement absorbed a soldier, a constitution and a suffrage from 1848.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ 1848: revolutions in Europe; liberal-national attempts fail, the issue survives.
2. ✅ Palermo revolt, January 1848; constitutions conceded in several Italian states.
3. ✅ The Statuto Albertino, March 1848, survived the defeat and remained the basis of Italy's constitution into the twentieth century.
4. ✅ Charles Albert declared war on Austria in March 1848; defeated at Custoza, July 1848; defeated again at Novara, March 1849; abdicated in favour of Victor Emmanuel II.
5. ✅ Roman Republic, February 1849, with Mazzini in the triumvirate and Garibaldi organising its defence; crushed by French intervention by July 1849, restoring Pope Pius IX.
6. ✅ Venice, under Manin, surrendered to Austria in August 1849.
7. ✅ The Frankfurt National Assembly met from May 1848; adopted a constitution on 28 March 1849 with a hereditary emperor and a parliament elected by universal male suffrage; offered the crown to Frederick William IV in April 1849; he refused it.
8. ✅ Punctation of Olmutz, November 1850: Prussia abandoned its own union scheme and Austrian primacy was reaffirmed.
9. ⚠️ Little-German = Germany under Prussia excluding Austria; great-German = including Austria's German lands; ✅ the 1866 settlement was small-German in form.
10. ⚠️ Four lessons: legitimacy does not move troops; the national question is a European question; a state with an army beats a coalition of movements; aims survive while methods change.
11. ⚠️ Three carry-overs: Garibaldi's personnel, the Statuto's constitutionalism, and universal male suffrage reappearing in 1867 and 1871.
12. ⚠️ The formulation to memorise: 1848 discredited the instruments of nationalism, not the idea.
13. ⚠️ Rome's dependence on French protection, demonstrated in 1849, is the reason Rome becomes available in 1870.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why the refusal of the imperial crown in April 1849 is more useful evidence in a German unification answer than the dispersal of the Frankfurt assembly, and connect the refusal to the form the German Empire eventually took.

**Model answer:** The dispersal shows only that the assembly ended; the refusal shows *why* it could never have succeeded, and it does so in three moves that a good answer can use directly. First, it establishes the constitutional objection: a crown conferred by an elected assembly implies that sovereignty belongs to the nation and is merely delegated upward, which a Prussian king was unwilling to accept, so unification by popular grant was ruled out and unification by princely grant was the only remaining route - exactly the form used in January 1871, when the empire was proclaimed by princes at Versailles rather than conferred by any parliament. Second, it establishes the inter-state objection: accepting the crown would have meant claiming leadership of Germany against Austria, a claim Prussia was not yet willing to make and, as the Punctation of Olmutz of November 1850 then demonstrated, not yet able to sustain; so the Austro-Prussian question had to be settled by force before any German constitution could be written, which is what 1866 did. Third, it establishes the power objection: when the king declined, the assembly had no army, no revenue and no administration with which to insist, which is the general proposition that liberal nationalism possessed legitimacy without an instrument of force. The connection to the eventual form is direct: the empire of 1871 adopted the liberal programme's parliament elected by universal male suffrage, which had appeared in the Frankfurt constitution of March 1849, while withholding from that parliament any control over the army, the chancellor or foreign policy - unity granted from above, with the democratic form retained and the democratic substance withheld.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the Frankfurt assembly as merely a talking shop that achieved nothing. It completed a constitution, selected the little-German solution, and established universal male suffrage as a German national principle; all three reappeared in the settlement of 1867 and 1871. Its failure was a failure of enforcement, not of thought, and an answer that dismisses it loses the strongest available demonstration of why force determined the outcome.

**Bridge to Lesson 5:** If the lesson of 1849 was that a movement must attach itself to a state that possesses force, the immediate question becomes what kind of state can perform that role - and whether Piedmont-Sardinia, twice beaten by Austria, could be rebuilt into one. The next lesson examines how a small kingdom was deliberately converted into an instrument: its reforms, its finances, its army, and the most unusual investment in the topic, a war in the Crimea that had nothing to do with Italy.

## Lesson 5 - Converting a small kingdom into an instrument: Cavour's Piedmont

Progress: 5/20 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: what a "core state" must possess; Cavour's programme of reform, diplomacy and war; the Crimean intervention of 1855 and the Congress of Paris of 1856; the moderate capture of the national movement; Victor Emmanuel II and the division of labour inside the Piedmontese state

### Visual: what a core state has to be able to do

```text
   THE REQUIREMENT                          PIEDMONT'S ANSWER, 1849-1859
   ==============================================================================
   1. SURVIVE DEFEAT WITH ITS
      INSTITUTIONS INTACT       ------->   the 1848 constitution is kept after the
                                           1849 defeat; a parliament, a press and
                                           a legal opposition continue to function
   ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   2. BE ABLE TO PAY            ------->   reform of finances, commerce and
      FOR A WAR                            infrastructure; the state borrows,
                                           invests and taxes as a modern government
   ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   3. ATTRACT THE NATIONAL      ------->   exiles, liberals and moderates from every
      MOVEMENT'S TALENT                    Italian state converge on the one place
                                           where politics is legal
   ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   4. BE TREATED AS A           ------->   TROOPS TO THE CRIMEA, 1855
      GREAT-POWER PARTNER                  CONGRESS OF PARIS, 1856: the Italian
      RATHER THAN A PETITIONER             question is raised at the table where
                                           great powers settle Europe's business
   ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   5. OBTAIN AN ALLY WILLING    ------->   the missing input - supplied in 1858
      TO FIGHT AUSTRIA                     (Lesson 6)
   ==============================================================================

   THE DIVISION OF LABOUR INSIDE THE PIEDMONTESE STATE

        VICTOR EMMANUEL II  ----  the crown: legitimacy, army, the person to
        (king from 1849)          whom conquered territory can be offered
                 |
                 |  appoints and can dismiss
                 v
        CAVOUR              ----  the minister: finance, diplomacy, parliament,
        (prime minister)          the alliance, the timing of the war
                 |
                 |  tolerates, uses and restrains
                 v
        THE MOVEMENT        ----  volunteers, insurrections, Garibaldi's prestige:
        (Mazzinian and            deniable, cheap, and dangerous to its employer
         democratic currents)
```

*The five-row table is the definition of a core state, and it is reusable: Lesson 11 applies exactly the same five requirements to Prussia and finds that four of them were already satisfied there before any statesman arrived. The lower diagram is the machine that actually did the work - a king who could receive territory, a minister who could obtain it, and a movement that could take risks the state could disown.*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

After 1849 the Italian national movement had a verdict but no vehicle. The verdict was that insurrection alone could not beat an imperial army. The vehicle had to be a state - and the only candidate was a medium-sized kingdom in the north-west that had just lost two wars in two years.

So the practical question of the 1850s was: can a state of that size be made capable of defeating Austria? The honest answer was no, not alone, and it remained no throughout. What could be changed was everything around that fact. A state can make itself solvent. It can make itself attractive to the talent of a whole peninsula. It can make itself useful to a great power, and thereby acquire the right to ask a great power for something. And it can make itself the obvious beneficiary of any future Italian upheaval, so that when insurrections happen elsewhere, the territory ends up attached to it rather than to a republic.

That is the programme of the 1850s, and it is why this lesson is about finance, railways, parliaments and an expedition to a war in the Black Sea rather than about battles in Italy.

### 2. Cavour, stated exactly

✅ **Fact:** the standard account for this topic records **Cavour** as prime minister of Piedmont-Sardinia, who used diplomacy and war against Austria, and classifies his role as the political strategist of unification.

✅ **Fact:** the historiographical companion records that Piedmont used **diplomacy, reform and war with French help in 1859**, and draws from it the general proposition that unification required a functioning state.

✅ **Fact:** the standard account's comparison bank states Cavour's method as diplomacy plus war against Austria with French help in 1859, and contrasts it with Bismarck's: minister-president from 1862, three wars. Its conclusion from the contrast is that **Italy needed an external great-power ally and Prussia did not**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the word to hold on to in the standard account's formulation is **reform**, because it is the part candidates omit. Diplomacy and war are visible; the unglamorous decade of budgets, commercial treaties, railways, harbours and administrative modernisation is what made the diplomacy credible. A great power does not ally with a bankrupt client. The sequence is therefore reform first, standing second, alliance third, war fourth - and an answer that starts at the war has skipped the causal chain.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this evidence base holds no budget totals, railway mileages, tariff schedules or trade figures for Piedmont, so the reform programme is taught here as a direction of change - a state made solvent, connected and commercially open - and no number is asserted. That limitation is recorded rather than concealed.

### 3. The Crimean intervention, and what it bought

✅ **Fact:** in **1855**, under Cavour, Piedmont-Sardinia sent troops to join Britain and France in the **Crimean War** against Russia. ⚠️ The figure usually given for the contingent is approximately eighteen thousand men. It is approximate and contested; it is carried here only as the usual account, not as a figure this evidence base can independently confirm, and it must not be asserted as exact.

✅ **Fact:** the intervention allowed Cavour to take part in the **Congress of Paris in 1856**, where he raised the **Italian question** - the continued Austrian presence in the peninsula and the condition of the Italian states - before the assembled great powers.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is the most instructive single manoeuvre in the Italian half of the topic, because it looks irrational and is not. Piedmont had no interest whatsoever in the Black Sea. It sent soldiers to fight and die in a quarrel that concerned it not at all. What did it buy?

| What was spent | What was bought | Why it could not be bought otherwise |
|---|---|---|
| A military contingent in a war of no Italian relevance | The status of a *participant* rather than a supplicant | Great powers grant audiences to petitioners and seats to allies; the difference is not politeness but standing |
| The budgetary cost of the expedition | A working relationship with France and Britain at the level of governments and armies | Personal appeals and pamphlets had been tried since the 1830s and produced sympathy without commitment |
| The political risk of an unpopular foreign war | The right to place the Italian question on a great-power agenda in 1856 | There was no other forum; the Confederation of Europe's congresses were the only venue where the map was discussed |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** three consequences follow, and they should be stated separately in an answer.

1. **The Italian question was Europeanised on Italy's own initiative.** In 1848 Austria and France had intervened in Italy for their own reasons. In 1856 an Italian government placed Italy on the agenda for *its* reasons. That is a reversal of who holds the initiative.
2. **The claim was reframed from revolution to misgovernment.** ⚠️ Raising the condition of the Italian states before a congress of monarchies converts the demand from "the people should rise" into "the existing arrangements are unstable and badly run", which is a claim conservative powers can entertain without endorsing insurrection.
3. **It made an alliance thinkable.** ⚠️ A government that has fought alongside you and sat with you at a peace congress is a government you can plausibly meet in secret two years later to plan a war. The 1856 congress is the necessary precondition of the 1858 meeting in Lesson 6.

> 🔑 **Answer line:** Cavour's most important military decision before 1859 was to fight a war that had nothing to do with Italy - because it converted Piedmont from a petitioner into a participant, and only participants get alliances.

### 4. The moderate capture of the national movement

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** while the state was being rebuilt, a second and quieter process was running: the transfer of the national movement's centre of gravity from the republicans to the moderates. It happened through four mechanisms, none of which required anyone to change their mind about Italy.

1. **Legality.** Piedmont was the only Italian state where a liberal could publish, organise, stand for election and speak in a chamber without being arrested. Talent migrates to where it can act.
2. **Demonstrated failure of the alternative.** ✅ The standard account's judgement that after 1848 unification moved toward diplomacy and war is a statement about where credibility had gone; the republican route had been tested publicly and had lost.
3. **A visible beneficiary.** Once Piedmont was accepted as the nucleus, every local upheaval anywhere in Italy acquired an obvious destination: union with Piedmont rather than a local republic. ⚠️ This converts scattered discontent into a single directional force, which is the political meaning of the standard account's statement that **Piedmont-Sardinia became the nucleus of unification**.
4. **Employment of the opposition's assets.** ⚠️ The monarchy did not need to destroy Garibaldi's prestige; it needed to be the address to which his victories were delivered. Lesson 8 is the working out of exactly that relationship.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the cost of the capture must be stated with the benefit, because the examiner will expect it. ✅ The standard account records as a limit of Italian unification that Mazzini's republic was displaced by a monarchy and that the mass movement's own programme was not what was built. A movement that gets its territory by borrowing a monarchy's army ends up with the monarchy's constitution.

### 5. The division of labour: king, minister and movement

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** Piedmont's effectiveness came from a division of labour among three actors whose interests overlapped without coinciding.

| Actor | Contribution | Interest | Risk to the others |
|---|---|---|---|
| ✅ **Victor Emmanuel II**, king of Piedmont-Sardinia and then king of Italy in 1861 | Legitimacy, command of the army, and a throne to which conquered territory could be attached | Enlarging his dynasty's kingdom | Might prefer dynastic gains to national ones, or accept a compromise peace |
| ✅ **Cavour**, prime minister | Finance, diplomacy, parliamentary management, the alliance and the timing | A liberal, enlarged, north-Italian-led kingdom | Might restrain the movement at the moment it was most effective |
| ⚠️ **The democratic and Mazzinian currents**, including Garibaldi's volunteers | Manpower, insurrectionary energy and moral prestige | A united Italy, ideally a republic | Might act at the wrong moment and provoke a European coalition |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the relationship between the second and third rows is the heart of the Italian case and the sharpest available contrast with Germany. A volunteer force is **deniable**: when it acts, the state can disclaim it; if it succeeds, the state can bless the result. That instrument allowed Piedmont to expand into territories it could not have invaded openly without a European crisis. It also created a permanent hazard, because a deniable force has its own politics - and in 1860 it came within sight of acting on them.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** for a "from above or from below" directive, this table is the answer in miniature. Italy's unification used both, in a specific and unequal partnership: the movement supplied force the state could not openly use, and the state supplied the legitimacy and the international settlement the movement could not obtain. Neither could have finished the job alone, and the state ended up owning the result.

### Strongest objection, reply and residual

**Objection:** This account makes Piedmont sound like a national liberation movement with a treasury. It was nothing of the kind. It was an ambitious dynastic state pursuing territorial aggrandisement in northern Italy, willing to cede Italian-speaking provinces to France when it suited, and interested in the south only when a volunteer army threatened to create a rival power there. Calling that "unification" is accepting the winners' description of their own conduct.

**Reply:** The objection is substantially right about motive and wrong about the conclusion it draws. Three points keep the account honest without surrendering the analysis. First, the dynastic motive is conceded and is visible in the record: ✅ the territory ceded to France in 1860 was Italian-speaking, and ✅ the standard account itself records the monarchy absorbing the popular gain in 1861 as the "from above" half of the process. Second, motive does not determine effect: a dynastic state pursuing expansion under a national banner still produced a national state, and the banner was not optional, because without the national claim the other Italian populations would have had no reason to prefer annexation to Piedmont over their existing rulers. Third, the interesting analytical question is not whether Cavour was sincere but what the *combination* of a dynastic state and a national movement could achieve that neither could achieve alone - which is exactly what the division-of-labour table sets out. ⚠️ The strongest formulation for an answer is that Italian unification was a national project executed by a dynastic instrument, and that the instrument shaped the result.

**Residual:** What this evidence base cannot settle is the internal history of Piedmontese decision-making - how far the king and the minister agreed, how far each used the volunteers knowingly, and what alternatives were considered and rejected. No correspondence, cabinet record or parliamentary debate is held here. The session therefore teaches the division of labour as a functional description of what the three actors contributed, and refuses to narrate intentions that it cannot document.

### UPSC application

This lesson supplies the part of the Italian answer that most candidates omit entirely: the decade of preparation between the failure and the war. In a 15-mark answer, two sentences on it change the structure of the whole script - because they convert "Cavour allied with France and defeated Austria" into "Cavour spent ten years making Piedmont solvent, respectable and useful, which is why France was available as an ally in 1858". For a question on the role of Cavour, the Crimean-to-Paris sequence is the single most efficient evidence unit in the topic, since it demonstrates strategy rather than biography. For a "from above or from below" question, the three-actor table gives you a structured answer instead of a verdict.

### Examiner traps in this lesson

- Describing Cavour only as a diplomat, and omitting the reform programme that made the diplomacy credible.
- Treating the Crimean expedition as a curiosity, when it is the mechanism by which standing was acquired.
- Confusing the Congress of Paris of 1856 with the later peace settlements of the Italian wars.
- Supplying Piedmontese budget, railway or trade figures, none of which is held in this evidence base.
- Writing that Cavour aimed from the beginning at a united Italy including the south, which this evidence base does not establish and Lesson 8 shows to be doubtful.
- Presenting the monarchy and the movement as simple allies, when the relationship was one of use and restraint.
- Forgetting that Victor Emmanuel II succeeded in 1849 as a consequence of the defeat at Novara, which ties this lesson back to the previous one.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Cavour was prime minister of Piedmont-Sardinia and the political strategist of unification; his method was diplomacy and war against Austria.
2. ✅ Piedmont used diplomacy, reform and war with French help in 1859; unification required a functioning state.
3. ✅ Piedmont-Sardinia became the nucleus of unification.
4. ⚠️ Five requirements of a core state: survive defeat with institutions intact; be able to pay for a war; attract the movement's talent; be treated as a partner; obtain an ally.
5. ✅ 1855: Piedmontese troops sent to the Crimean War alongside Britain and France.
6. ✅ 1856: at the Congress of Paris, Cavour raised the Italian question before the great powers.
7. ⚠️ What the intervention bought: standing rather than sympathy; a reframing from revolution to misgovernment; and the possibility of a secret alliance two years later.
8. ⚠️ Four mechanisms of the moderate capture: legality, the demonstrated failure of insurrection, a visible beneficiary for every local upheaval, and employment of the opposition's best soldier.
9. ✅ The cost of the capture: Mazzini's republic was displaced by a monarchy, and the mass movement's own programme was not what was built.
10. ⚠️ The three-actor division of labour: the king supplies legitimacy and a throne, the minister supplies finance and diplomacy, the movement supplies deniable force.
11. ⚠️ A deniable volunteer force lets a state expand where it cannot openly invade - and creates the hazard that it may act on its own politics.
12. ⚠️ No Piedmontese budget, railway, tariff or trade figure is available here; teach direction of change.
13. ⚠️ Sentence to carry: Cavour's decisive pre-war move was a war that had nothing to do with Italy.

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate writes: "Cavour was a clever diplomat who persuaded France to help Italy." Explain what this sentence leaves out about how a small state acquires a great-power ally, using the events of 1855 and 1856.

**Model answer:** It leaves out the difference between persuasion and standing. Appeals to France on Italy's behalf had been made by exiles and publicists since the 1830s and had produced sympathy without commitment, because a great power does not commit an army on the strength of an argument made by people who can offer nothing in return. What changed in the 1850s was Piedmont's position rather than the eloquence of its case. First, the state was rebuilt: the constitution granted in 1848 survived the defeat of 1849, so Piedmont remained the one Italian state with a parliament, a press and a legal politics, and its finances, commerce and infrastructure were reformed so that it could be treated as a solvent partner rather than a client. Second, in 1855 it sent troops to the Crimean War alongside Britain and France, a war in which it had no Italian interest whatever, thereby purchasing the status of a co-belligerent. Third, that status admitted Cavour to the Congress of Paris in 1856, where he raised the Italian question before the assembled great powers - which both placed Italy on the European agenda at Italian initiative and reframed the demand from insurrection into a problem of instability and misgovernment that conservative cabinets could discuss. Only after all of that was a secret meeting to plan a joint war a realistic proposition. The corrected sentence is therefore that Cavour made Piedmont useful to France before he asked France for anything, and the usefulness, not the persuasion, produced the alliance.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that the alliance of 1858-59 was obtained by diplomacy alone in the sense of skilful negotiation. Negotiation distributed the gains; the reason a negotiation was possible at all was that Piedmont had spent a decade making itself solvent, respectable and militarily useful. An answer that treats diplomacy as persuasion rather than as the exchange of services misses the mechanism the examiner is testing.

**Bridge to Lesson 6:** The instrument now exists, and it has a relationship with a great power. What remains is the transaction itself - what was agreed, what was fought, what was actually obtained, and what the ally charged. The next lesson takes the alliance from the secret meeting of July 1858 to the abrupt armistice of July 1859 and the cession of two provinces in March 1860, and draws from it the general rule about the cost of borrowed force.

## Lesson 6 - Plombieres, the war of 1859, and the price of a borrowed army

Progress: 6/20 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the secret agreement of July 1858; the war of 1859 and the gain of Lombardy; the armistice of Villafranca and the incompleteness it created; the cession of Nice and Savoy in March 1860; the general rule that an ally's war aims are not the client's

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the transaction, its execution, and its shortfall

```text
   STEP 1 - THE BARGAIN IS STRUCK BEFORE THE WAR
   21 July 1858, PLOMBIERES: a secret meeting between Cavour and
   Napoleon III plans a Franco-Piedmontese alliance against Austria,
   with territorial gains foreseen for BOTH sides.
                          |
                          v
   STEP 2 - THE WAR IS FOUGHT, 1859
   Piedmont-Sardinia and France defeat Austria.
                          |
          +---------------+----------------+
          |                                |
   EXPECTED OUTCOME                  ACTUAL OUTCOME
   Austria expelled from both        11 July 1859, VILLAFRANCA:
   Lombardy AND Venetia              France makes an armistice with
                                     Austria; LOMBARDY is gained,
                                     VENETIA IS NOT
                          |
                          v
   STEP 3 - THE BILL IS PRESENTED ANYWAY
   24 March 1860, TREATY OF TURIN: NICE AND SAVOY are ceded by
   Piedmont-Sardinia to France, as agreed in exchange for French support.
                          |
                          v
   THE NET POSITION AT THE END OF THE TRANSACTION
   GAINED : Lombardy; the demonstration that Austria can be beaten;
            a wave of upheaval in the central states (Lesson 7)
   LOST   : two provinces, one of them Garibaldi's birthplace
   UNPAID : Venetia - which Italy will obtain only in 1866, through
            somebody else's war
   ------------------------------------------------------------------
   THE GENERAL RULE THIS TEACHES
   A borrowed army fights for the lender's war aims, stops when the
   lender's aims are met, and charges the agreed price in full even
   when the agreed objective is not delivered.
   ------------------------------------------------------------------
```

*Follow the four boxes in order and the whole lesson is visible: a plan made in advance, a war that half-succeeded, a bill paid in full, and a debt left outstanding for seven years. The last box is the transferable principle, and it is what a "role of foreign assistance" question is actually testing.*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

You have a state, a treasury and an army that cannot beat Austria. A neighbouring great power can. How do you get it to fight your war?

You cannot pay it in money, because you do not have enough. You cannot compel it. You can only offer it something it wants - influence, territory, prestige, or a strategic result it desires for its own reasons - and hope that the exchange survives contact with events. The essential feature of such a bargain is that the two parties want *different* things from the same war. Your aim is the expulsion of Austria from Italy. The ally's aim is a weakened Austria, an enlarged sphere of influence, some territory, and a manageable outcome that does not provoke the other great powers into forming a coalition against it.

Those two aims coincide for a while and then diverge - and they diverge at the moment when continuing the war would produce more for you and more risk for the ally. That is what happened in July 1859, and understanding it is worth more than any number of battle names.

### 2. The bargain, stated exactly

✅ **Fact:** on **21 July 1858** a secret meeting took place at **Plombieres** between **Cavour** and **Napoleon III**, at which a Franco-Piedmontese alliance against Austria was planned, with territorial gains foreseen for both sides.

✅ **Fact:** the standard account for this topic records that in **1859** Piedmont-Sardinia and France **defeated Austria** and that **Lombardy** was gained, with the significance that Italian unification moved from ideas to state action.

✅ **Fact:** the historiographical companion records that **French help mattered in 1859** and contrasts this directly with Bismarck's practice of isolating his enemies diplomatically - the sharpest single line of difference between the two unifications.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** three features of the bargain are examinable.

1. **It was secret, and it was prior.** The alliance was planned in July 1858, ten months before the fighting. This is the decisive evidence against any account of 1859 as a spontaneous national uprising that France joined. The war was an instrument of policy, arranged in advance between two governments.
2. **It was a purchase, not a gift.** Territorial gains were foreseen for both sides. An answer that describes French help as sympathy for the Italian cause has misread the transaction; the price is recorded in the agreement and was paid in 1860.
3. **It required a pretext.** ⚠️ A war planned between two governments still has to *begin* in a way that does not make the planners look like aggressors, because the reaction of the other great powers depends on who appears to have started it. ⚠️ This session does not hold a documented account of the manoeuvres by which the war was begun, so it teaches the requirement - a defensive appearance is a strategic asset - without narrating the means. The same requirement reappears in a much better documented form in Lesson 15, where the provocation of 1870 is on the record.

### 3. Villafranca: what an ally's exit costs

✅ **Fact:** the **Armistice of Villafranca** of **11 July 1859** ended the fighting between France and Austria, closing the war of 1859.

✅ **Fact:** the standard account for this topic records the outcome as Lombardy gained in 1859 and ✅ **Venetia joining only in 1866**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the gap between the two facts is the lesson. The stated aim of the war, from the Italian side, was the removal of Austrian rule from northern Italy. Austria was removed from Lombardy and remained in Venetia. The war stopped when the ally decided it should stop, at a moment when the client would have continued.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the reasons a great power halts a successful war are general and worth naming, because they recur throughout nineteenth-century diplomacy and are the best available answer when an examiner asks why French help was "limited".

| Reason a great power stops early | How it applies here |
|---|---|
| The cost of victory has risen faster than its value | Continuing meant attacking Austria's fortified positions in Venetia rather than fighting in the open |
| Other powers are becoming alarmed | A war that expels Austria from Italy entirely alters the European balance and invites a coalition; a war that transfers one province does not |
| Domestic constituencies object | ⚠️ A Catholic power assisting a movement whose next target is the territory of the papacy faces opposition at home; this consideration returns decisively in 1870 |
| The client's success is becoming a problem | ⚠️ A strong united Italy on France's south-eastern frontier is not obviously in France's interest; a moderately enlarged Piedmont is |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the fourth row is the one that converts a narrative into an argument. A patron does not want its client to become a rival. The same structural fact explains why France later garrisoned Rome, why France was alarmed by Prussian success in 1866, and why - as Lesson 15 shows - France was drawn into a war in 1870 that completed the very unification it feared. ⚠️ There is a general proposition available here for a 20-mark answer: in the nineteenth century, every unification was assisted by a great power up to the point at which the assistance began to create a competitor.

### 4. The price: Nice and Savoy, March 1860

✅ **Fact:** by the **Treaty of Turin** of **24 March 1860**, **Nice and Savoy** were ceded by the Kingdom of Sardinia to France, as agreed in exchange for French support.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** four consequences, each usable.

1. **The price was paid although the objective was not fully delivered.** Venetia was still Austrian. This is the cleanest available illustration of the asymmetry of power inside an alliance between a great power and a small one.
2. **The territory was Italian-speaking.** A state claiming to unite a nation on linguistic and cultural grounds ceded populations that its own principle would have claimed. ⚠️ The cession is therefore the single most effective piece of evidence for the argument that dynastic and strategic calculation, rather than national principle, governed the decisions of the Italian state - and it is the fact to deploy against any romantic account of the Risorgimento.
3. **It created a personal and political grievance inside the movement.** ⚠️ Nice was Garibaldi's birthplace, and the cession of his native city by the government he was about to serve is part of the strained relationship between the volunteers and the state that Lesson 8 examines.
4. **It bought French acquiescence for the next stage.** ⚠️ The cession was closely bound up with the annexations in central Italy that followed within days; a great power that has been paid has less reason to object to what its client does next. The sequence - cession in March 1860, plebiscites in the central duchies in the same month - is not a coincidence, and Lesson 7 takes it up.

### 5. What 1859 actually achieved, correctly weighed

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the war is often described as the decisive step in Italian unification. That is defensible but needs precision, because it achieved less territorially and more politically than the summary suggests.

| Achievement | What it actually amounted to |
|---|---|
| **Territorial** | One province. Lombardy was gained; Venetia was not; Rome and the south were untouched |
| **Military-demonstrative** | ⚠️ Austria was shown to be beatable in Italy by a coalition, which made every subsequent Italian upheaval more plausible to its participants and more alarming to its rulers |
| **Political** | ✅ The standard account's own formulation: unification moved from ideas to state action - the national question ceased to be a matter of conspiracies and became a matter of state policy |
| **Catalytic** | ⚠️ The war's disturbance of the peninsula is the immediate context of the upheavals in the central states, which Lesson 7 treats as the second stage |
| **Instructive** | ⚠️ It taught the Italian state the limits of borrowed force - a lesson visible in 1866, when Italy again obtained territory through another power's war and again did not win it by its own arms |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the sharpest formulation for an answer is that 1859 converted the Italian question from an insurrectionary problem into a diplomatic and military one, and that the conversion, not the province, was the gain.

### Strongest objection, reply and residual

**Objection:** The emphasis on French assistance diminishes Italian agency. Italians fought, died, organised and voted; the war of 1859 was accompanied by upheaval across the peninsula that no foreign power arranged; and treating unification as something France permitted reproduces exactly the great-power condescension that nationalism existed to reject.

**Reply:** The objection is right that agency must be preserved and wrong if it implies that the assistance was marginal. Both propositions can be held at once, and holding both is the mark of a strong script. ✅ The historiographical companion records that French help mattered in 1859, and ✅ the standard account for this topic records that Piedmont-Sardinia and France together defeated Austria - the assistance is in the record and cannot be minimised. But the same record shows Italian agency doing work that France neither performed nor controlled: the decade of Piedmontese state-building, the Crimean intervention, the placing of the Italian question before the Congress of Paris, the upheavals in the central states, and above all the southern campaign of 1860, which was carried out by Italians against the wishes of the French emperor and the anxieties of the Piedmontese government. ⚠️ The correct formulation is that foreign help was necessary for the first stage and irrelevant to the third; and an answer that says so has made a comparative judgement rather than issued a verdict.

**Residual:** What this evidence base does not settle is the content of the Plombieres agreement in detail - what precisely was promised on each side, what territorial arrangements for the rest of Italy were contemplated, and how far the later outcome departed from the plan. The evidence available here establishes the meeting, its date, its participants and the fact that gains were foreseen for both sides; it does not supply the text. Any claim about specific clauses is therefore outside what this session may assert.

### UPSC application

This lesson is the evidence base for every question about foreign assistance, and it is where a comparison with Germany is most efficiently made. The structure that scores is: the alliance was planned before the war, the ally stopped the war when its own aims were met, the price was paid in full in Italian-speaking territory, and the original objective was completed only seven years later through a third party's war. Four clauses, each factual, and together they prove the dependence that distinguishes the Italian route. Contrast with Prussia in one further sentence - ✅ the historiographical companion's point that Bismarck aimed to isolate his enemies diplomatically rather than to borrow anyone's army - and the comparison is made analytically rather than by adjective.

### Examiner traps in this lesson

- Describing French help as sympathy for the Italian cause, when territorial gains were foreseen for both sides in advance.
- Treating 1859 as a spontaneous national rising that France supported, when the alliance was planned in July 1858.
- Writing that the war of 1859 expelled Austria from Italy, when Venetia remained Austrian until 1866.
- Omitting the cession of Nice and Savoy, which is the price and the best evidence against a romantic reading.
- Asserting the contents of the Plombieres agreement clause by clause, which this evidence base does not hold.
- Supplying battle names, dates or casualty figures for 1859 beyond what the sources support.
- Failing to convert the episode into the general rule about borrowed force, which is what the comparison question rewards.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ 21 July 1858, Plombieres: a secret meeting between Cavour and Napoleon III plans the alliance, with gains foreseen for both sides.
2. ✅ 1859: Piedmont-Sardinia and France defeat Austria; Lombardy is gained; unification moves from ideas to state action.
3. ✅ 11 July 1859, Villafranca: the armistice ends the war early.
4. ✅ Venetia is not obtained; it joins only in 1866.
5. ✅ 24 March 1860, Treaty of Turin: Nice and Savoy ceded to France in exchange for French support.
6. ⚠️ The price was paid although the objective was incomplete - the asymmetry inside an unequal alliance.
7. ⚠️ The ceded territory was Italian-speaking, which is the strongest evidence against a purely national reading of the state's conduct.
8. ⚠️ Nice was Garibaldi's birthplace, which feeds the strain between the volunteers and the government.
9. ⚠️ Four reasons a great power halts a successful war: rising costs, alarm among other powers, domestic objection, and the risk of creating a competitor.
10. ⚠️ General rule: in the nineteenth century, great-power assistance to unification continued only up to the point at which it began to create a rival.
11. ⚠️ What 1859 achieved: one province territorially; a demonstration militarily; the conversion of the national question into state policy politically; and a catalyst for the central states.
12. ✅ The comparison line: French help mattered in 1859, whereas Bismarck aimed to isolate his enemies diplomatically.
13. ⚠️ The clauses of the Plombieres agreement are not held in this evidence base and must not be asserted.

### Concept check

**Question:** "Italian unification was made possible by France." Assess this claim using the events of 1858 to 1860, and state precisely where it is true and where it fails.

**Model answer:** The claim is true for the first stage and false as a general account. It is true in that the alliance was planned in advance at the secret meeting of 21 July 1858 between Cavour and Napoleon III, with territorial gains foreseen for both sides; that the war of 1859 was won by Piedmont-Sardinia and France together against Austria; and that Lombardy was gained as a result, which is what converted Italian unification from an idea into state action. Without a great-power army, Piedmont had already been beaten twice by Austria in 1848-49, and nothing in its own capacity had changed enough to reverse that. But the claim fails in three respects. First, the assistance was bought rather than given: by the Treaty of Turin of 24 March 1860, Nice and Savoy were ceded to France in payment, and they were Italian-speaking territories, so the Italian state paid for its enlargement with populations its own national principle would have claimed. Second, the assistance was withdrawn before the objective was met: the armistice of Villafranca on 11 July 1859 ended the war with Austria still holding Venetia, which Italy obtained only in 1866 and then through Prussia's war rather than its own. Third, the decisive act of the following year was carried out against French preference: Garibaldi's conquest of Sicily and Naples in 1860 added the entire south by Italian action that no foreign power arranged or wanted. The precise formulation is therefore that French help was necessary for the opening stage, insufficient for the middle, and absent from the third, and that the pattern illustrates a general rule - a great power assists a unification only up to the point at which the assistance threatens to create a competitor.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating Villafranca as a betrayal to be described in moral terms. An ally stops a war when its own aims are met and its risks rise, which is what a state does by definition; the analytically useful point is not that France was unreliable but that a borrowed army is always fighting the lender's war, so the client's objective is completed only where the two aims coincide.

**Bridge to Lesson 7:** The war disturbed the whole peninsula, and within months several central Italian states had expelled their rulers and were asking to join Piedmont. That raised a question the movement had never had to answer before: by what procedure does a territory *join* a state, and what does that procedure prove? The next lesson examines the annexation of the central duchies and the political instrument used to legitimise it.

## Lesson 7 - The central duchies, and what a plebiscite actually proves

Progress: 7/20 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the upheaval in the central states after 1859; annexation by plebiscite in March 1860; the four functions of the plebiscite instrument; its internal and external audiences; the limits of what a vote under these conditions demonstrates

### Visual: the annexation machine, and the two audiences it addresses

                                     |
                                     v
