   popular votes            Modena and Romagna approve           becomes a
   are organised            annexation to Piedmont-Sardinia      lawful act
   ===========================================================================

   WHY A VOTE, AND NOT SIMPLY A CONQUEST?  -  TWO AUDIENCES

   AUDIENCE ONE: EUROPE                   AUDIENCE TWO: ITALY
   The great powers had built the 1815    The republicans had claimed that only
   order on DYNASTIC LEGITIMACY.          the PEOPLE could make a nation.
   A plebiscite substitutes POPULAR       A plebiscite lets the monarchy make
   CONSENT as an alternative legal        the same claim - and thereby take
   ground for a transfer of territory.    the republicans' own argument away.

   COST OF USING THE INSTRUMENT: once you concede that populations may
   choose their state, you have created a principle that can later be turned
   against you - by minorities inside your own new state, and by neighbours.
```

*The upper half is the mechanism; the lower half is the reason it was chosen. A plebiscite is not primarily a measurement of opinion. It is a legal and rhetorical device that converts a fact on the ground into a title deed - and it is aimed at two different audiences simultaneously.*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

In 1859 a war was fought in northern Italy. While it was being fought, several rulers in the central states lost control of their capitals, and provisional authorities took their place. When the fighting stopped, those territories were in an odd condition: nobody was clearly sovereign in them.

That situation posed a question the Italian movement had never squarely faced. Everyone agreed that Italy should be united. But by what *procedure* does a territory move from one sovereign to another? Conquest is a procedure, but Piedmont had not conquered these states and could not admit to having arranged their revolutions. Inheritance is a procedure, but no dynastic claim existed. Treaty is a procedure, but it requires the departing sovereign's consent, which was not going to be given.

The answer chosen was a vote. Its interest for an examination lies less in what the votes recorded than in why a monarchy that had spent a decade distancing itself from revolution suddenly found a popular consultation convenient.

### 2. What happened, stated exactly

✅ **Fact:** in **March 1860**, plebiscites were held in **Tuscany, Parma, Modena and Romagna**, in which popular votes approved annexation to **Piedmont-Sardinia**.

✅ **Fact:** the **Treaty of Turin** ceding **Nice and Savoy** to France was concluded on **24 March 1860**, in the same month.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the juxtaposition is the point. In a single month the Piedmontese state paid its ally in territory and acquired the central Italian states by popular vote. The two transactions belong to one settlement: the great power that had been paid had little reason to obstruct the annexations, and the annexations made the payment politically survivable at home by delivering more than had been surrendered. ⚠️ An answer that treats the cession and the annexations as separate events has missed the exchange.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** notice also what the plebiscites did *not* cover. They did not extend to Venetia, which remained Austrian; to Rome and its surrounding territory, which remained papal and French-protected; or to the southern kingdom, which still had its own dynasty. The March 1860 settlement produced an enlarged northern and central Italian state, not a united Italy - and it is the failure of that settlement to look final that made the next stage possible.

### 3. The four functions of a plebiscite

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** a plebiscite in this period performs four distinct functions, and they should be separated because an examiner's follow-up question usually targets one of them.

| Function | What it achieves | Who it is aimed at |
|---|---|---|
| **Legitimation** | Supplies a legal ground for a transfer of sovereignty where neither conquest nor dynastic right is available | The great powers, whose recognition determines whether the transfer sticks |
| **Substitution of principle** | Replaces dynastic legitimacy, the basis of the 1815 order, with popular consent as the ground of territorial title | Conservative Europe, and the settlement being dismantled |
| **Appropriation** | Allows a monarchy to claim the democratic argument that the republicans had made their own | The Italian movement, whose radical wing is thereby outflanked |
| **Closure** | Makes the annexation difficult to reopen, since reversing it means denying a population's expressed choice | Future claimants, including the displaced rulers |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the third function is the most under-used in answers and the most valuable. ✅ The standard account's from-above and from-below bank records exactly this pattern in the parallel case of 1860-61 - the popular-military conquest of the south, followed by the monarchy absorbing the popular gain. The plebiscite is the civilian version of the same operation: the movement's own principle, popular sovereignty, is put to work in the service of a dynasty. ⚠️ That is what "revolution from above" concretely means, and it is a better illustration than any abstract definition.

### 4. What a plebiscite under these conditions can and cannot prove

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** here the examiner is testing judgement, and the disciplined answer distinguishes three levels of claim.

| Claim | Status | Reason |
|---|---|---|
| "A transfer of sovereignty was formally approved by a popular vote" | Defensible | This is what the record states |
| "The populations of the central states preferred annexation to Piedmont over the alternatives available to them" | Probable but qualified | It depends on which alternatives were actually on offer, who organised the vote, and under whose armed protection it was held |
| "A majority of the population of these territories held Italian national consciousness" | Not established | A vote for annexation can express a preference for order, for a stronger state, for lower tariffs or against a restored ruler, and cannot be read as a measurement of national feeling |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** four structural cautions apply to any plebiscite of this type and should be stated in one sentence rather than laboured.

1. **The question is set by the organiser**, who decides which options appear and which do not.
2. **The alternatives are constrained by the situation**: where the old ruler has fled and a republic would invite foreign intervention, annexation may be the only outcome that appears safe.
3. **The conditions are not neutral**: a vote held while a friendly army is nearby, and an unfriendly one recently defeated, is not a free-standing expression of preference.
4. **The recorded outcome is not independently audited**, and this session holds no vote figures it regards as reliable - which is why no percentage appears anywhere in it.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** none of this means the plebiscites were meaningless. It means their meaning is *political* rather than demoscopic: they show what the actors believed they needed in order to make an annexation stick, which is itself a major historical finding about how the nineteenth-century states system was changing.

### 5. The instrument's afterlife, and the boomerang

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the device was not abandoned after March 1860. The same instrument of popular ratification was used at later stages of Italian unification as further territories were incorporated. ⚠️ This session verified the March 1860 plebiscites specifically and does not assert dates, organisers or results for the later consultations; the point carried here is the *pattern* - annexation followed by ratification - rather than a list.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the principle, once conceded, could not be confined to Italy's convenience, and this is where the lesson connects to the rest of the topic.

- **Inside the new state.** If populations may choose their sovereign, then populations dissatisfied with the new arrangement have a principle to appeal to. ⚠️ The southern discontent of the 1860s, examined in Lesson 10, is precisely a case where large numbers of people evidently did not regard the new state as theirs.
- **On the frontiers.** ✅ The standard account records that the new German Empire contained Polish, Danish and French-speaking populations, and that Alsace-Lorraine became a permanent French grievance. ⚠️ A Europe that had accepted popular consent as a ground of title in Italy had acquired a standing argument against every annexation made without it - and the 1871 annexation was made without it.
- **Against the multinational empires.** ⚠️ If nationality entitles a population to choose its state, the Habsburg monarchy's many nationalities have an argument for dismemberment. That consequence belongs to the topic on the world in 1914, which owns the Habsburg nationality crisis; this session records only that the principle generalised.

> 🔑 **Answer line:** The plebiscite converted conquest into consent and made annexation legally durable; but by making popular choice a ground of territorial title, it armed every future claimant against the states that had used it.

### Strongest objection, reply and residual

**Objection:** Dwelling on the conditions of the plebiscites is a way of denying that Italians wanted unification. The populations concerned had every reason to prefer a constitutional kingdom to a restored duke propped up by Austria, and they said so. Treating their votes as stage management is condescension dressed as scepticism.

**Reply:** The objection is right to insist that preferences were real and wrong to treat scepticism about measurement as denial of preference. The distinction is between what was chosen and what the choice demonstrates. It is entirely plausible - and the reply concedes it - that in several of these territories annexation was the genuinely preferred outcome among those who had a view, for reasons including constitutional government, commercial advantage and dislike of restored rulers. What cannot be inferred is the stronger proposition that a measured majority held a developed national consciousness, because that would require the vote to have been a free choice among real alternatives, independently recorded, and this session holds no figures and no audit. The careful answer therefore affirms the political achievement - a transfer of sovereignty legitimised by consent rather than dynastic right - and refuses the demoscopic claim. That combination is not condescension; it is the difference between evidence and inference.

**Residual:** What this evidence base cannot settle is the actual distribution of opinion in the central states, the conduct of the voting, or the degree of coercion involved. No electoral figures, procedures or observer accounts are held. The session therefore teaches the instrument's functions and limits and records the empirical question as open.

### UPSC application

This lesson supplies the answer to two distinct demands. For "how did nationalism redraw the map of Europe?", the plebiscite is the concrete mechanism by which the principle of dynastic legitimacy was replaced by the principle of popular consent as a ground of territorial title - a better answer than a list of new borders. For "from above or from below?", the plebiscite is the sharpest single example of how the two were fused: a popular instrument, organised by a state, to legitimise an annexation by a monarchy. In a 20-mark answer, the boomerang paragraph - the principle generalising against its own users in Alsace-Lorraine and in the multinational empires - is the kind of consequence-level analysis that lifts a script, provided the Habsburg material is handed to its own owner rather than narrated here.

### Examiner traps in this lesson

- Treating the plebiscites as evidence of measured national consciousness rather than as an instrument of legitimation.
- Quoting approval percentages, none of which this session treats as reliable.
- Separating the cession of Nice and Savoy from the central annexations, when they belong to one month and one settlement.
- Writing that the central states were "conquered" by Piedmont, which is precisely the description the instrument existed to avoid.
- Implying that March 1860 completed Italian unification, when Venetia, Rome and the whole south were still outside.
- Missing the boomerang - that popular consent, once admitted as a ground of title, becomes an argument against later annexations, including Alsace-Lorraine.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ March 1860: plebiscites in Tuscany, Parma, Modena and Romagna approve annexation to Piedmont-Sardinia.
2. ✅ 24 March 1860: Nice and Savoy are ceded to France by the Treaty of Turin - the same month as the annexations.
3. ⚠️ The two transactions belong to one settlement: the ally is paid, and therefore does not obstruct.
4. ⚠️ Four functions of a plebiscite: legitimation, substitution of principle, appropriation of the democratic argument, and closure against reopening.
5. ⚠️ Two audiences: conservative Europe, which needed a legal ground other than conquest; and the Italian radicals, whose principle was being borrowed.
6. ⚠️ Three levels of claim: formal approval is defensible; a preference among available alternatives is probable but qualified; measured national consciousness is not established.
7. ⚠️ Four cautions: the organiser sets the question; the alternatives are constrained; the conditions are not neutral; the results are unaudited here.
8. ⚠️ No plebiscite percentage appears anywhere in this session.
9. ⚠️ The instrument was reused at later stages; this session asserts only the March 1860 consultations by date.
10. ⚠️ The boomerang: popular consent as a ground of title arms later claimants - southern discontent inside Italy, ✅ Alsace-Lorraine as a permanent French grievance, and the nationalities of the multinational empires.
11. ⚠️ March 1860 produced an enlarged northern and central Italian state, not a united Italy.
12. ⚠️ The plebiscite is the civilian form of revolution from above: a popular instrument used by a state to legitimise a dynasty's acquisition.

### Concept check

**Question:** Why did the Piedmontese state prefer annexation by plebiscite to straightforward conquest in the central Italian states, and what did the choice cost it in the longer run?

**Model answer:** Conquest was unavailable and, even had it been available, would have been counterproductive. It was unavailable because Piedmont had not defeated these states and could not admit to having arranged their upheavals without confirming to the great powers that it was a revolutionary agent. It would have been counterproductive because the European order still rested on dynastic legitimacy, so a transfer of sovereignty had to rest on some recognised ground, and popular consent was the only ground available once conquest and dynastic right were ruled out. The plebiscites of March 1860 in Tuscany, Parma, Modena and Romagna therefore performed four functions at once: they legitimised the transfer before Europe; they substituted popular consent for dynastic right as the basis of territorial title; they appropriated for a monarchy the democratic argument that the republicans had made their own, which outflanked the radicals inside the movement; and they made the annexations difficult to reverse, since reversal would have required denying an expressed popular choice. The cost was that the principle could not be confined. Once populations are admitted to have a say in which state they belong to, the argument becomes available to anyone dissatisfied with the resulting arrangement - to the southern populations of the new Italian kingdom in the 1860s, to the French-speaking population annexed by Germany in 1871 without any such consultation, and to the many nationalities of the multinational empires whose claims that principle later underwrote. The instrument that made Italian annexation durable also armed the next generation of claimants.

**Misconception to avoid:** Reading the plebiscites as opinion surveys that prove a nation existed. They prove what the actors believed they needed in order to make an annexation legally durable, which is a finding about the changing basis of territorial title rather than a measurement of national feeling; and this session holds no figures that would support the stronger claim.

**Bridge to Lesson 8:** The March settlement left the whole south outside, under its own dynasty, and no plan existed for incorporating it. Within weeks a volunteer force sailed for Sicily without the government's open authorisation and conquered a kingdom in six months. The next lesson examines what happens when the deniable instrument succeeds beyond the state's intentions - and how the state recovered control of an achievement it had not made.

   WHAT EACH SIDE GAINED AND LOST AT TEANO
   MONARCHY GAINED : half the peninsula, won at no diplomatic cost
   MONARCHY LOST   : nothing material; it acquired an obligation of
                     gratitude it was not required to honour
   MOVEMENT GAINED : a united Italy, which was its stated aim
   MOVEMENT LOST   : the republic, the unitary democratic constitution,
                     and any institutional claim on the state it had made
   ==================================================================
```

*The crisis point in the middle box is the analytical core of the lesson. Everything before it is a military narrative; everything after it is a political settlement. The question the diagram poses - why did a victorious commander hand his conquest to a king? - is the question an examiner is really asking when the paper says "from above or from below".*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

By spring 1860 the Italian question had an odd shape. The north and centre had been reorganised around Piedmont. The south was a separate kingdom with its own dynasty, its own army and no intention of joining anything. No European power proposed to remove it, and Piedmont could not invade it: an open attack by one Italian state on another would have destroyed the careful diplomatic position built since 1856 and invited intervention.

So the south was, in the strict sense, unobtainable by the methods that had obtained everything else. What changed the situation was not a new diplomatic idea but an old revolutionary one: a volunteer expedition, launched without formal state authorisation, which the government could disown if it failed.

The expedition succeeded. And success created a problem larger than failure would have done - because a victorious revolutionary army in possession of half of Italy is not obviously going to hand its conquest to a king.

### 2. What happened, stated exactly

✅ **Fact:** the standard account for this topic records that in **1860** Garibaldi's **Red Shirts** conquered **Sicily and Naples**, with the significance that southern Italy joined the unification process; and it records **Garibaldi** as the leader of the Red Shirts who supplied the popular-military energy from below.

✅ **Fact:** Garibaldi's volunteer force, the **Thousand**, landed at **Marsala in Sicily on 11 May 1860**, beginning the southern campaign.

✅ **Fact:** on **26 October 1860** Garibaldi met **Victor Emmanuel II** near **Teano** and handed over control of the conquered southern territory.

✅ **Fact:** the standard account records that the **Kingdom of Italy** was proclaimed under Victor Emmanuel II in **1861**, with political unification beginning formally; ✅ the proclamation is dated **17 March 1861**.

✅ **Fact:** the standard account's from-below and from-above bank pairs these events explicitly: the popular-military conquest of the south in 1860 stands in the "from below" column, and the monarchy's absorption of the popular gain through the proclamation of 1861 stands opposite it.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the standard account's pairing is the examinable structure of the whole episode, and it is worth stating as a sequence of four steps: a revolutionary force conquers territory the state cannot attack; the state neither authorises nor prevents it; the conquest is transferred to the monarchy at the moment of victory; and the monarchy's title is then formalised in a proclamation. Each step is a decision, and each can be explained.

### 3. Why a volunteer force could go where an army could not

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the value of the volunteer instrument lies in a single property - **deniability** - and it has four components.

| Property | What it enables | What it costs |
|---|---|---|
| **Deniability** | The state can say it did not send them, which prevents the expedition from being an act of war by one state against another | The state cannot direct them either, and must accept whatever they do |
| **Low cost** | Volunteers equip themselves and live off the campaign; no budget line is exposed | Capability is limited; such a force can beat a demoralised garrison but not a great power |
| **Legitimacy of a different kind** | The campaign appears as a liberation by Italians rather than an annexation by Piedmont | It also appears as a revolution, which alarms the conservative powers whose acquiescence matters |
| **Speed** | No parliamentary vote, no mobilisation, no diplomatic notice | Also no brakes: the force can reach a target the state urgently does not want attacked |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the fourth row is the crisis. Once Naples had fallen, the next territory to the north was the papal state, and behind the papal state stood France, whose protection of the papacy had been demonstrated in 1849. A volunteer march on Rome would have converted an Italian civil conflict into a war with a great power - and would have done so at the initiative of a force the Italian state did not control.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** that is why the meeting at Teano is a political event rather than a ceremonial one. The transfer of the conquest to the king simultaneously removed the danger of an uncontrolled advance on Rome, converted a revolutionary title into a monarchical one, and closed the possibility of a southern republic. ⚠️ This session holds no documentary record of what the two men said or of the negotiations that preceded the meeting, so it teaches what the handover *accomplished* and refuses to narrate motives it cannot document.

### 4. Why did the revolution surrender its conquest? Four explanations, weighed

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the question is genuinely difficult, and four explanations are usually offered. A strong answer ranks them rather than listing them.

1. **National priority over constitutional form.** The stated aim of the movement was a united Italy; the form of the state was secondary for many of its participants. Handing the south to the king unified Italy immediately; refusing would have produced two Italian states.
2. **Military and financial reality.** A volunteer force that had beaten the southern kingdom's garrisons could not have held the south against the Piedmontese army, nor governed it, nor funded it. ⚠️ The alternative to transfer was not an independent republic but a collapse or a civil war between Italian nationalists.
3. **The diplomatic constraint.** A republic in the south, or a march on Rome, would have invited foreign intervention and risked losing everything already gained since 1859. The transfer protected the gains of the north and centre.
4. **The character of the commander.** ⚠️ The historiographical companion's formulation is that Garibaldi was a volunteer-revolutionary hero rather than a state strategist, and a commander of that kind acts for the cause rather than for a share of power. ⚠️ This is the weakest of the four as an explanation, because it substitutes character for circumstance, and it should be used only as a supplement.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the defensible ranking is that the second and third explanations - capability and diplomacy - were decisive, the first supplied the justification that made the decision honourable, and the fourth made it prompt. ⚠️ An answer that offers only the fourth has written a moral anecdote; an answer that offers the second and third has written history.

### 5. What the settlement of 1861 actually was

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the proclamation of March 1861 is best understood as the terms on which the revolution's achievement was retained by the monarchy, and the terms had four elements.

| Element | Content | Consequence |
|---|---|---|
| **Title** | ✅ Victor Emmanuel II became king of Italy; the kingdom was proclaimed on 17 March 1861 | The new state was a continuation of the Piedmontese monarchy, not a new foundation |
| **Constitution** | ⚠️ The Piedmontese constitutional order of 1848 was extended to the new territories rather than a new national constitution being negotiated | ⚠️ The south received northern institutions it had not helped design - the origin of much later resentment, examined in Lesson 10 |
| **Territory** | ✅ Venetia and Rome remained outside | The state was proclaimed incomplete, which kept the national question alive as government policy |
| **The movement's position** | ✅ The standard account records as a limit that Mazzini's republic was displaced by a monarchy and the mass movement's own programme was not what was built | The republicans became an opposition inside the state they had helped create |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the second row deserves emphasis because it is the bridge to the whole of Lesson 10. Extending one region's institutions to a whole peninsula is administratively efficient and politically expensive. It produces a unified legal order quickly and creates, just as quickly, a population that experiences unification as the arrival of an external administration.

> 🔑 **Answer line:** Italy was made from below and kept from above: the volunteers took the south, the monarchy took the south from the volunteers, and the constitution that followed was the one the volunteers had not asked for.

### Strongest objection, reply and residual

**Objection:** The "absorption" framing makes the monarchy sound like a thief. Garibaldi was a monarchist by 1860 in the practical sense that he accepted Victor Emmanuel II as the necessary instrument of unity; the transfer at Teano was not a defeat imposed on him but a decision he had announced in advance and pursued deliberately. Reading it as expropriation imports a class analysis the evidence does not support.

**Reply:** The objection corrects an overstatement and leaves the analysis standing. It is right that the transfer was not extracted by force and that the commander's own stated aim was Italian unity under the Piedmontese crown rather than a southern republic; the reply concedes both. But three things remain true and are what the "absorption" language is meant to capture. ✅ The standard account records as a limit of the process that the republic was displaced by a monarchy and that the mass movement's own programme was not what was built - so a programme was in fact set aside, whatever the commander personally preferred. ⚠️ The state that emerged extended its own constitution outward instead of negotiating a new one, so the territories brought in by the movement had no share in designing the state they entered. And ⚠️ the sequence of gain and loss in the diagram above is asymmetric: the monarchy obtained half a peninsula and incurred no obligation it was required to honour, while the movement obtained its stated aim and lost its institutional claim. The honest formulation is that the transfer was voluntary and the settlement that followed was unequal, and both halves of that sentence are needed.

**Residual:** What this evidence base cannot settle is the internal politics of the expedition and the handover - the negotiations preceding Teano, the intentions of the Piedmontese government toward the expedition before it succeeded, and the degree of prior coordination between the movement and the state. No correspondence or official record is held here. This session therefore describes the functions performed and refuses to narrate the intentions behind them.

### UPSC application

This lesson is the single most productive evidence unit in the Italian half of the topic, because it answers the question that is actually set. "From above or from below" is not a question about enthusiasm; it is a question about who held the territory at the end. The structure that scores is: the south was conquered from below by a volunteer force that landed at Marsala on 11 May 1860; the conquest was transferred to the monarchy at Teano on 26 October 1860; the kingdom was proclaimed on 17 March 1861; and the constitution extended was the north's. That sequence demonstrates fusion rather than alternatives, which is the mature answer. It also supplies the sharpest contrast with Germany available anywhere in the topic - ✅ the standard account's observation that Germany had no equivalent mass military hero of the same kind - and Lesson 17 builds the comparison on exactly this foundation.

### Examiner traps in this lesson

- Reducing Teano to a handshake between a soldier and a king, and losing the political transfer it accomplished.
- Writing that Garibaldi "gave Italy to the king" without explaining why a march on Rome was impossible.
- Ignoring the French protection of the papacy, which is what made the papal state untouchable in 1860 and available in 1870.
- Treating the expedition as officially sponsored, which removes the deniability that made it usable.
- Claiming that the Kingdom of Italy in 1861 was territorially complete.
- Explaining the handover solely by the commander's character, which substitutes anecdote for analysis.
- Asserting conversations, orders or agreements at Teano that this evidence base does not hold.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ 1860: Garibaldi's Red Shirts conquered Sicily and Naples, bringing southern Italy into the unification process.
2. ✅ 11 May 1860: the Thousand landed at Marsala in Sicily.
3. ✅ 26 October 1860: Garibaldi met Victor Emmanuel II near Teano and handed over control of the conquered south.
4. ✅ 17 March 1861: the Kingdom of Italy was proclaimed under Victor Emmanuel II; political unification began formally.
5. ✅ Venetia and Rome remained outside the new kingdom.
6. ⚠️ The volunteer instrument's key property is deniability; its costs are limited capability and no brakes.
7. ⚠️ The crisis point was the papal state, protected by France; a volunteer march on Rome meant war with a great power.
8. ⚠️ Four explanations for the transfer, ranked: capability and diplomacy decisive, national priority as justification, character as supplement.
9. ✅ The standard account's pairing: popular-military conquest from below in 1860; the monarchy's absorption of the gain in 1861.
10. ⚠️ The 1861 settlement extended Piedmontese institutions outward rather than negotiating a new constitution.
11. ✅ The recorded limit: Mazzini's republic was displaced by a monarchy and the mass movement's programme was not what was built.
12. ✅ The comparison: Germany had no equivalent mass military hero of the same kind - this is the sharpest contrast in the topic.
13. ⚠️ Sentence to carry: Italy was made from below and kept from above.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why the transfer of the conquered south to Victor Emmanuel II at Teano was a political necessity rather than a personal gesture, and what it settled about the character of the Italian state.

**Model answer:** It was a necessity because the alternatives were all worse for the national cause. A volunteer force that had defeated the southern kingdom's garrisons could not have held, governed or financed half a peninsula against the Piedmontese army and the hostility of the great powers, so the practical alternative to transfer was not a southern republic but either collapse or a conflict between Italian nationalists. It was also a necessity because of geography: the territory immediately north of Naples was the papal state, whose protection by France had been demonstrated when French troops destroyed the Roman Republic in 1849, so an advance on Rome by an uncontrolled volunteer army would have meant war with a great power and would have put at risk everything gained since 1859. Transferring the conquest to the king on 26 October 1860 removed that danger, converted a revolutionary title into a monarchical one that Europe could recognise, and foreclosed the possibility of a rival Italian state in the south. What it settled about the character of the Italian state is the decisive point: the kingdom proclaimed on 17 March 1861 was a continuation of the Piedmontese monarchy, which extended its own constitution and administration outward rather than negotiating a new national settlement, so the territories brought in by popular action had no share in designing the state they entered. Italy was therefore made from below and kept from above - the volunteers supplied force the state could not openly use, the state supplied the legitimacy and the international settlement the volunteers could not obtain, and the state retained the result.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the handover as evidence that the Italian movement was fundamentally monarchist all along, or alternatively as a betrayal of the revolution. Neither reading survives the evidence: the transfer was voluntary and was made for compelling strategic reasons, and the settlement that followed was nonetheless unequal, since the republic was displaced and the movement's own programme was not what was built. The analytically strong answer holds both halves together.

**Bridge to Lesson 9:** The kingdom of 1861 was proclaimed without Venetia and without Rome, and it had no means of taking either. Both were obtained within a decade - and neither was won by Italian arms. The next lesson examines how a state acquires territory through other powers' wars, and what the Roman question cost the new kingdom for the next sixty years.

## Lesson 9 - Venetia and Rome: completing a country through other people's wars

Progress: 9/20 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the Prussian alliance of 1866 and the transfer of Venetia; Custoza and Lissa; Rome in September 1870 and the withdrawal of French protection; the Roman question, the Law of Guarantees and the papal refusal; the long settlement of 1929

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the standard account's timeline entries that Prussia defeated Austria in the Seven Weeks' War of 1866 with Venetia going to Italy, and that Rome joined Italy in 1870 after French withdrawal, completing Italian unification territorially; its limits bank recording that Rome joined only in 1870 after French withdrawal and that the papacy's relationship with the new state remained hostile for decades, with the formal settlement coming much later; the core world-history source's record that the papacy had been hostile to the Italian government since 1870 when the territory belonging to the papacy was incorporated into the new kingdom, and its account of the 1929 treaty that ended the quarrel; the verified 1866 and 1870 evidence carried from this session's single verification pass
CA search: not sought - this topic carries exactly one current linkage, held in Lesson 10
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: two acquisitions, neither of them won

```text
   ACQUISITION ONE - VENETIA, 1866
   ---------------------------------------------------------------
   PRUSSIA needs Austria's forces divided        ITALY wants Venetia
                 \                                      /
                  \___ ALLIANCE OF 1866 _______________/
                                |
          Prussia fights in Bohemia  |  Italy fights in Venetia
                                |
          KONIGGRATZ, 3 July 1866   |   CUSTOZA (land) and
          Prussia wins decisively   |   LISSA (sea): Italy LOSES both
                                |
                    AUSTRIA IS DEFEATED - BY PRUSSIA
                                |
          3 October 1866: PEACE OF VIENNA - Austria cedes Venetia
          FOR TRANSFER to Italy. Austria does not surrender it to
          Italy directly; the transfer passes through France.
                                |
          RESULT: Italy gains a province having lost every battle
          it fought for it.

   ACQUISITION TWO - ROME, 1870
   ---------------------------------------------------------------
   1849: French troops destroy the Roman Republic and restore the
         Pope. FRENCH PROTECTION is what makes Rome untouchable.
                                |
   1860: Garibaldi cannot march on Rome (Lesson 8) - same reason.
                                |
   1870: THE FRANCO-GERMAN WAR. The French emperor is defeated at
         Sedan on 1-2 September and his regime falls.
                                |
         THE PROTECTOR IS GONE.
                                |
   20 SEPTEMBER 1870: Italian troops enter Rome; the papal territory
         is incorporated into the kingdom; unification is completed
         territorially.
   ---------------------------------------------------------------
   THE PATTERN: BOTH final acquisitions were produced by wars fought
   by other powers for their own reasons. Italy's completion was a
   by-product of German unification.
```

*The two panels make one argument. Italy's territorial completion was real, and it was not achieved by Italian victories. That is not a slight on Italian arms; it is the structural position of a middling power in a system run by great ones, and it is exactly the evidence an examiner wants when the question asks how far Italian unification depended on external factors.*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

In March 1861 the Kingdom of Italy existed, and it did not contain two of the places most Italians would have named first. Venetia was Austrian. Rome was papal, garrisoned by French soldiers, and the obvious capital of any Italian state.

Neither could be taken. Italy could not defeat Austria alone - it had failed to do so in 1848 and had needed France in 1859. Italy could not attack Rome without fighting France, whose protection of the papacy had been demonstrated in 1849 and was still in place. The new kingdom was therefore in the peculiar position of having a declared national aim it had no means of achieving.

What resolved the deadlock was not a change in Italian capability but a change in the European situation: another state decided to fight Austria, and then to fight France. Italy's completion is, in the most precise sense, a consequence of German unification.

### 2. Venetia, 1866: gaining a province by losing a war

✅ **Fact:** the standard account for this topic records that in **1866** Prussia defeated Austria in the **Seven Weeks' War** and that **Venetia went to Italy**, with Austria excluded from German affairs and Italy advancing.

✅ **Fact:** in the war of 1866 Italy was **allied with Prussia** against Austria; a campaign was fought in Venetia between the Austrian army of the south and the Italians; and Italian forces were defeated at **Custoza** on land and **Lissa** at sea.

✅ **Fact:** by the **Peace of Vienna of 3 October 1866**, Austria **ceded Venetia for transfer to Italy**, the transfer being made through France rather than directly.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** four consequences follow, and they are more interesting than the campaign.

1. **Italy's gain was produced by Prussia's victory.** The decisive engagement of the war was fought in Bohemia, not in Italy. ⚠️ Italy's military contribution was to oblige Austria to divide its forces - a real service to Prussia, and one that Prussia had sought when making the alliance - but the province was delivered by the outcome in the north.
2. **The manner of the transfer was itself humiliating.** A cession *for transfer* rather than a direct surrender to the victorious claimant is a diplomatic statement: Austria was declining to acknowledge Italy as the power that had defeated it. ⚠️ Resentment of that form is part of the background to Italian foreign policy for decades afterwards.
3. **The Italian state learned the wrong lesson twice.** ⚠️ In 1859 and again in 1866 Italy obtained territory through alliance with a stronger power rather than through its own capability. The pattern encouraged a foreign policy of seeking gains through attachment to a greater state, and the habit outlived the circumstances that produced it.
4. **The alliance is a mirror of the Italian case as a whole.** ✅ The standard account's comparison bank records the decisive external defeat of Austria as 1859 with French help for Italy, and the Seven Weeks' War of 1866 for Germany, and concludes that **Austria's exclusion is the common precondition** of both unifications. ⚠️ In 1866 the two processes literally share a single war.

### 3. Rome, 1870: the vacancy created by a distant defeat

✅ **Fact:** the standard account for this topic records that in **1870 Rome joined Italy after French withdrawal**, completing Italian unification territorially; and its limits bank records that **Rome joined only in 1870, after French withdrawal**, and that the papacy's relationship with the new state remained hostile for decades.

✅ **Fact:** with the French emperor no longer in power to protect them after the defeat at **Sedan on 1-2 September 1870**, the **papal territories were annexed by Italy on 20 September 1870**, completing that nation's unification; Italian troops entered the city through the **Porta Pia**.

✅ **Fact:** the core world-history source read for this session records that **the papacy had been hostile to the Italian government since 1870**, when all the territory belonging to the papacy was incorporated into the new kingdom of Italy.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the chain of causation is worth writing out in full in an answer, because it is short, factual and demonstrates dependence better than any adjective.

```text
   Prussia provokes and defeats France (Lesson 15)
        -> the French emperor is captured at Sedan, 1-2 September 1870
        -> the regime that protected the papacy falls
        -> French troops are no longer available to defend Rome
        -> Italian forces enter Rome on 20 September 1870
        -> Italian territorial unification is completed
        -> the papacy is left without temporal territory and refuses
           to recognise the new position
```

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** three points sharpen this.

- **Italy did not defeat France, and did not need to.** It needed France to be defeated. The distinction is the whole of the Italian position in the European system.
- **The timing was tight and opportunistic**, which is not a criticism; acting within weeks of the protector's collapse is what a competent state does with a window.
- ⚠️ **Rome's acquisition immediately created the state's largest internal problem.** Territorial completion and political difficulty arrived on the same day.

### 4. The Roman question: what the new state had actually acquired

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the incorporation of the papal territory did not merely annex land. It abolished the temporal sovereignty of an institution with a worldwide religious constituency, and it did so in the capital city of the new state. Three consequences followed, and each is examinable.

**The papal refusal.** ✅ The standard account records the papacy's relationship with the new state as hostile for decades. ✅ The core source confirms hostility dating from 1870 and the incorporation of the papal territory.

**The Italian attempt at a unilateral settlement.** ✅ the Italian parliament passed a **Law of Guarantees in 1871**, establishing the papacy's prerogatives after the annexation of Rome. ⚠️ Its logic was to settle the question by domestic statute - guaranteeing the Pope's personal inviolability, his freedom of communication with the Church and certain privileges - rather than by treaty. ⚠️ Its weakness was structural: a guarantee granted by a parliament can be withdrawn by that parliament, so accepting it would have meant accepting that the papacy's position depended on Italian domestic politics. The papacy therefore rejected it, and the position of a sovereign who refuses to recognise the state surrounding him became a standing feature of Italian politics.

**The withdrawal of Catholics from national politics.** ✅ a papal decree of **1874**, known by its Latin formula as the **Non Expedit**, discouraged and in effect forbade Italian Catholics from taking part in national parliamentary elections, as part of the Church's protest at the loss of the temporal power; its influence persisted into the early twentieth century. ⚠️ The political effect was substantial: in a country where the great majority of the population was Catholic, a significant body of opinion was kept out of the parliamentary system at the moment when the new state most needed to build loyalty. ⚠️ This is one of the strongest available illustrations of the gap between territorial unification and political integration.

**The eventual settlement, outside this topic's period.** ✅ The core source records that the quarrel was ended by the **Lateran Treaty of 1929**, by which Italy recognised the Vatican City as a sovereign state, paid the pope compensation for his losses, accepted the Catholic faith as the official state religion and made religious instruction compulsory in schools. ⚠️ The standard account explicitly routes that settlement to the later topic that owns interwar Italy; it is recorded here in one sentence to show that the Roman question lasted **fifty-nine years**, and is not narrated further.

> 🔑 **Answer line:** Italy completed its territory in 1870 and acquired, on the same day, a conflict with a universal church that no Italian government could settle for another fifty-nine years.

### 5. The balance sheet of completion

| Acquired | How | Cost |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ Lombardy, 1859 | A great-power ally's army | ✅ Nice and Savoy, ceded 1860 |
| ✅ Central duchies, 1860 | Local upheaval plus ratification by plebiscite | ⚠️ A principle of popular consent that could later be turned against the state |
| ✅ Sicily and Naples, 1860 | A volunteer campaign from below | ✅ The displacement of the movement's own republican programme |
| ✅ Venetia, 1866 | Another power's victory, transferred through a third | ⚠️ Two defeats, at Custoza and Lissa, and a humiliating form of cession |
| ✅ Rome, 1870 | The collapse of the protector in another power's war | ⚠️ A fifty-nine-year quarrel with the papacy and the withdrawal of Catholics from national politics |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** read the right-hand column downward and the argument of Lesson 10 is already visible. Every acquisition carried a liability, and the liabilities were of a kind that accumulated inside the new state rather than being discharged by the act of unification.

### Strongest objection, reply and residual

**Objection:** The insistence that Italy "did not win" Venetia or Rome is a rhetorical trick. States acquire territory through diplomacy and favourable circumstances all the time; that is what statecraft is. Italy allied intelligently in 1866 and acted decisively in 1870, and describing those as other people's achievements is a way of denying Italian competence.

**Reply:** The objection is right that using circumstance is a skill and wrong to treat the distinction as rhetorical, because the distinction has consequences that can be tested. First, it explains the *timing*: Italy did not choose 1866 or 1870; both dates were set by Prussian decisions, which is why the completion of Italian unification is dated by events in Bohemia and at Sedan. Second, it explains the *form*: a cession for transfer rather than a direct surrender is a diplomatic statement about who had actually defeated Austria, and Italy had to accept it. Third, it explains the *sequel*: ⚠️ a state whose gains came from attachment to stronger powers tends to continue seeking gains that way, and the habit is visible in Italian policy long afterwards. Competence in exploiting an opening is real and is conceded; the structural dependence is also real, and it is what distinguishes the Italian case from the German one in the comparison the examiner sets.

**Residual:** What this evidence base cannot settle is the internal decision-making on the Italian side in 1866 and 1870 - the terms of the alliance with Prussia, the conduct of the Venetian campaign, the deliberations that preceded the entry into Rome, and the drafting of the 1871 statute. The evidence base establishes the events, dates and outcomes; it holds no diplomatic or parliamentary record. Claims about motives and negotiations are therefore outside what this session may assert.

### UPSC application

This lesson is where "how far did Italian unification depend on external factors?" is answered, and the answer should be graded rather than absolute. The graded version runs: the first stage depended on a purchased great-power alliance; the second stage, in the central states and the south, was substantially internal and in the southern case was carried out against the preferences of the external patron; and the final two acquisitions depended entirely on wars fought by others. That is a three-part verdict with evidence for each part, which is what "how far" directives reward. The causal chain from Sedan to the Porta Pia is also the single most efficient way to show the two unifications interlocking, and it belongs in any answer that compares them.

### Examiner traps in this lesson

- Writing that Italy defeated Austria in 1866, when Italian forces lost at Custoza and Lissa and the decision was reached in Bohemia.
- Omitting the form of the Venetian cession, which is where the diplomatic humiliation lies.
- Treating the entry into Rome as an Italian victory over France rather than as action taken after the protector's collapse.
- Dating Rome to 1861, the standard error the standard account warns against.
- Describing the Law of Guarantees as a settlement of the Roman question, when it was a unilateral statute the papacy rejected.
- Narrating the Lateran Treaty of 1929, which belongs to the later owner and is recorded here only as an endpoint.
- Missing the Non Expedit, which is the best evidence that territorial completion did not produce political integration.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ 1866: Prussia defeated Austria in the Seven Weeks' War; Venetia went to Italy; Austria was excluded from German affairs.
2. ✅ Italy fought in 1866 as Prussia's ally and was defeated at Custoza on land and Lissa at sea.
3. ✅ Peace of Vienna, 3 October 1866: Austria ceded Venetia for transfer to Italy, through France rather than directly.
4. ⚠️ Italy gained a province having lost the battles it fought for it; the decision was reached in Bohemia.
5. ✅ 1870: Rome joined Italy after French withdrawal, completing unification territorially.
6. ✅ The French defeat at Sedan on 1-2 September 1870 removed the protection of the papacy; Italian troops entered Rome on 20 September 1870 through the Porta Pia.
7. ✅ The papacy had been hostile to the Italian government since 1870, when the papal territory was incorporated into the kingdom.
8. ✅ Law of Guarantees, 1871: a unilateral Italian statute regulating the papacy's position, which the papacy rejected.
9. ⚠️ A guarantee granted by statute can be withdrawn by statute - the structural reason for the refusal.
10. ✅ Non Expedit, 1874: Catholics discouraged from voting in national elections, with influence persisting into the twentieth century.
11. ✅ The quarrel was settled only by the Lateran Treaty of 1929 - a fifty-nine-year dispute, owned by the later topic.
12. ⚠️ The pattern: both final acquisitions were by-products of wars fought by other powers; Italy's completion is a consequence of German unification.
13. ⚠️ Every acquisition carried a liability, and the liabilities accumulated inside the new state.

### Concept check

**Question:** Trace the causal chain that made Rome available to Italy in September 1870, and explain what the chain demonstrates about the Italian state's position in the European system.

**Model answer:** The chain begins outside Italy. Prussia's war with France, provoked in July 1870, produced a decisive French defeat at Sedan on 1-2 September, at which the emperor was captured and his regime fell. That regime had been the protector of the papacy's temporal position: French troops had destroyed the Roman Republic in 1849 and restored papal authority, and French protection was the reason Rome could not be attacked either by Piedmont in the 1850s or by Garibaldi's volunteers in 1860. With the protector removed, Italian forces entered Rome on 20 September 1870 through the Porta Pia, and the papal territory was incorporated into the kingdom, completing Italian unification territorially. The chain demonstrates three things about Italy's position. First, the timing of Italian unification was set by other powers' decisions rather than by Italian capability, which is also true of Venetia in 1866, gained when Prussia defeated Austria while Italian forces lost at Custoza and Lissa. Second, Italy's competence lay in exploiting openings rather than in creating them, and acting within weeks of the protector's collapse was exactly the right use of a window. Third, the dependence had a lasting cost, because the acquisition that completed the territory simultaneously created the Roman question: the papacy was left without temporal territory, refused the unilateral Law of Guarantees of 1871 on the ground that a parliamentary statute could be repealed by the same parliament, and by the decree of 1874 discouraged Catholics from participating in national elections - so the state completed its map and lost the participation of a large part of its own population in the same act.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that because Italy entered Rome while France was at war, Italy defeated France or acted against it. Italy neither fought France nor needed to; it required France to be defeated by somebody else, and the whole point of the episode is that a middling power's national completion was produced by the collapse of its former patron in a war it had no part in.

**Bridge to Lesson 10:** The map was complete and the state was not. Under the surface of the proclamation lay a peninsula joined together administratively but divided economically, socially and religiously, with an alienated church, an aggrieved south and territories still claimed beyond the frontier. The next lesson examines what Italy actually got - and carries this session's single current linkage, because the deepest of those divisions is still being argued about today.

## Lesson 10 - What Italy actually got: the state that arrived before the nation

Progress: 10/20 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the recorded weaknesses of the new state; the southern question, brigandage and the northern institutional template; the church problem restated; the republican defeat; trasformismo; unredeemed lands; the single current linkage of this session

### Visual: five unfinished businesses, and where each came from

```text

   (Lesson 8)                              a far poorer south under northern

                                           of Italian politics

   Papal territory incorporated,       ->  THE CHURCH PROBLEM
   Rome taken in 1870 (Lesson 9)           a hostile papacy; the Law of
                                           Guarantees refused; Catholics
                                           discouraged from voting, 1874;
                                           settled only in 1929
   --------------------------------------------------------------------------
   The monarchy absorbed the           ->  THE REPUBLICAN DEFEAT
   popular gain (Lesson 8)                 the mass movement's own programme
                                           was not what was built; its heirs
                                           became an opposition inside the
                                           state they had helped make
   --------------------------------------------------------------------------
   A parliament without organised      ->  TRASFORMISMO
   national parties, in a state            government by shifting centrist
   whose citizens were partly              coalitions assembled by co-opting
   excluded                                opponents; stability without
                                           genuine alternation
   --------------------------------------------------------------------------
   Frontiers drawn by war and          ->  ITALIA IRREDENTA
   diplomacy, not by nationality           Italian-speaking lands still under
                                           Austrian rule - Trentino, Trieste -
                                           keeping a territorial claim alive
   ==========================================================================
   THE COMMON PATTERN: THE STATE ARRIVED BEFORE THE NATION.
   Unification produced a single sovereignty over populations that had not
   yet been given, and in some cases were actively refused, a share in it.
```

*Five rows, five liabilities, one pattern. The examiner's phrase for the pattern is usually "the limits of unification", and the marks are in showing that each limit was produced by a specific act of unification rather than by some general Italian backwardness.*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

On 20 September 1870 the Italian state contained everything it had claimed except some border districts. By any territorial measure the project was finished. And yet for the next fifty years Italian politics was dominated by the question of whether the country was actually one country.

The reason is that unification had solved a problem of sovereignty and had not touched a set of problems underneath it. There was now one army, one customs frontier, one legal code and one parliament. There was not yet one economy, one set of loyalties, or one experience of what the state was for. In the south, unification was experienced by many as the arrival of a distant administration with new taxes and conscription. Among practising Catholics, it was experienced as an attack on the Church. Among republicans, it was experienced as the theft of their own victory.

This lesson is about that gap, and it is the single most reliable source of higher marks in the topic, because almost every candidate stops at the proclamation.

### 2. The recorded verdict on the new state

✅ **Fact:** the core world-history source read for this session states that **the unification of Italy was only completed in 1870, and the new state suffered from economic and political weaknesses**.

✅ **Fact:** the historiographical companion records that Italy was unified but **not fully homogenised**, that north-south inequalities and tensions with the papacy persisted, and that **political unification did not mean social integration**.

✅ **Fact:** the standard account's limits bank records three Italian limits directly: the **north-south divide**, in which unification joined a commercialising north to a far poorer south under northern institutions so that the southern question became a permanent feature of Italian politics; the **church problem**, in which Rome joined only in 1870 after French withdrawal and the papacy's relationship with the new state remained hostile for decades; and the **republican defeat**, in which Mazzini's republic was displaced by a monarchy and the mass movement's own programme was not what was built.

✅ **Fact:** the same account attaches an explicit caution to the southern question: keep it **qualitative** and do not cite income or literacy figures.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this session obeys that caution throughout. No Italian economic, demographic or educational figure appears anywhere in it. The argument is made with mechanisms and institutions, which is in any case what an examiner rewards.

### 3. The southern question, taught as a mechanism

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** "the south was poorer" is a description. The examinable content is the mechanism by which unification converted a difference in development into a political problem, and it has four steps.

1. **Institutional extension rather than negotiation.** ⚠️ The kingdom extended the northern constitutional and administrative order outward instead of negotiating a federal or regionally differentiated settlement. That produced a single legal order quickly and gave the south institutions designed elsewhere for other conditions.
2. **Fiscal and military obligations arrived first.** ⚠️ For most people in a newly incorporated territory, the state's first appearances are the tax collector and the conscription officer. Benefits, where they came, came later. The sequence produces a predictable pattern of resentment that has nothing to do with national sentiment in the abstract.
3. **Exposure of a weaker economy to a single market.** ⚠️ Joining a customs union with a more industrialised region exposes local production to competition without conferring the capital, infrastructure or skills to meet it. ⚠️ This session asserts no figures for the effect, in accordance with the standard account's caution, but the direction of the pressure is well established and may be stated as a mechanism.
4. **Coercion when resistance followed.** ✅ after unification the Italian government fought a protracted campaign against **brigandage in the south between 1861 and 1865**, an insurgency reflecting southern discontent with a northern-dominated government, conscription and economic measures unfavourable to peasants. ⚠️ That the new state's first sustained military operation was against its own newly acquired population is the sharpest single fact available on the limits of unification.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the term **Mezzogiorno** for the Italian south and the phrase "the southern question" are standard, and the standard account permits such terms as analytical references rather than as sourced facts of this folder. Use them as vocabulary, and carry the mechanism rather than the label.

### 4. Politics in a state with absent citizens: trasformismo

✅ **Fact:** from **1876**, under the premiership of Agostino Depretis, Italian government was conducted through the practice known as **trasformismo** - the building of flexible centrist coalitions by co-opting moderate opponents into shifting parliamentary alliances, which maintained governmental stability but fostered corruption and political cynicism.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** trasformismo is usually taught as a curiosity of Italian parliamentary life. It is better taught as the predictable result of the three conditions unification had created.

| Condition produced by unification | Effect on the party system |
|---|---|
| ✅ Catholics discouraged from national elections after 1874 | Removes one of the two natural mass constituencies from parliamentary competition |
| ✅ The republican and democratic movement displaced and embittered | Removes, or marginalises, the other |
| ⚠️ A narrow parliamentary class drawn largely from propertied and northern elites | Leaves a chamber in which no deep alternative exists, so politics becomes the management of personal and local alignments |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the consequence is a parliamentary system that is stable and hollow at the same time: governments change, policy does not, and the electorate has no mechanism for turning one bloc out in favour of another. ⚠️ The core source's account of the post-1918 crisis, which belongs to the later owner, records precisely the collapse of confidence in such a system; this session notes the continuity in one sentence and hands the sequel over.

✅ **Fact:** the core source also records that **the industrialisation of Italy in the years after unification led to the development of a strong socialist party and trade unions**. ⚠️ A new mass constituency therefore grew outside the trasformismo system rather than inside it, which compounded the same problem: the parliamentary arena did not contain the country's real political divisions.

### 5. Unredeemed lands, and the frontier that nationalism could not accept

✅ **Fact:** the term **italia irredenta**, or "unredeemed Italy", referred to Italian-speaking territories still under Austrian rule after unification, notably **Trentino** and **Trieste**; the idea became a rallying cry for nationalists and contributed to Italy's later entry into the First World War on the Allied side, after which those regions were annexed.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the analytical value of the term is that it shows what a *nationalist* criterion of completeness implies. Once a state declares that it exists to unite a nation defined by language and culture, any speaker of that language outside its frontiers is an argument against the state's own completeness. That is a permanent source of foreign-policy pressure, and it cannot be settled by any particular frontier.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** it is also the point at which the Italian case rejoins the general European story. ✅ The standard account records that the German Empire contained Polish, Danish and French-speaking populations and that Alsace-Lorraine became a named source of friction in 1914. ⚠️ Unification by nationality created, in both countries, populations on the wrong side of a frontier - and the claims arising from that fact were carried into the crisis that the 1914 owner examines. This session names the linkage and hands it over.

### 6. The single current linkage carried by this session

📰 **Current linkage, reported and dated.** This topic carries exactly one current linkage, and it belongs here because it is a direct continuation of the topic's own material.

| What was searched | What the search returned | Use made of it |
|---|---|---|
| Italy's north-south divide and the status of the differentiated-autonomy law in 2026 | Reported: Italy's law on differentiated autonomy, enacted in 2024, allowed individual regions to negotiate additional devolved powers; it was challenged by several southern and centre-left regions; in judgment number 192 of 2024 the Constitutional Court declared several of its provisions unconstitutional while upholding the general framework, requiring devolution to respect national solidarity, essential service levels and fiscal equalisation; implementation agreements with northern regions have continued, and further challenges from southern regions were reported into 2026 | Lesson 10 only - to show that the institutional question opened in 1861 is still an open constitutional question in Italy, and for no historical claim whatever |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the linkage illuminates and proves nothing. What it shows is that the specific problem this lesson describes - how to run a single state across regions with very different economic capacities, and on what terms the wealthier may diverge - has never been closed. The vocabulary of the 2024-26 dispute is the vocabulary of 1861: solidarity against divergence, national standards against regional autonomy, and the fear that formal equality will entrench substantive inequality. ⚠️ No historical fact in this session depends on this item, and no figure from it is used.

### Strongest objection, reply and residual

**Objection:** The catalogue of limits is unfair to a state that had existed for a decade. Every new state has an incomplete integration; measuring Italy against an ideal of social homogeneity that no European country had achieved in 1871 is a standard designed to produce failure. And the persistence of regional inequality is a fact about economic geography rather than a verdict on unification.

**Reply:** The objection is right that the standard must be reasonable and wrong that the limits are therefore unremarkable, for three reasons that the evidence supports. First, the sources themselves record the weakness: ✅ the core world-history account states plainly that the new state suffered from economic and political weaknesses, and ✅ the standard account records the north-south divide, the church problem and the republican defeat as limits of *this* unification, not as generic features of new states. Second, several of the limits were produced by identifiable choices rather than by geography: extending one region's institutions outward, incorporating the papal territory without a negotiated settlement with the papacy, and absorbing the popular movement's conquest without conceding its programme were decisions with predictable consequences. Third, the consequences were not merely regrettable but politically decisive: ✅ a military campaign against brigandage in the south from 1861 to 1865, ✅ the withdrawal of Catholics from national elections after 1874, and ⚠️ a parliamentary system that managed coalitions instead of representing divisions together describe a state that had not converted sovereignty into integration. ⚠️ The fair verdict is comparative rather than absolute: Italy's unification was genuine, its integration was shallower than its territorial success suggested, and the gap was created in the manner of unification itself.

**Residual:** What this evidence base cannot settle is the magnitude of the regional gap or its trajectory after 1861. No income, output, literacy, emigration or public-expenditure series is held here, and the standard account's caution forbids asserting any. The session therefore establishes mechanisms and institutional facts and records the quantitative question as open - which is also why no claim is made about whether unification widened or narrowed the divide.

### UPSC application

This lesson is where 20-mark answers are won. ✅ The standard account states it explicitly: unification questions almost always reward candidates who continue past the proclamation. The structure that scores is to take two or three limits, attach each to the specific act of unification that produced it, and close with a verdict about the state-nation gap. Two sentences of evidence are enough for each: institutions extended outward and a counter-insurgency in the south from 1861 to 1865; the papal territory annexed and Catholics withdrawn from elections after 1874; the popular conquest absorbed and the republican programme abandoned. The verdict sentence to rehearse is that both new states were unified politically before they were unified socially, which is why the years after 1871 were dominated by the question of who really belonged to the new nation - a formulation the standard account supplies and which works for Germany as well as for Italy.

### Examiner traps in this lesson

- Ending the answer at 1870 or 1871, which is the commonest structural failure on this topic.
- Citing Italian income, literacy, emigration or output figures, which the standard account's caution forbids and this evidence base does not hold.
- Treating the southern question as a fact of geography rather than as the product of institutional choices.
- Omitting the counter-insurgency of 1861-65, which is the strongest single evidence of incomplete integration.
- Presenting the Law of Guarantees as having settled the Roman question.
- Treating trasformismo as a quirk rather than as the predictable politics of a parliament from which the two natural mass constituencies were absent.
- Narrating the Lateran Treaty or the rise of fascism, both of which belong to the later owner.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ The unification of Italy was only completed in 1870, and the new state suffered from economic and political weaknesses.
2. ✅ Italy was unified but not fully homogenised; political unification did not mean social integration.
3. ✅ Three recorded limits: the north-south divide, the church problem, and the republican defeat.
4. ✅ The southern comparison must be kept qualitative; no income or literacy figure may be cited.
5. ⚠️ The southern mechanism in four steps: institutions extended rather than negotiated; taxes and conscription arrive first; a weaker economy is exposed to a single market; coercion follows resistance.
6. ✅ A campaign against brigandage in the south ran from 1861 to 1865, reflecting discontent with northern-dominated government, conscription and economic measures.
7. ✅ Non Expedit, 1874: Catholics discouraged from national elections, with influence into the twentieth century.
8. ✅ Trasformismo from 1876 under Depretis: shifting centrist coalitions built by co-opting opponents; stability with corruption and cynicism.
9. ⚠️ Trasformismo is the predictable result of removing both natural mass constituencies from parliamentary competition.
10. ✅ Industrialisation after unification produced a strong socialist party and trade unions - a new constituency outside the parliamentary system.
11. ✅ Italia irredenta: Italian-speaking Trentino and Trieste under Austrian rule; a nationalist rallying cry that contributed to Italy's entry into the First World War.
12. ⚠️ A nationality criterion of completeness can never be satisfied by any particular frontier - the same logic that produces ✅ the Alsace-Lorraine grievance in the German case.
13. 📰 Reported current linkage: Italy's 2024 differentiated-autonomy law was partly struck down by Constitutional Court judgment 192 of 2024, with the framework upheld and the dispute continuing - the 1861 institutional question is still open. No historical claim rests on it.

### Concept check

**Question:** "Italy was unified in 1870." Examine that statement, distinguishing clearly between the senses in which it is true and the senses in which it is not.

**Model answer:** In the territorial sense it is true and precisely datable: the last claimed territory, Rome, was incorporated on 20 September 1870 after the French protector's defeat, and the core account for this period records that the unification of Italy was only completed in 1870. In the constitutional sense it is also true, since a single legal order, army and customs frontier now covered the peninsula, the Piedmontese constitutional settlement having been extended outward from 1861. In every other sense the statement requires qualification, and the qualifications were produced by the manner of unification itself. Socially, a commercialising north was joined to a far poorer south under institutions designed in the north, and the state's first sustained military operation was a campaign against brigandage in the south between 1861 and 1865, reflecting discontent with a northern-dominated government, conscription and economic measures unfavourable to peasants - so the state was fighting its own new population within months of acquiring it. Religiously, the annexation of the papal territory produced a papacy hostile to the new state from 1870, a Law of Guarantees passed unilaterally in 1871 and refused because a grant made by statute can be revoked by the same parliament, and a decree of 1874 discouraging Catholics from voting in national elections, so a large part of the population stood outside the political system. Politically, the republican programme that had supplied much of the movement's energy was displaced by a monarchy, and the resulting parliament, missing both its natural mass constituencies, governed from 1876 by trasformismo - shifting centrist coalitions built by co-opting opponents, which produced stability without alternation. Territorially again, the nationality principle left Italian-speaking Trentino and Trieste outside the frontier as italia irredenta, keeping a claim alive that contributed to Italy's entry into the First World War. The accurate formulation is therefore that Italy acquired a state in 1870 and spent the following decades attempting to acquire a nation to match it.

**Misconception to avoid:** Explaining the limits by Italian backwardness or by geography alone. Each limit traces to a specific act: institutions extended outward rather than negotiated, papal territory annexed without a negotiated settlement, and the popular conquest absorbed without conceding the movement's programme. Attributing the outcome to national character or to the map removes the analysis the examiner is testing.

**Bridge to Lesson 11:** The Italian sequence is now complete from the starting condition to its unfinished business. The German sequence begins from the same settlement of 1815 and runs on a different logic, and it is best entered not through Bismarck but through the institution he had to work with and against. The next lesson examines why the German Confederation could not unify Germany, how Prussia and Austria had become rivals for a single role, and why a domestic constitutional crisis in 1862 made a minister of Bismarck's kind necessary.

## Lesson 11 - Why the Confederation could not unify Germany, and what made Bismarck necessary

Progress: 11/20 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the structure of the German Confederation and its built-in incapacity; Austro-Prussian dualism; the five core-state requirements applied to Prussia; the army reorganisation and the constitutional conflict over the budget; the appointment of September 1862
