### Visual: a body designed so that nothing could happen

```text
   THE GERMAN CONFEDERATION, 1815-1866
   ============================================================================
   MEMBERS      39 sovereign states, ranging from two great powers to
                free cities and tiny principalities

   ORGAN        a diet of delegates - INSTRUCTED BY THEIR GOVERNMENTS,
                not elected, not deliberative in any sovereign sense

   PRESIDENCY   AUSTRIA

   POWERS       to co-ordinate, to preserve, and above all TO PREVENT
                no common citizenship, no common army under one command,
                no power to compel a member to do anything important

   PURPOSE      to keep central Europe stable and non-revolutionary:
                it was BUILT to prevent change, and it worked
   ============================================================================

   THE STRUCTURAL PROBLEM: TWO GREAT POWERS INSIDE ONE BODY

        AUSTRIA                              PRUSSIA
        presides                             excluded from the chair
        multinational empire: Germans        overwhelmingly German
        are a minority of its subjects       population
        needs Germany WEAK and PLURAL,       needs Germany ORGANISED,
        because a strong German state        because only then can it
        would either exclude it or           lead it
        dismember it
                    \                     /
                     \                   /
                      DUALISM: two powers, one role
                                |
                  This cannot be settled by a vote, because
                  the diet cannot compel a great power.
                  It cannot be settled by agreement, because
                  the two interests are contradictory.
                  IT CAN ONLY BE SETTLED BY FORCE - or not at all.
   ============================================================================
   TEST CASE: 1850, OLMUTZ. Prussia tries to organise a German union
   without Austria and is compelled to abandon it. Austrian primacy
   is reaffirmed. The question is posed and answered - for the moment.
```

*The top panel explains why no German parliament, congress or diet could have unified Germany: the Confederation was not a weak government, it was a successful anti-government. The middle panel explains why the question had to be settled by war: two great powers wanted incompatible things from the same institution, and no procedure existed that could overrule either of them.*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

Imagine an association of thirty-nine states in which two of the members are great powers, the chair is permanently held by one of them, decisions require the agreement of members whose interests conflict, and nothing in the rules allows the association to compel anybody to do anything significant. Then ask that association to abolish itself in favour of a single state.

It will not happen. Not because the delegates lack imagination, but because the body's design is the obstacle. The Confederation was created in 1815 to keep central Europe quiet: ✅ the account of the Vienna settlement records that the German lands were offered a thirty-nine-state confederation under Austrian influence instead of unity, and that national unity was thereby postponed rather than solved. An institution built to prevent reorganisation cannot be the agent of reorganisation.

So the German question had a peculiar structure. Everybody knew which two states could answer it. Neither could answer it inside the Confederation's rules. And the two answers were mutually exclusive.

### 2. Dualism: why Austria and Prussia wanted incompatible things

✅ **Fact:** the historiographical companion records that the German Confederation consisted of **thirty-nine states under Austrian dominance** after Vienna, and states plainly that this explains **the scale of the German problem**.

✅ **Fact:** the standard account for this topic records the same structure - a loose confederation of thirty-nine states under Austrian influence - as the Vienna settlement's German output.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the two great powers' positions were not simply competitive; they were structurally opposed, and the opposition can be stated in four rows.

| Question | Austria's interest | Prussia's interest |
|---|---|---|
| **What is Germany for?** | A buffer and a source of influence; an arena Austria presides over | A potential enlargement; the natural field of Prussian leadership |
| **Should Germany be organised more tightly?** | No - a tighter Germany would need a single leader, and a German national state would either exclude Austria or require the Habsburg monarchy to break itself up | Yes - but only under Prussian direction, because a tighter Germany led by Austria would subordinate Prussia permanently |
| **Who are the people of this state?** | A multinational empire in which Germans are a minority of subjects; a German national principle is a solvent applied to Austria's own body | An overwhelmingly German state, for which a German national principle is an asset |
| **What follows if the question is forced?** | Austria must resist any exclusive German organisation, by arms if necessary | Prussia must either accept permanent second place or eventually fight |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** row three is the deepest and is usually missed. ✅ The standard account records that the eventual unification was "small German" in form because Austria was excluded in 1866. The reason Austria had to be excluded is not that Austrians were not German; it is that the Habsburg monarchy could not join a German national state without either abandoning its non-German subjects or importing them into Germany. The national principle and the multinational empire are incompatible in principle, not merely in policy - which is why this topic's outcome feeds directly into the Habsburg nationality crisis owned by the 1914 topic.

✅ **Fact:** Prussia had already challenged Austria in **1850**, when the complete failure of its mobilisation compelled it to accept at **Olmutz** the somewhat humiliating terms of Austria, abandoning its own scheme for a smaller German union without Austria.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** Olmutz matters for three reasons. It proves that the little-German project was already Prussian policy twelve years before Bismarck. It proves that the project's obstacle was Austrian resistance backed by force. And it establishes what had to change before the project could be attempted again: not the aim, but the capability. Everything Prussia does in the 1860s addresses that gap.

### 3. Prussia measured against the five requirements of a core state

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** Lesson 5 set out five requirements a state must meet before it can unify a nation. Applying the same five to Prussia is the most efficient comparison available in this topic, because four of them were already satisfied.

| Requirement | Piedmont-Sardinia | Prussia |
|---|---|---|
| **1. Survive defeat with institutions intact** | Satisfied after 1849, keeping its constitution | Satisfied: Olmutz in 1850 was a diplomatic humiliation, not a destruction |
| **2. Be able to pay for a war** | Achieved by a decade of reform | ✅ Already satisfied: strengthened at Vienna by the Rhineland, Westphalia and Saxon lands, and the leader of a customs union covering most of Germany since 1834 |
| **3. Attract the national movement's talent** | Achieved through legality and refuge | ⚠️ Partly satisfied and contested: German liberalism was divided about Prussia and would remain so until 1866 |
| **4. Be treated as a great-power partner** | Had to be purchased through the Crimea and the Congress of Paris | ✅ Already satisfied: Prussia was a European great power in its own right |
| **5. Obtain an ally willing to fight the enemy** | Indispensable; purchased with territory | ⚠️ **Not required.** Prussia needed the *neutrality* of others, not their armies - which is why its diplomacy aimed at isolating enemies rather than recruiting allies |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the last row is the single cleanest statement of the difference between the two unifications, and ✅ it is the standard account's own conclusion - Italy needed an external great-power ally and Prussia did not. The consequence is that Prussian diplomacy has a different shape from Piedmontese diplomacy: Cavour's problem was to find someone to fight alongside, Bismarck's problem was to ensure that nobody fought alongside his opponents.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** requirement three is where the German case is genuinely difficult. A liberal movement that wanted a constitutional, parliamentary Germany had every reason to distrust a conservative monarchy that had refused the Frankfurt crown in 1849. That distrust is the background to the crisis of 1862, and its resolution in 1866 is the most consequential domestic event in the whole German sequence.

### 4. The army-budget conflict: a constitutional crisis that produced a chancellor

✅ **Fact:** the Prussian army was reorganised in this period, with **Helmuth von Moltke** as chief of the general staff and strategist and **Albrecht von Roon** as army organiser; and Prussia, with Bismarck as statesman and these two as strategist and organiser, had **prepared methodically** for a fresh challenge to Austria after the failure of 1850.

✅ **Fact:** **Otto von Bismarck was appointed Prussian minister-president in September 1862**, in the middle of a **constitutional conflict over the Prussian army budget** between King William I and the Prussian parliament.

✅ **Fact:** the standard account for this topic records that Bismarck became Prussia's minister-president in **1862** and pursued **practical power politics**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the crisis itself is worth stating precisely, because the examinable content is a constitutional principle rather than a quarrel about money.

```text
   THE CROWN WANTS            a larger army with longer service, funded
                              permanently: the instrument of great-power
                              status and, in due course, of the German
                              question
                 |
   THE CHAMBER REFUSES        because the budget is the one place where a
                              parliament can control a monarchy: whoever
                              controls the army's money controls the state
                 |
   THE CONSTITUTION IS        it does not say clearly what happens if crown
   SILENT ON DEADLOCK         and chamber cannot agree on a budget
                 |
   THE GOVERNMENT'S           if the constitution provides no procedure for
   POSITION                   a deadlock, government must continue and
                              revenue must be collected anyway
                 |
   THE RESULT                 the state is governed and the army is funded
                              WITHOUT the chamber's consent, for four years
                 |
   THE STAKE                  not military expenditure, but whether Prussia
                              is a constitutional state in which a parliament
                              can stop the executive
```

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** three things follow, and they organise the rest of the German sequence.

1. **Bismarck was appointed because the crisis required somebody willing to govern unconstitutionally.** A minister who would not defy the chamber was of no use to the crown; the appointment is therefore a symptom of the crisis, not an accident of court politics.
2. **The army that fought the three wars was built during a period when parliament had refused to authorise it.** ⚠️ The instrument of German unification was created against the objection of German liberalism's own representatives - which makes the liberal capitulation of 1866 in Lesson 14 both explicable and damaging.
3. **Unification and the domestic constitutional question were bound together from the start.** ⚠️ Success abroad would retrospectively settle the argument at home, and everybody involved understood that. That is why the wars have a domestic function as well as a diplomatic one, and why an answer that treats German unification purely as foreign policy is missing half the mechanism.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the famous formulation about iron and blood belongs to this crisis - it was addressed to the budget committee in September 1862 and argued that the German question would be decided by power rather than by parliamentary debate and resolutions. ✅ The standard account records an explicit control against reproducing it verbatim and carries the point with the term **Realpolitik**. The disciplined use is therefore: name the occasion, state the argument - that the previous decade had shown decisions of that magnitude were not made by assemblies - and move to the method, which is Lesson 12.

### Strongest objection, reply and residual

**Objection:** Presenting the Confederation as incapable is hindsight. It kept central Europe at peace for half a century, contained two great powers without a war between them, and accommodated a customs union that integrated the German economy. An institution with that record is not a failure; it simply had a different purpose from the one nationalists wanted it to have.

**Reply:** The objection is correct and is, properly understood, the argument rather than a refutation of it. The Confederation succeeded at its actual purpose, which was stability, and that success is precisely why it could not perform the different task of unification. The two purposes require opposite designs: stability among sovereign members requires that nobody be compellable, while unification requires that somebody be. ⚠️ The reply also strengthens the case, because it explains why the German question could not be settled peacefully within the existing framework: the framework was working as intended. ✅ And the test case is on the record - when Prussia attempted a German reorganisation without Austria in 1850, the attempt collapsed at Olmutz. That is not hindsight; it is an experiment that had already been run.

**Residual:** What this evidence base cannot settle is the internal constitutional argument in Prussia between 1862 and 1866 - the legal reasoning deployed on each side, the parliamentary divisions and the extent of public opposition. The evidence base establishes the existence of the conflict, its subject and its timing. This session therefore teaches the constitutional stake and refuses to narrate the debates or to quantify the opposition.

### UPSC application

This lesson answers "why Prussia and not Austria?" without recourse to national character or to Bismarck's genius, which is what distinguishes a strong script. The four-row dualism table is the argument; the Olmutz test case is the evidence; and the five-requirement comparison with Piedmont is the ready-made comparative paragraph for any question that joins the two unifications. The budget conflict supplies something rarer: a demonstration that German unification had a domestic political purpose as well as a national one, which is exactly the material needed for questions about whether unification was a revolution from above.

### Examiner traps in this lesson

- Describing the Confederation as "weak" without explaining that it was designed to prevent action, which is a different and more interesting claim.
- Treating Austro-Prussian rivalry as a personality clash or a matter of prestige rather than a structural incompatibility between a national principle and a multinational empire.
- Omitting Olmutz, and therefore presenting 1866 as a first attempt rather than a second.
- Giving Bismarck's appointment without its context, which makes it look like a court appointment rather than a response to a constitutional crisis.
- Reproducing the iron-and-blood formulation verbatim, against the standard account's explicit control.
- Treating the army reorganisation as a technical matter, when the dispute was about whether a parliament could control an executive.
- Writing that German liberals supported Prussian leadership from the start, when the army conflict shows them in direct opposition to the government that later unified Germany.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ The German Confederation: thirty-nine states under Austrian dominance, created in 1815, with national unity postponed rather than solved.
2. ⚠️ It was designed to prevent reorganisation and therefore could not be the agent of reorganisation.
3. ⚠️ Dualism in four rows: what Germany is for; whether it should be tightened; who its people are; and what follows if the question is forced.
4. ⚠️ The deepest incompatibility: a German national state and a multinational Habsburg empire cannot both exist in the same Germany.
5. ✅ Olmutz, 1850: Prussia's mobilisation failed, it abandoned its own union scheme, and Austrian terms were accepted.
6. ⚠️ Olmutz shows the little-German aim predates Bismarck by twelve years; what changed afterwards was capability, not aim.
7. ✅ Prussia was strengthened at Vienna by the Rhineland, Westphalia and Saxon lands and had led the customs union since 1834.
8. ⚠️ Of the five core-state requirements, Prussia already met four; the fifth - a great-power ally - it did not need.
9. ✅ Italy needed an external great-power ally and Prussia did not; Prussian diplomacy therefore aimed at isolating enemies rather than recruiting allies.
10. ✅ Prussia prepared methodically after 1850, with Moltke as strategist and Roon as army organiser.
11. ✅ Bismarck was appointed minister-president in September 1862, during the constitutional conflict over the army budget between the king and the Prussian parliament.
12. ⚠️ The stake was whether a parliament could stop an executive; the army that unified Germany was funded without parliamentary consent.
13. ⚠️ Foreign success would retrospectively settle the domestic argument - which is why the wars had a domestic function too.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why the German question could not have been settled by agreement inside the German Confederation, and show why the constitutional conflict of 1862 is part of the same explanation rather than a separate domestic story.

**Model answer:** It could not be settled inside the Confederation because the Confederation's design and the participants' interests both prevented it. By design, the body consisted of thirty-nine sovereign states with a diet of instructed delegates presided over by Austria, possessing no power to compel a member in anything important; it had been created in 1815 to keep central Europe stable, and an institution built to prevent reorganisation cannot carry one out. By interest, the two great powers inside it wanted incompatible outcomes: Austria, a multinational empire in which Germans were a minority of subjects, needed Germany plural and weak, because a German national state would either exclude Austria or require the Habsburg monarchy to dismember itself, while Prussia, an overwhelmingly German state, could gain only from a Germany organised under its own leadership. No procedure existed that could overrule either great power, so the question could be decided only by force - and the point had already been tested, since Prussia's attempt to organise a German union without Austria collapsed in 1850 at Olmutz, where it was compelled to accept Austrian terms after its mobilisation failed. The constitutional conflict of 1862 belongs to the same explanation because it was about the instrument required for the eventual settlement. The crown demanded a larger, longer-serving army funded permanently; the Prussian chamber refused, since control of the army's budget was the one lever by which a parliament could restrain a monarchy; the constitution provided no procedure for deadlock, and the government collected revenue and funded the army without parliamentary consent. Bismarck was appointed in September 1862 precisely because the crown needed a minister willing to govern in that manner. The army that fought the three wars was therefore built against the objection of the liberal chamber, and success abroad was expected to settle the argument at home - which makes unification and the domestic constitutional question two halves of one process.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the budget crisis as a domestic quarrel about money that happened to coincide with unification. The dispute was about whether a parliament could stop an executive, the disputed expenditure built the instrument that unified Germany, and the eventual victory abroad was used to close the constitutional argument at home - which is why Lesson 14's Indemnity Bill is a political event of the first importance rather than an accounting formality.

**Bridge to Lesson 12:** A minister had now been appointed whose task was to settle by power a question that no assembly could settle by agreement. Before the wars are narrated, the method itself has to be understood - what Realpolitik commits its user to, what it explains, and what it cannot explain. The next lesson examines the method, its five operating rules, and the standard misreadings that cost marks.

## Lesson 12 - Realpolitik: the method, its rules, and what it does not explain

Progress: 12/20 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the definition the standard account supplies; five operating rules of the method; how a conservative statesman used liberal nationalism without sharing its aims; the planner-or-opportunist question; the limits of the method as an explanation

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the standard account's definition that Realpolitik means politics guided by practical state interest rather than abstract principle, and its instruction to use the term to explain why Bismarck could ally with liberal nationalism without sharing its aims; its record that Bismarck became minister-president in 1862 and pursued practical power politics; the historiographical companion's trap warning that Bismarck did not create nationalism but used it instrumentally under Prussian leadership, and its record of Bismarck as a state strategist contrasted with Garibaldi as a volunteer-revolutionary hero; the verified evidence that the 1866 campaign was a carefully planned stage in unification under Prussia with Bismarck as principal agent, carried from this session's single verification pass

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the method as a decision procedure

```text
   THE QUESTION A STATESMAN OF PRINCIPLE ASKS:
        "What is right? What does my doctrine require?"

   THE QUESTION REALPOLITIK ASKS:
        "What will actually increase my state's power, at what risk,
         and who will object strongly enough to act?"
   ============================================================================

   RULE 1 - DEFINE THE OBJECTIVE NARROWLY
      Not "German unity" as an ideal, but a specific: exclude Austria;
      then attach the southern states. Narrow objectives can be measured,
      timed and abandoned.
                |
   RULE 2 - ISOLATE THE OPPONENT BEFORE THE FIGHT
      The work is done before the war: ensure that the other great powers
      will not intervene. ✅ The standard account's contrast: Bismarck aimed to isolate
      enemies diplomatically; Cavour had to RECRUIT an ally.
                |
   RULE 3 - MAKE THE OPPONENT APPEAR THE AGGRESSOR
      Who starts a war determines who else joins it. A provocation that
      produces the other side's declaration is worth an army corps.
                |
   RULE 4 - LIMIT THE WAR AIM AND STOP ON TIME
      Take what is needed and no more. A humiliated enemy is a permanent
      enemy; a defeated but intact enemy may become a future partner.
                |
   RULE 5 - USE ANY FORCE AVAILABLE, INCLUDING YOUR OPPONENTS' IDEAS
      Liberal nationalism wants a German nation-state. A conservative
      monarchy can supply one - on its own terms. ⚠️ Borrow the demand,
      keep the power.
   ============================================================================
   WHAT THE METHOD IS NOT
   - not an ideology: it prescribes no end-state, only a way of choosing
   - not mere cynicism: it requires accurate assessment, and punishes wishful
     thinking more severely than principle does
   - not a guarantee: it improves the odds and does not remove contingency
```

*The five rules are not a biography. They are a procedure, and the value of the lesson is that you can apply the procedure to each of the three wars in Lessons 13 to 15 and predict what was done before being told.*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

Politics conducted on principle has a simple decision rule: work out what is right, then pursue it. The trouble is that in a states system the other players are not bound by your principles, and a state that acts on doctrine against players who act on interest will usually lose.

Realpolitik is the alternative rule. It says: treat the state's power and security as the object, assess the situation as it actually is, and choose the means that work, whatever their ideological colour. It is not a philosophy of life and it prescribes no particular Germany; it is a way of deciding.

The reason it matters here is that it makes an otherwise puzzling career intelligible. A deeply conservative servant of a monarchy fought three wars to create a national state, granted a parliament elected by universal male suffrage, and allied at different times with liberals and against them. On a doctrinal reading that is incoherent. On this reading it is one consistent policy: increase Prussia's power, and use whatever was to hand.

### 2. The definition, stated exactly

✅ **Fact:** the standard account for this topic defines **Realpolitik** as politics guided by **practical state interest rather than abstract principle**, and instructs that the term be used to explain **why Bismarck could ally with liberal nationalism without sharing its aims**.

✅ **Fact:** the standard account records that Bismarck became Prussia's minister-president in **1862** and pursued **practical power politics**; the historiographical companion records him as the **central architect of state-led unification** and as a **state strategist**, in contrast to Garibaldi as a volunteer-revolutionary hero.

✅ **Fact:** the historiographical companion states the principal trap directly: **do not write that Bismarck "created nationalism"** - he used nationalism **instrumentally** under Prussian leadership.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the standard account's phrase "ally with liberal nationalism without sharing its aims" is the most compressed statement of the method available, and it should be unpacked in three moves in any answer that uses it.

1. **The demand already existed.** German national feeling, the Frankfurt programme of 1848-49 and the desire for a single German state were not created in the 1860s; they were available.
2. **The demand had no instrument.** As Lesson 4 established, the movement could not compel a king or defeat an army.
3. **A state could supply the demand's content while withholding its conditions.** Unity could be delivered; popular sovereignty need not be. ⚠️ That exchange - a nation-state granted from above in return for the abandonment of parliamentary control - is the transaction at the heart of German unification, and Lesson 16 shows the terms written into a constitution.

### 3. The five rules, and how to recognise them

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the rules below are an analytical reconstruction from what was actually done. They are offered as a procedure to apply, and each is tested against the wars that follow.

| Rule | What it means in practice | Where it becomes visible |
|---|---|---|
| **Narrow the objective** | Replace an ideal with a specific, achievable, measurable aim | 1864: not "German unity" but the Schleswig-Holstein question; 1866: the exclusion of Austria, not the conquest of it |
| **Isolate before fighting** | Secure the neutrality of the other great powers in advance | ✅ The recorded contrast with Cavour: isolate rather than recruit; ✅ in 1866, the alliance with Italy divided Austria's forces |
| **Appear defensive** | Ensure that the opponent takes the step that starts the war | ✅ 1870: the published telegram infuriated the French government and provoked it into declaring war, and ✅ France declared war on 19 July 1870 |
| **Limit the aim and stop** | Take what the objective requires, refuse what would create a permanent coalition against you | ✅ 1866: Austria was excluded from Germany and, by the Treaty of Prague, not dismembered |
| **Use the opponent's ideas** | Adopt the national and even democratic demands of your domestic opponents, on your own terms | ✅ 1867 and 1871: a national parliament elected by universal male suffrage, without control of the executive |

✅ **Fact:** the campaign of 1866 was **a carefully planned stage in the unification of Germany under Prussia's ruling dynasty**, of which **Bismarck was the principal agent**, and Prussia had prepared methodically after the failure of 1850, with Bismarck as statesman, Moltke as strategist and Roon as army organiser.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** that single verified sentence is the strongest available evidence for the "planner" reading of the sequence, and it should be used precisely - it establishes planning for 1866, not a master plan for all three wars from 1862. Lesson 19 takes up what can and cannot be claimed about design.

### 4. Why a conservative could grant a democratic-looking constitution

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the hardest thing for candidates to accept is that granting universal male suffrage for a national parliament was a conservative move. It becomes intelligible once three considerations are separated.

| Consideration | The reasoning |
|---|---|
| **Suffrage is not power** | A parliament elected by everybody but unable to appoint or dismiss the government, control the army or direct foreign policy exercises influence, not control. Lesson 16 shows exactly which powers were withheld |
| **A wide electorate need not be a radical one** | ⚠️ In a largely rural society, a broad franchise may return conservative and deferential majorities more reliably than a narrow propertied one dominated by urban liberals - which was the constituency actually obstructing the government in Prussia |
| **It outflanks the domestic opposition** | ⚠️ A liberal chamber demanding constitutional control is embarrassed by a government that grants a wider suffrage than the liberals themselves proposed, while conceding none of the powers they wanted |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the general proposition is worth carrying into an answer: in the nineteenth century the *extent* of the franchise and the *location* of executive power were separate variables, and a regime could be generous with the first in order to retain the second. ⚠️ That is the single most useful analytical distinction available for the German constitutional material in Lesson 16.

### 5. What the method does not explain

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** answers regularly over-apply the method until it explains everything, at which point it explains nothing. Four limits should be stated.

1. **It does not explain the existence of the national demand.** ✅ The standard account's trap is explicit: Bismarck did not create nationalism. The demand came from the movements of Lessons 3 and 4; the method used it.
2. **It does not explain outcomes on the battlefield.** ⚠️ Plans require armies that can execute them, and the Prussian army's reorganisation, general staff and mobilisation capacity are a separate and necessary cause - which is why Lesson 14 treats the military instrument in its own right.
3. **It does not remove contingency.** ⚠️ Opponents make mistakes, third parties behave unexpectedly, and a plan that succeeds looks inevitable afterwards. Lesson 19 handles the resulting illusion directly.
4. **It does not settle whether there was a single master plan.** ⚠️ The evidence establishes deliberate preparation and a planned campaign in 1866; it does not establish that the whole sequence from 1862 to 1871 was designed in advance, and this session does not assert that it was.

> 🔑 **Answer line:** Realpolitik did not invent German nationalism; it purchased it. The movement wanted a nation-state and could not obtain one; a conservative monarchy could supply the state and kept the power - and the price of unity was the abandonment of the constitutional demand.

### Strongest objection, reply and residual

**Objection:** Treating Realpolitik as a coherent method is flattering hindsight. What actually happened was a series of improvisations by an opportunist who exploited crises he had not created - a Danish succession dispute, a Spanish throne vacancy, a French government's miscalculation - and who was rescued repeatedly by an army better than anyone expected. Calling that a method is reading design into luck.

**Reply:** The objection identifies a real danger and overcorrects. Three things are on the record and resist the "pure improvisation" reading: ✅ the 1866 campaign is recorded as a carefully planned stage in unification under Prussia, with Bismarck as its principal agent; ✅ Prussia had prepared methodically after the failure of 1850, with a named strategist and a named army organiser; and ✅ the 1870 provocation was a deliberate act with a stated purpose, since the telegram was edited and published in order to infuriate the French government and provoke a declaration of war. Those are not descriptions of accident. What the objection is right about is the *scope* of the design: none of this shows that the three wars were conceived as a single sequence in 1862, and this session does not claim that. ⚠️ The defensible position is that the objectives were pursued deliberately and the openings were exploited opportunistically - method in the use of opportunity rather than prophecy about its arrival - and Lesson 19 supplies the language for writing exactly that.

**Residual:** What this evidence base cannot settle is the content of Bismarck's intentions at any date - what he expected, what he would have accepted, and how far his later account of his own consistency was retrospective construction. No correspondence, memorandum or memoir is held here. The session therefore describes the method from what was done, marks the reconstruction as analytical, and declines to narrate a mind.

### UPSC application

"Assess the role of Bismarck" is a recurring demand, and it is a trap: the expected answer is a life sketch, and the rewarded answer is a functional analysis. This lesson supplies the second. Use the five rules as the structure, attach one piece of evidence to each, and then apply the two limits - he did not create nationalism, and he did not control the battlefield or the contingencies. That produces a balanced assessment with a verdict, which is what "assess" requires. The suffrage paradox is also worth carrying: a candidate who can explain why a conservative granted universal male suffrage has demonstrated exactly the analytical control that separates the top band.

### Examiner traps in this lesson

- Writing that Bismarck created German nationalism, against the standard account's explicit warning.
- Reproducing the iron-and-blood formulation as a quotation instead of using Realpolitik.
- Treating Realpolitik as cynicism or as an ideology, when it is a decision procedure that prescribes no end-state.
- Attributing the whole sequence of 1862-71 to a single master plan, which the evidence does not establish.
- Explaining the constitution's universal male suffrage as a concession to democracy rather than as a means of retaining executive power.
- Reducing the analysis to Bismarck's personality, which the historiographical companion's contrast with Garibaldi is designed to prevent.
- Forgetting the army: the method required an instrument, and the instrument was built during the budget conflict.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Realpolitik: politics guided by practical state interest rather than abstract principle.
2. ✅ Use it to explain why Bismarck could ally with liberal nationalism without sharing its aims.
3. ✅ Bismarck became minister-president in 1862 and pursued practical power politics; he was the central architect of state-led unification and a state strategist.
4. ✅ Trap: Bismarck did not create nationalism; he used it instrumentally under Prussian leadership.
5. ⚠️ Five rules: narrow the objective; isolate before fighting; appear defensive; limit the aim and stop; use the opponent's ideas.
6. ✅ Evidence for each: 1864 as a limited question; the recorded contrast of isolating rather than recruiting; the 1870 telegram provoking a French declaration; the Treaty of Prague leaving Austria intact; universal male suffrage without executive control.
7. ✅ The 1866 campaign was a carefully planned stage in unification under Prussia, with Bismarck as principal agent and methodical preparation after 1850.
8. ⚠️ Suffrage and executive power are separate variables; a regime may widen the first to retain the second.
9. ⚠️ A broad rural franchise may be less radical than a narrow urban propertied one - the constituency actually obstructing the Prussian government.
10. ⚠️ Four limits of the method: it does not create the national demand, win battles, remove contingency, or prove a master plan.
11. ⚠️ The transaction at the heart of German unification: the state supplies unity, the movement surrenders the constitutional demand.
12. ⚠️ Assess Bismarck functionally, not biographically - the examiner is testing method, not character.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain how a conservative minister could work with liberal nationalism without sharing its aims, and identify the price each side paid in that arrangement.

**Model answer:** He could do so because the national movement's demand and its instrument had been separated since 1849. The demand was for a single German nation-state, and it had been formulated by liberals: the Frankfurt assembly drafted a constitution, selected the little-German solution excluding Austria and offered the crown to the Prussian king in April 1849. The instrument was absent, because the assembly had no army, no revenue and no means of compelling a monarch, and the refusal of the crown demonstrated it. Realpolitik - politics guided by practical state interest rather than abstract principle - allowed a conservative government to supply the content of that demand while withholding its conditions: Prussia could create the nation-state the liberals wanted, and create it in a form that left the monarchy, the army and foreign policy outside parliamentary control. That is why the settlement of 1867 and 1871 granted a national parliament elected by universal male suffrage while withholding from it the power to appoint or dismiss the government, control the army or direct foreign policy - a wide franchise being compatible with, and in a largely rural society even helpful to, the retention of executive power. The price to the liberals was their own programme: they obtained national unity and abandoned the claim that a parliament should control the executive, a claim they had pressed hard during the Prussian budget conflict of the 1860s. The price to the government was that it had to deliver a real national state, including a national parliament and a wider franchise than Prussian conservatives would have chosen, and that it thereby legitimised nationalism as the organising principle of German politics - a force which, as the treatment of Poles, Danes, Alsatians, Catholics and socialists after 1871 would show, it could invoke but never fully control.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that using nationalism means having created it. The national demand was produced by the movements of the 1830s and 1840s and had been formulated politically at Frankfurt; the method purchased that demand rather than inventing it, which is why an answer crediting Bismarck with the creation of German nationalism misstates both the chronology and the mechanism.

**Bridge to Lesson 13:** The method is now stated, and the first of the three wars is the cleanest possible demonstration of it. A dispute over two duchies on the Danish frontier had nothing obvious to do with German unification - and was converted, in two years, into the quarrel that would justify a war against Austria. The next lesson examines the war of 1864 and the device of 1865 that made 1866 possible.

## Lesson 13 - The Danish war of 1864, and the manufacture of a quarrel

### Visual: a war fought to obtain a quarrel

```text
   STAGE 1 - A DISPUTE THAT LOOKS LIKE SOMEBODY ELSE'S BUSINESS
   Two duchies on the Danish frontier, Schleswig and Holstein, with
   mixed German and Danish populations and a tangled legal status.
   To most of Europe: a technical succession question.
   To German national opinion: German populations under a foreign crown.
                                |
   STAGE 2 - PRUSSIA INVITES AUSTRIA TO JOIN
   The war of 1864 is fought by PRUSSIA AND AUSTRIA TOGETHER against
   Denmark. Austria is a co-victor.
        WHY INVITE YOUR RIVAL TO SHARE A VICTORY?
        - it prevents Austria from opposing the operation
        - it prevents the Confederation's smaller states from
          organising a rival German solution
        - it places Austria, a power with no interest anywhere near
          the Baltic, in joint possession of territory it cannot
          administer conveniently
                                |
   STAGE 3 - THE JOINT POSSESSION IS THE POINT
   Austria and Prussia hold the duchies JOINTLY. Joint possession
   between rivals is not a settlement; it is a permanent source of
   friction that either side can activate at a time of its choosing.
                                |
   STAGE 4 - GASTEIN, AUGUST 1865: DIVIDE THE ADMINISTRATION
   SCHLESWIG administered by PRUSSIA | HOLSTEIN administered by AUSTRIA
        Austria now administers a territory adjoining Prussia,
        far from its own lands, surrounded by Prussian influence,
        and dependent on Prussian goodwill for access.
                                |
   STAGE 5 - THE QUARREL IS AVAILABLE ON DEMAND
   ✅ The dispute over the administration of the duchies became the
   PRETEXT for the war of 1866.
   =========================================================================
   THE LESSON: the first war's purpose was not the duchies. It was to
   place Austria in a position where a quarrel could be produced whenever
   Prussia was ready for it.
```

*Read the five stages as a single operation. If you remember only one thing from this lesson, remember that the 1864 war was fought jointly with the state that the 1866 war was fought against - and that the joint victory is what made the second war possible.*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

Suppose you intend to fight a great power and you need three things: a reason that will look legitimate, the neutrality of the other great powers, and an opponent who appears to be the obstinate party. You cannot simply announce that you intend to settle a fifty-year-old question of primacy by force; that would unite Europe against you.

So you need a quarrel. Not any quarrel - one in which your national public believes you are in the right, in which the other great powers see no interest of their own, and in which your intended opponent can be manoeuvred into an untenable position.

The duchies on the Danish frontier supplied exactly that. German national opinion regarded their populations as German. The dispute had a technical legal character that made it look like a matter of treaty interpretation rather than aggression. And by fighting the war jointly with Austria, Prussia obtained something far more valuable than the territory: a shared possession that could be turned into a grievance at a moment of its own choosing.

### 2. What happened, stated exactly

✅ **Fact:** the standard account for this topic records that in **1864 Denmark was defeated by Prussia and Austria**, and describes this as the **first of Bismarck's three wars**; its phase table names the **Danish War** and records that the **Schleswig-Holstein question opens the path**.

✅ **Fact:** the historiographical companion records that Denmark, Austria and France were defeated in sequence in 1864, 1866 and 1870-71, and that **war became the instrument of nation-building**.

✅ **Fact:** **Austria and Prussia seized Schleswig and Holstein from Denmark in 1864 and thereafter held them jointly**; the subsequent **dispute over the administration of Schleswig and Holstein** was the actual **pretext** found for the war of 1866.

✅ **Fact:** the **Convention of Gastein of August 1865** divided the administration, with **Schleswig administered by Prussia and Holstein by Austria**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the sequence is a textbook illustration of the first three rules of Lesson 12, and it should be written that way rather than narrated.

| Rule from Lesson 12 | Its application in 1864-65 |
|---|---|
| Narrow the objective | The stated question is the status of two duchies, not the leadership of Germany. A limited question attracts limited opposition |
| Isolate before fighting | Austria is not merely neutralised but *enlisted*, which removes the one power capable of organising German resistance to Prussian action |
| Appear legitimate | The operation is conducted on behalf of German populations and under a claim of treaty right, not as a war of conquest |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** and then the fourth move, which belongs to no rule and is the most instructive of all: **create a future pretext**. Joint possession of distant territory by two rivals is an arrangement that cannot last. Gastein's division of administration looks like a settlement and functions as a fuse: it gives Austria an administrative responsibility it cannot discharge without Prussian cooperation, in a place where Prussia has every advantage.

### 3. Why Austria accepted

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** candidates frequently ask why Austria joined a war whose gains it could not keep. Four considerations make the decision intelligible without requiring Austrian incompetence.

1. **Refusing was worse.** If Prussia acted alone in a German national cause and Austria stood aside, Austria's claim to lead Germany would have been visibly abandoned to its rival.
2. **The German public expected it.** A power presiding over the German Confederation could not allow the defence of German populations to become a Prussian monopoly.
3. **Joint action promised restraint.** Participating meant a share in the settlement and a check on what Prussia could do unilaterally.
4. **The alternative dispute was not yet obvious.** ⚠️ Joint possession looked like a guarantee of continued Austrian involvement in northern Germany, which is exactly how a well-designed trap is supposed to look.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the general proposition is usable well beyond this topic: an invitation to share in a victory is one of the most effective instruments available to a state that intends to fight its partner later, because refusal has immediate costs and acceptance has deferred ones.

### 4. What the duchies actually settled, and what they did not

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** three distinct results should be kept apart.

| Result | Content | Significance |
|---|---|---|
| **Territorial** | ✅ Schleswig-Holstein was ultimately assigned to Prussia by the Treaty of Prague in 1866 | The territory was obtained, but only after the second war - so 1864 did not by itself deliver it |
| **Diplomatic** | ⚠️ Austria was drawn into a commitment far from its own interests and left administering an exposed province | This is the operative result: a rival in an indefensible position |
| **National** | ⚠️ Prussia appeared as the defender of German populations, which improved its standing with the national movement it would later need | A conservative monarchy acquired national credentials at low cost |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the national result deserves emphasis because it connects to Lesson 12. A government fighting a constitutional battle at home against liberal opponents was simultaneously acting, in the eyes of national opinion, as the protector of Germans abroad. ⚠️ That is the beginning of the process by which the movement's demands were transferred to the state that opposed the movement's constitutional programme.

### 5. Reading the sequence forward

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** three features of this war should be carried into Lessons 14 and 15, because the same features recur.

- **Each war produces the conditions of the next.** 1864 creates the dispute used in 1866; 1866 creates the North German Confederation and leaves the southern states outside, which 1870 resolves. ⚠️ That is the strongest available evidence for a purposeful sequence, and it is also the reason Lesson 19 has to distinguish carefully between a sequence that was designed and one that was exploited.
- **The opponent is defeated but not destroyed.** ✅ Denmark lost the duchies and remained a state; ✅ Austria in 1866 was excluded but not dismembered. The pattern is deliberate and is the fourth rule of the method.
- **The prize is never the stated object.** The duchies mattered far less than Austria's position in relation to them. ⚠️ Any answer that treats the 1864 war as being about Schleswig-Holstein has taken the pretext for the purpose.

> 🔑 **Answer line:** The Danish war of 1864 was fought to obtain a quarrel, not a province: Austria was invited to share a victory it could not consolidate, and the joint possession that resulted was the reason a war between the victors could be produced two years later.

### Strongest objection, reply and residual

**Objection:** This reads a fully formed design into events that had their own momentum. The duchies question was a genuine European dispute with a long legal history, German national opinion demanded action, and Prussia responded to that demand. The Gastein arrangement was a real attempt to manage a joint occupation, not an ingenious fuse. The trap reading is available only because we know what happened in 1866.

**Reply:** The objection is right that the dispute was genuine and wrong that the reading requires hindsight. Two items in the record resist the accidental interpretation. ✅ First, the pass records explicitly that the dispute over the administration of the duchies was the **pretext found by Bismarck in 1866** - the word is pretext, not cause, and it comes from the evidence rather than from inference. ✅ Second, the same source records that Prussia had prepared methodically for a fresh challenge to Austria since the failure of 1850, with a statesman, a strategist and an army organiser named. A state that is preparing for a confrontation and then arranges a joint possession with its intended opponent is not stumbling. ⚠️ What the objection legitimately restrains is the stronger claim that the whole shape of 1866 was fixed in 1863-64; this session does not make that claim, and Lesson 19 distinguishes deliberate preparation from prophecy.

**Residual:** What this evidence base cannot settle is the internal legal and dynastic content of the Schleswig-Holstein question, the diplomatic exchanges of 1863-64, or the terms on which the two powers agreed to act together. The evidence base establishes the joint seizure, the joint possession, the Gastein division of administration and the later use of the dispute as a pretext. The legal history, the settlement of 1864 itself and the negotiations are outside what this session may assert.

### UPSC application

The 1864 war is the part of the German sequence that candidates either omit or misreport, and it is therefore an easy place to gain a comparative advantage. Two sentences are enough in a 250-word answer: Prussia and Austria seized the duchies jointly from Denmark in 1864, and the resulting dispute over their administration - divided at Gastein in 1865, with Schleswig under Prussia and Holstein under Austria - supplied the pretext for the war of 1866. Those two sentences demonstrate that you understand the wars as a connected operation rather than as three separate events, which is precisely what an "assess the role of Bismarck" or "war as an instrument of unification" question rewards.

### Examiner traps in this lesson

- Writing that Prussia fought Denmark alone in 1864, which destroys the entire mechanism.
- Treating the duchies as the objective rather than as the instrument.
- Omitting Gastein, and therefore being unable to explain where the 1866 quarrel came from.
- Reversing the Gastein administration - Schleswig was administered by Prussia and Holstein by Austria.
- Stating that Prussia annexed the duchies in 1864; the assignment to Prussia came with the Treaty of Prague in 1866.
- Presenting the war as an outburst of German national feeling, when it was a limited operation with a legal pretext.
- Asserting the legal details of the succession question, which this evidence base does not hold.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ 1864: Denmark was defeated by Prussia and Austria - the first of Bismarck's three wars.
2. ✅ The Schleswig-Holstein question opened the path to the later wars.
3. ✅ Austria and Prussia seized Schleswig and Holstein from Denmark in 1864 and held them jointly.
4. ✅ Convention of Gastein, August 1865: Schleswig administered by Prussia, Holstein by Austria.
5. ✅ The dispute over the administration of the duchies was the pretext found for the war of 1866.
6. ⚠️ Joint possession between rivals is not a settlement but an activatable grievance.
7. ⚠️ Four reasons Austria accepted: refusal would concede German leadership; German opinion expected action; joint action promised restraint; the trap did not look like one.
8. ⚠️ Three results: territorial assignment deferred to 1866; Austria placed in an exposed position; Prussia given national credentials cheaply.
9. ⚠️ Each war produces the conditions of the next - the strongest evidence for a purposeful sequence.
10. ✅ Opponents are defeated and not destroyed - Denmark survived, and Austria was excluded rather than dismembered in 1866.
11. ⚠️ The prize is never the stated object; taking the pretext for the purpose is the standard error.
12. ⚠️ General proposition: inviting a rival to share a victory is an effective way to prepare a war against it.

### Concept check

**Question:** Why did Prussia fight the war of 1864 in alliance with the very power it intended to fight in 1866, and what did the alliance actually deliver?

**Model answer:** Because the alliance delivered something more useful than a province. Fighting jointly removed Austria's capacity to oppose the operation or to organise the smaller German states behind a rival solution, since a power that is a co-belligerent cannot simultaneously lead resistance to its partner. It also gave Prussia national credentials at low cost, because the operation was conducted on behalf of German populations under a claim of treaty right, so a conservative government then fighting its own liberal chamber over the army budget appeared to national opinion as the defender of Germans abroad. Above all, the joint victory produced joint possession: Austria and Prussia seized Schleswig and Holstein from Denmark in 1864 and held them together, and joint possession between two rivals is not a settlement but a standing source of friction that either party can activate when it chooses. The Convention of Gastein of August 1865, which divided the administration so that Prussia administered Schleswig and Austria administered Holstein, appeared to stabilise the arrangement and in fact completed the trap, since Austria was now administering an exposed territory far from its own lands, adjoining Prussia and dependent on Prussian cooperation. The dispute over that administration was the pretext found for the war of 1866. The alliance therefore delivered a rival placed in an indefensible position, a quarrel available on demand, and a reputation as the champion of German interests - none of which could have been obtained by fighting Denmark alone.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the war as being about Schleswig-Holstein. The duchies were the occasion; the object was Austria's position in relation to them. Note also that the territory itself was assigned to Prussia only by the Treaty of Prague in 1866, so an answer that has Prussia annexing the duchies in 1864 has both the fact and the mechanism wrong.

**Bridge to Lesson 14:** The quarrel now existed, the army had been rebuilt, and the other great powers had no interest in the duchies. What remained was to ensure that Austria fought on two fronts and that Europe stood aside. The next lesson examines the war that excluded Austria from Germany, the state that was built in its aftermath, and the moment at which German liberalism surrendered the constitutional claim it had defended since 1848.

## Lesson 14 - Koniggratz, Prague and the North German Confederation

### Visual: seven weeks, and the four things they settled

```text
   Prussia and Austria both claim            PRUSSIA

   the Confederation cannot decide                  HANOVER and other German states
   between them                              ALLIED WITH PRUSSIA: ITALY
                                             (which divides Austria's forces)
                     |
                     v
   DECIDED IN BOHEMIA, most decisively at KONIGGRATZ, 3 JULY 1866
   A second Prussian force, the army of the Main, deals with Bavaria and
   the other German states that sided with Austria; a simultaneous campaign
   is fought in Venetia against the Italians
                     |
                     v
   TREATY OF PRAGUE, 23 AUGUST 1866 - AND NOTE WHAT IS *NOT* IN IT
   +--------------------------------------------------------------+
   | IN   : Austria EXCLUDED from Germany                          |
   | IN   : Schleswig-Holstein assigned to Prussia                 |
   | IN   : Prussia annexes HANOVER, HESSE-KASSEL, NASSAU and      |
   |        FRANKFURT outright - acquiring the territory that had  |
   |        SEPARATED ITS EASTERN AND WESTERN HALVES               |
   | OUT  : no dismemberment of Austria, no territorial amputation |
   |        of the Habsburg monarchy by Prussia                    |
   +--------------------------------------------------------------+
                     |
   3 OCTOBER 1866: by the Peace of Vienna, Austria cedes VENETIA
   for transfer to Italy
                     |
                     v
   1867: NORTH GERMAN CONFEDERATION - a federal state under Prussia,
   with a Reichstag elected by UNIVERSAL MALE SUFFRAGE
                     |
                     v
   1866 AT HOME: THE INDEMNITY BILL
   The chamber retrospectively approves four years of expenditure it
   had refused to authorise. The constitutional conflict ends in the
   government's favour, by consent.
   ==================================================================
   FOUR THINGS SETTLED IN SEVEN WEEKS:
   1. who leads Germany           3. whether Prussia is territorially whole
   2. whether Austria is in it    4. whether a parliament can stop an executive
```

*The most important box in the diagram is the one labelled OUT. A victor who refuses available spoils is not being generous; he is choosing which enemies he intends to have five years later. That single decision is the clearest instance of Realpolitik in the whole topic.*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

By early 1866 the confrontation was ready. The instrument existed - an army rebuilt and funded without parliamentary consent. The pretext existed - a dispute over the administration of two duchies held jointly with the intended opponent. What remained was the hardest part: making sure that a war between the two German great powers did not become a European war in which Prussia faced a coalition.

That required three things. Austria had to fight on more than one front. France had to stand aside. And the war had to be short, because a long war invites intervention. All three were achieved, and the third was achieved so completely that the conflict is named for its length.

Then came the part that is easy to overlook and is more consequential than the battle: deciding what to demand from the defeated.

### 2. The war, stated exactly

✅ **Fact:** the standard account for this topic records that in **1866 Prussia defeated Austria in the Seven Weeks' War**, that **Venetia went to Italy**, and that **Austria was excluded from German affairs** while Italy advanced; its phase table records the outcome as Austria excluded and the North German Confederation emerging.

✅ **Fact:** the war of 1866 was fought between **Prussia** on one side and **Austria, Bavaria, Saxony, Hanover and certain minor German states** on the other; it ended in a Prussian victory which meant **the exclusion of Austria from Germany**; the issue was decided in **Bohemia**, most decisively at the **Battle of Koniggratz**; a Prussian detachment known as the **army of the Main** dealt with the forces of Bavaria and the other German states that had sided with Austria; and a simultaneous campaign was fought in **Venetia** between the Austrian army of the south and the Italians, who had made an alliance with Prussia.

✅ **Fact:** **by the alliance with Italy, Bismarck contrived to divert part of the Austrian forces to the south**, and this advantage, together with Prussia's modernised army discipline, produced the victory; diplomatic exchanges began in January 1866 and military preparations somewhat later, while hostilities broke out only in the middle of June.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the Italian alliance is the exact mirror image of the Italian position in 1859 and should be taught as such. In 1859 Piedmont obtained a great power's army and paid in territory. In 1866 Prussia obtained a secondary power's army and paid in territory that was not its own - Venetia came from Austria. ⚠️ The comparison is the single most economical way to demonstrate the difference between recruiting an ally and using one.

### 3. The terms: what was taken, and what was deliberately refused

✅ **Fact:** the war was formally concluded on **23 August 1866 by the Treaty of Prague**, which assigned **Schleswig-Holstein to Prussia**; Prussia also **annexed Hanover, Hesse-Kassel, Nassau and Frankfurt outright**, thereby acquiring the territory that had **separated the eastern and the western parts of the Prussian state**; and by the **Peace of Vienna of 3 October 1866** Austria ceded **Venetia for transfer to Italy**. The victory enabled Prussia to organise the **North German Confederation**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** four points, in ascending order of importance.

1. **The annexations solved a geographical problem.** Prussia had been a state in two pieces since 1815, its Rhenish and Westphalian lands separated from its eastern core. The 1866 annexations made it contiguous. ⚠️ A state that cannot move troops between its own halves without crossing foreign territory is not a first-class power; after 1866 Prussia was territorially whole.
2. **The German states that fought against Prussia were treated variously.** Some were annexed outright; others survived and were incorporated into the new confederation. ⚠️ The differential treatment is itself a Realpolitik decision: annex where consolidation is essential, preserve where a resentful but surviving monarchy is more useful than a conquered province.
3. **Austria lost no territory to Prussia.** ✅ It was excluded from Germany and ceded Venetia for transfer to Italy, but the Habsburg monarchy was not dismembered by its victor.
4. **That restraint was the decisive political act of the war.** ⚠️ A humiliated Austria would have been a permanent enemy seeking revenge and a natural ally for France; an Austria excluded from Germany but intact could accept the new situation and even become a future partner. ⚠️ The contrast with the treatment of France in 1871, examined in Lesson 15, is the sharpest available illustration of how a peace settlement determines the next generation's alliances - and Lesson 18 uses exactly this contrast.

> 🔑 **Answer line:** In 1866 Prussia took everything it needed and nothing it merely wanted; in 1871 it took what it wanted, and the difference explains why Austria became a partner and France a permanent enemy.

### 4. What was built: the North German Confederation

✅ **Fact:** the historiographical companion records the 1866 outcome as **Austria excluded, the North German Confederation formed**, and **"Little Germany" under Prussia prevailing**; ✅ the standard account's limits bank records that Austria was excluded in 1866 so that unification was **small German in form**, and notes that this exclusion is the direct root of the later question of union between Germany and Austria - a question owned by a subsequent topic.

✅ **Fact:** the **constitution of the North German Confederation came into force on 1 July 1867**, establishing a federal state headed by Prussia, with a **Reichstag elected by universal male suffrage**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the new confederation is not a footnote between two wars; it is the German state in its first form, and three of its features are examinable.

| Feature | Content | Why it matters |
|---|---|---|
| **Scope** | Germany north of the river Main; the southern states remained outside | The unification was deliberately incomplete, which left the task that 1870 completed |
| **Form** | A federal state led by Prussia rather than a confederation of equals | ⚠️ The distinction between a federal *state* and a confederation is the difference between a body that can compel its members and one that cannot - the exact defect of the old Confederation, now corrected in Prussia's favour |
| **Representation** | A national parliament elected by universal male suffrage | ⚠️ The liberal programme's democratic form, adopted by a conservative government, with executive power retained outside it - the transaction described in Lesson 12 and constitutionalised in Lesson 16 |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the southern exclusion deserves a sentence of its own. Leaving Bavaria, Wurttemberg and Baden outside was partly a concession to French sensitivity and partly a recognition that the southern states were Catholic, particularist and unwilling. ⚠️ It created a specific and identifiable unfinished business, and it meant that the final step would require something capable of overcoming southern reluctance - which a war against France supplied.

### 5. The Indemnity Bill: where German liberalism surrendered

✅ **Fact:** after the victory of 1866, an **Indemnity Bill** was passed, **retroactively legalising the government's previously unauthorised expenditure** during the constitutional conflict.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is a constitutional event of the first order, and it is where the domestic thread of Lesson 11 concludes.

```text
   1862-66   the government governs and funds the army WITHOUT the
             chamber's consent; the chamber maintains that this is
             unconstitutional
                        |
   July 1866  the army built with that money wins a decisive victory
             and excludes Austria from Germany
                        |
   1866      THE CHAMBER RETROSPECTIVELY APPROVES the expenditure
                        |
   MEANING   the liberal majority accepts that the government acted
             rightly - or at least that success has settled the
             question. The claim that a parliament may stop an
             executive is not defeated in argument; it is ABANDONED
             in exchange for a nation-state.
```

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** three consequences follow, and they are the basis of much of Lesson 16 and of the historiography in Lesson 19.

1. **The constitutional question was settled by foreign policy.** A domestic dispute about parliamentary power was closed by a battlefield result, which established the principle that success abroad legitimises action at home.
2. **German liberalism split and the larger part accommodated itself** to a government it had opposed. ⚠️ The movement obtained unity and surrendered the constitutional claim, which is the concrete content of the standard account's observation that German unification fulfilled unity under conservative-Prussian dominance rather than the liberal parliamentary hopes of 1848.
3. **The precedent outlived the men.** ⚠️ A parliament that has once retrospectively approved unauthorised government is weaker in every subsequent confrontation, and the constitution written in 1867 and extended in 1871 institutionalised exactly that weakness.

### Strongest objection, reply and residual

**Objection:** Calling the Indemnity Bill a surrender is a judgement rather than an analysis. The liberals obtained a great deal: a federal German state, a national parliament elected by universal male suffrage, and the exclusion of Austria, which was their own programme from 1849. Accepting a retrospective settlement of a four-year dispute in exchange for the realisation of that programme was a rational bargain, not a capitulation.

**Reply:** The objection is right that a real exchange took place and wrong to conclude that nothing was lost. Both halves must be stated, and the strong answer states both. What was gained is genuine and should be conceded: a German state north of the Main, a national parliament on a wider franchise than most of Europe then possessed, and a small-German solution the liberals themselves had chosen in 1849. What was lost is equally concrete: the principle that a parliament controls the executive through the budget, which is the single mechanism by which a chamber can restrain a government, and which had been the whole subject of the conflict. ⚠️ The decisive test is what happened next: the constitution written in 1867 and carried into 1871 withheld from the national parliament any control over the chancellor, the army or foreign policy - so the concession was not a temporary accommodation but a permanent constitutional settlement. ✅ The standard account's judgement is available and exact: German unification fulfilled unity, but under conservative-Prussian dominance rather than under the liberal parliamentary hopes of 1848.

**Residual:** What this evidence base cannot settle is the internal politics of the liberal split - which groupings accepted the bill, on what reasoning, and what the electoral consequences were. The evidence base establishes the bill and its retrospective character. This session therefore teaches the constitutional meaning and declines to narrate party alignments or to quantify the division.

### UPSC application

This lesson supplies the central evidence for three distinct demands. For "war as an instrument of nation-building", it gives you a war whose terms were chosen with the next war in mind - the exclusion of Austria without dismemberment. For "revolution from above", the Indemnity Bill is the sharpest single piece of evidence in the topic, because it shows the liberal chamber itself conceding the constitutional point. For any comparison with Italy, the Italian alliance of 1866 gives you a perfectly symmetrical contrast: in 1859 Italy hired a great power and paid with its own territory; in 1866 Prussia hired Italy and paid with somebody else's. Use the restraint at Prague as the hinge to Lesson 18, where the contrast with 1871 becomes an argument about the origins of the later European alignment.

### Examiner traps in this lesson

- Writing that Prussia dismembered or annexed Austrian territory in 1866, when Austria was excluded and not partitioned.
- Omitting the Italian alliance, which is what divided Austria's forces.
- Treating Koniggratz as the whole war, when a second Prussian force dealt with the German states allied to Austria and a third front ran in Venetia.
- Forgetting that several German states fought *against* Prussia, which is the fact that disproves any simple story of German national enthusiasm for Prussian leadership.
- Omitting the annexations of Hanover, Hesse-Kassel, Nassau and Frankfurt, and therefore missing that Prussia became territorially contiguous.
- Skipping the North German Confederation and jumping from 1866 to 1871, which loses the state in which the German constitutional settlement was actually invented.
- Ignoring the Indemnity Bill, which is where the domestic constitutional question was decided.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ 1866: Prussia defeated Austria in the Seven Weeks' War; Austria was excluded from German affairs; Venetia went to Italy.
2. ✅ The opposing coalition included Austria, Bavaria, Saxony, Hanover and other German states; Italy was allied with Prussia.
3. ✅ The issue was decided in Bohemia, most decisively at Koniggratz on 3 July 1866; the army of the Main dealt with the southern German states; a third campaign ran in Venetia.
4. ✅ The Italian alliance diverted part of Austria's forces; diplomatic exchanges began in January and hostilities in mid-June.
5. ✅ Treaty of Prague, 23 August 1866: Austria excluded; Schleswig-Holstein assigned to Prussia.
6. ✅ Prussia annexed Hanover, Hesse-Kassel, Nassau and Frankfurt outright, acquiring the territory that had separated its eastern and western parts.
7. ✅ Peace of Vienna, 3 October 1866: Austria ceded Venetia for transfer to Italy.
8. ⚠️ Austria lost no territory to Prussia - restraint chosen to avoid creating a permanent enemy, in contrast with the treatment of France in 1871.
9. ✅ The North German Confederation's constitution came into force on 1 July 1867: a federal state under Prussia with a Reichstag elected by universal male suffrage.
10. ⚠️ The southern states remained outside - a deliberate incompleteness that the 1870 war resolved.
11. ✅ "Little Germany" under Prussia prevailed; ✅ unification was small German in form, which is the root of the later Austro-German union question owned by a subsequent topic.
12. ✅ The Indemnity Bill of 1866 retrospectively legalised four years of unauthorised expenditure.
13. ⚠️ The liberal exchange: unity gained, parliamentary control of the executive abandoned; ✅ unity under conservative-Prussian dominance rather than the liberal hopes of 1848.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why the moderation of the Treaty of Prague was a strategic decision rather than an act of generosity, and what the Indemnity Bill of the same year settled inside Prussia.

**Model answer:** The moderation was strategic because the object of the war was exclusion, not conquest, and because the terms of a peace determine the alignments of the following decade. Austria was excluded from Germany, Schleswig-Holstein was assigned to Prussia, and Venetia was ceded for transfer to Italy, but the Habsburg monarchy lost no territory to its victor and was not dismembered. An amputated Austria would have been a permanent revisionist enemy on Prussia's south-eastern flank and the natural partner of any future French coalition; an Austria excluded from Germany but intact could accept the new arrangement and eventually become a partner - which is precisely the contrast with 1871, when the annexation of Alsace and part of Lorraine from France produced a grievance that survived for decades and shaped the European alignment down to 1914. What the war also delivered, at home, was decided by the Indemnity Bill of the same year. Since 1862 the Prussian government had funded the reorganised army without the chamber's consent, in a constitutional conflict over whether a parliament could restrain an executive through the budget. After the victory the chamber retrospectively approved that expenditure, which meant the liberal majority accepted that the government had acted rightly, or at least that success had settled the question. The constitutional claim was therefore not defeated in argument but abandoned in exchange for a German state, and the exchange was made permanent by the constitution of the North German Confederation, in force from 1 July 1867, which gave Germany a parliament elected by universal male suffrage while withholding from it any control over the chancellor, the army or foreign policy.

**Misconception to avoid:** Reading the mildness toward Austria as evidence of Bismarck's moderation as a statesman. The same government annexed four German states outright in the same settlement and imposed a punitive peace on France five years later. The consistent principle is not restraint but calculation: take what the objective requires and refuse what would create a coalition, which explains both the leniency of 1866 and the severity of 1871.

**Bridge to Lesson 15:** North of the Main a German federal state now existed; south of it, three states remained outside, Catholic and unwilling, and France had watched a rival transform itself in seven weeks. The last step required something that would bring the southern states into a Prussian-led Germany by their own choice - and the only force capable of producing that was a war against France in which France appeared to be the aggressor. The next lesson examines how that war was obtained and what its settlement cost Europe.

## Lesson 15 - The French war of 1870-71, and the empire proclaimed in a French palace

Progress: 15/20 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: why the southern states had to be brought in by a war; the Spanish candidature and the Ems telegram; the French declaration of 19 July 1870; Sedan and the siege of Paris; the proclamation of 18 January 1871; the Treaty of Frankfurt, Alsace-Lorraine and the indemnity; the consequences the settlement guaranteed
