### Visual: from a vacant Spanish throne to a German empire

```text
   THE PROBLEM AFTER 1866
   North of the Main: a federal German state under Prussia.
   South of the Main: BAVARIA, WURTTEMBERG, BADEN - outside it,
   Catholic, particularist, unwilling to be absorbed.
   WHAT WOULD MAKE THEM JOIN BY THEIR OWN CHOICE?
   -> a war in which GERMANY IS ATTACKED.
                              |
   THE OCCASION
   1868  the Spanish queen is deposed; the throne is vacant
   June 1870  Prince LEOPOLD OF HOHENZOLLERN-SIGMARINGEN, related to
              the Prussian royal house, is persuaded to accept the
              Spanish throne. FRANCE IS ALARMED: Prussia and Spain
              on two frontiers.
   July 1870  the candidacy is WITHDRAWN under French diplomatic
              pressure - France has won the point
                              |
   THE OVER-REACH AND THE PROVOCATION
   France demands a further guarantee: that the candidacy will NEVER
   be renewed. King William I declines to give it.
   14 JULY 1870  Bismarck EDITS the king's telegraphed account of the
   interview and PUBLISHES it - the EMS TELEGRAM - with the stated
   purpose of infuriating the French government and provoking a
   declaration of war.
                              |
   19 JULY 1870  FRANCE DECLARES WAR. The aggressor is identified.
   -> BAVARIA, WURTTEMBERG AND BADEN SIDE WITH PRUSSIA.
                              |
   THE WAR
   6 Aug    Worth; French right wing retreats
   16/18 Aug Mars-la-Tour and Gravelotte; Bazaine shut up in Metz
   1-2 SEPT SEDAN: the emperor and Mac-Mahon captured; ~104,000
            prisoners; the war is effectively decided
   4 Sept   Paris: the emperor is deposed; the THIRD REPUBLIC begins
   19 Sept  the SIEGE OF PARIS begins
   27 Oct   Metz capitulates with 140,000 troops intact
   18 JAN 1871  WILLIAM I PROCLAIMED GERMAN EMPEROR AT VERSAILLES
   28 Jan   Paris surrenders; armistice
   10 MAY 1871  TREATY OF FRANKFURT: ALSACE and half of LORRAINE with
            METZ annexed; indemnity of FIVE BILLION FRANCS plus the
            costs of occupation until it is paid
                              |
   THE CONSEQUENCE BUILT INTO THE SETTLEMENT
   "The French resented the loss of Alsace-Lorraine" -> an unstable
   peace from 1871 to 1914 -> handed to the 1914 owner
```

*The diagram contains the whole method of Lesson 12 in one sequence: a narrow occasion, an opponent manoeuvred into declaring war, third parties who therefore join the defender, a short decisive campaign - and then, at the end, a settlement that breaks the fourth rule and creates a permanent enemy.*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

After 1866 Germany was two-thirds built. The missing third was a group of southern states that had fought against Prussia in that very war, were Catholic in a largely Protestant-led union, and had no wish to be absorbed by a northern power.

They could not be conquered: annexing German states by force in peacetime would have destroyed the national legitimacy on which the whole project rested and would have alarmed every great power. They could not be persuaded by argument, because the argument had been available for twenty years and had not persuaded them.

There was one circumstance in which they would join voluntarily and immediately: a war in which Germany was attacked by France. Under attack, particularism becomes treason and a common defence becomes a common state. The problem was therefore not military but procedural - how to obtain a war in which the other side declares first.

### 2. The provocation, stated exactly

✅ **Fact:** Prussia's defeat of Austria in the Seven Weeks' War of 1866 had **confirmed Prussian leadership of the German states and threatened France's position as the dominant power in Europe**.

✅ **Fact:** the immediate cause of the war was the candidacy of **Prince Leopold of Hohenzollern-Sigmaringen**, related to the Prussian royal house, for the **Spanish throne**, vacant since **Queen Isabella II was deposed in 1868**; **Bismarck** and Spain's leader **Juan Prim** persuaded the reluctant Leopold to accept in **June 1870**; France was alarmed by the prospect of a Prussian-Spanish combination; the candidacy was **withdrawn under French diplomatic pressure**; but the Prussian king **William I** was unwilling to accept the French ambassador's demand that he promise never again to allow the candidacy; **Bismarck edited William's telegraphed description of this interview and on 14 July published this provocative message - the Ems telegram - which accomplished his purposes of infuriating the French government and provoking it into a declaration of war**.

✅ **Fact:** **Napoleon III declared war on Prussia on 19 July 1870**, because his military advisers told him the French army could defeat Prussia and that such a victory would restore his declining popularity; the French believed their army reorganisation of 1866 had made them superior and had great faith in the breech-loading **chassepot** rifle and the newly invented **mitrailleuse**.

✅ **Fact:** **Bismarck saw war with France as an opportunity to bring the south German states into unity with the Prussian-led North German Confederation and to build a strong German Empire**; the Germans had superiority of numbers because **Bavaria, Wurttemberg and Baden regarded France as the aggressor and sided with Prussia**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** three features make this the purest demonstration of the method in the topic.

1. **The provocation is documented, not inferred.** ✅ The purpose of the edited publication is recorded: to infuriate and to provoke a declaration. This is the one place in the sequence where intention is on the record rather than reconstructed, which is why Lesson 12 uses it as its strongest evidence and Lesson 19 treats it as the anchor of the "planner" reading.
2. **The diplomatic object was the identity of the aggressor, not the substance.** France had already won the substantive point when the candidacy was withdrawn; the war arose from the demand for a guarantee about the future. ⚠️ A state that presses a won argument into a demand for a permanent guarantee has handed its opponent the means to make it look aggressive.
3. **The southern states' adherence was the war's real prize.** ✅ The recorded reason they sided with Prussia is that they regarded France as the aggressor. ⚠️ The war therefore performed, in a few weeks, the political work that twenty years of national argument had not performed - which is the most compressed possible statement of how German unification was completed.

### 3. The campaign, compressed to what an answer needs

✅ **Fact:** German mobilisation delivered **380,000 troops to the forward zone within eighteen days**, against confusion and delay on the French side; the Prussian army's general staff, under chief of staff **Helmuth von Moltke**, planned the rapid movement of large numbers of troops and exploited German superiority in numbers in most engagements.

✅ **Fact:** after a check at the **Battle of Worth on 6 August 1870**, Marshal **Mac-Mahon** retreated westward; Marshal **Bazaine** was driven back to the fortress of **Metz**, his further retreat checked at the battles of **Mars-la-Tour and Gravelotte on 16 and 18 August**; Mac-Mahon's force, accompanied by Napoleon III, was encircled at **Sedan on 31 August**, and in the battle of **1-2 September 1870**, under artillery bombardment from the surrounding heights, the French lost **17,000 men** and **surrendered on 2 September**, with about **104,000 officers and men taken prisoner, including both Napoleon and Mac-Mahon**, against German losses of **460 officers and 8,500 men**; the war's result was virtually decided by this surrender.

✅ **Fact:** a **government of national defence** assumed power in Paris on **4 September 1870**, proclaiming the deposition of the emperor and the establishment of the **Third Republic**; the Germans began to **besiege Paris on 19 September**; negotiations by the foreign minister **Jules Favre** broke off when Germany **demanded Alsace and Lorraine**; **Leon Gambetta** escaped from besieged Paris by balloon and organised new armies in the countryside, which engaged but could not defeat the German forces; **Bazaine capitulated at Metz with 140,000 troops intact on 27 October**; and **Paris surrendered on 28 January 1871**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** an examination answer needs three of these and not twenty. Sedan, because it decided the war and removed the emperor who had protected the papacy - which is the hinge to Lesson 9. The siege of Paris, because it shows a war continued by a republic after the regime that began it had fallen, which is what made the settlement so bitter. And the demand for Alsace and Lorraine during the negotiations, because it establishes that the annexation was a German war aim before the final victory rather than an afterthought.

### 4. The proclamation and the settlement

✅ **Fact:** the standard account for this topic records that in **January 1871 the German Empire was proclaimed at Versailles** and that **William I of Prussia became emperor**; ✅ **the culminating triumph came on 18 January 1871, when King William I of Prussia was proclaimed German emperor at Versailles, the former palace of the kings of France**.

✅ **Fact:** between the ratification of the preliminary settlement on 1 March and the conclusion of the formal **Treaty of Frankfurt on 10 May 1871**, the republican government faced an insurrection in Paris in which radicals established a short-lived government, the **Paris Commune**, suppressed after two months; the treaty's harsh provisions were then implemented - **Germany annexed Alsace and half of Lorraine, with Metz**, and **France had to pay an indemnity of five billion francs** and cover the costs of German occupation of its northern provinces until the indemnity was paid.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the place of the proclamation is not decoration and should be stated in any answer about consequences. A German empire proclaimed in the palace of the French kings, while a French capital was under siege, fused the new national state with a humiliation of France in a single image. ⚠️ It made the Empire's founding inseparable from a French defeat, which guaranteed that French national feeling and German national feeling would be organised against each other from the first day of the new state's existence.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the annexation is the decisive analytical point of the lesson, because it is where the method's own fourth rule was broken. ✅ In 1866 Austria was excluded and left intact, and became a power that could accept the outcome. ✅ In 1871 France lost territory with a major fortress and was burdened with an indemnity and an occupation. ✅ The core world-history source read for this session records the result flatly: **the French resented the loss of Alsace-Lorraine to Germany at the end of the Franco-Prussian War of 1871**, and **the French were obsessed with taking back Alsace-Lorraine, which the Germans had taken in 1871**. ✅ Germany's annexation of Alsace-Lorraine aroused a deep longing for revenge in the French people, and the years from 1871 to 1914 were marked by an extremely unstable peace.

✅ **Fact:** the standard account's limits bank records exactly this: Alsace-Lorraine was lost in 1871 and **French revanchism is a named source of friction in 1914**, described as the clearest long causal link in the folder.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** why was the rule broken? Two explanations are available and both should be offered rather than one chosen: a military argument, that the annexed territory with its fortress covered the invasion route into southern Germany and was demanded on strategic grounds; and a political argument, that a new state assembled out of a war needed a visible trophy to bind its members and to justify the cost. ⚠️ This session holds no documentary record of the internal debate, so both are marked as inference; what is not in doubt is the consequence, which is on the record.

> 🔑 **Answer line:** German unification was completed by a war France declared, and sealed by a peace that ensured France would never accept the result: the Empire was born with its principal enemy already created.

### 5. What the war produced, listed exactly

✅ **Fact:** the war established both the **German Empire** and the **French Third Republic**; with Napoleon III no longer in power to protect them, the **Papal States were annexed by Italy on 20 September 1870**, completing Italian unification; the German victory consolidated faith in **Prussian militarism**, which remained a dominant force in German society; the Prussian system of conscript armies controlled by a highly trained general staff was soon adopted by the other great powers; and the mutual animosity produced by the annexation was the driving force behind the confrontation that followed.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** five outputs, one war - and the fact that two of them are the completion of *Italian* unification and the creation of the *French* republic is the strongest possible demonstration that this topic's two halves and their neighbours form a single European process.

| Output | Owner |
|---|---|
| The German Empire | This topic, Lesson 16 |
| Italy's acquisition of Rome | This topic, Lesson 9 |
| The French Third Republic | Not owned here; recorded as a consequence |
| The prestige of Prussian militarism and the spread of the general-staff conscript model | This topic, Lesson 18, as a consequence for Europe |
| French revanchism and the unstable peace to 1914 | Handed to the topic on the world in 1914 |

### Strongest objection, reply and residual

**Objection:** The account makes France a passive victim of manipulation. France was a great power with its own ambitions; its government chose to demand a humiliating guarantee, chose to declare war, and did so because its advisers believed it would win and because a victory would shore up the regime at home. A telegram cannot force a state to mobilise.

**Reply:** The objection is correct and is part of the analysis rather than a refutation of it. ✅ The record is explicit that the French emperor declared war on 19 July 1870 because his military advisers told him the French army could defeat Prussia and that victory would restore his declining popularity, and that French confidence rested on their army reorganisation and on two new weapons. That is a decision, taken for reasons of its own, and the responsibility for it lies where it fell. ⚠️ What the edited telegram did was not to compel but to choose the moment and the appearance: it converted a dispute France had already won into a public affront, at a time of Prussia's choosing, in a form that made the French government's own bellicosity decisive. ✅ The purpose is recorded - to infuriate and to provoke a declaration - and the purpose succeeded because of French choices, not instead of them. ⚠️ The formulation for an answer is that Prussia supplied the occasion and France supplied the decision, and that the method consists precisely in arranging matters so that an opponent's own character completes the plan.

**Residual:** What this evidence base cannot settle is the internal German debate over the annexation of Alsace and Lorraine - who argued for it, on what grounds, and whether the strategic or the political argument prevailed. The evidence base establishes the demand during the negotiations, the terms of the treaty and the consequences. The motives are marked as inference and are not narrated.

### UPSC application

This lesson supplies two high-value units. The first is the provocation sequence - candidacy, withdrawal, demand for a guarantee, edited telegram of 14 July, French declaration of 19 July, southern states joining the defender - which in four clauses demonstrates the whole method and explains why the southern states joined voluntarily. The second is the annexation and its consequence, which is the topic's strongest causal link to the twentieth century, and which the standard account itself identifies as the clearest long causal link in the folder. Use it to end any answer on the consequences of German unification, and hand the alliance system and the July crisis to the 1914 owner explicitly rather than drifting into them.

### Examiner traps in this lesson

- Writing that Prussia declared war on France, when France declared war on 19 July 1870 and the whole design depended on that.
- Describing the Ems telegram as a forgery; it was an edited and published account, and the record says edited.
- Omitting the southern German states' adherence, which was the war's political object.
- Treating Sedan as the end of the war, when the siege of Paris ran until 28 January 1871 and a republic continued the fight.
- Dating the proclamation to the treaty or the treaty to the proclamation - 18 January 1871 and 10 May 1871 are different events.
- Presenting the annexation of Alsace-Lorraine as a minor clause, when it is the topic's principal causal link to 1914.
- Narrating the alliance system, the arms race or the July crisis, all of which belong to the 1914 owner.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ The 1866 victory confirmed Prussian leadership and threatened France's position as the dominant European power.
2. ✅ The immediate cause was the Hohenzollern-Sigmaringen candidacy for the Spanish throne, vacant since Isabella II was deposed in 1868; Leopold accepted in June 1870 and the candidacy was withdrawn under French pressure.
3. ✅ William I declined to promise that the candidacy would never be renewed; Bismarck edited and published his telegraphed account on 14 July 1870 - the Ems telegram - to infuriate the French government and provoke a declaration of war.
4. ✅ Napoleon III declared war on 19 July 1870, advised that France could win and that victory would restore his popularity; French confidence rested on the chassepot rifle and the mitrailleuse.
5. ✅ Bismarck saw the war as the opportunity to bring the south German states into unity with the North German Confederation; Bavaria, Wurttemberg and Baden regarded France as the aggressor and sided with Prussia.
6. ✅ German mobilisation delivered 380,000 troops to the forward zone in eighteen days; Moltke's general staff planned the movement.
7. ✅ Worth, 6 August; Mars-la-Tour and Gravelotte, 16 and 18 August; Sedan, **1-2 September 1870**, with the encirclement completed on 31 August, 17,000 French losses, about 104,000 prisoners including the emperor and Mac-Mahon, and German losses of 460 officers and 8,500 men.
8. ✅ The Third Republic was proclaimed on 4 September 1870; the siege of Paris began on 19 September; Metz capitulated on 27 October with 140,000 troops; Paris surrendered on 28 January 1871.
9. ✅ Negotiations broke off when Germany demanded Alsace and Lorraine - the annexation was a war aim, not an afterthought.
10. ✅ William I was proclaimed German emperor at Versailles on 18 January 1871, in the former palace of the kings of France.
11. ✅ Treaty of Frankfurt, 10 May 1871: Alsace and half of Lorraine with Metz annexed; an indemnity of five billion francs plus occupation costs.
12. ✅ The French resented and were obsessed with recovering Alsace-Lorraine; revanchism is a named source of friction in 1914, and the years 1871-1914 were an extremely unstable peace.
13. ✅ Five outputs: the German Empire, Italy's acquisition of Rome, the French Third Republic, the prestige of Prussian militarism and the general-staff model, and the Franco-German antagonism.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why a war against France was the only available means of bringing the south German states into a Prussian-led Germany, and identify the single decision in the settlement that most damaged the new Empire's long-term position.

**Model answer:** After 1866 the German state existed only north of the river Main; Bavaria, Wurttemberg and Baden remained outside it, were Catholic and particularist, and had fought against Prussia in the war that created it. They could not be annexed, because forcibly absorbing German states in peacetime would have destroyed the national legitimacy on which Prussian leadership rested and alarmed every great power, and they could not be persuaded, since the argument for union had been available for two decades without effect. The one circumstance that would make them join immediately and voluntarily was an attack on Germany by a foreign power, under which particularism becomes disloyalty and common defence becomes common statehood. The problem was therefore to obtain a war in which France declared first. The Hohenzollern candidacy for the vacant Spanish throne supplied the occasion; France won the substantive point when the candidacy was withdrawn, then demanded a guarantee that it would never be renewed; William I declined; Bismarck edited and published the king's account of the interview on 14 July 1870 with the recorded purpose of infuriating the French government and provoking a declaration; France declared war on 19 July; and the southern states, regarding France as the aggressor, sided with Prussia. The war thus accomplished in weeks what argument had not accomplished in twenty years. The most damaging decision in the settlement was the annexation of Alsace and half of Lorraine with Metz by the Treaty of Frankfurt of 10 May 1871, together with an indemnity of five billion francs. In 1866 Austria had been excluded from Germany but left territorially intact, and could therefore accept the outcome; in 1871 France was given a permanent grievance, resented the loss and became obsessed with recovering it, so the years from 1871 to 1914 were an extremely unstable peace and French revanchism became a named source of friction in the crisis that followed. The Empire was created with its principal enemy already made.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the Ems telegram as a document that caused a war. It was an edited publication designed to provoke a decision that France then took for its own reasons - confidence in its army and a regime's need for a victory. The analytically correct formulation is that Prussia chose the occasion and the appearance while France chose the war, which is exactly how the method was designed to work.

**Bridge to Lesson 16:** The Empire now existed, proclaimed by princes in a French palace, with a parliament elected by every adult man and an army nobody in that parliament could control. What kind of state was it, and who counted as a German inside it? The next lesson examines the constitutional settlement of 1871 and the decade of measures by which the new Empire decided which of its own populations were to be trusted.

## Lesson 16 - The constitution of 1871, and the question of who belonged

### Visual: where power actually sat

```text
                        THE GERMAN EMPIRE, 1871
   ==========================================================================
                          KAISER (the King of Prussia)
                     commands the army | directs foreign policy
                     appoints and dismisses the chancellor
                                    |
                                    | appoints
                                    v
                          CHANCELLOR (Reichskanzler)
                   head of government | presides over the Bundesrat
           RESPONSIBLE TO THE KAISER, NOT TO THE ELECTED CHAMBER
                                    |
             +----------------------+----------------------+
             |                                             |
        BUNDESRAT                                     REICHSTAG
   federal council of                          national parliament,
   delegates from the                          ELECTED BY UNIVERSAL
   member states;                              MALE SUFFRAGE
   PRUSSIA'S WEIGHT IS                                |
   DECISIVE HERE                               CAN: debate, legislate
                                                     with the Bundesrat,
                                                     vote on budgets
                                               CANNOT: appoint or dismiss
                                                     the government,
                                                     control the army,
                                                     direct foreign policy
   ==========================================================================
   THE TWO-SENTENCE VERDICT
   ✅ "A modern parliament with Prussian dominance and monarchical control
      of the army and foreign policy."
   ✅ "Do not call the Empire simply undemocratic or simply constitutional -
      it was both."
   ==========================================================================
   AND INSIDE PRUSSIA ITSELF: the imperial franchise did NOT change the
   Prussian state franchise, which remained weighted by wealth. The largest
   member state's own politics stayed conservative while the national
   parliament was elected by every adult man.
```

*The diagram's purpose is to separate two things that candidates fuse: the breadth of the franchise and the location of executive power. The Empire was generous with the first and immovable on the second, and every judgement about the character of the German state turns on holding those apart.*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

A state had been created by war and proclaimed by princes. It now needed a constitution, and the constitution had to do three contradictory things at once.

It had to satisfy the national movement, which had wanted a German nation-state with a national parliament since 1848. It had to preserve the monarchy's control of the army and foreign policy, which was the whole point of the constitutional conflict of the 1860s. And it had to reassure the member states - especially the southern kingdoms that had just joined - that they were not simply being absorbed into Prussia.

The instrument that emerged solved all three problems simultaneously, and it solved them by separating the *form* of representation from the *substance* of power. Understanding that separation is the core of this lesson, and it is also the answer to every question about whether German unification was a liberal achievement.

### 2. The architecture, stated exactly

✅ **Fact:** under the imperial constitution of **1871**, the **Kaiser** was the King of Prussia; the **Chancellor**, appointed by the Kaiser, was head of government and president of the **Bundesrat**, the federal council of representatives of the German states; the **Reichstag** was the national parliament, elected by **universal male suffrage**; the Empire was federal, but the Prussian monarch and Prussian officials retained great power. ✅ Its immediate predecessor, the constitution of the **North German Confederation**, in force from **1 July 1867**, established a federal state headed by Prussia with a Reichstag elected by universal male suffrage.

✅ **Fact:** the standard account's limits bank states the character of the resulting state precisely: the Empire **combined a modern parliament with Prussian dominance and monarchical control of the army and foreign policy**, and it instructs explicitly that the Empire must **not** be called simply undemocratic or simply constitutional, because **it was both**.

✅ **Fact:** the historiographical companion records that Germany unified under an **authoritarian-monarchical structure**, not under the liberal parliamentary hopes of 1848, and that advanced answers therefore connect German unification to later militarism and the changed balance of power.

✅ **Fact:** the core world-history source read for this session records **Bismarck as chancellor from 1870 to 1890**, and describes the system that existed under him as one **in which the Reichstag had much less power** - a formulation the source uses when explaining why conservatives in a later crisis wished to return to it.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** that shorthand compresses two offices and should not be reproduced as an office date. Bismarck was **chancellor of the North German Confederation from 1 July 1867**, when its constitution came into force, and **imperial chancellor of the German Empire from 21 March 1871** until his dismissal in **March 1890**. The proclamation of 18 January 1871 created the empire; the imperial chancellorship dates from the following March. Use those dates, and treat "1870-90" as a source shorthand rather than as a term of office.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** four consequences of the architecture, each usable directly.

| Feature | Consequence |
|---|---|
| The chancellor is responsible to the Kaiser, not to the chamber | A parliamentary majority cannot remove a government; the central mechanism of parliamentary government is absent |
| The army and foreign policy sit with the crown | The two decisions that determine a state's fate are outside elected control - and this is precisely what the 1860s conflict had been about |
| Prussia's weight is decisive in the federal council | Federalism does not disperse power; it houses Prussian preponderance inside a form acceptable to the other states |
| The Reichstag is elected by universal male suffrage | The Empire is more democratic in *franchise* than many contemporaries and less democratic in *control* than a parliamentary monarchy |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the last row is where the standard account's "it was both" instruction earns its marks. An answer that calls the Empire a military autocracy cannot explain why it held national elections on the widest franchise in Europe. An answer that calls it a constitutional monarchy cannot explain why a chancellor governed for twenty years without parliamentary confidence. ⚠️ The correct formulation is that the Empire institutionalised a *division*: the nation was represented, and the state was not controlled.

### 3. Who belonged? The internal boundaries of the new nation

✅ **Fact:** the standard account for this topic records that the new Empire contained **Polish, Danish and French-speaking populations** - the last in Alsace-Lorraine - and that it **treated Catholics and later socialists as suspect national communities**; the standard account permits the standard name for the campaign against the Catholic Church to be used only as an **analytical reference**, since the folder supplies no detailed source for it.

✅ **Fact:** carried with the standard account's caution attached: the **Kulturkampf** of **1871-1878** was a conflict initiated by Bismarck against the Catholic Church and its political representation, the **Centre Party**, aiming to reduce the Church's influence in education and civil life and to assert state authority over clerical appointments, through measures including civil marriage and state oversight of clerical training; the backlash **strengthened the Centre Party**, and most of the anti-Catholic laws were gradually repealed from the late 1870s as the government shifted its attention to socialism.

✅ **Fact:** the **Anti-Socialist Laws of 1878-1890** banned socialist organisations, publications and meetings after two attempts on the life of the emperor; instead of destroying the social-democratic movement they drove it underground, and it emerged stronger when the laws lapsed in 1890.

✅ **Fact:** **Bismarck was dismissed by Wilhelm II in March 1890**, ending the era of his domestic system.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** these episodes are not a list of repressive measures. They are the working out of a specific problem created by unification, and the problem can be stated in one sentence: a state justified by nationality must decide which of its own subjects are reliably national.

| Group | Why the new state treated it as a problem | What the attempt revealed |
|---|---|---|
| **Catholics** | A universal church with loyalties beyond the nation, concentrated in the recently incorporated south and west, with its own political party | ⚠️ The campaign failed: the Centre Party grew, and the laws were largely withdrawn. Nationality could not be enforced against religion |
| **Socialists** | A movement organised around class rather than nation, and internationalist in doctrine | ⚠️ Twelve years of prohibition left the movement stronger, which demonstrates the limits of legal repression against a social constituency |
| **Poles and Danes** | ✅ Populations inside the Empire whose national identification lay elsewhere | ⚠️ The nationality principle used to justify the Empire's creation was an argument available to them against it |
| **Alsatians** | ✅ A French-speaking population annexed in 1871 without consultation | ✅ Alsace-Lorraine became a named source of friction, and its recovery a French national objective |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the pattern across all four rows is the same, and it is the most sophisticated point in the German half of this topic. **A nation-state created by state power rather than by popular consent cannot subsequently produce the consent it dispensed with.** Having declined to found the Empire on popular sovereignty, the government had no mechanism except coercion when populations inside it declined to be national in the way required - and coercion produced stronger opponents in both the Catholic and the socialist cases.

> 🔑 **Answer line:** Both new states were unified politically before they were unified socially, which is why the years after 1871 were dominated by the question of who really belonged to the new nation.

### 4. The Italian comparison, made precisely

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the two states faced the same problem and solved it differently, and the comparison is worth a short paragraph in any answer about limits.

| Dimension | Italy | Germany |
|---|---|---|
| **Constitutional form** | ✅ A constitutional monarchy with persistent internal divisions; the Piedmontese constitution extended outward | ✅ A powerful empire dominated by Prussia; a constitution written for the purpose |
| **Franchise** | ⚠️ Restricted for decades; ✅ and Catholics were discouraged from voting after 1874, so participation was narrower still | ✅ Universal male suffrage for the national parliament from 1867 |
| **Executive control** | ⚠️ Parliamentary, but hollowed out by trasformismo from 1876 | ✅ Not parliamentary at all: the chancellor answered to the Kaiser |
| **The excluded** | ✅ The south, practising Catholics, republicans | ✅ Catholics, socialists, Poles, Danes, Alsatians |
| **Method used against them** | ⚠️ Counter-insurgency in the south, 1861-65, and political management thereafter | ✅ Legal prohibition: the anti-Catholic campaign of the 1870s and the anti-socialist laws of 1878-1890 |
| **Result** | ⚠️ A weak state with shallow integration | ⚠️ A strong state with contested integration |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the paradox worth naming is that the state with the wider franchise had the narrower distribution of real power, and the state with the narrower franchise had the weaker government. ⚠️ Neither had converted a national claim into a national society, and both discovered that the instruments available to them - repression in one case, co-option in the other - produced political systems in which large constituencies operated outside the constitutional order.

### 5. The dismissal of 1890, and what it revealed about the design

✅ **Fact:** **Bismarck was dismissed by Wilhelm II in March 1890**, ending a continuous tenure that ran from the **North German chancellorship of 1 July 1867** and the **imperial chancellorship of 21 March 1871**; ✅ the core source compresses the whole of it into the shorthand "1870 to 1890".

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the dismissal is usually treated as the end of an era and is more useful as a constitutional demonstration. The chancellor who had governed for two decades, who had created the Empire, and who commanded a substantial base of support was removed by the emperor alone - not by a parliamentary vote, not by an election, and not by any process in which the represented nation participated. ⚠️ That single event proves the architecture better than any description of it: the man who designed the office could be removed by the only person the office actually answered to.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the consequence, which belongs to later owners and is recorded here in one sentence, is that a state in which the direction of foreign and military policy depended on the personal judgement of a monarch had no institutional means of correcting that judgement. ⚠️ This session states the structural point and hands the sequel to the topic on the world in 1914.

### Strongest objection, reply and residual

**Objection:** Measuring the Empire against parliamentary government is anachronistic. In 1871 no continental great power was governed by a parliamentary executive; the Empire's universal male suffrage was more advanced than the franchise in most European states, its legal order was real, and its administration was effective. Calling it authoritarian imports a later standard.

**Reply:** The objection is right about the comparative context and wrong about the standard, because the standard applied here is the one the Germans themselves were using. The constitutional conflict of 1862-66 was fought, inside Prussia, over precisely the question of whether a parliament could control an executive; the Frankfurt constitution of March 1849 had already envisaged a parliamentary national state; and the liberal chamber's retrospective approval of unauthorised expenditure in 1866 was a decision to abandon a claim it had itself advanced. ⚠️ The measure is therefore internal and contemporary rather than anachronistic. ✅ The standard account's own instruction sets the balance required: the Empire was neither simply undemocratic nor simply constitutional but both, and the accurate formulation is that it combined a modern parliament with Prussian dominance and monarchical control of the army and foreign policy. That formulation concedes the objection's substance - the franchise and the legal order were real - while retaining the structural finding that the powers that mattered were outside elected control.

**Residual:** What this evidence base cannot settle is the effectiveness of the Reichstag in practice - how far governments were in fact constrained by the need for legislative majorities, and how far the constitutional position understates or overstates parliamentary influence. The evidence base establishes the formal architecture and the principal episodes. The session therefore teaches the formal structure and the recorded outcomes, and records the practical question as open.

### UPSC application

Two demands are answered here. "What kind of state did German unification produce?" is answered by the standard account's dual formulation, with the four-row architecture table as evidence and the 1890 dismissal as the clinching demonstration. "Assess the limits of unification" is answered by the belonging problem: a nation-state founded on state power rather than consent had no mechanism except coercion when its own populations declined to be national, and the coercion failed in both the Catholic and the socialist cases. The Italian comparison table is the paragraph that converts a German answer into a comparative one, and the paradox line - wider franchise, narrower power - is the kind of formulation examiners remember.

### Examiner traps in this lesson

- Calling the Empire simply undemocratic or simply constitutional, against the standard account's explicit instruction.
- Treating universal male suffrage as evidence that the Empire was liberal, when the powers that mattered were outside the chamber's reach.
- Confusing the imperial franchise with the Prussian state franchise, which remained weighted by wealth.
- Presenting the campaign against the Catholic Church as a success, when it strengthened the party it targeted and the laws were largely repealed.
- Presenting the anti-socialist laws as effective, when the movement emerged stronger after 1890.
- Asserting detailed provisions of either campaign, which the standard account permits only as analytical references and which this session carries only at the level the evidence supports.
- Narrating the reign of Wilhelm II or the origins of the First World War, both of which belong to later owners.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Constitution of 1871: the Kaiser is the King of Prussia; the chancellor, appointed by the Kaiser, heads the government and presides over the Bundesrat; the Reichstag is elected by universal male suffrage; the Empire is federal with Prussia preponderant.
2. ✅ The predecessor constitution of the North German Confederation came into force on 1 July 1867, with the same suffrage principle.
3. ✅ The Empire combined a modern parliament with Prussian dominance and monarchical control of the army and foreign policy.
4. ✅ Do not call the Empire simply undemocratic or simply constitutional - it was both.
5. ✅ Germany unified under an authoritarian-monarchical structure, not under the liberal parliamentary hopes of 1848.
6. ✅ Bismarck was chancellor of the North German Confederation from 1 July 1867 and imperial chancellor from 21 March 1871 until his dismissal in March 1890, in a system in which the Reichstag had much less power; the core source's "1870-90" is a shorthand for that tenure, not an office date.
7. ⚠️ Franchise and executive control are separate variables; the Empire was generous with the first and immovable on the second.
8. ✅ The Empire contained Polish, Danish and French-speaking populations and treated Catholics and later socialists as suspect national communities.
9. ✅ Kulturkampf, 1871-78: a campaign against the Catholic Church and the Centre Party, including civil marriage and state oversight of clerical training; it strengthened the Centre Party and was largely repealed.
10. ✅ Anti-Socialist Laws, 1878-1890: socialist organisations, publications and meetings banned; the movement emerged stronger when the laws lapsed.
11. ✅ Bismarck was dismissed by Wilhelm II in March 1890 - by the emperor alone, which proves where power lay.
12. ⚠️ The central proposition: a nation-state created by state power rather than consent cannot afterwards manufacture the consent it dispensed with.
13. ⚠️ The comparison: wider franchise and narrower power in Germany; narrower franchise and weaker government in Italy; neither had converted a national claim into a national society.

### Concept check

**Question:** "The German Empire was a democracy in its franchise and an autocracy in its government." Examine this statement and use it to explain the Empire's treatment of Catholics and socialists.

**Model answer:** The statement is a useful compression provided both halves are held together, which is what the recorded instruction requires: the Empire was neither simply undemocratic nor simply constitutional, but both. On the franchise it was genuinely advanced: the Reichstag was elected by universal male suffrage, a principle first established in the North German Confederation's constitution of 1 July 1867 and carried into the imperial constitution of 1871, and it was wider than the franchise of most contemporary European states. On government it was not parliamentary in any sense: the chancellor was appointed by the Kaiser, who was the King of Prussia, and was responsible to him rather than to the elected chamber, which could not appoint or dismiss a government; the army and foreign policy remained under monarchical control; and Prussia's weight in the federal council made the federal form a housing for Prussian preponderance rather than a dispersal of power. The clinching demonstration is the dismissal of Bismarck by Wilhelm II in March 1890: the chancellor who had created the Empire and governed it for two decades was removed by the emperor alone, without any parliamentary process. That division explains the treatment of Catholics and socialists directly. A state justified by nationality must decide which of its subjects are reliably national, and a state that has declined to found itself on popular consent has no mechanism for producing that reliability except coercion. Hence the campaign against the Catholic Church between 1871 and 1878, directed at its influence in education and civil life and at the Centre Party, and the Anti-Socialist Laws of 1878-1890 banning socialist organisations, publications and meetings. Both failed in their own terms: the Centre Party grew and the anti-Catholic laws were largely repealed, while the social-democratic movement emerged stronger when the prohibition lapsed in 1890. The Empire could compel obedience and could not manufacture belonging, which is the precise limit of unification from above.

**Misconception to avoid:** Inferring liberalism from the franchise. The extent of the vote and the location of executive power are separate variables, and a conservative regime may widen the first in order to secure the second - particularly where a broad rural electorate is less radical than the narrow propertied urban one that had obstructed the government during the Prussian budget conflict.

**Bridge to Lesson 17:** Both states are now complete and both have been examined on their own terms. What remains is the comparison the examiner almost always sets - not two narratives placed side by side, but a single question about agency, method and result run through both cases. The next lesson builds that comparison axis by axis and shows how to write it under a word limit.

## Lesson 17 - The comparison: agency, method, timing and the states that resulted

Progress: 17/20 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: the seven comparative axes; from above and from below in each case separately; economic against military-diplomatic factors; popular against elite agency; why the shared elements must be stated before the differences; how to write the comparison under a word limit

Book context: queried - the standard account's directive and demand map, its qualified thesis templates, its mark-scaled structures, its evidence bank A on the comparison made analytical with seven axes, and its evidence bank B on from above against from below; the historiographical companion's comparative-insight table on the mass image, foreign help, the final structure and the effect on Europe, and its main-debate table giving the best synthesis for each case; the subject revision chart's Vienna-Italy-Germany comparison row
CA search: not sought - this topic carries exactly one current linkage, held in Lesson 10
CA found: none carried for this lesson

### Visual: one question run through two cases

```text
   THE WRONG STRUCTURE                    THE RIGHT STRUCTURE
   ------------------                     -------------------
   Para 1: Italy's story                  Thesis: who supplied the decisive
   Para 2: Germany's story                        force, and with what result?
   Para 3: "thus both were unified"              |
                                          Axis 1: the shared obstacle
   The examiner must do the                      -> Austria, in both cases
   comparing. Marks are lost                     |
   for the work you did not do.           Axis 2: the core state
                                                 -> Piedmont v. Prussia
                                                 |
                                          Axis 3: the missing input
                                                 -> an ally v. no ally needed
                                                 |
                                          Axis 4: the popular element
                                                 -> decisive v. absent
                                                 |
                                          Axis 5: timing and completion
                                                 -> staggered v. concentrated
                                                 |
                                          Axis 6: the resulting state
                                                 -> middling v. strongest
                                                 |
                                          Axis 7: the resulting Europe
                                                 -> one struggle closed v.
                                                    a new centre created
                                                 |
                                          Verdict: same enemy, same era,
                                          opposite methods - and the methods
                                          produced the states
   =========================================================================
   THE RULE: one organising question, run through both cases, axis by axis.
   Never two narratives with a comparative sentence at the end.
```

*The diagram is the marking scheme. A script that moves down the right-hand column has already earned the analysis marks before any evidence is supplied; a script that uses the left-hand column has to earn them back with content, and usually cannot.*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

Comparison questions look easy and are not. The difficulty is structural rather than factual: you know both stories, and the temptation is to tell them in turn. But a comparison is an argument about *why* two things differ, and an argument requires that the same question be asked of both cases in the same terms.

So the first task is to choose the question. For this topic the standard account effectively supplies it: ✅ its directive map says that a "compare the two unifications" prompt is really testing the **difference in agency and method, not two parallel narratives**, and warns explicitly against writing Italy's story and then Germany's story.

The second task is to choose the axes. Seven are set out below, and they are deliberately ordered so that the shared elements come first - because a comparison that begins with the differences has failed to establish that the two cases are comparable at all.

### 2. The shared elements, stated first

✅ **Fact:** the standard account's comparison bank records that **both nuclei were created by the settlement they overthrew** - Piedmont-Sardinia strengthened at Vienna by Genoa, Prussia by the Rhineland, Westphalia and Saxon lands - and that **Austria's exclusion is the common precondition** of both unifications.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** five elements are genuinely shared, and stating them takes three sentences and buys the right to make the contrasts.

| Shared element | Italy | Germany |
|---|---|---|
| A settlement that refused the national question | ✅ 1815: divided, with Austria dominant | ✅ 1815: a thirty-nine-state confederation under Austrian influence |
| A nucleus created by that settlement | ✅ Piedmont-Sardinia, strengthened by Genoa | ✅ Prussia, strengthened by the Rhineland, Westphalia and Saxon lands |
| A failure in 1848-49 that redirected the method | ✅ Liberal-national attempts fail; the issue survives | ✅ The Frankfurt crown is refused; the assembly disperses |
| Austria as the power to be defeated | ✅ 1859, with French help | ✅ 1866, alone |
| Completion by a monarchy, not by the movement | ✅ The monarchy absorbs the popular gain | ✅ Unity under conservative-Prussian dominance, not the liberal hopes of 1848 |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the fifth row is the most important shared element and the least often noticed. In both countries the movement that generated the national demand did not govern the state that satisfied it. ⚠️ That is the general proposition about nineteenth-century unification, and the differences below are variations inside it.

### 3. The seven axes

✅ **Fact:** the standard account's evidence bank A supplies the comparison in this form, and the entries below reproduce its content with this session's teaching attached.

| Axis | Italy | Germany | ⚠️ What the difference proves |
|---|---|---|---|
| **1. Core state** | ✅ Piedmont-Sardinia, strengthened at Vienna by Genoa | ✅ Prussia, strengthened at Vienna by the Rhineland, Westphalia and Saxon lands | Both nuclei were created by the settlement they overthrew - the shared irony with a mechanism |
| **2. Strategist** | ✅ Cavour: diplomacy plus war against Austria, with French help in 1859 | ✅ Bismarck: minister-president from 1862; three wars | ⚠️ Italy needed an external great-power ally; Prussia did not |
| **3. Popular-military element** | ✅ Garibaldi's Red Shirts conquered Sicily and Naples in 1860 | ✅ No equivalent mass military hero | ✅ The standard account's own judgement: this is the single sharpest contrast |
| **4. Ideological founder** | ✅ Mazzini's Young Italy of 1831, republican and unitary | ⚠️ No single equivalent; the Frankfurt liberalism of 1848 failed | ⚠️ Italy's republican tradition was defeated *within* unification |
| **5. Decisive defeat of Austria** | ✅ 1859 with France; Venetia gained 1866 | ✅ The Seven Weeks' War, 1866 | ✅ Austria's exclusion is the common precondition |
| **6. Completion** | ✅ Kingdom of Italy 1861; Venetia 1866; Rome 1870 | ✅ Empire proclaimed at Versailles, January 1871 | ✅ Italy's completion was staggered; Germany's was concentrated in seven years |
| **7. Resulting power** | ⚠️ A middling power with weak state capacity | ⚠️ The strongest continental military-economic power | ✅ This feeds directly into the crisis of 1914, owned by a later topic |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the axes are not equal in value. Axes 2, 3 and 7 carry the argument; axes 1, 5 and 6 supply structure and evidence; axis 4 is the one to use when a question asks specifically about ideas. ⚠️ In a 150-word answer use two axes; in 250 words use four; a 20-mark answer can carry all seven with a limits paragraph attached.

### 4. From above or from below, answered separately for each case

✅ **Fact:** the historiographical companion's main-debate table gives the best synthesis for each case separately: for **Italy**, emotional-national energy came from below and completion came from above; for **Germany**, unification was **overwhelmingly state-led**.

✅ **Fact:** the standard account's evidence bank B pairs the two columns with named evidence: republican nationalism organised early through Mazzini's Young Italy of 1831, against diplomacy securing great-power permission through Cavour's alliance with France in 1859; popular-military conquest of the south in 1860, against the monarchy absorbing the popular gain in 1861; the revolutions of 1848 putting unity on the agenda, against their failure redirecting the movement toward statecraft; and south German popular feeling rallying to Prussia in 1870, against war manufacturing the national moment through Bismarck's three wars.

✅ **Fact:** the standard account's directive map warns explicitly against giving **one verdict for both countries**, and its qualified thesis template for this demand is that nationalism was generated from below and executed from above, the movements supplying legitimacy and the states supplying the means, with **neither able to succeed alone**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the graded answer has three levels, and the third is what distinguishes a top script.

1. **Italy: genuinely mixed, with the balance shifting by stage.** From below in the ideological phase and in the southern campaign of 1860; from above in the diplomacy of 1855-59, in the plebiscite machinery of 1860 and in the settlement of 1861.
2. **Germany: overwhelmingly from above, with one significant qualification.** The liberal national movement supplied the *demand* and the little-German design as early as 1849, and southern popular feeling rallied to Prussia in 1870 - but no popular force conquered anything, and the constitution was granted rather than negotiated.
3. **The relationship is the same in both cases even though the proportions differ.** ⚠️ In both, the movement's legitimacy was transferred to a state and the state's power was lent the movement's justification. ⚠️ The difference is how much force the movement itself contributed: in Italy, half a peninsula; in Germany, none.

> 🔑 **Answer line:** Nationalism was generated from below and executed from above in both countries; what differed was how much of the execution the movement itself performed - and in Italy it performed the conquest of the south, while in Germany it performed nothing.

### 5. Economic against military-diplomatic factors

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is the second great comparative axis, and it is where the customs union of Lesson 3 earns its place.

| Factor | Italy | Germany |
|---|---|---|
| **Economic integration before unification** | ⚠️ Absent. Piedmont reformed *itself*; no peninsular institution existed | ✅ Present and institutional: a customs union from 1834 covering, in time, all Germany outside Austria |
| **Economic integration as a cause** | ⚠️ Cannot be claimed; the Italian national market had to be built after 1861 | ⚠️ Real but indeterminate: it made Prussian leadership routine and Austrian participation awkward, without deciding the political question |
| **Military capability** | ⚠️ Insufficient alone; defeated in 1848-49 and dependent in 1859 and 1866 | ✅ Decisive: a methodically prepared army with a general staff, reorganised in the 1860s |
| **Diplomatic capability** | ✅ Exceptional in its own register - buying standing through the Crimea and the Congress of Paris, and converting it into an alliance | ✅ Exceptional in a different register - isolating opponents and arranging for them to declare war |
| **Which factor is decisive?** | ⚠️ Diplomacy first, popular force second, economy not at all | ⚠️ Military-diplomatic capability decisive; economic integration a necessary background condition rather than a cause |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the honest verdict on the German case is that the economic factor is a *condition* and the military-diplomatic factor is the *cause* - and that the condition mattered because it determined which of the two candidate leaders the war would be fought to confirm. ⚠️ This session holds no series by which the weights could be measured, so the ranking is offered as a reasoned judgement and is labelled as one.

### 6. Popular against elite agency, stated without sentiment

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the question "who made these nations?" has a tempting answer - the people - and a defensible one, which is more interesting.

| Contribution | Made by | Evidence |
|---|---|---|
| The national idea and its programme | Intellectuals, exiles and organisers | ✅ Mazzini's Young Italy, 1831; ✅ the Frankfurt constitution of 28 March 1849 |
| Mass legitimacy for annexation | Populations, through ratification | ✅ The plebiscites of March 1860 |
| Actual military force from below | Volunteers - in one country only | ✅ Garibaldi's Red Shirts, Sicily and Naples, 1860; ✅ no German equivalent |
| Decisive military force | State armies | ✅ France and Piedmont in 1859; ✅ Prussia in 1864, 1866 and 1870-71 |
| The terms of the settlement | Monarchs and ministers | ✅ The 1861 extension of Piedmontese institutions; ✅ the constitutions of 1867 and 1871 |
| The exclusion of populations from the result | The new states themselves | ✅ The Italian south and the Non Expedit of 1874; ✅ Catholics, socialists, Poles, Danes and Alsatians |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** read the table downward and a single proposition emerges that answers almost any framing of the question: **popular agency was indispensable at the level of legitimacy and, in Italy, at the level of force; elite agency was decisive at the level of outcome; and the settlement was written by the elites in terms that excluded much of the population whose name had been invoked.** ⚠️ That sentence, adapted, can close any "from above or from below" answer.

### 7. Writing the comparison under a word limit

✅ **Fact:** the standard account supplies the mark-scaled structures directly: at **10 marks**, thesis, two axes of comparison - core state and method - with named evidence, one limit and a verdict; at **15 marks**, thesis, the shared obstacle of the Vienna settlement and Austria, Italy's sequence, Germany's sequence, the decisive difference, limits and verdict; at **20 marks**, all of that plus the popular-versus-elite question, minorities and the post-1871 consequences for European power, then the verdict.

✅ **Fact:** the standard account's verdict scaffolds are available verbatim in substance: for "compare", that the two cases had the **same enemy, same era, opposite methods** - Italy unified by combining diplomacy with insurrection and remained weak, Germany unified by concentrating military and diplomatic power in one state and became too strong for the system that produced it; for "ideas or state power", that **ideas made unification thinkable and legitimate while state power made it happen**, and the gap between the two explains why the unified states disappointed the nationalists who had demanded them.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** three practical rules follow.

1. **Never allocate equal space to both countries by default.** Allocate by the question: a prompt on Bismarck's role gets a short Italian paragraph as a control case; a prompt on comparison gets balanced treatment; a prompt on the role of popular movements gets more Italy than Germany, because the evidence is there.
2. **Attach one dated item to each axis.** An axis without evidence is an assertion; an axis with 1831, 1859, 1860, 1866, 1867 or January 1871 attached is an argument.
3. **Close with a qualified verdict, never a summary.** ⚠️ A summary repeats; a verdict decides something and concedes something, which is what "compare", "examine" and "discuss" all require.

### Strongest objection, reply and residual

**Objection:** The axis method produces mechanical answers. Real historical comparison should identify what is genuinely comparable and what is not, and much of this table is forced - Cavour and Bismarck faced different problems in different systems with different resources, and lining them up in a grid manufactures similarities that did not exist.

**Reply:** The objection is right about badly used grids and wrong about this one, for two reasons. First, the axes were chosen because the cases genuinely share the variables: both faced the same settlement, the same opposing great power, the same failure in 1848-49 and the same structural need for a core state, and the shared-elements table is presented *before* the differences precisely to establish comparability rather than assume it. Second, the grid's purpose is to locate the differences, not to manufacture similarities - and the differences it locates are substantive: an ally purchased with territory against opponents isolated diplomatically, a popular army against no popular army, a staggered completion dependent on other powers' wars against a concentrated one executed by a single state. ⚠️ The legitimate residue of the objection is that no grid should be allowed to suppress the asymmetries: Italy's problem included expelling an occupier, which Germany's did not, and Germany's problem included the multinational structure of its rival, which Italy's did not. ⚠️ A good answer states one such asymmetry explicitly, which demonstrates that the comparison is being controlled rather than applied.

**Residual:** What cannot be settled here is the counterfactual comparison - whether either method would have worked in the other's circumstances. Italy had no Prussia and Germany had no Garibaldi, and whether that reflects structural conditions or contingency is exactly the question Lesson 19 treats as open.

### UPSC application

This lesson is the topic's answer-writing engine, and its contents are directly usable. The seven axes are the body of any comparison answer; the shared-elements table is its opening; the from-above-and-from-below grading is the response to the most frequently set directive; and the mark-scaled structures determine how much of it to use. The single most valuable habit is the one the standard account states: never write Italy's story followed by Germany's story. Run one question through both cases, attach a dated item to each axis, and close with a verdict that concedes something specific.

### Examiner traps in this lesson

- Writing two parallel narratives and leaving the comparison to the examiner.
- Giving one verdict on "from above or from below" for both countries, against the standard account's explicit warning.
- Treating Cavour and Bismarck as equivalents, when one had to recruit an ally and the other had to isolate opponents.
- Claiming that economic integration unified Germany, when it was a condition rather than a cause and no measurement is available.
- Omitting the shared elements, which makes the comparison look arbitrary.
- Allocating equal space to both countries irrespective of what the question asks.
- Ending with a summary instead of a qualified verdict.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ A "compare" prompt tests difference in agency and method, not two parallel narratives.
2. ✅ Shared: a settlement that refused the national question; nuclei created by that settlement; the failure of 1848-49; Austria as the power to be defeated; completion by a monarchy rather than by the movement.
3. ✅ Axis 1: Piedmont-Sardinia strengthened by Genoa; Prussia by the Rhineland, Westphalia and Saxon lands.
4. ✅ Axis 2: Cavour with French help in 1859; Bismarck with three wars from 1862 - Italy needed an ally, Prussia did not.
5. ✅ Axis 3: Garibaldi's Red Shirts in 1860; no German equivalent - the sharpest contrast.
6. ✅ Axis 4: Mazzini's Young Italy of 1831; no single German equivalent, and Frankfurt liberalism failed.
7. ✅ Axis 5: Austria defeated in 1859 with France, and in 1866 by Prussia alone - exclusion is the common precondition.
8. ✅ Axis 6: 1861, 1866, 1870 staggered against a concentrated 1864-71.
9. ✅ Axis 7: a middling power with weak state capacity against the strongest continental military-economic power.
10. ✅ From below and from above: Italy mixed, with energy from below and completion from above; Germany overwhelmingly state-led; never one verdict for both.
11. ⚠️ Economic integration is a condition in Germany and a cause nowhere; military-diplomatic capability is the cause; no measurement is available here.
12. ⚠️ Popular agency was indispensable for legitimacy and, in Italy, for force; elite agency was decisive for outcome; and the settlement excluded much of the population invoked.
13. ✅ Mark-scaled structures: two axes at 10 marks, the full sequence plus limits at 15, and the popular-elite question, minorities and consequences at 20.

### Concept check

**Question:** Write the opening and closing sentences of a 250-word comparison of the two unifications, and state which two axes you would use for the body and why.

**Model answer:** The opening sentence must establish comparability and announce the organising question: "Both unifications began from the same settlement of 1815, which left Italy divided under Austrian domination and Germany a confederation of thirty-nine states under Austrian influence, and both required Austria to be defeated - but they differed in who supplied the decisive force, and that difference produced two very different states." The closing sentence must decide something and concede something: "Same enemy, same era, opposite methods - Italy unified by combining diplomacy with insurrection and remained a middling power with weak state capacity, Germany by concentrating military and diplomatic power in a single state and became too strong for the system that had produced it; the shared limitation is that in both countries the movement that generated the national demand did not govern the state that satisfied it." For a 250-word body I would use the strategist axis and the popular-military axis. The strategist axis carries the central mechanism, since Cavour had to purchase a great-power ally - fighting in the Crimea in 1855, raising the Italian question at the Congress of Paris in 1856, planning the alliance at Plombieres in July 1858 and paying with Nice and Savoy in March 1860 - whereas Bismarck's problem was the opposite, to isolate opponents so that Prussia could fight alone, as in 1866 when the Italian alliance merely divided Austria's forces and in 1870 when France was manoeuvred into declaring war. The popular-military axis carries the sharpest single contrast, because Garibaldi's Red Shirts conquered Sicily and Naples in 1860 while Germany had no equivalent mass military hero, which is why the Italian settlement had to absorb a popular conquest and the German settlement did not. Together the two axes deliver agency and method, which is what the directive tests.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that a comparison requires equal coverage of both countries. Allocation should follow the question: a prompt about the role of popular movements demands more Italian evidence because that is where the evidence exists, and a prompt about state power demands more German evidence for the same reason. Equal space applied automatically is a symptom of the parallel-narrative structure the directive is designed to penalise.

**Bridge to Lesson 18:** The two states now exist and have been compared. What they did to the European system, however, is a separate question - and it is the one in which this topic hands over to the topics that follow. The next lesson sets out the consequences for Europe, distinguishes carefully between what this topic owns and what it must route elsewhere, and identifies the long causal link the folder itself identifies as the clearest of all.

## Lesson 18 - What the two unifications did to Europe, and where this topic stops

Progress: 18/20 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: the replacement of the Vienna order; the new centre of gravity and the end of the five-power equilibrium; Austria excluded and reoriented; French revanchism as the clearest long causal link; minorities on the wrong side of frontiers; the export of the Prussian military model; the four boundaries this topic must respect
