Book context: queried - the standard account's limits bank recording Alsace-Lorraine lost in 1871 and French revanchism as a named source of friction in 1914, described as the clearest long causal link in the folder, and recording that the exclusion of Austria in 1866 made unification small German in form and is the direct root of the later Austro-German union question; its verdict scaffold that nationalism replaced a settlement based on dynastic legitimacy with one based on national states, creating both the Alsace-Lorraine grievance and the Habsburg nationality crisis; the historiographical companion's record that Italy completed one nationality struggle while Germany created a major new continental power; the core world-history source's record of French resentment over Alsace-Lorraine; the verified evidence on the consequences of the war of 1870-71 carried from this session's single verification pass
CA search: not sought - this topic carries exactly one current linkage, held in Lesson 10
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the system before and after

```text
   EUROPE UNDER THE VIENNA ORDER              EUROPE AFTER 1871
   =========================================================================
   PRINCIPLE                                  PRINCIPLE
   dynastic legitimacy and a balance          national states; territory
   among five great powers                    justified by nationality
   -------------------------------------------------------------------------
   CENTRAL EUROPE                             CENTRAL EUROPE
   a plural space - 39 German states,         ONE POWERFUL GERMAN EMPIRE
   a divided Italy - which no single          occupying the centre, with the
   power dominated and which absorbed         strongest army in Europe and a
   pressure between the flanks                rapidly growing economy
   -------------------------------------------------------------------------
   AUSTRIA                                    AUSTRIA
   presides over Germany; a German            EXCLUDED from Germany; its
   power first, a Balkan power second         centre of gravity shifts toward
                                              the south-east and its internal
                                              nationality problem sharpens
   -------------------------------------------------------------------------
   FRANCE                                     FRANCE
   the power the system was built to          DEFEATED AND AGGRIEVED: Alsace
   contain; still the dominant                and half of Lorraine annexed in
   continental state after 1815               1871; "obsessed with taking back
                                              Alsace-Lorraine"
   -------------------------------------------------------------------------
   ITALY                                      ITALY
   a geographical expression                  ✅ one nationality struggle
                                              completed; a middling power
   -------------------------------------------------------------------------
   THE MECHANISM OF PEACE                     THE MECHANISM OF PEACE
   concert and congress; adjustments          ⚠️ no agreed successor principle;
   negotiated among the five                  an armed peace, 1871-1914
   =========================================================================
   WHERE THIS TOPIC STOPS: the alliance system, the arms race, the Balkan
   crises and the July 1914 decisions belong to the topic on the world in
   1914. This topic delivers the structural change and hands over.
```

*The right-hand column is the deliverable of this topic. Every row is a consequence of the two unifications, and the last row is the one that makes the following half-century intelligible: the old system had a procedure for managing change, and nothing had replaced it.*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

Between 1859 and 1871 the map of Europe was redrawn in its centre. Two of the five great powers had their position transformed - one by defeat and amputation, one by exclusion - and a sixth had been created out of the space between them.

That is not merely a territorial change. A states system is a set of expectations about who can do what, and those expectations had been built around a central Europe that was plural and weak. A single strong state in that position changes every calculation made in Paris, Vienna, St Petersburg and London simultaneously.

The task of this lesson is to state those changes precisely, to identify the one causal link that runs furthest forward, and - equally important for an examination - to stop at the point where the next topic's material begins.

### 2. The replacement of one ordering principle by another

✅ **Fact:** the standard account's verdict scaffold for the question of how nationalism reshaped the map states it exactly: nationalism **replaced a settlement based on dynastic legitimacy with one based on national states** - and in doing so created **both the Alsace-Lorraine grievance and the Habsburg nationality crisis** that broke Europe in 1914.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** that single sentence contains three distinct claims, and unpacking them is what converts it from a quotation into an argument.

1. **The basis of territorial title changed.** Under the Vienna order, territory belonged to a dynasty by right. After the plebiscites of 1860 and the unifications they served, territory was held to belong to a nation. ⚠️ Lesson 7 showed the instrument by which the substitution was made.
2. **The change was not universal, and the exceptions were explosive.** ✅ Alsace-Lorraine was annexed in 1871 without any consultation of its population, by a state whose own creation had been justified by nationality. ⚠️ A principle applied selectively supplies its victims with the annexing power's own argument.
3. **The multinational empires were placed in an impossible position.** ⚠️ If nationality entitles a population to a state, then a monarchy ruling many nationalities is permanently on trial. ✅ The standard account names the Habsburg nationality crisis as one of the two creations of the change; that crisis is owned by the topic on the world in 1914, and this session names it and hands it over.

### 3. The new centre of gravity

✅ **Fact:** the historiographical companion records the effect on Europe in one line for each country: Italy **completed one nationality struggle**; Germany **created a major new continental power**. ✅ It also records that advanced answers should connect German unification to later militarism and the changed balance of power.

✅ **Fact:** the German victory of 1870-71 **consolidated faith in Prussian militarism**, which remained a dominant force in German society, and **the Prussian system of conscript armies controlled by a highly trained general staff was soon adopted by the other great powers**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** three structural consequences follow, and they should be distinguished because examiners test them separately.

| Consequence | Content |
|---|---|
| **A new hegemon in the centre** | ⚠️ The strongest army in Europe now sat between France and Russia, which meant that no continental combination could be formed without reference to it, and that Germany's own security calculations acquired a two-front character |
| **The diffusion of a military model** | ✅ Conscript armies directed by a general staff were adopted by the other great powers, so the next European war would be fought by mass armies mobilised on railway timetables |
| **The devaluation of the congress method** | ⚠️ The Vienna system had managed change by negotiation among the five; the three wars of 1864-71 had shown that a determined state could change the map by force without provoking a general war - a precedent that encouraged imitation |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the third row is the deepest and is the one that connects this topic to the crisis of 1914 at the level of *method* rather than of grievance. ⚠️ A system in which quick, limited, successful wars appear to be an available instrument of policy is a system in which the threshold for using force has fallen.

### 4. The clearest long causal link

✅ **Fact:** the standard account's limits bank records that **Alsace-Lorraine was lost in 1871** and that **French revanchism is a named source of friction in 1914**, adding that this is **the clearest long causal link in the folder**.

✅ **Fact:** the core world-history source read for this session records the same link twice: **the French resented the loss of Alsace-Lorraine to Germany at the end of the Franco-Prussian War of 1871**, listing it among the causes of friction that threatened the peace of Europe; and **the French were obsessed with taking back Alsace-Lorraine, which the Germans had taken in 1871**.

✅ **Fact:** Germany's annexation of Alsace-Lorraine **aroused a deep longing for revenge in the French people**, and the years from **1871 to 1914** were marked by an **extremely unstable peace**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the link should be used precisely rather than dramatically. It does not prove that the First World War was caused by German unification; the 1914 owner records a set of structural causes of which French revanchism is one. ⚠️ What it does prove is that the *settlement* of 1871, and specifically the choice to annex rather than merely to defeat, converted a defeated great power into a permanently dissatisfied one - and a states system containing a permanently dissatisfied great power cannot return to equilibrium by negotiation.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the comparison with 1866 is the argument, and it is available in a single sentence: ✅ Austria was excluded from Germany in 1866 and left territorially intact by the Treaty of Prague, and became a power that could eventually align with Germany; ✅ France was defeated and amputated in 1871, and never accepted the result. ⚠️ Same statesman, same method, opposite settlements - and the difference in outcome is the best available evidence for the proposition that peace terms determine alliances.

### 5. Minorities, frontiers and the unfinished business of both states

✅ **Fact:** the standard account for this topic records that the new German Empire contained **Polish, Danish and French-speaking populations**, and that Austria's exclusion in 1866 made unification **small German in form**, which is **the direct root** of the later question of union between Germany and Austria - a question owned by a subsequent topic.

✅ **Fact:** recorded in Lesson 10: the Italian-speaking territories of **Trentino and Trieste** remained under Austrian rule as **italia irredenta**, a nationalist rallying cry that contributed to Italy's entry into the First World War on the Allied side.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the symmetry is exact and is worth one sentence in a consequences answer: each new nation-state contained populations that did not identify with it, and each left populations outside its frontiers that it claimed. ⚠️ That double mismatch is the structural legacy of unification by nationality, and it guaranteed that the new frontiers would be contested from both sides.

### 6. The four boundaries this topic must respect

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this lesson is where answers most often trespass, so the boundaries should be stated explicitly and applied.

| Boundary | What belongs to the other owner | What this topic supplies |
|---|---|---|
| **The Vienna settlement** | Its negotiation, principles, personalities and congress machinery | The output of 1815 as the starting condition, and the finding that it postponed the national question |
| **Nationalism as a doctrine** | Its definition, varieties and relation to liberalism, conservatism and socialism | Nationalism as a historical force used by states and movements |
| **The world in 1914** | The alliance system, the arms race, the Balkan crises, the Habsburg nationality crisis and the July decisions | The structural change of 1859-71, French revanchism, the exclusion of Austria and the new centre of gravity |
| **Interwar Italy and the Austro-German question** | Fascism, the Lateran settlement of 1929 and the union of 1938 | The unresolved Roman question, the weak Italian state, and the small-German form that left Austria's Germans outside |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the practical rule is to state the hand-off in one clause rather than to drift across it. "French revanchism, together with the alliance system and the Balkan crises examined elsewhere, produced the instability of 1871-1914" is a controlled sentence; a paragraph on the Triple Alliance is trespass, and examiners notice when an answer starts narrating another topic's material.

> 🔑 **Answer line:** The two unifications replaced a system organised by dynastic right with one organised by nationality, placed the strongest army in Europe in the centre of the continent, left a great power permanently aggrieved, and destroyed the congress method without putting anything in its place.

### Strongest objection, reply and residual

**Objection:** Treating 1871 as the origin of Europe's instability is a familiar exaggeration. The Franco-German antagonism was one factor among several; the alliance system was built by choices made in the 1880s and after; Russia, Britain and the Balkan states had their own dynamics; and forty-three years of peace followed the settlement. Running a causal arrow from Alsace-Lorraine to 1914 flattens a complicated history.

**Reply:** The objection is right about monocausality and wrong to dismiss the structural point, and the distinction between the two is what a graded answer must show. ✅ The record itself supports a bounded claim rather than an unbounded one: the standard account calls the Alsace-Lorraine grievance the clearest long causal link in the folder, not the cause of the war, and the 1914 owner holds a set of structural causes of which French revanchism is one. ✅ The core source likewise lists the resentment of the loss among several causes of friction, including naval rivalry and colonial disappointment. ⚠️ What remains after the objection is accepted is still substantial: a permanently dissatisfied great power, a new centre of military gravity, the diffusion of the conscript-and-general-staff model, and the absence of any agreed successor to the congress method. ⚠️ Those are conditions rather than causes, and the correct formulation for an answer is that the unifications created the structure within which later choices were made - which concedes the objection and retains the analysis.

**Residual:** What this evidence base cannot settle is the relative weight of the structural legacy against the specific decisions of the following decades. No comparative assessment of causes is held here beyond the standard account's listing, and the topic that owns the outbreak of 1914 holds the graded verdict. This session therefore delivers the structural change and explicitly declines to adjudicate the causes of the war.

### UPSC application

Consequence questions are where this topic most often appears in an unfamiliar form - "how did nationalism reshape the European map?", "how far did the unifications transform the balance of power?", or a question about the origins of later instability that expects unification as background. The structure that scores is the before-and-after table converted into prose: principle, centre of gravity, Austria, France, Italy, and the mechanism of peace. Attach the standard account's verdict scaffold on the replacement of dynastic legitimacy by national states, use the 1866-against-1871 contrast to show that the settlement was a choice, and hand the alliance system to its owner in a single clause. That combination demonstrates both range and discipline, which is exactly what a 20-mark consequences question is designed to test.

### Examiner traps in this lesson

- Narrating the alliance system, the arms race or the July crisis, which belong to the 1914 owner.
- Claiming that German unification caused the First World War, rather than that it created structural conditions.
- Forgetting Italy in a consequences answer - one nationality struggle was completed, and a middling power was added to the system.
- Omitting the contrast between the terms of 1866 and 1871, which is the evidence that the settlement was chosen.
- Treating the diffusion of the Prussian military model as a technical detail, when it changed how the next war would be fought.
- Ignoring the double mismatch - minorities inside each new state and claimed populations outside it.
- Drifting into the Habsburg nationality crisis, which this topic names and routes rather than narrating.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Nationalism replaced a settlement based on dynastic legitimacy with one based on national states, creating both the Alsace-Lorraine grievance and the Habsburg nationality crisis.
2. ⚠️ Three claims inside that sentence: the basis of title changed; the change was applied selectively; and multinational empires were placed permanently on trial.
3. ✅ Italy completed one nationality struggle; Germany created a major new continental power.
4. ✅ The victory of 1870-71 consolidated faith in Prussian militarism, which remained dominant in German society.
5. ✅ The Prussian conscript-army-and-general-staff model was soon adopted by the other great powers.
6. ⚠️ Three structural consequences: a new hegemon in the centre; the diffusion of a military model; the devaluation of the congress method.
7. ✅ Alsace-Lorraine was lost in 1871; French revanchism is a named source of friction in 1914 and the clearest long causal link in the folder.
8. ✅ The French resented the loss and were obsessed with recovering it; the years 1871-1914 were an extremely unstable peace.
9. ⚠️ The 1866-against-1871 contrast: Austria excluded and intact became alignable; France defeated and amputated never accepted the result.
10. ✅ The Empire contained Polish, Danish and French-speaking populations; ✅ Trentino and Trieste remained outside Italy as unredeemed lands.
11. ✅ The small-German form of 1866 is the direct root of the later Austro-German union question, owned by a subsequent topic.
12. ⚠️ Four boundaries: the Vienna settlement, nationalism as a doctrine, the world in 1914, and interwar Italy with the Austro-German question.
13. ⚠️ State the hand-off in a clause; do not drift into another topic's material.

### Concept check

**Question:** "The unifications of Italy and Germany destroyed the European order established in 1815." Examine this claim, distinguishing what was destroyed, what replaced it, and what was left unresolved.

**Model answer:** What was destroyed is precise. The 1815 order rested on dynastic legitimacy as the basis of territorial title and on a balance managed by congresses among five great powers, with a plural central Europe - thirty-nine German states and a divided Italy - absorbing pressure between the flanks. By 1871 all three elements had gone: territory was now held to belong to nations, a claim institutionalised by the plebiscites of March 1860 and by the national justification of both new states; the plural centre had been replaced by a single powerful German Empire with the strongest army in Europe; and the congress method had been shown to be unable to prevent three wars that redrew the map in seven years. What replaced it was a system organised by nationality but applied selectively. The annexation of Alsace and half of Lorraine in 1871 was made without consulting the population, by a state whose own creation had been justified by nationality, so the new principle supplied its victims with the annexing power's own argument; the French resented the loss, were obsessed with recovering it, and the years from 1871 to 1914 were an extremely unstable peace, French revanchism being a named source of friction in 1914. What was left unresolved is twofold. First, there was no agreed successor to the congress mechanism for managing change, while the diffusion of the Prussian conscript army directed by a general staff lowered the threshold at which force appeared usable. Second, the nationality principle created a double mismatch: each new state contained populations - Poles, Danes and Alsatians in Germany, the alienated south and the excluded Catholics in Italy - that did not identify with it, while each claimed populations outside its frontiers, Italy in Trentino and Trieste and Germany's exclusion of Austria's Germans leaving the small-German question open. The claim is therefore correct, with the qualification that destruction is not the same as causation: the unifications created the structure within which later choices were made, and the alliance system, the Balkan crises and the decisions of July 1914 belong to the topic that owns the outbreak of the war.

**Misconception to avoid:** Converting the structural legacy into a single causal arrow running from 1871 to 1914. The evidence supports the claim that the settlement created a permanently dissatisfied great power, a new centre of military gravity and the absence of a successor to the congress method; it does not support the claim that the later war followed from unification, and the standard account that holds the outbreak records several structural causes of which French revanchism is one.

**Bridge to Lesson 19:** The consequences are now set out as the record supports them. What remains is the layer above the record: the arguments historians have had about how to describe these processes, what was planned and what was seized, and which alternative outcomes an examination answer may responsibly entertain. The next lesson sets out those debates as positions, states the attribution limit openly, and disciplines the counterfactuals.

## Lesson 19 - The debates, the attribution limit, and the counterfactuals an answer may use

Progress: 19/20 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: five live debates stated as positions; why this session names positions rather than schools; the planner-or-opportunist question; the continuity thesis and the one named attribution the evidence base holds; permitted and forbidden counterfactuals; how to write a historiographical paragraph without over-claiming

Book context: queried - the historiographical companion's main-debate table on from below against from above with the best synthesis for each case; its judgement that Germany unified under an authoritarian-monarchical structure and that advanced answers therefore connect unification to later militarism and the changed balance of power; the standard account's instruction that both unifications be treated as non-identical processes and its caution against calling the Empire simply undemocratic or simply constitutional; the core world-history source's record of a named historian's continuity argument linking later German policy to earlier rulers including Bismarck, and its record that conservatives in 1932 wished to return to the system that had existed under Bismarck; the verified planning evidence for 1866 and the verified provocation evidence for 1870 carried from this session's single verification pass
CA search: not sought - this topic carries exactly one current linkage, held in Lesson 10
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: five debates, and what decides each

```text
   DEBATE 1  FROM ABOVE OR FROM BELOW?
   below: Mazzini 1831, the risings of 1848-49, Garibaldi 1860,
          the plebiscites of 1860, south German feeling in 1870
   above: Cavour's alliance 1858-59, the monarchy's absorption 1861,
          Prussia's three wars, the constitutions of 1867 and 1871
   DECIDED BY: asking the question SEPARATELY for each country
   -------------------------------------------------------------------------
   DEBATE 2  PLANNER OR OPPORTUNIST?
   planner:      ✅ 1866 recorded as "a carefully planned stage";
                 ✅ methodical preparation after 1850;
                 ✅ the 1870 telegram edited to provoke a declaration
   opportunist:  the Danish succession, the Spanish vacancy and French
                 miscalculation were not manufactured by Prussia
   DECIDED BY: separating the OBJECTIVE (pursued deliberately) from the
               OCCASIONS (exploited as they arose)
   -------------------------------------------------------------------------
   DEBATE 3  DID THE NATION MAKE THE STATE, OR THE STATE THE NATION?
   nation first: shared language, literature, a movement since the 1830s
   state first:  the phrase about "making Italians"; the campaigns against
                 Catholics and socialists; the southern insurgency of 1861-65
   DECIDED BY: evidence about popular consciousness - WHICH THIS SESSION
               DOES NOT HOLD. Report the debate; do not settle it.
   -------------------------------------------------------------------------
   DEBATE 4  ECONOMIC BASE OR MILITARY-DIPLOMATIC DECISION?
   economic:   the customs union of 1834; industrial and railway capacity
   military:   three wars, a general staff, an army funded against a chamber
   DECIDED BY: distinguishing CONDITIONS from CAUSES - and admitting that
               no series exists here by which weights could be measured
   -------------------------------------------------------------------------
   DEBATE 5  RUPTURE OR CONTINUITY - DOES 1871 EXPLAIN WHAT CAME LATER?
   continuity: ✅ a named historian's argument that later German policy
               continued that of earlier rulers INCLUDING BISMARCK;
               ✅ conservatives in 1932 wished to RETURN to the system
               that had existed under Bismarck, with a weaker Reichstag
   rupture:    later regimes differed in kind, not merely in degree
   DECIDED BY: distinguishing an inherited STRUCTURE from an inherited
               INTENTION - and routing the later period to its own owner
```

*Five debates; five different decision rules. Notice that two of them are decided by evidence this session does not hold, which is itself the most examinable fact about them: a historiographical paragraph that reports an unresolved question accurately outscores one that resolves it confidently on no evidence.*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

Everything so far has been about what happened. This lesson is about the arguments historians have had over how to describe it - and about a discipline that matters more here than anywhere else in the topic: knowing what your evidence entitles you to say.

There is a particular temptation in historiography paragraphs. They look impressive, they invite name-dropping, and they are the easiest place in an answer to write something that cannot be supported. This session therefore does two things. It teaches each debate as a *position* with the evidence on each side, so that you can argue it. And it states openly where the evidence base read for this session cannot attach those positions to named historians, so that you do not invent attributions in order to sound learned.

### 2. The attribution limit, stated once and applied throughout

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the two standard accounts held for this topic record historiographical *positions* - from above against from below, state-led against popular, unity achieved without liberty - without attaching them to named historians or schools. ✅ The folder's own answer discipline requires that no invented attribution, quotation or citation appear.

✅ **Fact:** there is exactly one named historiographical attribution in the evidence base read for this session, and it comes from the core world-history source. In its account of the debate on the origins of the Second World War, that source records that the historian **A. J. P. Taylor** argued that the German leader of the 1930s had aims **similar to those of previous German rulers** and was **"simply continuing the policies of leaders like Bismarck, Kaiser Wilhelm II and Stresemann"**, differing only in the ruthlessness of his methods.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** that single attributed claim is genuinely useful here, and its use must be disciplined in three ways. It is evidence that a **continuity thesis exists** and that it explicitly names Bismarck, which is what Debate 5 needs. It is **not** evidence that the thesis is correct; the source records it as a controversial argument within a debate belonging to another topic. And the debate it belongs to - the origins of the Second World War - is owned elsewhere, so it is cited here as a position and not pursued.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** for every other debate in this lesson, write the positions and not the names. A paragraph that says "historians divide between those who see unification as a revolution from above and those who emphasise popular movements" is accurate and safe; a paragraph that assigns those views to specific scholars on the strength of half-memory is a risk with no compensating reward.

### 3. Debate 2 in full: planner or opportunist?

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is the debate most likely to appear in a question about Bismarck's role, and it has a clean resolution that most candidates miss.

**The case for design.** ✅ The 1866 campaign is recorded as **a carefully planned stage in the unification of Germany under Prussia's ruling dynasty**, of which Bismarck was the **principal agent**. ✅ Prussia had prepared **methodically** for a renewed challenge to Austria after the failure of 1850, with a statesman, a strategist and an army organiser. ✅ The war of 1864 produced a joint possession whose administrative division at Gastein in 1865 supplied the **pretext** used in 1866. ✅ The Ems telegram of 14 July 1870 was **edited and published** with the express purpose of infuriating the French government and **provoking it into a declaration of war**. ✅ Bismarck saw the French war as the **opportunity to bring the south German states into unity** with the North German Confederation. Five items, all in the record, all indicating purpose.

**The case for opportunism.** ⚠️ Prussia did not create the Danish succession dispute, did not vacate the Spanish throne, and did not compel the French government to demand a guarantee or to declare war. ✅ The French decision of 19 July 1870 was taken because French advisers believed France would win and that a victory would restore the regime's popularity - a calculation Prussia influenced but did not make. ⚠️ Nor does anything in this evidence base show that the three wars were conceived as a single sequence in 1862.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the resolution is to separate two things that the question deliberately confuses.

| What was deliberate | What was contingent |
|---|---|
| The **objective**: Prussian leadership of a Germany excluding Austria, pursued since at least 1850 | The **occasions**: a succession dispute, a vacant throne, a rival's miscalculation |
| The **instrument**: an army rebuilt and funded through a constitutional conflict | The **outcomes**: battles could have gone otherwise, and a longer war would have invited intervention |
| The **method**: isolate, provoke, limit the aim, stop on time | The **timing**: set by openings that arose rather than by a calendar |

> 🔑 **Answer line:** The objective was pursued with method and the openings were seized as they came; Bismarck was a planner of aims and an opportunist of occasions, and an answer that chooses one of those has answered half the question.

### 4. Debate 5 in full: does 1871 explain what came later?

✅ **Fact:** the historiographical companion records that Germany unified under an **authoritarian-monarchical structure**, not under the liberal parliamentary hopes of 1848, and that this is **why advanced answers often connect German unification to later militarism and the changed balance of power**.

✅ **Fact:** recorded in Lesson 16: the victory of 1870-71 **consolidated faith in Prussian militarism, which remained a dominant force in German society**.

✅ **Fact:** the core source records that in 1932 German conservatives hoped to **return to the system that had existed under Bismarck, chancellor from 1870 to 1890, in which the Reichstag had much less power** - the same shorthand corrected in Lesson 16, where the two chancellorships of 1 July 1867 and 21 March 1871 are distinguished - evidence that the constitutional arrangement of 1871 remained a live political model sixty years later.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the continuity case therefore has three legs: a constitutional structure in which the executive was insulated from the elected chamber; the prestige of the army as an institution; and the documented later appeal of that system as a model. ⚠️ The rupture case is equally serious: a constitutional monarchy with a parliament, a legal order and an independent judiciary differs in kind from a later regime organised around racial doctrine and unlimited executive power, and treating the second as the mere continuation of the first collapses distinctions that matter.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the disciplined formulation distinguishes an inherited **structure** from an inherited **intention**. The Empire bequeathed a set of institutional habits - executive autonomy, military prestige, a parliament without control - that later actors could exploit. It did not bequeath the purposes to which they were eventually put. ⚠️ And the whole of the later story belongs to other owners; this session states the structural bequest and routes the sequel.

### 5. The remaining debates, in the form an answer needs

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the three other debates can be compressed without loss.

| Debate | The two positions | What this session can and cannot settle |
|---|---|---|
| **From above or from below** | Popular movements generated the national demand and, in Italy, conquered half the peninsula; states executed and owned the result | ✅ Settleable, and the standard accounts settle it separately for each case: Italy mixed with energy from below and completion from above; Germany overwhelmingly state-led. ✅ Never one verdict for both |
| **Nation before state, or state before nation** | A nation existed and acquired a state; or a state was created and then set about producing a nation | ⚠️ Not settleable here. The evidence base holds no measurement of popular national consciousness at any date, and the post-1861 evidence - the southern insurgency, the withdrawal of Catholics, the campaigns against Catholics and socialists - shows the state working on its population rather than the reverse. Report the debate |
| **Economic base or military-diplomatic decision** | The customs union and industrial capacity made German unification possible; three wars made it happen | ⚠️ Partly settleable. ✅ The union of 1834 is recorded and its exclusion of Austria is significant, but no trade, output or revenue series is held here, and members of the union fought against Prussia in 1866. Treat the economy as a condition and the wars as the cause, and say that the weighting cannot be measured from this evidence |

### 6. Counterfactuals: which may be used, and which may not

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** examiners sometimes invite counterfactual reasoning - "was unification inevitable?", "could the liberal route have succeeded?" - and the difference between a disciplined and an undisciplined answer is whether the counterfactual is anchored to a documented decision point.

| Counterfactual | Status | Why |
|---|---|---|
| If the Prussian king had accepted the crown in April 1849, Germany would have been unified on liberal terms | ⚠️ Permitted as a *structural* argument, not as a prediction | The refusal is documented and its reasons are recorded; but Austria's resistance, demonstrated at Olmutz in 1850, means acceptance would probably have produced a war rather than a settlement - which is itself the useful point |
| If France had not been available as an ally in 1859, Italian unification would have been delayed | ⚠️ Permitted | Piedmont had already been defeated twice by Austria alone in 1848-49, so the dependence is established by evidence rather than assumed |
| If Austria had been dismembered in 1866, the European alignment would have differed | ⚠️ Permitted as a comparison with 1871 | The contrast between the intact Austria of Prague and the amputated France of Frankfurt is documented, and the divergent outcomes are recorded |
| If Bismarck had not existed, Germany would not have been unified | ❌ Not permitted as stated | It requires a claim about an individual's indispensability that no evidence here supports; the little-German aim predates him by twelve years. Rewrite it as a question about method rather than about a person |
| If the plebiscites had gone the other way, the central states would have stayed independent | ❌ Not permitted | It assumes the votes measured free preferences, which Lesson 7 shows cannot be established from this evidence |
| Unification was inevitable once nationalism existed | ❌ Not permitted | It is a teleological claim contradicted by 1848-49, by Olmutz and by the forty years in which nationalism existed and unification did not occur |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the general rule is that a counterfactual is admissible when it is tied to a documented decision and used to identify what was *necessary*, and inadmissible when it is used to predict an alternative world. ⚠️ Examiners reward the first because it demonstrates causal analysis; they penalise the second because it is speculation.

### Strongest objection, reply and residual

**Objection:** Refusing to name historians makes the historiography paragraph vague. Examiners reward specificity, and a candidate who can attribute the revolution-from-above thesis or the debate about German continuity to particular scholars will always outscore one who writes "some historians argue".

**Reply:** The objection describes a real marking advantage and prescribes the wrong way of obtaining it. Two considerations settle the question. First, an invented or misremembered attribution is a factual error, and in a history paper a factual error in the most conspicuous sentence of the answer costs more than the vagueness it was meant to cure. ✅ The folder's own answer discipline requires that no invented citation appear. Second, and more usefully, specificity does not require names. "Some historians argue" is weak because it is empty, not because it is anonymous; "the continuity argument rests on the executive's insulation from the elected chamber and on the army's institutional prestige, while the rupture argument rests on the difference in kind between a constitutional monarchy and a later regime organised around racial doctrine" is highly specific and contains no attribution at all. ⚠️ Where a name is genuinely held - as with the one continuity attribution this evidence base records - it may be used, and this session uses it. Everywhere else, specify the *content* of the position and let that carry the marks.

**Residual:** What this evidence base cannot settle is most of the historiography itself: which interpretations are currently dominant, what the principal works are, and how the debates have moved. The session therefore teaches the positions and their evidence and states the limit rather than filling it with recollection.

### UPSC application

A historiographical paragraph belongs in a 20-mark answer and almost never in a 10-mark one. When it belongs, the structure that works is: name the debate, give one sentence of evidence for each side, and resolve it with a distinction rather than a preference - deliberate objective against contingent occasion for Bismarck, inherited structure against inherited intention for the continuity question, condition against cause for the economic question. The counterfactual table is the second application: "was it inevitable?" is answered by pointing to 1848-49, Olmutz and the decades in which nationalism existed without unification, which converts a speculative prompt into an evidenced one.

### Examiner traps in this lesson

- Attributing positions to named historians on the strength of recollection.
- Choosing between planner and opportunist instead of distinguishing objective from occasion.
- Treating the continuity thesis as established, or dismissing it as absurd - both misstate a live debate.
- Settling the nation-before-state question, which this evidence base cannot support either way.
- Claiming that economic integration caused German unification, when the recorded position supports condition rather than cause.
- Using counterfactuals as predictions of alternative worlds rather than as tests of necessity.
- Writing that unification was inevitable, which 1848-49 and Olmutz directly contradict.

### Revision notes

1. ⚠️ The standard accounts record historiographical positions without attaching them to named historians; ✅ invented attributions are forbidden by the folder's answer discipline.
2. ✅ The one named attribution held here: a historian's argument that later German policy continued that of earlier rulers, including Bismarck, differing in ruthlessness of method - recorded as a controversial position in a debate owned elsewhere.
3. ✅ Design evidence: the 1866 campaign as a carefully planned stage; methodical preparation after 1850; Gastein producing the 1866 pretext; the telegram edited to provoke a declaration; the French war seen as the opportunity to bring in the south.
4. ⚠️ Contingency evidence: the Danish succession, the Spanish vacancy and the French decision of 19 July 1870 were not manufactured by Prussia.
5. ⚠️ Resolution: deliberate objective, deliberate instrument, deliberate method; contingent occasions, outcomes and timing.
6. ✅ Germany unified under an authoritarian-monarchical structure, not under the liberal hopes of 1848.
7. ✅ The victory of 1870-71 consolidated faith in Prussian militarism; ✅ in 1932 conservatives wished to return to the Bismarckian system in which the Reichstag had much less power.
8. ⚠️ Continuity resolution: an inherited structure is not an inherited intention, and the later period belongs to its own owners.
9. ✅ From above or from below: settle separately - Italy mixed, Germany overwhelmingly state-led.
10. ⚠️ Nation before state: not settleable here; no measurement of popular consciousness is held.
11. ⚠️ Economic against military-diplomatic: economy as condition, wars as cause, weighting unmeasurable from this evidence.
12. ⚠️ Permitted counterfactuals: the refused crown, the indispensability of the French alliance, the 1866-against-1871 contrast. Forbidden: Bismarck's personal indispensability, reversed plebiscites, and inevitability.
13. ⚠️ Specify the content of a position rather than a name; content earns the marks that attribution is wrongly assumed to earn.

### Concept check

**Question:** "Bismarck was a master planner who designed German unification from the moment he took office." Examine this claim using the evidence available, and state the formulation you would use in an answer.

**Model answer:** The claim is half right and needs to be split. The evidence for deliberate design is substantial and specific: Prussia had prepared methodically for a renewed challenge to Austria after its earlier attempt collapsed at Olmutz in 1850, with Bismarck as statesman, Moltke as strategist and Roon as army organiser; the campaign of 1866 is recorded as a carefully planned stage in the unification of Germany under Prussia's ruling dynasty, of which Bismarck was the principal agent; the joint war of 1864 produced a joint possession whose administrative division at Gastein in August 1865 supplied the pretext used against Austria the following year; the Ems telegram of 14 July 1870 was edited and published with the express purpose of infuriating the French government and provoking it into declaring war; and the war with France was seen as the opportunity to bring the south German states into unity with the North German Confederation. But the evidence for contingency is equally real. Prussia did not create the Danish succession dispute, did not vacate the Spanish throne, and did not make the French decision of 19 July 1870, which was taken because French advisers believed their reorganised army would win and that a victory would restore the regime's popularity. Nothing in the available evidence shows the three wars conceived as a single sequence in 1862, and battles could have gone otherwise. The formulation to use is therefore that the objective, the instrument and the method were deliberate - Prussian leadership of a Germany excluding Austria, an army rebuilt during a constitutional conflict, and a method of isolating opponents, provoking their declarations and limiting war aims - while the occasions, the timing and the outcomes were contingent. Bismarck was a planner of aims and an opportunist of occasions, and the claim as stated collapses that distinction.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that evidence of planning for one war proves a master plan for all three. The record establishes deliberate preparation after 1850 and a planned campaign in 1866; it does not establish a design fixed in 1862 for the whole sequence, and asserting one imports a confidence the evidence does not support.

**Bridge to Lesson 20:** Everything the topic contains has now been taught, compared, bounded and qualified. What remains is the conversion: the exact syllabus clause this topic answers, the directives that are actually set, the structures that fit each mark allocation, the sentences worth memorising and the factual controls that prevent avoidable losses. The final lesson assembles all of it into an answer-writing instrument.

### Visual: from the printed clause to a finished script

```text
   THE PRINTED CLAUSE
   "History of the world will include events from 18th century such as
    industrial revolution, world wars, REDRAWAL OF NATIONAL BOUNDARIES,
    colonization, decolonization, political philosophies like communism,
    capitalism, socialism etc.- their forms and effect on the society."
                              |
        this topic sits under "redrawal of national boundaries"
        and supplies evidence to "political philosophies" without
        owning that clause
                              |
                              v
   WHAT THE EXAMINER CAN ASK              WHERE THE ANSWER COMES FROM
   ---------------------------------------------------------------------
   compare the two unifications       ->  Lesson 17, seven axes
   from above or from below?          ->  Lesson 17 section 4, per country
   role of Cavour/Bismarck/           ->  Lessons 5, 6, 12, 13-15;
     Garibaldi/Mazzini                    function, never biography
   ideas or state power?              ->  Lessons 3, 4, 12
   how did nationalism redraw         ->  Lessons 7, 18
     the map?
   limits of unification              ->  Lessons 10, 16
   effects on the balance of power    ->  Lesson 18
   was it inevitable?                 ->  Lesson 19, counterfactual table
                              |
                              v
   THE FOUR-MOVE SCRIPT (any mark allocation)
   1. THESIS that answers the directive in one sentence
   2. AXES or STAGES with one dated item attached to each
   3. LIMITS - the paragraph most candidates omit
   4. QUALIFIED VERDICT that concedes something specific
```

*The bottom panel is the whole of answer-writing for this topic. Everything above it is the material; the four moves are the shape into which the material must be poured, and the third move is where the difference between a good and an excellent script is usually made.*

### 1. What the syllabus actually licenses

✅ **Fact:** the folder's syllabus mapping records the verbatim General Studies Paper I clause: "History of the world will include events from 18th century such as industrial revolution, world wars, redrawal of national boundaries, colonization, decolonization, political philosophies like communism, capitalism, socialism etc.- their forms and effect on the society."

✅ **Fact:** the standard account for this topic records that the official syllabus explicitly includes the **redrawal of national boundaries** and that **both unifications directly fit that line**.

✅ **Fact:** the syllabus mapping assigns the "political philosophies" sub-clause - liberalism, conservatism, nationalism, capitalism, socialism, communism and the rest - to the overview topic that owns it, not to this one.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** three consequences for answer-writing.

1. **This topic is directly examinable** under a named sub-clause, so a question can be set on it without any framing device.
2. **Nationalism as a doctrine is not owned here.** If a question asks what nationalism *is*, that is the overview topic's material; if it asks what nationalism *did* between 1815 and 1871, that is this topic's.
3. **The clause's phrase is "redrawal of national boundaries", which is a consequences phrase.** ⚠️ An answer that stops at the act of unification and never reaches the redrawn map has under-answered the clause it is being examined on.

### 2. The directive and demand map

✅ **Fact:** the standard account for this topic supplies this map directly, and it is reproduced here with the lesson references attached.

| If the question says | It is really testing | Do NOT write | Answer from |
|---|---|---|---|
| *Compare the two unifications* | Difference in **agency and method**, not two parallel narratives | Italy's story, then Germany's story | Lesson 17 |
| *From above or from below?* | Whether elite statecraft or popular mobilisation was decisive, **case by case** | One verdict for both countries | Lesson 17 section 4 |
| *Role of Bismarck / Cavour / Garibaldi / Mazzini* | **Function within a sequence**, not biography | A life sketch | Lessons 5, 6, 8, 12 |
| *Ideas or state power?* | Ideology as **necessary but insufficient** | Choosing one side | Lessons 3, 4, 12 |
| *How did nationalism reshape the map?* | **Boundary consequences** and who was excluded | Only the two unifications | Lessons 7, 18 |
| *Assess the limits of unification* | Regional, social, religious and minority **incompleteness** | Ending at 1871 | Lessons 10, 16 |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the right-hand "do not write" column is the more useful half of the table, because each entry names a failure mode that is both common and easy to avoid. Read it once before writing and the commonest structural errors disappear.

### 3. Qualified thesis templates

✅ **Fact:** the standard account for this topic supplies three, and each answers a different directive.

- ⚠️ **Comparative:** "Both unifications required a strong core state and a war against Austria, but Italy needed a popular military campaign that Germany never had, and Germany achieved a concentration of power that Italy never achieved."
- ⚠️ **Above and below:** "Nationalism was generated from below and executed from above; the movements supplied legitimacy and the states supplied the means, and neither could have succeeded alone."
- ⚠️ **Limits:** "Both states were unified politically before they were unified socially, which is why the years after 1871 were dominated by the question of who really belonged to the new nation."

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** a thesis of this kind does three jobs in one sentence - it answers the directive, it announces the structure, and it pre-concedes the obvious counter-position, which prevents the examiner's first objection. ⚠️ Write it first, and let the body deliver what it promises.

### 4. Mark-scaled structures

✅ **Fact:** the standard account for this topic supplies the scaling directly.

| Marks | Structure |
|---|---|
| **10** | Thesis → **two** axes of comparison, core state and method, with named evidence → one limit → verdict |
| **15** | Thesis → the shared obstacle, the Vienna settlement and Austria → Italy's sequence → Germany's sequence → the decisive difference → limits → verdict |
| **20** | All of the above → **plus** the popular-versus-elite question, minorities and the post-1871 consequences for European power → verdict |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the practical translation into word budgets, at the repository's standard ceilings of 150 words for 10 marks and 250 for 15 and 20.

| Element | 150 words | 250 words |
|---|---|---|
| Thesis | 20-25 | 25-30 |
| Body | 90-100 across two axes | 150-170 across four axes or two sequences |
| Limits | 15-20 | 30-40 |
| Verdict | 15-20 | 25-30 |

### 5. Verdict scaffolds

✅ **Fact:** the standard account for this topic supplies three, one for each of the commonest directives.

- **"Compare."** → "Same enemy, same era, opposite methods: Italy unified by combining diplomacy with insurrection and remained weak; Germany unified by concentrating military and diplomatic power in one state and became too strong for the system that produced it."
- **"Ideas or state power?"** → "Ideas made unification thinkable and legitimate; state power made it happen - and the gap between the two explains why the unified states disappointed the nationalists who had demanded them."
- **"How did nationalism reshape the map?"** → "It replaced a settlement based on dynastic legitimacy with one based on national states - and in doing so created both the Alsace-Lorraine grievance and the Habsburg nationality crisis that broke Europe in 1914."

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** each scaffold has the same internal shape: a decision, then a concession or consequence that shows the decision was made with the counter-evidence in view. ⚠️ A verdict without a concession reads as assertion; a concession without a decision reads as a hedge.

### 6. The complete factual-risk controls

✅ **Fact:** the standard account for this topic sets out the following controls, and they are reproduced in full because breaching any one of them costs marks mechanically.

| Control | Correct position |
|---|---|
| Rome's accession | ✅ Rome joined in **1870**; the Kingdom of Italy was proclaimed in **1861**; Venetia joined in **1866** |
| Mazzini's role | ✅ He inspired unification; **Cavour, Garibaldi and Victor Emmanuel II** completed it |
| Austria's role in Germany | ✅ **Prussia** led German unification, after defeating Austria in 1866 |
| The instrument of German unity | ✅ **Prussian power and war** were decisive, not popular assemblies |
| Bismarck's dates | ✅ **September 1862** as minister-president; **1 July 1867** as North German chancellor; **18 January 1871** for the proclamation; **21 March 1871** as imperial chancellor; **March 1890** for the dismissal - and no date outside that set |
| Quoting Bismarck | ✅ Do not quote him verbatim; **paraphrase as Realpolitik** |
| Terms not held as folder facts | ✅ Do not present the campaign against the Catholic Church, the term for the Italian south or the anti-socialist legislation as sourced facts of this folder; use them as analytical references |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this session adds five controls of its own, arising from its evidence base.

1. **No plebiscite percentage, population, income, literacy, emigration or output figure** appears anywhere; the standard account's caution requires the southern comparison to remain qualitative.
2. **No force or casualty figure** is used except the small verified set for 1870-71 in Lesson 15.
3. **The "make Italians" phrase** is not offered as a sourced quotation; its attribution is traditional and disputed.
4. **No historian is named** except the single continuity attribution held in the core source.
5. **No clause of the Plombieres agreement** is asserted; only the meeting, its date, its participants and the fact that gains were foreseen for both sides.

### 7. The four boundaries, restated for the hall

✅ **Fact:** the standard account's closing boundary note states it directly: Topic 05 owns the Vienna settlement, Topic 07 owns New Imperialism and Africa, and Topic 09 owns the pre-1914 alliance system, causes and outbreak; this topic uses Vienna and Alsace-Lorraine only as the **opening condition and bounded consequence** of national unification.

| Boundary | The clause to use in an answer |
|---|---|
| Vienna | "The settlement of 1815, which postponed rather than resolved the national question, left Italy divided and Germany in a thirty-nine-state confederation." Then stop |
| Nationalism as doctrine | "Nationalism supplied the claim that a people entitled to govern itself should possess a state." Then use it, do not define it further |
| 1914 | "French revanchism over Alsace-Lorraine became one of the named sources of friction in 1914." Then hand over |
| India | "European national unification and Indian anti-colonial nationalism are different projects and should not be collapsed into one template." Then stop |

✅ **Fact:** the historiographical companion states the Indian boundary explicitly: these unifications show that nationalism does not always produce democracy first, and they should be compared **cautiously** with Indian nationalism, which was a broader anti-colonial movement with mass politics; the two must not be collapsed into one template.

### 8. Twelve sentences worth memorising

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** each of these compresses a lesson and can be deployed directly.

1. Both nuclei were created by the settlement they overthrew.
2. Italy faced an occupier and needed borrowed force; Germany faced a rival and needed only to exclude it.
3. 1848 discredited the instruments of nationalism, not the idea.
4. In Germany the customs frontier drew the map that the army later enforced.
5. Cavour's decisive pre-war move was a war that had nothing to do with Italy.
6. A borrowed army fights the lender's war and charges the agreed price in full.
7. The plebiscite converted conquest into consent - and armed every future claimant.
8. Italy was made from below and kept from above.
9. Italy completed its territory in 1870 and acquired a fifty-nine-year quarrel with the papacy on the same day.
10. In 1866 Prussia took everything it needed and nothing it merely wanted; in 1871 it took what it wanted.
11. The Empire represented the nation and did not let it govern.
12. Both states were unified politically before they were unified socially.

### Strongest objection, reply and residual

**Objection:** A lesson of templates produces formulaic answers. Examiners can recognise a coached structure, and a script assembled from memorised scaffolds will read as mechanical beside one written from genuine understanding.

**Reply:** The objection is right about templates used as substitutes and wrong about templates used as containers. Two distinctions matter. First, what is being standardised here is the *shape* of the argument - thesis, axes with evidence, limits, qualified verdict - not its content, and that shape is simply what an analytical answer looks like under time pressure; an examiner penalises a formula when it replaces thinking, not when it organises it. Second, the content that fills these containers is topic-specific and evidenced: dated markers, named mechanisms, recorded limits and controlled qualifications. ⚠️ The real risk the objection identifies is deploying a scaffold that does not fit the question asked, which is why the directive map exists and why the "do not write" column is the most useful part of it. ⚠️ The correct use of this lesson is to internalise the four moves and the controls, and then to answer the question in front of you.

**Residual:** What cannot be supplied here is practice. The structures, controls and sentences are instruments; their value depends on being used under timed conditions against unfamiliar stems, which is what the practice sections that follow are for.

### UPSC application

This lesson is itself the application, and its use is procedural. Before writing: identify the directive, find it in the map, take the thesis template, choose the mark-scaled structure. While writing: attach one dated item to each axis or stage, and check the factual controls as you pass them - Rome in 1870, Mazzini as inspiration, Prussia as leader, no Bismarck quotation, no figures. Before finishing: write the limits paragraph, which most candidates omit, and close with a verdict that concedes something specific. Four moves, six controls, one verdict.

### Examiner traps in this lesson

- Deploying a memorised scaffold that does not match the directive actually set.
- Writing the thesis last, so that the body has no announced structure.
- Omitting the limits paragraph because the word count is tight - it is the highest-value paragraph in the answer.
- Ending with a summary rather than a verdict.
- Defining nationalism at length, which belongs to the overview owner.
- Drifting into the alliance system or the July crisis, which belong to the 1914 owner.
- Comparing European unification with Indian nationalism without the caution the historiographical companion requires.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ The clause: "redrawal of national boundaries" - both unifications fit it directly.
2. ✅ Nationalism as a doctrine belongs to the overview owner; this topic owns nationalism as a historical force.
3. ✅ Six directives and their real demands: compare, from above or below, role of a named figure, ideas or state power, reshaping the map, limits.
4. ✅ Three thesis templates: comparative, above-and-below, limits.
5. ✅ Mark-scaled structures: two axes at 10; the full sequence with limits at 15; plus popular-elite, minorities and consequences at 20.
6. ⚠️ Word budgets: thesis 20-30, body 90-170, limits 15-40, verdict 15-30 according to the ceiling.
7. ✅ Three verdict scaffolds: same enemy and opposite methods; ideas made it thinkable and state power made it happen; dynastic legitimacy replaced by national states.
8. ✅ Seven recorded controls: Rome 1870; Mazzini inspired; Prussia led; power and war decided; Bismarck's two dates; no Bismarck quotation; named campaigns only as analytical references.
9. ⚠️ Five session controls: no plebiscite or socio-economic figures; no force figures outside 1870-71; no "make Italians" quotation; no historians named beyond the one held; no Plombieres clauses.
10. ✅ Four boundaries: Vienna, nationalism as doctrine, 1914, and the Indian comparison - each with a one-clause hand-off.
11. ✅ The Indian caution: European national unification and Indian anti-colonial nationalism are different projects with different social bases.
12. ⚠️ Twelve memorisable sentences, each compressing one lesson.
13. ⚠️ Four moves: thesis, axes with dated evidence, limits, qualified verdict.

### Concept check

**Question:** You are given 250 words and the stem: "Nationalism made the nineteenth-century European map, but the states it made were not the states nationalists had demanded. Discuss." Set out the structure you would use, the evidence you would attach, and the verdict you would write.

**Model answer:** The directive is "discuss", so the answer must hold two propositions together rather than choosing between them, and the stem's own structure supplies the axes. The thesis would run: nationalism supplied the legitimacy that redrew the map between 1859 and 1871, but in both Italy and Germany the redrawing was executed by dynastic states that retained the power, so the movements obtained the nation and lost the constitution. The body takes three moves. First, nationalism made the map: the plebiscites of March 1860 in Tuscany, Parma, Modena and Romagna substituted popular consent for dynastic right as the ground of territorial title; the Kingdom of Italy was proclaimed on 17 March 1861 and completed with Venetia in 1866 and Rome on 20 September 1870; and the German Empire was proclaimed at Versailles on 18 January 1871. Second, the states were not what was demanded: Mazzini's republic was displaced by a monarchy and the mass movement's programme was not what was built; Germany unified under an authoritarian-monarchical structure rather than the liberal parliamentary hopes of 1848, with a Reichstag elected by universal male suffrage but no control over the chancellor, the army or foreign policy, a settlement the liberal chamber itself conceded through the Indemnity Bill of 1866. Third, the limits: the Italian south was fought as an insurgency between 1861 and 1865 and Catholics were discouraged from voting after 1874, while the Empire treated Catholics, socialists, Poles, Danes and Alsatians as suspect. The verdict: ideas made unification thinkable and legitimate while state power made it happen, and the gap between the two explains why the unified states disappointed the nationalists who had demanded them.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating "discuss" as an invitation to describe both unifications. The stem contains a claim with two halves, and the marks lie in evidencing each half and then explaining the relationship between them; a script that narrates the two sequences without addressing the gap between demand and outcome has answered a question that was not asked.
