## What to rehearse, given a nil return

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** a topic with no owned question is prepared differently from one with a bank of past papers, and the difference is a matter of method rather than of effort.

1. **Rehearse architectures, not answers.** The six directive families in Lesson 20 exhaust what can realistically be asked, and each has a structure. Practise producing the structure from the stem in under a minute.
2. **Rehearse the controls, because they are what a first attempt gets wrong.** Rome in 1870; Mazzini as inspiration rather than completer; Prussia as leader; no Bismarck quotation; no figures.
3. **Rehearse the limits paragraph separately.** ✅ The standard account records that unification questions almost always reward candidates who continue past the proclamation, and the limits material of Lessons 10 and 16 is the part most likely to be missing under time pressure.
4. **Rehearse the hand-offs.** Four boundaries, one clause each. A script that trespasses into the 1914 owner's material loses time and gains nothing.
5. **Use the standard accounts' own probable framings as the practice bank.** ✅ The standard account for this topic lists three - comparing the processes, how nationalism reshaped the European map after 1815, and whether unification was achieved more by ideas or by state power - and ✅ the historiographical companion lists three more - the "revolution from above and compromise between above and below" proposition, an assessment of Bismarck's role in the making of modern Germany, and how far the two unifications transformed the European balance of power. The six original questions in the next section but one are built to cover all six framings.

# CUMULATIVE CONCEPT CHECKS

These run across lesson blocks rather than within a single lesson. Attempt each from memory before reading the model answer; the misconception line names the error that most often costs marks in that block.

## Block A - Starting conditions and the failure of the first method, Lessons 1 to 4

### Cumulative check A1 - from a settlement to two different problems

**Question:** Show how the single settlement of 1815 produced two structurally different national problems, and explain why the same settlement also supplied the instruments that solved them.

**Model answer:** The settlement produced two problems because it applied the same principle - dynastic legitimacy and a balance among great powers - to two different situations. In Italy it left the peninsula divided among Austrian-controlled or Austrian-influenced states, the Papal States and Sardinia-Piedmont, with Austria dominating Lombardy-Venetia directly, so that the Italian national problem contained an occupying foreign power and required liberation before consolidation could even be attempted. In Germany it created a confederation of thirty-nine sovereign states under Austrian influence, with no foreign occupation anywhere but with the presidency held by a power that was itself only partly German and that could not permit a German national state, since such a state would either exclude Austria or require the Habsburg monarchy to dismember itself. The German problem was therefore exclusion rather than expulsion, and it turned on which German state would lead. The same settlement supplied both instruments because a barrier against France required strong states on France's flanks: Sardinia-Piedmont was strengthened by the addition of Genoa, and Prussia by the Rhineland, Westphalia and Saxon lands, which placed it in the coal and iron districts on which its later power rested. Both nuclei were thus created by the settlement they overthrew - not by accident of sentiment but because a balance-of-power system rewards capable states and capable states may use their capacity for purposes the system never intended.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the two problems as the same problem in two places. The difference between a dominating occupier and a presiding rival determines everything that follows - whether a foreign ally was necessary, whether a popular army was needed, and whether completion was staggered or concentrated.

### Cumulative check A2 - what 1848 proved, and what survived it

**Question:** Using the Italian and German events of 1848-49, state the single proposition that the year established, and identify three things that survived the failure and reappeared in the successful phase.

**Model answer:** The proposition is that national legitimacy cannot command force. In Italy the most capable Italian state fought Austria and was beaten twice, at Custoza in July 1848 and at Novara in March 1849, after which Charles Albert abdicated in favour of Victor Emmanuel II; the Roman Republic proclaimed in February 1849, with Mazzini in its triumvirate and Garibaldi organising its defence, was destroyed by French intervention by July 1849, and Venice surrendered to Austria in August. In Germany the Frankfurt National Assembly met from May 1848, adopted a constitution on 28 March 1849 providing for a hereditary emperor and a parliament elected by universal male suffrage, offered the imperial crown to Frederick William IV in April 1849 and was refused - and when the king declined, nothing followed, because the assembly had no army, no revenue and no administration. Three things survived. The personnel survived: Garibaldi, who organised the Roman Republic's defence, commanded the decisive popular campaign of 1860 in a monarchy's name. The constitutional achievement survived: the Statuto granted in Piedmont in March 1848 outlived the defeat and became the constitutional frame of the future Italian kingdom. And the programme survived in a mutilated form: universal male suffrage for a national parliament, and the little-German solution excluding Austria, both reappeared in the constitutions of 1867 and 1871, granted from above and without parliamentary control of the executive. The generation that followed abandoned the instruments of 1848 and kept its aims.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that 1848 failed because its participants were naive. The Piedmontese war was fought by a functioning state with an army and still lost, and the Frankfurt assembly completed a constitution and selected a solution that was later adopted. The failure was of enforcement, not of thought or of effort.

## Block B - The Italian sequence, Lessons 5 to 8

### Cumulative check B1 - the four-stage Italian mechanism

**Question:** Set out Italian unification between 1855 and 1861 as a four-stage mechanism, naming what each stage supplied that the previous one could not.

**Model answer:** The first stage supplied standing. Piedmont-Sardinia, rebuilt through reform after 1849 and retaining the only constitutional politics in Italy, sent troops to the Crimean War in 1855 in a quarrel of no Italian relevance, and the resulting status as a co-belligerent admitted Cavour to the Congress of Paris in 1856, where he raised the Italian question before the great powers - converting a petitioner into a participant and reframing the demand from insurrection into a problem of instability that conservative cabinets could discuss. The second stage supplied force. The secret meeting at Plombieres on 21 July 1858 planned a Franco-Piedmontese alliance against Austria with territorial gains foreseen for both sides; the war of 1859 was won jointly and Lombardy was gained, though the armistice of Villafranca on 11 July 1859 left Venetia Austrian, and the price was paid in full by the cession of Nice and Savoy on 24 March 1860. The third stage supplied legal title over the centre. Local upheaval in Tuscany, Parma, Modena and Romagna was converted into annexation by the plebiscites of March 1860, which substituted popular consent for dynastic right as the ground of transfer and appropriated the republicans' own principle for a monarchy. The fourth stage supplied the south, which no other method could reach: Garibaldi's Thousand landed at Marsala on 11 May 1860, conquered Sicily and Naples, and handed the conquest to Victor Emmanuel II at Teano on 26 October 1860, after which the Kingdom of Italy was proclaimed on 17 March 1861. Each stage supplied exactly what the previous one lacked - standing, then force, then title, then territory a state could not attack openly.

**Misconception to avoid:** Presenting the four stages as a single continuous plan executed by one actor. They involved different agents with different aims - a minister buying standing, a foreign emperor pursuing his own interests, provisional authorities in the central states, and a volunteer commander acting without formal authorisation - and the state's achievement was to end up owning the result.

### Cumulative check B2 - the cost of each Italian gain

**Question:** For each territorial gain between 1859 and 1861, name the liability it created, and state what the pattern shows about the Italian state that emerged.

**Model answer:** Lombardy in 1859 was gained through a great-power ally, and the liability was the price: Nice and Savoy, both Italian-speaking, ceded to France in March 1860 by a state whose own justifying principle was nationality - which is the strongest single piece of evidence that dynastic and strategic calculation, rather than national principle, governed the state's decisions. The central duchies in 1860 were gained by plebiscite, and the liability was the principle itself: once populations are admitted to choose their sovereign, the argument becomes available to anyone dissatisfied with the result, including the southern populations of the new kingdom and, later, the French-speaking population annexed by Germany in 1871 without any such consultation. Sicily and Naples in 1860 were gained by a popular campaign, and the liability was the settlement that followed: the conquest was transferred to the monarchy at Teano, the republican programme was displaced, and Piedmontese institutions were extended outward instead of a new national settlement being negotiated, so the territories brought in from below had no share in designing the state they entered. The proclamation of 1861 carried its own liability, since the kingdom was declared without Venetia and Rome and therefore announced its own incompleteness as government policy. The pattern shows a state that acquired territory faster than it acquired consent, and that solved each problem with an instrument which created the next problem - which is why the limits examined later are not a separate topic but the accumulated cost of the method.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the liabilities as unfortunate side-effects. Each followed directly from the instrument chosen - a purchased alliance charges a price, a plebiscite concedes a principle, and a borrowed popular army must be paid in political settlement - so the costs were structural rather than accidental.

## Block C - The German sequence, Lessons 11 to 15

### Cumulative check C1 - three wars as one operation

**Question:** Show how the three wars of 1864, 1866 and 1870-71 form a single operation, identifying what each supplied to the next.

**Model answer:** The first war supplied a quarrel. Prussia and Austria seized Schleswig and Holstein from Denmark jointly in 1864 and held them together; joint possession between rivals is not a settlement but a standing grievance, and the Convention of Gastein of August 1865, which gave Prussia the administration of Schleswig and Austria that of Holstein, left Austria responsible for an exposed territory far from its own lands. The dispute over that administration was the pretext used in 1866. The second war supplied exclusion and a state. Prussia, allied with Italy so that Austrian forces were divided, decided the issue in Bohemia and most decisively at Koniggratz on 3 July 1866; by the Treaty of Prague of 23 August Austria was excluded from Germany without losing territory to Prussia, Schleswig-Holstein was assigned to Prussia, and Hanover, Hesse-Kassel, Nassau and Frankfurt were annexed outright, making Prussia territorially contiguous for the first time; the North German Confederation, with its constitution in force from 1 July 1867, was the German state in its first form, and the Indemnity Bill of 1866 closed the domestic constitutional conflict in the government's favour. The third war supplied the south. Bavaria, Wurttemberg and Baden remained outside the new confederation and could be neither conquered nor persuaded, but they would join a defensive war; the Hohenzollern candidature for the Spanish throne, the French demand for a guarantee, and the editing and publication of the Ems telegram on 14 July 1870 produced a French declaration of war on 19 July, whereupon the southern states, regarding France as the aggressor, sided with Prussia. The empire was proclaimed at Versailles on 18 January 1871. Each war produced precisely the condition the next required.

**Misconception to avoid:** Concluding from this that the whole sequence was designed in 1862. The record establishes methodical preparation after 1850, a carefully planned campaign in 1866 and a deliberate provocation in 1870; it does not establish a single master plan, and the defensible formulation is deliberate objectives with opportunistically chosen occasions.

### Cumulative check C2 - the two settlements compared

**Question:** Compare the settlement of 1866 with that of 1871, and explain what the comparison proves about the relationship between peace terms and later alliances.

**Model answer:** In 1866 the objective was exclusion, and the terms matched it. By the Treaty of Prague of 23 August Austria was excluded from German affairs and ceded Venetia for transfer to Italy, but lost no territory to Prussia and was not dismembered; the German states that had fought alongside it were treated variously, with four annexed outright to make Prussia contiguous and others preserved and incorporated. The result was an Austria that could accept the new arrangement and eventually align with the power that had defeated it. In 1871 the objective had been achieved before the peace was made - the southern states had joined and the empire was proclaimed on 18 January - yet the Treaty of Frankfurt of 10 May annexed Alsace and half of Lorraine with Metz and imposed an indemnity of five billion francs together with the costs of occupation until it was paid. The result was a France that resented the loss, became obsessed with recovering it, and never accepted the settlement, so that the years from 1871 to 1914 were an extremely unstable peace and French revanchism became a named source of friction in the crisis that followed. The comparison proves that peace terms, not victories, determine the alignments of the following decades: the same government, using the same method, produced a reconcilable defeated power in one case and a permanently dissatisfied one in the other, and the difference lay entirely in whether territory was taken. It also shows that the method's own rule - take what the objective requires and refuse what would create a coalition against you - was observed in 1866 and broken in 1871.

**Misconception to avoid:** Reading the leniency of 1866 as evidence of moderation as a personal quality. The same government annexed four German states in that very settlement and imposed a punitive peace five years later; the consistent principle is calculation about future coalitions rather than restraint.

## Block D - The two states compared, Lessons 16 to 17

### Cumulative check D1 - who belonged, in each new state

**Question:** Explain why both new states faced a problem of belonging, identify the different instruments each used, and say what the outcomes show.

**Model answer:** Both faced it because a state justified by nationality must decide which of its subjects are reliably national, and because both had been created by state power rather than by popular consent and therefore had no mechanism for producing the consent they had dispensed with. In Italy the excluded were the south, practising Catholics and the republicans: the government fought a campaign against brigandage in the south from 1861 to 1865 against discontent with a northern-dominated administration, conscription and economic measures unfavourable to peasants; the annexation of Rome in 1870 produced a hostile papacy that rejected the unilateral Law of Guarantees of 1871 and, by the decree of 1874, discouraged Catholics from voting in national elections; and the republican programme had already been displaced by the monarchy. In Germany the suspect communities were Catholics and, later, socialists, alongside Polish, Danish and French-speaking populations: the campaign against the Catholic Church and the Centre Party ran from 1871 to 1878 with measures including civil marriage and state oversight of clerical training, and the Anti-Socialist Laws of 1878 to 1890 banned socialist organisations, publications and meetings. The instruments differed - counter-insurgency and political management in Italy, legal prohibition in Germany - and the outcomes were similar: the Centre Party grew and the anti-Catholic laws were largely repealed, the social-democratic movement emerged stronger when the prohibition lapsed in 1890, and Italian politics from 1876 was conducted by trasformismo, the building of shifting centrist coalitions, precisely because the two natural mass constituencies were absent from parliamentary competition. Both states could compel obedience and neither could manufacture belonging.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the German measures as evidence of a uniquely authoritarian character and the Italian ones as ordinary state-building. Both were responses to the same structural problem, and in both cases the coercive instrument produced a stronger opponent, which is the analytically important result.

### Cumulative check D2 - the paradox of franchise and power

**Question:** Explain why the German Empire had a wider franchise and a narrower distribution of power than the Italian kingdom, and what this shows about nineteenth-century constitutional design.

**Model answer:** The Empire's Reichstag was elected by universal male suffrage, a principle established in the North German Confederation's constitution of 1 July 1867 and carried into the imperial constitution of 1871, which was wider than most contemporary European franchises and far wider than Italy's restricted one - narrower still in practice after 1874, when Catholics were discouraged from participating in national elections. Yet the German parliament could not appoint or dismiss a government, since the chancellor was appointed by and responsible to the Kaiser, who was the King of Prussia; the army and foreign policy remained under monarchical control; Prussia's weight in the federal council made federalism a housing for Prussian preponderance; and the Prussian state franchise itself remained weighted by wealth. Italy, by contrast, had a genuinely parliamentary executive, though it was hollowed out from 1876 by trasformismo. The explanation is that the extent of the franchise and the location of executive power were separate variables. A conservative government could widen the first precisely in order to secure the second: a broad, largely rural electorate was less radical than the narrow propertied urban one that had obstructed the Prussian government during the budget conflict of the 1860s, and a wide suffrage outflanked liberal critics who were demanding constitutional control rather than more voters. The clinching demonstration is the dismissal of Bismarck by Wilhelm II in March 1890 - the chancellor who had created the Empire removed by the emperor alone, with no parliamentary process whatever. The lesson for constitutional analysis is that democratic form and democratic substance must be assessed separately, which is why the recorded instruction is that the Empire was neither simply undemocratic nor simply constitutional but both.

**Misconception to avoid:** Inferring the character of a regime from its franchise. A wide suffrage attached to an executive that no chamber can remove produces representation without control, which is exactly the settlement the German liberals accepted in exchange for national unity.

## Block E - Consequences, judgement and answer conversion, Lessons 18 to 20

### Cumulative check E1 - the structural legacy, stated without over-claiming

**Question:** State the structural legacy of the two unifications for Europe, and show how to make the claim without asserting that they caused the First World War.

**Model answer:** The legacy has four components, each supportable. First, the ordering principle changed: a settlement based on dynastic legitimacy was replaced by one based on national states, a substitution made concrete by the plebiscite instrument of 1860 and by the national justification of both new states. Second, the centre of gravity moved: a plural central Europe of thirty-nine German states and a divided peninsula was replaced by a single empire with the strongest army on the continent, so that no continental combination could be formed without reference to it. Third, a military model spread: the victory of 1870-71 consolidated faith in Prussian militarism, and the Prussian system of conscript armies directed by a highly trained general staff was soon adopted by the other great powers, so that future wars would be fought by mass armies mobilised on timetables. Fourth, a great power was left permanently dissatisfied: Alsace and half of Lorraine were annexed in 1871, the French resented the loss and were obsessed with recovering it, and the years from 1871 to 1914 were an extremely unstable peace. The claim is made without over-reaching by distinguishing conditions from causes. These four items are the structure within which later choices were made; the alliance system, the arms race, the Balkan crises and the decisions of July 1914 are those choices, and they belong to the topic that owns the outbreak of the war, which records several structural causes of which French revanchism is one. The disciplined formulation is that the unifications destroyed the Vienna order and supplied no successor mechanism for managing change - which made the next crisis harder to contain without making it inevitable.

**Misconception to avoid:** Running a single causal arrow from 1871 to 1914. The evidence supports a claim about conditions - a dissatisfied great power, a new military centre, a diffused military model and the collapse of the congress method - and the standard account that holds the outbreak records a set of causes, not one.

### Cumulative check E2 - the whole topic in one verdict

**Question:** Write a single closing paragraph, of the kind that would end a 20-mark answer, that covers both unifications, their methods, their limits and their consequences.

**Model answer:** Same enemy, same era, opposite methods: both nations began from a settlement that had refused the national question and both required Austria to be defeated, but Italy needed a purchased great-power ally in 1859 and a popular army in 1860 to complete a unification spread across eleven years and dependent twice on other powers' wars, while Prussia used its own army and its own diplomacy to exclude Austria in 1866 and to obtain from France in 1870 the war that brought the southern states in, concentrating the whole process into seven years. The methods produced the states: a middling power with weak capacity, fighting its own south between 1861 and 1865 and losing its Catholic citizens to an electoral boycott after 1874, and the strongest continental military-economic power, representing its nation in a parliament elected by every adult man while allowing that parliament no control over the chancellor, the army or foreign policy. In both, the movement that generated the national demand did not govern the state that satisfied it, which is why both were unified politically before they were unified socially and why the years after 1871 were dominated by the question of who really belonged to the new nation. And in Europe the consequence was structural rather than merely territorial: dynastic legitimacy gave way to nationality as the basis of title, the centre of the continent acquired a single dominant power, the congress method lost its authority, and a defeated France was given a grievance it never abandoned - so that the system emerged from unification with a new organising principle, a new hegemon and no agreed means of managing the next change.

**Misconception to avoid:** Ending with a summary of what was covered. A verdict decides something and concedes something; a summary repeats. If the final paragraph could be written before the body, it is a summary.

# ORIGINAL 10-, 15- AND 20-MARK MAINS MODEL PRACTICE

These six questions are original to this session and are not verified PYQs; the topic owns none, and the two standard accounts held for this topic record six probable framings, all of which are covered below. Attempt each under timed conditions before reading the model answer. Each model sits at or below its stated word ceiling - 150 words at 10 marks and 250 words at 15 and 20 marks, the established repository convention - and each carries a scoring note identifying the single element that most often separates a good script from an average one on that demand.

## Question 1 - 10 marks, 150 words

> Explain why Italian unification required an external great-power ally while German unification did not. (Answer in 150 words)

### Model answer

The difference lay in the obstacle and in the instrument. Italy faced Austria as a dominating power holding Lombardy-Venetia outright, so liberation had to precede consolidation; and Piedmont-Sardinia, beaten alone at Custoza in 1848 and Novara in 1849, could not defeat Austria by itself. Cavour therefore bought an ally: troops to the Crimea in 1855, the Italian question raised at the Congress of Paris in 1856, the alliance planned at Plombieres in July 1858, and the price paid in Nice and Savoy in March 1860.

Germany faced Austria as a presiding rival inside a confederation, so the task was exclusion, not expulsion. Prussia already possessed a first-rank army and leadership of a customs union covering Germany outside Austria since 1834. It needed neutrality, not assistance; hence isolation of opponents, an Italian alliance in 1866 merely to divide Austrian forces, and a French declaration of war obtained in 1870.

*Scoring note: the separator is the word "obstacle". Scripts that answer with relative strength alone miss that the two states faced different kinds of enemy, and it is the kind of enemy - occupier against rival - that determines whether borrowed force is necessary at all.*

## Question 2 - 10 marks, 150 words

> "Ideas made unification thinkable; state power made it happen." Examine this claim with reference to Italy and Germany. (Answer in 150 words)

### Model answer

The claim is defensible if both halves are evidenced. Ideas supplied programme and personnel: Mazzini's Young Italy of 1831 fixed a national end-state and recruited a generation, and the Frankfurt assembly's constitution of 28 March 1849 chose the little-German solution and universal male suffrage.

But ideas had no instrument. Piedmont was beaten twice by Austria in 1848-49, and when Frankfurt offered the imperial crown in April 1849 the Prussian king refused and nothing followed. Power delivered what conviction could not: an alliance in 1859, a volunteer conquest absorbed by a monarchy in 1861, and three Prussian wars ending in the proclamation of 18 January 1871.

The qualification completes the claim. The states adopted the movements' demands and withheld their conditions - Mazzini's republic displaced, German liberals conceding executive control in the Indemnity Bill of 1866 - which is why the unified states disappointed the nationalists who had demanded them.

*Scoring note: the separator is the third paragraph. Scripts that stop after establishing both halves have illustrated the quotation; scripts that explain what the states did with the ideas they borrowed have analysed it, and that is where the "examine" directive is satisfied.*

## Question 3 - 15 marks, 250 words

> Compare the processes by which Italy and Germany were unified between 1848 and 1871. (Answer in 250 words)

### Model answer

Both processes began from the settlement of 1815, which left Italy divided under Austrian domination and Germany a thirty-nine-state confederation, and both required Austria to be defeated. Both nuclei were created by that settlement: Piedmont-Sardinia strengthened by Genoa, Prussia by the Rhineland, Westphalia and Saxon lands. The comparison lies in agency and method.

The core states differed in capacity. Prussia possessed a first-rank army and had led a customs union covering Germany outside Austria since 1834; Piedmont had to purchase standing through the Crimea in 1855 and the Congress of Paris in 1856, then purchase force at Plombieres in July 1858, paying with Nice and Savoy in March 1860.

The popular element is the sharpest contrast. Garibaldi's Thousand landed at Marsala on 11 May 1860 and conquered Sicily and Naples, handing the south to Victor Emmanuel II at Teano on 26 October; Germany had no equivalent mass military campaign, and its national settlement was granted, not conquered from below.

Timing followed method. Italy's completion was staggered - 1861, Venetia in 1866 through Prussia's war, Rome in 1870 after France's defeat - and twice dependent on others. Germany's was concentrated: 1864, 1866, the North German Confederation of 1867, and the proclamation of 18 January 1871.

The results diverged accordingly: a middling power fighting its own south between 1861 and 1865, and the strongest continental military-economic power. Same enemy, same era, opposite methods - and in both the movement that generated the demand did not govern the state that satisfied it.

*Scoring note: the separator is structural, not factual. A script organised by axes - core state, popular element, timing, result - has performed the comparison; a script organised as Italy's story followed by Germany's story has left the comparison to the examiner and cannot recover the marks with evidence alone.*

## Question 4 - 15 marks, 250 words

> "German unification was a revolution from above; Italian unification was a compromise between above and below." Discuss. (Answer in 250 words)

### Model answer

The proposition is broadly correct and needs grading in both halves.

For Germany it is strong. The national demand came from below - the Frankfurt constitution of 28 March 1849 chose the little-German solution and universal male suffrage - but no popular force conquered anything. Prussia's army, funded during the constitutional conflict of 1862-66 against the chamber's refusal, won three wars; the North German Confederation of 1867 and the empire of 1871 were granted from above; and the liberal chamber itself conceded the constitutional point in the Indemnity Bill of 1866. Even the southern states' adherence in 1870 came through a defensive war, not a rising.

For Italy "compromise" is apt but asymmetrical. From below: Mazzini's Young Italy of 1831, the revolutions of 1848-49, Garibaldi's conquest of Sicily and Naples in 1860, and ratification by the plebiscites of March 1860. From above: Cavour's alliance of 1858-59, the monarchy's absorption of the southern conquest at Teano in October 1860, and the extension of Piedmontese institutions from 1861 rather than a negotiated settlement.

The qualification is that the relationship was identical in kind and different in proportion. In both, the movement's legitimacy was transferred to a state and the state's power was lent the movement's justification; what differed was how much force the movement itself supplied - half a peninsula in Italy, nothing in Germany.

Hence the shared outcome: Mazzini's republic displaced, German liberalism accommodated, and both states unified politically before they were unified socially.

*Scoring note: the separator is the refusal to give one verdict for both countries. The directive invites a single judgement, and the mark-earning response grades each case separately before identifying what they nonetheless share, which is the transfer of legitimacy from movement to state.*

## Question 5 - 20 marks, 250 words

> Assess the role of Bismarck in the making of modern Germany. (Answer in 250 words)

### Model answer

Bismarck's role must be assessed functionally, not biographically.

What he did not supply: the national demand, which existed from the 1830s and was formulated at Frankfurt in 1849; the little-German aim, which was Prussian policy by 1850, when the Olmutz agreement forced Prussia to abandon it; the economic base, built by the customs union of 1834; or the army, reorganised with Roon and directed by Moltke.

What he supplied was method. Appointed minister-president in September 1862 amid the army-budget conflict, he governed without the chamber's consent. He narrowed objectives: the duchies in 1864 rather than German unity. He isolated opponents rather than recruiting allies, using Italy in 1866 only to divide Austrian forces. He manufactured pretexts - joint possession after 1864, divided at Gastein in 1865 - and provocations, editing and publishing the Ems telegram on 14 July 1870 to obtain the French declaration of 19 July. He limited aims, excluding Austria at Prague in 1866 without dismembering it. And he used his opponents' ideas, granting a Reichstag elected by universal male suffrage while withholding control of the chancellor, army and foreign policy.

The limits are equally clear. Occasions were contingent - a Danish succession, a Spanish vacancy, a French miscalculation - and outcomes depended on an army he did not build. The 1871 annexation of Alsace-Lorraine broke his own rule and created a permanent enemy.

The verdict: an indispensable method applied to an existing objective, and the author of both the Empire's creation and its most damaging inheritance.

*Scoring note: the separator is the "what he did not supply" paragraph. It pre-empts the charge of great-man history, demonstrates that the structural conditions are understood, and converts the remaining material into a genuine assessment rather than a tribute.*

## Question 6 - 20 marks, 250 words

> How far did the unifications of Italy and Germany transform Europe, and how complete were they within the new states themselves? (Answer in 250 words)

### Model answer

The transformation of Europe was structural. The basis of territorial title shifted from dynastic legitimacy to nationality, a substitution made concrete by the plebiscites of March 1860. A plural central Europe was replaced by a single empire with the continent's strongest army, so no combination could be formed without reference to it. The Prussian conscript army directed by a general staff was adopted by the other powers. Austria, excluded in 1866, reoriented south-eastward with its nationality problem unresolved. France, annexed of Alsace and half of Lorraine in 1871 and charged five billion francs, resented the loss and sought its recovery, and the years to 1914 were an extremely unstable peace - the clearest long causal link the record supplies, though the alliance system and the July crisis belong elsewhere.

Internally, completeness was partial in both. Italy's state arrived before its nation: Piedmontese institutions were extended outward, the south was fought as an insurgency between 1861 and 1865, the papacy rejected the Law of Guarantees of 1871 and discouraged Catholics from voting after 1874, government from 1876 ran by trasformismo, and Trentino and Trieste remained outside. Germany represented its nation without letting it govern: a Reichstag on universal male suffrage, a chancellor answerable only to the Kaiser, and Catholics, socialists, Poles, Danes and Alsatians treated as suspect, with the anti-Catholic campaign of 1871-78 and the anti-socialist laws of 1878-90 both failing.

Both were unified politically before they were unified socially, and Europe was transformed before either nation was made.

*Scoring note: the separator is the joining sentence at the end. Two strong halves scored separately produce a good script; a closing move that connects the external transformation to the internal incompleteness - the system changed faster than the societies did - produces a distinguished one.*

# REMEDIATION

## Error map

| # | The error | Why it costs marks | The correction | Taught in |
|---:|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Writing that both countries "were divided and then unified" | Describes the surface and supplies no mechanism | Italy faced a dominating occupier and Germany a presiding rival; the kind of obstacle determines the method | Lesson 1 |
| 2 | Using "nation" and "state" interchangeably | Makes the transfer of legitimacy from movement to monarchy invisible | Nation = a claim to self-government; state = the organisation holding sovereign authority | Lesson 1 |
| 3 | Claiming either country was "unified" at one stroke | The four levels - territorial, constitutional, economic, social - were reached at different times | Specify which level and which date | Lesson 1 |
| 4 | Assuming German economic union followed political union | The recorded sequence is the reverse | The customs union of 1834 preceded the state | Lessons 1, 3 |
| 5 | Dating Rome's accession to 1861 | The single most penalised factual error in the topic | Kingdom of Italy 1861; Venetia 1866; Rome 1870 | Lessons 2, 9 |
| 6 | Giving Bismarck dates outside the supported set, or reading "1870-90" as an office date | Asserts a date this evidence base does not hold | September 1862; 1 July 1867; 18 January 1871; 21 March 1871; March 1890 | Lessons 2, 11, 16 |
| 7 | Quoting Bismarck on iron and blood | Breaches an explicit recorded control, and no authenticated text is held | Name the occasion and paraphrase as Realpolitik | Lessons 2, 12 |
| 8 | Quoting the "make Italians" phrase as sourced | Its attribution is traditional but disputed | Teach the problem it names - a state ahead of its society | Lessons 2, 10 |
| 9 | Supplying plebiscite percentages | No vote share is treated as reliable here | Analyse the instrument and refuse the number | Lessons 2, 7 |
| 10 | Supplying Italian income, literacy or emigration figures | Breaches the standard account's explicit caution | Keep the north-south comparison qualitative | Lessons 2, 10 |
| 11 | Treating the Risorgimento as one movement with one aim | Loses the three rival programmes and their structural flaws | Republican-unitary, federal-papal, moderate-monarchical | Lesson 3 |
| 12 | Writing that the customs union caused German unification | Confuses a condition with a cause | It made Prussian leadership plausible; a war decided the question | Lessons 3, 19 |
| 13 | Omitting that Austria was outside the customs union | Loses the whole point of citing it | All Germany outside Austria was eventually inside it | Lesson 3 |
| 14 | Writing that 1848 "failed everywhere" without specification | The aims survived; the instruments failed | Name what was defeated: insurrection, assembly, princely war | Lesson 4 |
| 15 | Omitting the Frankfurt Parliament from a German answer | Loses the proof that the liberal route was tried and refused | The crown was offered in April 1849 and refused | Lesson 4 |
| 16 | Saying the Frankfurt assembly was dispersed by force | The decisive event was a refusal it could not contest | It had no army, revenue or administration | Lesson 4 |
| 17 | Omitting Olmutz | Makes 1866 look like a first attempt | Prussia had tried and failed in 1850 | Lessons 4, 11 |
| 18 | Presenting Italian 1848 as purely republican | The decisive war was declared by a monarch | The Statuto of March 1848 survived and became Italy's constitution | Lesson 4 |
| 19 | Describing Cavour only as a diplomat | Omits the reform that made diplomacy credible | Reform, then standing, then alliance, then war | Lesson 5 |
| 20 | Treating the Crimean expedition as a curiosity | It is the mechanism by which standing was acquired | Troops in 1855; the Italian question at Paris in 1856 | Lesson 5 |
| 21 | Describing French help as sympathy | Territorial gains were foreseen for both sides in advance | Plombieres, 21 July 1858; Nice and Savoy, 24 March 1860 | Lesson 6 |
| 22 | Writing that 1859 expelled Austria from Italy | Venetia remained Austrian until 1866 | Villafranca, 11 July 1859, ended the war early | Lesson 6 |
| 23 | Asserting the clauses of the Plombieres agreement | The text is not held in this evidence base | Assert the meeting, date, participants and mutual gains only | Lesson 6 |
| 24 | Reading the plebiscites as measurements of national feeling | The organiser sets the question and the conditions | They legitimised a transfer; they did not measure consciousness | Lesson 7 |
| 25 | Separating the cession of Nice and Savoy from the central annexations | They belong to one month and one settlement | March 1860 contains both | Lesson 7 |
| 26 | Saying the central states were "conquered" | That is exactly the description the instrument existed to avoid | Annexation ratified by popular vote | Lesson 7 |
| 27 | Reducing Teano to a handshake | Loses the political transfer of a conquest | 26 October 1860: control of the south handed to the king | Lesson 8 |
| 28 | Treating the Thousand as officially sponsored | Removes the deniability that made the expedition usable | A volunteer force the state could disown | Lesson 8 |
| 29 | Ignoring French protection of the papacy | Explains neither 1860 nor 1870 | It made Rome untouchable until the protector fell | Lessons 8, 9 |
| 30 | Explaining the handover by Garibaldi's character alone | Substitutes anecdote for analysis | Capability and diplomacy were decisive | Lesson 8 |
| 31 | Writing that Italy defeated Austria in 1866 | Italian forces lost at Custoza and Lissa | The decision was reached in Bohemia; Venetia was ceded for transfer | Lesson 9 |
| 32 | Presenting the entry into Rome as a victory over France | Italy neither fought France nor needed to | It acted after Sedan removed the protector | Lesson 9 |
| 33 | Calling the Law of Guarantees a settlement | The papacy rejected it | A unilateral statute a parliament could repeal | Lesson 9 |
| 34 | Ending the answer at 1870 or 1871 | The commonest structural failure on this topic | Continue past the proclamation to the limits | Lessons 10, 16 |
| 35 | Treating the southern question as geography | Removes the institutional analysis | Institutions extended, taxes and conscription first, then coercion | Lesson 10 |
| 36 | Omitting the campaign against brigandage, 1861-65 | The strongest single evidence of incomplete integration | The state's first sustained operation was against its own new population | Lesson 10 |
| 37 | Omitting the Non Expedit of 1874 | Loses the best evidence that territory is not integration | Catholics discouraged from national elections | Lessons 10, 16 |
| 38 | Treating trasformismo as a quirk | It is the predictable politics of a chamber missing both mass constituencies | Shifting centrist coalitions from 1876 | Lesson 10 |
| 39 | Describing the German Confederation only as "weak" | Misses that it was designed to prevent action | An anti-government that worked | Lesson 11 |
| 40 | Treating Austro-Prussian rivalry as prestige | Misses the structural incompatibility | A national state and a multinational empire cannot coexist in one Germany | Lesson 11 |
| 41 | Giving Bismarck's appointment without the budget conflict | Makes it look like a court appointment | He was appointed to govern without the chamber | Lesson 11 |
| 42 | Writing that Bismarck created German nationalism | Breaches an explicit recorded trap | He used it instrumentally under Prussian leadership | Lesson 12 |
| 43 | Treating Realpolitik as cynicism or ideology | It is a decision procedure prescribing no end-state | Practical state interest rather than abstract principle | Lesson 12 |
| 44 | Attributing 1862-71 to a single master plan | The evidence establishes planning for 1866, not for the sequence | Deliberate objectives, contingent occasions | Lessons 12, 19 |
| 45 | Writing that Prussia fought Denmark alone in 1864 | Destroys the entire mechanism | Prussia and Austria seized the duchies jointly | Lesson 13 |
| 46 | Omitting Gastein | Leaves the 1866 quarrel unexplained | August 1865: Schleswig to Prussia, Holstein to Austria | Lesson 13 |
| 47 | Saying Prussia annexed the duchies in 1864 | The assignment came with the Treaty of Prague in 1866 | Joint possession first, assignment second | Lesson 13 |
| 48 | Writing that Prussia dismembered Austria in 1866 | Contradicts the settlement | Excluded, not partitioned; no territory lost to Prussia | Lesson 14 |
| 49 | Omitting the Italian alliance of 1866 | Loses the division of Austrian forces | Italy's role was to make Austria fight on two fronts | Lesson 14 |
| 50 | Forgetting that German states fought against Prussia | Disproves any simple story of German enthusiasm | Bavaria, Saxony, Hanover and others sided with Austria | Lesson 14 |
| 51 | Jumping from 1866 to 1871 | Loses the state in which the German constitution was invented | The North German Confederation, in force 1 July 1867 | Lesson 14 |
| 52 | Ignoring the Indemnity Bill | Loses the moment the constitutional question was settled | The chamber retrospectively approved unauthorised expenditure | Lesson 14 |
| 53 | Writing that Prussia declared war on France | The design depended on the opposite | France declared war on 19 July 1870 | Lesson 15 |
| 54 | Calling the Ems telegram a forgery | The record says edited and published | An edited account released to provoke a declaration | Lesson 15 |
| 55 | Omitting the southern states' adherence | It was the war's political object | They regarded France as the aggressor and joined Prussia | Lesson 15 |
| 56 | Treating Sedan as the end of the war | The siege of Paris ran to 28 January 1871 | A republic continued the war after the emperor fell | Lesson 15 |
| 57 | Confusing 18 January 1871 with 10 May 1871 | Proclamation and treaty are different events | Versailles proclamation; Treaty of Frankfurt | Lesson 15 |
| 58 | Calling the Empire simply undemocratic or simply constitutional | Breaches an explicit recorded instruction | It was both | Lesson 16 |
| 59 | Inferring liberalism from universal male suffrage | Franchise and executive control are separate variables | A parliament that could not remove a government | Lesson 16 |
| 60 | Confusing the imperial and Prussian franchises | They were different | The Prussian state franchise remained weighted by wealth | Lesson 16 |
| 61 | Presenting the anti-Catholic and anti-socialist campaigns as successes | Both strengthened their targets | The Centre Party grew; the socialists emerged stronger after 1890 | Lesson 16 |
| 62 | Writing two parallel narratives in a comparison | Leaves the comparison to the examiner | Run one question through both cases, axis by axis | Lesson 17 |
| 63 | Giving one from-above-or-below verdict for both countries | Breaches an explicit recorded warning | Italy mixed; Germany overwhelmingly state-led | Lesson 17 |
| 64 | Claiming German unification caused the First World War | Confuses conditions with causes | A structure within which later choices were made | Lessons 18, 19 |
| 65 | Narrating the alliance system or the July crisis | Trespasses on another owner | Hand over in one clause | Lessons 18, 20 |
| 66 | Omitting Italy from a consequences answer | Half the topic disappears | One nationality struggle completed; a middling power added | Lesson 18 |
| 67 | Naming historians from recollection | An invented attribution is a factual error in the most visible sentence | State the content of the position instead | Lesson 19 |
| 68 | Using counterfactuals as predictions | Speculation rather than causal analysis | Anchor them to documented decisions and test necessity | Lesson 19 |
| 69 | Writing that unification was inevitable | Contradicted by 1848-49, by Olmutz and by four decades of nationalism without unification | Treat inevitability as the thing to be disproved | Lesson 19 |
| 70 | Defining nationalism at length | Belongs to the overview owner | Use nationalism as a historical force and move on | Lesson 20 |
| 71 | Omitting the limits paragraph under time pressure | It is the highest-value paragraph available | Budget 15-40 words for it before writing | Lesson 20 |
| 72 | Closing with a summary instead of a verdict | A verdict decides and concedes; a summary repeats | If it could be written before the body, it is a summary | Lesson 20 |

## Corrective drills

Attempt each in two or three sentences, from memory, before checking the lesson named in the error map.

### Drill 1 - the two operations

State the difference between consolidation and liberation, and say which country had to perform both and why.

### Drill 2 - nation, state, nation-state

Define the three terms in one sentence each, and use them to describe Piedmont-Sardinia in 1858.

### Drill 3 - the four levels

Name the four levels at which "unified" is claimed, and say which level Germany reached first and which Italy reached last.

### Drill 4 - the settlement that armed its enemies

Explain in two sentences how the Vienna settlement created the two states that dismantled it.

### Drill 5 - ten markers

Write the ten dated markers from 1815 to January 1871 and attach one function to each.

### Drill 6 - the four controls

State the four factual-risk controls on Rome, Mazzini, Austria and popular assemblies, and give the correct formulation for each.

### Drill 7 - the three programmes

Name the three Italian programmes, their agents of change, and the structural flaw of each.

### Drill 8 - what Young Italy added

State three things Young Italy did that a conspiratorial society of the 1820s did not.

### Drill 9 - the customs union in one paragraph

Give the year, the leading state, the excluded great power, and the three political effects - without claiming it caused unification.

### Drill 10 - the refusal of April 1849

State the three components of the refusal of the imperial crown and the consequence of each for the later route.

### Drill 11 - little and great Germany

Define both options and say which the settlement of 1866-71 adopted and why the other was impossible.

### Drill 12 - what survived 1848

Name three things that survived the failure and reappeared in the successful phase.

### Drill 13 - the five requirements

List the five requirements of a core state and say which four Prussia already met.

### Drill 14 - buying standing

Explain in three sentences how a war in the Crimea produced an alliance against Austria.

### Drill 15 - the price of an ally

State what was gained in 1859, what was not, and what was paid in March 1860.

### Drill 16 - why allies stop

Give four reasons a great power halts a successful war, and apply one to Villafranca.

### Drill 17 - what a plebiscite proves

State one claim a plebiscite supports, one it supports only with qualification, and one it cannot support.

### Drill 18 - the boomerang

Explain how popular consent as a ground of territorial title later worked against the states that used it.

### Drill 19 - deniability

State the four properties of a volunteer force and the hazard the fourth creates.

### Drill 20 - why Teano happened

Give the two decisive reasons the conquest was transferred, and rank them against the two supporting ones.

### Drill 21 - the 1861 settlement

Name the four elements of the settlement of March 1861 and the consequence of the constitutional one.

### Drill 22 - two acquisitions, neither won

Explain in three sentences how Italy obtained Venetia and Rome without winning either.

### Drill 23 - the Roman question

State the papal position after 1870, the Italian statutory response of 1871, why it was refused, and the electoral consequence of 1874.

### Drill 24 - the southern mechanism

Set out the four steps by which unification converted a development gap into a political problem.

### Drill 25 - trasformismo derived

Explain why a parliament missing both natural mass constituencies produces coalition management rather than alternation.

### Drill 26 - unredeemed lands

Define italia irredenta and explain why a nationality criterion of completeness can never be satisfied.

### Drill 27 - dualism

State in four rows why Austria and Prussia wanted incompatible things from the same confederation.

### Drill 28 - the budget conflict

Explain what was actually at stake in the Prussian constitutional conflict, and why it required a particular kind of minister.

### Drill 29 - five rules

List the five operating rules of Realpolitik and attach one dated example to each.

### Drill 30 - the suffrage paradox

Explain why granting universal male suffrage was a conservative move.

### Drill 31 - a war to obtain a quarrel

Explain in three sentences why Prussia fought the 1864 war jointly with Austria.

### Drill 32 - what Prague refused

Name what the 1866 settlement took and what it deliberately declined to take, and explain the reasoning.

### Drill 33 - the indemnity of 1866

State what the Indemnity Bill did and what it settled about the German constitution.

### Drill 34 - obtaining a declaration

Reconstruct the sequence from the Spanish vacancy to the French declaration of 19 July 1870 in four steps.

### Drill 35 - 1866 against 1871

Contrast the two settlements in two sentences and state the general proposition about peace terms.

### Drill 36 - the architecture of 1871

Name the four institutions of the Empire and state, for each, what it could and could not do.

### Drill 37 - who belonged

Name the four groups the German Empire treated as doubtful and the outcome of the measures against two of them.

### Drill 38 - seven axes

Reproduce the seven comparative axes with one dated item attached to each.

### Drill 39 - the structural legacy

State the four components of the European legacy without asserting that unification caused the war of 1914.

### Drill 40 - planner or opportunist

Distinguish what was deliberate from what was contingent, in three pairs.

### Drill 41 - permitted counterfactuals

Give two counterfactuals an answer may use and two it may not, with the reason in each case.

### Drill 42 - four moves

State the four moves of an answer on this topic and the word budget for each at 250 words.
