## The seven comparative axes, in one table

| Axis | Italy | Germany | What the difference proves |
|---|---|---|---|
| Core state | Piedmont-Sardinia, strengthened by Genoa | Prussia, strengthened by the Rhineland, Westphalia and Saxon lands | Both nuclei were created by the settlement they overthrew |
| Strategist | Cavour: diplomacy plus war, with French help in 1859 | Bismarck: minister-president from 1862; three wars | Italy needed an external ally; Prussia did not |
| Popular-military element | Garibaldi's Red Shirts take Sicily and Naples, 1860 | No equivalent mass military hero | The single sharpest contrast in the topic |
| Ideological founder | Mazzini's Young Italy, 1831, republican and unitary | No single equivalent; Frankfurt liberalism failed in 1849 | Italy's republican tradition was defeated within unification |
| Defeat of Austria | 1859 with France; Venetia gained 1866 | Seven Weeks' War, 1866 | Austria's exclusion is the common precondition |
| Completion | 1861, 1866, 1870 | January 1871 | Staggered against concentrated |
| Resulting power | A middling power with weak state capacity | The strongest continental military-economic power | Feeds directly into the crisis of 1914 |

## Limits of the two unifications, side by side

| Dimension | Italy | Germany |
|---|---|---|
| Constitutional form | A constitutional monarchy; the Piedmontese order extended outward from 1861 | A federal empire; constitutions written in 1867 and 1871 |
| Franchise | Restricted, and narrowed further by the withdrawal of Catholics after 1874 | Universal male suffrage for the national parliament |
| Executive control | Parliamentary in form, hollowed by trasformismo from 1876 | Not parliamentary: the chancellor answered to the Kaiser |
| The excluded | The south; practising Catholics; republicans | Catholics; socialists; Poles; Danes; Alsatians |
| Instrument used against them | Counter-insurgency, 1861-65, then political management | Legal prohibition: 1871-78 and 1878-90 |
| Result of the instrument | Resentment institutionalised as the southern question | The targeted movements emerged stronger |
| Territory still claimed outside | Trentino and Trieste | Austria's Germans, excluded by the small-German form |
| Overall | A weak state with shallow integration | A strong state with contested integration |

## The two settlements: 1866 against 1871

| Feature | Treaty of Prague, 23 August 1866 | Treaty of Frankfurt, 10 May 1871 |
|---|---|---|
| Objective of the war | Exclude Austria from Germany | Bring the southern states in; defeat France |
| Territory taken from the defeated great power | None by Prussia; Venetia ceded for transfer to Italy | Alsace and half of Lorraine, with Metz |
| Financial burden | None recorded | Five billion francs, plus occupation costs |
| Effect on the defeated power's future posture | Able to accept the outcome and later to align | Permanently aggrieved; recovery became a national objective |
| Rule of the method | Observed: take what the objective requires | Broken: take what is wanted |
| Consequence for Europe | A settled northern question | An extremely unstable peace, 1871-1914 |

## Argument map: from above or from below?

```text
   CLAIM: unification was achieved from below, by national movements
        EVIDENCE: Mazzini's Young Italy, 1831 | the risings of 1848-49 |
        Garibaldi's conquest of Sicily and Naples, 1860 | the plebiscites
        of March 1860 | south German feeling rallying to Prussia, 1870
                              |
   COUNTER-CLAIM: unification was achieved from above, by states
        EVIDENCE: Cavour's alliance, 1858-59 | the monarchy's absorption
        of the southern conquest, 1861 | Prussia's three wars |
        the constitutions of 1867 and 1871, granted not negotiated |
        the Indemnity Bill of 1866
                              |
   THE MISTAKE BOTH MAKE: treating the two as alternatives
                              |
   RESOLUTION: nationalism was generated from below and executed from
   above; movements supplied legitimacy, states supplied means, and
   neither could have succeeded alone
                              |
   THE DIFFERENTIATION THAT EARNS THE MARKS:
   Italy - genuinely mixed; the movement supplied half a peninsula
   Germany - overwhelmingly state-led; the movement supplied no force
                              |
   SHARED RESIDUE: in both, the movement did not govern the state it
   had legitimised - Mazzini's republic displaced; German liberalism
   conceding executive control in exchange for unity
```

## Argument map: planner or opportunist?

```text
   DESIGN                                    CONTINGENCY
   the objective: Prussian leadership        the Danish succession dispute
     of a Germany excluding Austria,         the Spanish throne falling vacant
     policy since 1850                       the French demand for a guarantee
   the instrument: an army rebuilt and       the French decision to declare war
     funded against a chamber's refusal      the outcome of battles
   the method: narrow the aim, isolate,      the timing of every opening
     provoke, limit, stop
   the record: "a carefully planned stage"
     for 1866; the telegram edited to
     provoke a declaration in 1870
                    \                       /
                     \                     /
                      RESOLUTION: separate OBJECTIVE from OCCASION
                      deliberate aims, deliberate instrument,
                      deliberate method - opportunistically timed
                                  |
                      WHAT MAY NOT BE CLAIMED: a single master plan
                      fixed in 1862 for all three wars
```

## Argument map: did the nation make the state, or the state the nation?

```text
   NATION FIRST                              STATE FIRST
   shared language and literature            "making Italians" as a task
   a movement organised from 1831            announced after 1861
   a constitution drafted in 1849            the southern insurgency, 1861-65
   plebiscite majorities in 1860             Catholics withdrawn from elections, 1874
                                             campaigns against Catholics and
                                             socialists in Germany, 1871-90
                    \                       /
                     \                     /
                      WHAT WOULD SETTLE IT: evidence about the distribution
                      of popular national consciousness - NOT HELD HERE
                                  |
                      THEREFORE: report the debate, show the evidence on
                      each side, and decline to decide. An accurate
                      report of an open question outscores a confident
                      resolution of it on no evidence.
```

## Answer-writing spine

```text
   STEP 1  READ THE DIRECTIVE, NOT THE TOPIC
           compare / from above or below / role of X / ideas or state power /
           how did nationalism reshape the map / limits
                              |
   STEP 2  WRITE THE THESIS FIRST (20-30 words)
           it must answer the directive, announce the structure and
           pre-concede the obvious counter-position
                              |
   STEP 3  BUILD THE BODY BY AXES OR STAGES, NEVER BY COUNTRY
           attach ONE dated item to each axis:
           1831 | 1834 | Apr 1849 | 1856 | Jul 1858 | 1859 | Mar 1860 |
           May 1860 | Oct 1860 | Mar 1861 | Sept 1862 | 1864 | Aug 1865 |
           Jul 1866 | Aug 1866 | Jul 1867 | Jul 1870 | Sept 1870 | Jan 1871 |
           May 1871 | 1874 | 1878-90
                              |
   STEP 4  WRITE THE LIMITS PARAGRAPH - the one most candidates omit
           Italy: institutions extended; brigandage 1861-65; the papacy;
                  trasformismo; unredeemed lands
           Germany: a parliament without control; Catholics, socialists,
                  Poles, Danes, Alsatians
                              |
   STEP 5  CLOSE WITH A QUALIFIED VERDICT
           decide something AND concede something in the same sentence
                              |
   CONTROLS TO CHECK WHILE WRITING
   Rome 1870 not 1861 | Mazzini inspired, did not complete | Prussia led,
   not Austria | power and war decided, not assemblies | Bismarck: 1862 and
   January 1871 only | no Bismarck quotation | no figures
```

# COMPLETE CONSOLIDATED REGISTER NOTES

## 1. What "unification" names

- Two operations hidden in one word: **consolidation**, merging sovereignties, obstacle internal; **liberation**, removing foreign rule or veto, obstacle external.
- ✅ Italy after 1815: divided among Austrian-controlled or Austrian-influenced states, the Papal States and Sardinia-Piedmont, with Austria dominating Lombardy-Venetia - both operations needed, the external one first.
- ✅ Germany after 1815: a loose confederation of thirty-nine states under Austrian influence - no occupation, but a presiding rival; the task was **exclusion**, not expulsion.
- Four levels of the claim: territorial, constitutional, economic, social. ⚠️ Germany reached economic union first, Italy last.
- ✅ Owner's comparison: obstacle - Austrian domination plus fragmentation against Austrian rivalry plus fragmentation; key state - Piedmont-Sardinia against Prussia; mass leader - Garibaldi, with no German equivalent; strategist - Cavour against Bismarck; character - mixed against heavily state-driven and military.

## 2. The starting map and the dating controls

- ✅ Vienna strengthened **Sardinia-Piedmont with Genoa** and **Prussia with the Rhineland, Westphalia and part of Saxony**; ⚠️ both nuclei were created by the settlement they overthrew.
- ✅ Ten recorded markers: 1815, 1831, 1848, 1859, 1860, 1861, 1864, 1866, 1870, January 1871.
- ✅ Spans: Italy **1859-1871**, core phase 1859-61; Germany **1864-1871**.
- ✅ Four controls: Rome **1870** not 1861; Mazzini inspired and did not complete; **Prussia** led, not Austria; **power and war** decided, not assemblies.
- ✅ Bismarck's supported dates: minister-president from **September 1862**; chancellor of the North German Confederation from **1 July 1867**; empire proclaimed **18 January 1871 at Versailles**; imperial chancellor from **21 March 1871**; dismissed **March 1890**. ⚠️ Assert no Bismarck date outside that set, and do not quote him; use **Realpolitik**.
- ⚠️ No plebiscite percentage, population, income, literacy or output figure anywhere; force figures only for 1870-71.

## 3. Materials before 1848

- ✅ **Risorgimento** = the movement for Italian unification; ⚠️ the word claims a resurgence, converting innovation into restoration.
- ✅ **Mazzini** founded **Young Italy** (owner timeline: **1831**), arguing for a united **republican** Italy; classified as the movement's ideological inspiration. ✅ The historiographical companion names Mazzini, secret societies and Garibaldi as the channels of mobilisation from below.
- ⚠️ Three programmes and their flaws: republican-unitary (no army); federal-papal (the Pope would have to fight Catholic Austria); moderate-monarchical (needs a great-power ally - the only **repairable** flaw).
- ⚠️ Young Italy's three innovations: a fixed end-state; propaganda as a weapon equal to conspiracy; recruitment by generation.
- ✅ **Zollverein, 1834**, under Prussian leadership, **eighteen states** at foundation; preceded by the Prussian tariff law of **1818** and the southern and central unions of **1828**; accessions in **1854**, **1867** and **1888**; **all Germany outside Austria** eventually included; **Friedrich List** its most active advocate.
- ⚠️ Four effects: a little-German economic map; a peacetime instrument of Prussian leadership; commercial interests converted into national ones; politics later completing the sequence. ⚠️ It made Prussian leadership plausible; it did not decide the question.

## 4. 1848-49 and the lesson drawn

- ✅ Italy: Palermo revolt January 1848; **Statuto Albertino** March 1848, which survived and became Italy's constitution; Charles Albert's war; defeat at **Custoza** July 1848; **Roman Republic** February 1849 with **Mazzini** in the triumvirate and **Garibaldi** organising its defence; defeat at **Novara** March 1849 and the accession of **Victor Emmanuel II**; French intervention crushing the Roman Republic by July 1849 and restoring **Pope Pius IX**; Venice surrendering August 1849.
- ✅ Germany: the **Frankfurt National Assembly** from May 1848; constitution adopted **28 March 1849** with a hereditary emperor and a parliament on universal male suffrage; the crown offered to **Frederick William IV** in **April 1849** and **refused**; **Olmutz, November 1850**, abandoning Prussia's own union scheme.
- ⚠️ Little-German = Prussia-led, excluding Austria; great-German = including Austria's German lands. ✅ The 1866 settlement was small-German in form.
- ✅ The lesson: the failure pushed unification away from liberal idealism toward **statecraft, diplomacy and war**; idealist nationalism survived while power passed to monarchies and ministers.
- ⚠️ Four operational lessons: legitimacy does not move troops; the national question is a European question; a state with an army beats a coalition of movements; aims survive while methods change.
- ⚠️ Three carry-overs: Garibaldi's personnel; the Statuto's constitutionalism; universal male suffrage reappearing in 1867 and 1871.

## 5. Piedmont converted into an instrument

- ✅ **Cavour**, prime minister of Piedmont-Sardinia, political strategist of unification; method = **diplomacy and war against Austria**; ✅ Piedmont used **diplomacy, reform and war with French help in 1859**; ✅ unification required a **functioning state**; ✅ Piedmont-Sardinia became the **nucleus**.
- ⚠️ Five requirements of a core state: survive defeat with institutions intact; pay for a war; attract the movement's talent; be treated as a partner; obtain an ally.
- ✅ **1855**: troops to the **Crimean War**; ✅ **1856**: the **Italian question** raised at the **Congress of Paris**.
- ⚠️ What it bought: standing rather than sympathy; a reframing from revolution to misgovernment; the possibility of an alliance.
- ⚠️ Three-actor division of labour: the king supplies legitimacy and a throne; the minister supplies finance and diplomacy; the movement supplies **deniable** force.

## 6. The alliance, the war of 1859 and its price

- ✅ **21 July 1858, Plombieres**: a secret meeting between Cavour and Napoleon III plans the alliance, with gains foreseen for **both** sides.
- ✅ **1859**: Piedmont-Sardinia and France defeat Austria; **Lombardy** gained; unification moves from ideas to state action.
- ✅ **11 July 1859, Villafranca**: armistice; **Venetia not obtained**, joining only in 1866.
- ✅ **24 March 1860, Treaty of Turin**: **Nice and Savoy** ceded to France - ⚠️ Italian-speaking territory, and the strongest evidence against a purely national reading.
- ⚠️ Four reasons a great power halts a successful war: rising costs; alarm among others; domestic objection; the risk of creating a competitor.
- ⚠️ The general rule: assistance continues only up to the point at which it begins to create a rival.

## 7. The plebiscite instrument

- ✅ **March 1860**: plebiscites in **Tuscany, Parma, Modena and Romagna** approve annexation to Piedmont-Sardinia - the same month as the cession to France.
- ⚠️ Four functions: legitimation; substitution of popular consent for dynastic right; appropriation of the republicans' own principle; closure against reopening.
- ⚠️ Three levels of claim: formal approval, defensible; preference among available alternatives, probable but qualified; measured national consciousness, not established.
- ⚠️ The boomerang: popular consent as a ground of title arms later claimants - the Italian south, ✅ Alsace-Lorraine, and the nationalities of the multinational empires.

## 8. Garibaldi, the south, and the settlement of 1861

- ✅ **11 May 1860**: the **Thousand** land at **Marsala**; ✅ the **Red Shirts** conquer **Sicily and Naples**, bringing the south into the process.
- ✅ **26 October 1860, Teano**: Garibaldi meets **Victor Emmanuel II** and hands over the conquered south.
- ✅ **17 March 1861**: the **Kingdom of Italy** proclaimed; ✅ Venetia and Rome remain outside.
- ⚠️ Volunteer force = deniable, cheap, differently legitimate, fast - and unbrakeable; the crisis point was the French-protected papal state.
- ⚠️ Four explanations for the transfer, ranked: capability and diplomacy decisive; national priority as justification; character as supplement.
- ✅ The recorded limit: Mazzini's republic displaced by a monarchy; the mass movement's programme was not what was built.

## 9. Venetia, Rome and the Roman question

- ✅ **1866**: Italy allied to Prussia; defeats at **Custoza** and **Lissa**; ✅ **Peace of Vienna, 3 October 1866**: Austria cedes **Venetia for transfer**.
- ✅ **1870**: Rome joins Italy **after French withdrawal**; ✅ the papal territories were annexed on **20 September 1870**, Italian troops entering through the **Porta Pia**, after the French defeat at **Sedan on 1-2 September**.
- ✅ The papacy was **hostile to the Italian government from 1870**; ✅ the **Law of Guarantees of 1871** was unilateral and rejected; ✅ the **Non Expedit of 1874** discouraged Catholics from national elections; ✅ the quarrel ended only with the **Lateran Treaty of 1929** - fifty-nine years, and owned by a later topic.
- ⚠️ Both final acquisitions were by-products of other powers' wars; Italy's completion is a consequence of German unification.

## 10. What Italy got, and what it did not

- ✅ The unification of Italy was **only completed in 1870**, and the new state **suffered from economic and political weaknesses**; ✅ Italy was unified but not fully homogenised, and political unification did not mean social integration.
- ✅ Three recorded limits: the **north-south divide**; the **church problem**; the **republican defeat**. ✅ Keep the southern comparison qualitative.
- ⚠️ The southern mechanism: institutions extended rather than negotiated; taxes and conscription first; a weaker economy exposed to one market; coercion when resistance followed.
- ✅ **Brigandage campaign, 1861-65**, against discontent with a northern-dominated government, conscription and economic measures unfavourable to peasants.
- ✅ **Trasformismo from 1876 under Depretis**: shifting centrist coalitions built by co-opting opponents; stability with corruption and cynicism. ✅ Industrialisation after unification produced a strong socialist party and trade unions - a constituency outside the system.
- ✅ **Italia irredenta**: Trentino and Trieste under Austrian rule; a nationalist rallying cry contributing to Italy's entry into the First World War.
- 📰 Reported current linkage: Italy's 2024 differentiated-autonomy law was partly struck down by Constitutional Court judgment 192 of 2024, the framework upheld and the dispute continuing - the institutional question of 1861 remains open. No historical claim depends on it.

## 11. The German Confederation and the road to 1862

- ✅ **Thirty-nine states under Austrian dominance**, explaining the scale of the German problem; ⚠️ designed to prevent reorganisation and therefore incapable of carrying one out.
- ⚠️ Dualism: what Germany is for; whether it should be tightened; who its people are; what follows if the question is forced. ⚠️ A German national state and a multinational Habsburg empire cannot coexist in one Germany.
- ✅ **Olmutz, 1850**: the little-German aim predates Bismarck; what changed afterwards was capability.
- ✅ Prussia met four of the five core-state requirements already and **did not need an ally**; ✅ Prussia prepared methodically after 1850, with **Moltke** as strategist and **Roon** as army organiser.
- ✅ **September 1862**: Bismarck appointed minister-president during the **constitutional conflict over the army budget**; ⚠️ the stake was whether a parliament could stop an executive, and the army that unified Germany was funded without parliamentary consent.

## 12. Realpolitik as a method

- ✅ **Realpolitik** = politics guided by **practical state interest rather than abstract principle**; ✅ use it to explain why Bismarck could **ally with liberal nationalism without sharing its aims**.
- ✅ Trap: Bismarck **did not create nationalism**; he used it instrumentally under Prussian leadership.
- ⚠️ Five rules: narrow the objective; isolate before fighting; appear defensive; limit the aim and stop; use the opponent's ideas.
- ⚠️ Franchise and executive power are separate variables; a regime may widen the first to retain the second.
- ⚠️ Four limits of the method: it does not create the demand, win battles, remove contingency or prove a master plan.

## 13. The three wars

- ✅ **1864**: Denmark defeated by **Prussia and Austria jointly**; the duchies held **jointly**; ✅ **Gastein, August 1865**: Schleswig administered by Prussia, Holstein by Austria; ✅ the dispute over that administration was the **pretext** for 1866.
- ✅ **1866**: Prussia against Austria, Bavaria, Saxony, Hanover and others, with **Italy allied to Prussia** dividing Austria's forces; decided in **Bohemia**, most decisively at **Koniggratz, 3 July**; ✅ **Treaty of Prague, 23 August**: Austria **excluded, not dismembered**; Schleswig-Holstein to Prussia; **Hanover, Hesse-Kassel, Nassau and Frankfurt annexed**, making Prussia contiguous.
- ✅ **1 July 1867**: the **North German Confederation** constitution in force - a federal state under Prussia with a Reichstag on universal male suffrage; the southern states outside. ✅ **Indemnity Bill, 1866**: the chamber retrospectively approves unauthorised expenditure.
- ✅ **1870-71**: the **Hohenzollern-Sigmaringen candidature** for the Spanish throne, vacant since **1868**, accepted in June 1870 and withdrawn under French pressure; **William I** declines a guarantee; the **Ems telegram** edited and published on **14 July 1870** to provoke a declaration; **France declares war on 19 July**; Bavaria, Wurttemberg and Baden side with Prussia.
- ✅ **380,000** German troops forward in eighteen days; **Worth**, 6 August; **Mars-la-Tour and Gravelotte**, 16 and 18 August; **Sedan**, **1-2 September 1870** with the encirclement completed on 31 August - 17,000 French losses, about 104,000 prisoners including the emperor and Mac-Mahon, German losses 460 officers and 8,500 men; the **Third Republic** proclaimed 4 September; the **siege of Paris** from 19 September; **Metz** capitulating 27 October with 140,000; **Paris surrendering 28 January 1871**.
- ✅ **18 January 1871**: **William I proclaimed German emperor at Versailles**; ✅ **Treaty of Frankfurt, 10 May 1871**: **Alsace and half of Lorraine with Metz**, and an indemnity of **five billion francs** plus occupation costs.

## 14. The constitution of 1871 and the question of belonging

- ✅ Kaiser = King of Prussia, commanding the army and directing foreign policy; **Chancellor** appointed by the Kaiser and presiding over the **Bundesrat**; **Reichstag** elected by **universal male suffrage**; federal, with Prussia preponderant.
- ✅ The Empire **combined a modern parliament with Prussian dominance and monarchical control of the army and foreign policy**; ✅ it must not be called simply undemocratic or simply constitutional - **it was both**; ✅ it unified under an **authoritarian-monarchical** structure, not the liberal hopes of 1848.
- ✅ **Bismarck: chancellor of the North German Confederation from 1 July 1867 and imperial chancellor from 21 March 1871 to 1890**, in a system in which the **Reichstag had much less power**; ⚠️ the core source's "1870-90" is a shorthand for that tenure, not an office date; ✅ **dismissed by Wilhelm II in March 1890** - by the emperor alone.
- ✅ The Empire contained **Polish, Danish and French-speaking** populations and treated **Catholics and later socialists** as suspect national communities; ✅ **Kulturkampf 1871-78** against the Catholic Church and the **Centre Party**, which grew as a result and saw the laws largely repealed; ✅ **Anti-Socialist Laws 1878-90**, after which the movement emerged stronger.
- ⚠️ The central proposition: a nation-state created by state power rather than consent cannot afterwards manufacture the consent it dispensed with.

## 15. The comparison

- ✅ A "compare" prompt tests **difference in agency and method**, not two narratives; ✅ never give one from-above-or-below verdict for both countries.
- Seven axes: core state; strategist; popular-military element; ideological founder; defeat of Austria; completion; resulting power.
- ✅ Italy: energy from below, completion from above. ✅ Germany: overwhelmingly state-led.
- ⚠️ Economy as condition, wars as cause; the weighting is unmeasurable from this evidence.
- ⚠️ Popular agency was indispensable for legitimacy and, in Italy, for force; elite agency was decisive for outcome; the settlement excluded much of the population invoked.

## 16. Consequences for Europe

- ✅ Nationalism **replaced a settlement based on dynastic legitimacy with one based on national states**, creating both the **Alsace-Lorraine grievance** and the **Habsburg nationality crisis**.
- ✅ Italy **completed one nationality struggle**; Germany **created a major new continental power**.
- ✅ The victory of 1870-71 consolidated faith in **Prussian militarism**; ✅ the **conscript army directed by a general staff** was adopted by the other great powers.
- ✅ **French revanchism** over Alsace-Lorraine is a named source of friction in 1914 and the **clearest long causal link** in the folder; ✅ the years **1871-1914** were an **extremely unstable peace**.
- ⚠️ Three structural consequences: a new hegemon in the centre; the diffusion of a military model; the devaluation of the congress method.
- ✅ Four boundaries: the Vienna settlement; nationalism as a doctrine; the world in 1914; interwar Italy and the Austro-German question.

## 17. The debates

- ✅ Positions are recorded without attributions; ✅ invented citations are forbidden. ✅ The one named attribution held here is a historian's argument that later German policy continued that of earlier rulers **including Bismarck**, differing in ruthlessness of method - a controversial position in a debate owned elsewhere.
- ⚠️ Planner or opportunist: deliberate objective, instrument and method; contingent occasions, outcomes and timing.
- ⚠️ Continuity or rupture: an inherited **structure** is not an inherited **intention**; ✅ in 1932 conservatives wished to return to the Bismarckian system in which the Reichstag had much less power.
- ⚠️ Nation before state: not settleable here; no measurement of popular consciousness is held.
- ⚠️ Permitted counterfactuals: the refused crown of April 1849; the indispensability of the French alliance; the 1866-against-1871 contrast. Forbidden: Bismarck's personal indispensability; reversed plebiscites; inevitability.

## 18. Answer architecture

- ✅ Syllabus clause: **redrawal of national boundaries** - both unifications fit it directly; nationalism as a doctrine belongs to the overview owner.
- ✅ Six directives and their demands: compare (agency and method); from above or below (case by case); role of a named figure (function, not biography); ideas or state power (necessary but insufficient); reshaping the map (boundary consequences and who was excluded); limits (do not end at 1871).
- ✅ Three thesis templates and three verdict scaffolds, at 10, 15 and 20 marks with the standard account's mark-scaled structures.
- ⚠️ Four moves: thesis; axes with dated evidence; limits; qualified verdict.

## 20. Prohibited formulations

- ❌ Rome joined Italy in 1861. ❌ Mazzini completed unification. ❌ Austria led German unification. ❌ Germany was unified by popular assemblies.
- ❌ Any Bismarck quotation; ❌ any Bismarck date outside the supported set - September 1862, 1 July 1867, 18 January 1871, 21 March 1871 and March 1890; ❌ "Bismarck was chancellor 1870-90" given as an office date rather than as a source shorthand.
- ❌ The "make Italians" phrase presented as a sourced quotation from a named statesman.
- ❌ Any plebiscite percentage; ❌ any Italian income, literacy, emigration, population or output figure; ❌ any force or casualty figure outside the verified 1870-71 set.
- ❌ Any clause of the Plombieres agreement.
- ❌ Any named historian other than the single continuity attribution held in the core source.
- ❌ "The customs union unified Germany." ❌ "German unification caused the First World War." ❌ "Unification was inevitable."
- ❌ Detailed provisions of the campaigns against the Catholic Church or the socialists beyond what is recorded here; ❌ the Italian south's standard label treated as a sourced fact of this folder.

## Construction record for this edition

| Item | Status |
|---|---|
| Lesson count | Derived from the topic's own dependency chain and both owners rather than from a template; twenty lessons, with the Italian sequence completed before the German sequence begins so that the second may be read as a controlled comparison |
| Compiled four-option MCQ corpus | Not constructed, in accordance with the concept-check rule for this generation; one answer-free concept check, model answer and misconception note per lesson, plus ten cumulative checks across five blocks and forty-two corrective drills |
| Workbook operations | None performed; no MCQ corpus was created, copied or relocated, and no solved-PYQ workbook was generated or migrated |
| Solved PYQ model answers | Not included; the topic owns no verified question, and the one objective item that touches its names is displayed answer-neutrally as a boundary marker with no key asserted or inferred |
| Bounded History research rule | Applied. The substantive base is the complete basic owner, the complete advanced owner and one core OCR history source. External work was confined to exact PYQ verification and routing, one bounded material-fact verification pass for standard essentials absent from the owners, and one current linkage. Exactly one linkage is carried, in Lesson 10; the other nineteen checklists record truthfully that no current affairs were sought. No per-lesson searches and no optional corroboration were performed |
| Bounded material-fact pass | One pass, conducted because the two owners supply a ten-marker timeline and no dates for the standard connecting events - the Crimean intervention, the Congress of Paris, Plombieres, Villafranca, the cession of Nice and Savoy, the central-duchy plebiscites, Marsala, Teano, the proclamation, the Law of Guarantees, the Non Expedit, the Zollverein, the Frankfurt crown, Olmutz, Bismarck's appointment month, Gastein, Koniggratz, Prague, the transfer of Venetia, the North German Confederation constitution, the Ems telegram, the French declaration, Sedan, the proclamation of 18 January 1871, the Treaty of Frankfurt, the 1848-49 Italian sequence, the post-1871 German measures and the post-1861 Italian ones. Every item verified in that pass is labelled as such in the lesson that uses it and is listed in the source ledger |
| Material disputed fact or attribution | Two handled openly rather than silently resolved. The phrase about having made Italy and needing to make Italians is recorded as traditionally but disputably attributed, with no secure verbatim basis in the principal memoir of the statesman usually credited; it is therefore taught as a problem and never offered as a quotation. The famous 1862 formulation attributed to Bismarck is named by occasion and argument only, under the owner's explicit prohibition on verbatim quotation, because no authenticated text is held here |
| Chronology and numeric verification | Every date in this session was checked against the owner that holds it or against the bounded verification pass, and every figure against the source that supplies it. No date or number appears that is not held by a named source, and register notes 20 records the categories deliberately left empty |
| Named historians | One only - the single continuity attribution held in the core OCR history source, recorded as a controversial position in a debate owned elsewhere. All other historiographical positions are taught without attribution, because the evidence base records positions without names |
| Figures deliberately refused | No plebiscite percentage; no Italian or German population, income, literacy, emigration or output figure; no force or casualty figure outside the verified 1870-71 set; no membership figure for any organisation; no clause of the Plombieres agreement |
| Boundary discipline | Four boundaries stated in the opening, applied in Lessons 1, 2, 9, 10, 18 and 20 and in the PYQ index, and restated in the register notes; the Vienna settlement, nationalism as a doctrine, the 1914 crisis and the Indian reception of the Risorgimento are routed rather than annexed |
| Lesson architecture | Varied by content; no two lessons share an interchangeable internal structure, and the visual grammar differs across all twenty - branching panels, annotated timelines, comparison tables, decision rails, machine diagrams, balance sheets, argument maps and power-location diagrams |
| Mains word ceilings | 150 words for 10 marks and 250 for 15 and 20 marks, the established repository convention |
| Final Mains model lengths | Q1 148, Q2 149, Q3 249, Q4 243, Q5 249, Q6 248 - each within its stated ceiling, with every named evidence item and every unique scoring note retained |

## Book evidence and its controls

1. `books\WORLD HISTORY -- NORMAN LOWE -- ENGLISH  ##.pdf` - the folder's designated world-history spine and the **one core OCR history source** used for this topic under the bounded History research rule. Its complete text was extracted directly from the local PDF and searched term by term. It supplied: the statement that "the unification of Italy was only completed in 1870, and the new state suffered from economic and political weaknesses"; the record that the papacy "had been hostile to the Italian government since 1870 when all the territory belonging to the Papacy (Papal States) had been incorporated in the new kingdom of Italy", together with the 1929 treaty by which Italy recognised the Vatican City as a sovereign state, paid compensation, accepted the Catholic faith as the official state religion and made religious instruction compulsory; the record that "the industrialization of Italy in the years after unification led to the development of a strong socialist party and trade unions"; the listing of French resentment at "the loss of Alsace-Lorraine to Germany at the end of the Franco-Prussian War (1871)" among the causes of friction threatening European peace, and the statement that the French were "obsessed with taking back Alsace-Lorraine, which the Germans had taken in 1871"; the record of Bismarck as "Chancellor 1870-90" and of the system under him "in which the Reichstag had much less power", cited by that source when explaining a later conservative aspiration to return to it; and the single named historiographical attribution used in Lesson 19, in which a historian argues that a later German leader was "simply continuing the policies of leaders like Bismarck, Kaiser Wilhelm II and Stresemann", differing only in ruthlessness of method.
2. **Control result for the nineteenth-century core.** The same extracted text was searched for every central term of this topic. The searches returned **zero** occurrences of "Garibaldi", "Cavour", "Mazzini", "Zollverein", "Risorgimento", "Piedmont", "Sedan", "Koniggratz", "Sadowa" and "Frankfurt Parliament", and only four occurrences of "Bismarck", none of them in a narrative of unification. This confirms the folder README's statement that the book begins in 1914 and is structurally insufficient for Topics 01-08, and the book is therefore **not cited anywhere in this session for the narrative of either unification**. The zero-return is recorded as evidence of a check performed, and the six items listed above are the entirety of what the book legitimately supplies.
3. **Bounded-research control.** No second book was read for this topic. Under the bounded History research rule the substantive base is the complete basic owner, the complete advanced owner and this one core OCR history source, and no optional corroboration, alternative textbook or supplementary dossier was consulted. Where the bounded base leaves a point unresolved, it is recorded below as open uncertainty rather than filled from outside.
4. **Quotation control.** Every quoted phrase in this session is taken verbatim from the extracted text of the book named above or from the canonical owners. No statesman of the period is quoted, because no authenticated text of any such statement is held in this evidence base; the two famous phrases associated with this topic are handled as described in the construction record.

## The single bounded material-fact verification pass

⚠️ **Why the pass was necessary.** The two owners supply a ten-marker timeline, the actors, the comparison and the answer architecture, and they deliberately do not narrate the connecting events. Several of those events are standard essentials without which the teaching would have to use vague formulations - "an alliance was made", "the duchies were divided", "a provocation occurred" - where the standard account has exact names, dates and instruments. One bounded pass was therefore run, confined to those essentials, and every item it produced is labelled in the lesson that uses it.

| Cluster verified | Items carried |
|---|---|
| Italian connecting sequence | Piedmontese troops to the Crimean War, 1855, with the usual figure for the contingent marked as the standard account rather than as confirmed here; the Congress of Paris, 1856, and the raising of the Italian question; Plombieres, 21 July 1858; the Armistice of Villafranca, 11 July 1859; the Treaty of Turin ceding Nice and Savoy, 24 March 1860; the plebiscites in Tuscany, Parma, Modena and Romagna, March 1860; the landing of the Thousand at Marsala, 11 May 1860; the meeting at Teano, 26 October 1860; the proclamation of the Kingdom of Italy, 17 March 1861 |
| Italian 1848-49 sequence | The Palermo revolt of January 1848 and the wave of constitutions; the Statuto Albertino of March 1848 and its survival as the basis of Italy's constitution; Charles Albert's declaration of war; the defeat at Custoza, July 1848; the Roman Republic of February 1849 with Mazzini in the triumvirate and Garibaldi organising its defence; the defeat at Novara, March 1849, the abdication and the accession of Victor Emmanuel II; the French intervention that crushed the Roman Republic by July 1849 and restored Pope Pius IX; the surrender of Venice, August 1849 |
| Italian post-unification limits | The campaign against brigandage in the south, 1861-65, and its recorded causes; the Law of Guarantees of 1871 and the papal refusal; the Non Expedit of 1874 and its persistence; trasformismo from 1876 under Depretis; italia irredenta in Trentino and Trieste and its contribution to Italy's entry into the First World War; and the finding that the phrase about making Italians is traditionally but disputably attributed, with no secure verbatim basis in the principal memoir of the statesman usually credited |
| German institutional background | The Zollverein of 1834 under Prussian leadership with eighteen founding states, the Prussian tariff law of 1818, the southern and central unions of 1828, the accessions of 1854, 1867 and 1888, the eventual inclusion of all Germany outside Austria, and Friedrich List as the most active advocate |
| German 1848-51 sequence | The Frankfurt National Assembly from May 1848 at St Paul's Church; the constitution adopted 28 March 1849 with a hereditary emperor and a parliament on universal male suffrage; the offer of the crown to Frederick William IV in April 1849 and his refusal; the Punctation of Olmutz, November 1850 |
| German wars and settlements | Bismarck's appointment as minister-president in September 1862 amid the army-budget conflict; the joint seizure of Schleswig and Holstein from Denmark in 1864 and their joint possession; the Convention of Gastein of August 1865 dividing the administration; the 1866 war's participants, the decision in Bohemia and at Koniggratz on 3 July, the army of the Main, the simultaneous campaign in Venetia, the Italian alliance diverting Austrian forces, the methodical preparation after 1850 with Moltke and Roon, the description of the campaign as a carefully planned stage with Bismarck as principal agent, the Treaty of Prague of 23 August 1866 and its terms, the annexations of Hanover, Hesse-Kassel, Nassau and Frankfurt, and the Peace of Vienna of 3 October 1866 ceding Venetia for transfer; the North German Confederation constitution in force 1 July 1867 with a Reichstag on universal male suffrage; the Indemnity Bill of 1866; the Hohenzollern-Sigmaringen candidature and the Spanish vacancy of 1868, the acceptance of June 1870 and the withdrawal under French pressure, William I's refusal of a guarantee, the editing and publication of the Ems telegram on 14 July 1870 with its stated purpose, the French declaration of 19 July 1870 and its stated motives, the adherence of Bavaria, Wurttemberg and Baden, the mobilisation of 380,000 troops in eighteen days, Worth on 6 August, Mars-la-Tour and Gravelotte on 16 and 18 August, Sedan on 1-2 September 1870 with the encirclement completed on 31 August, and its recorded losses and prisoners, the Third Republic of 4 September, the siege of Paris from 19 September, the breaking off of negotiations over Alsace and Lorraine, Gambetta's escape and new armies, the capitulation at Metz on 27 October, the surrender of Paris on 28 January 1871, the proclamation of William I at Versailles on 18 January 1871, the Paris Commune, and the Treaty of Frankfurt of 10 May 1871 with its annexation and indemnity; and the recorded consequences - the German Empire and the French Third Republic established, the annexation of the Papal States by Italy on 20 September 1870, the consolidation of Prussian militarism, the adoption of the conscript-and-general-staff model by other powers, and the deep longing for revenge with an extremely unstable peace to 1914 |
| German post-1871 measures | The campaign against the Catholic Church and the Centre Party, 1871-78, with its measures and its outcome; the Anti-Socialist Laws of 1878-1890 and their outcome; the dismissal of Bismarck by Wilhelm II in March 1890 |

⚠️ **Control on the pass.** No item from the pass overrides an owner. Where an owner and the pass address the same point, the owner's formulation is used and the pass supplies only the missing precision - for example, the owner's "1861" is retained and the pass adds "17 March". Where the pass produced material outside the topic's period or boundary, it was not used. The pass was run once and is not reopened.

## Evidence and limitation controls

- No content from the excluded derived or generated package tree was read, cited or used, in accordance with the permanent live-session source exclusion; no learner-v2 artifact was used either, including this subject's own v2 learning sessions and solved workbooks and the complete-topic-package file that duplicates this topic.
- No workbook operation of any kind was performed: no MCQ corpus was constructed, copied, relocated or migrated, and no solved-PYQ workbook was created.
- No plebiscite percentage appears anywhere in this session, because no vote share is treated as reliable here; the instrument is analysed and the numbers are refused.
- No Italian or German population, electorate, income, literacy, emigration, urbanisation or output figure appears anywhere, in accordance with the owner's explicit caution that the southern comparison be kept qualitative and with the absence of any series in this evidence base.
- No force, casualty or order-of-battle figure appears for the Italian wars or for 1864 and 1866. The only military figures in this session are the small verified set for 1870-71 in Lesson 15, and the usual figure for the Piedmontese Crimean contingent, which is marked as the standard account rather than as independently confirmed here.
- No clause of the Plombieres agreement is asserted; only the meeting, its date, its participants and the recorded fact that gains were foreseen for both sides.
- No statesman of the period is quoted. The 1862 formulation associated with Bismarck is named by occasion and argument only, under the owner's explicit prohibition; the phrase about making Italians is recorded as traditionally but disputably attributed and is never offered as a quotation.
- No historian is named except the single continuity attribution held in the core OCR history source, which is recorded as a controversial position in a debate owned by another topic.
- The standard names for the campaign against the Catholic Church, for the Italian south and for the anti-socialist legislation are used in the owner's own manner - as analytical references rather than as sourced facts of this folder - with the bounded pass supplying only the basic facts stated in Lessons 10 and 16.
- Objective-question neutrality is preserved absolutely: the single displayed objective item carries no answer letter, truth-value marking, elimination, corrective mapping or wording that would identify a unique answer, and the routing ledger's record that the official key is unavailable locally is stated rather than worked around.
- The Vienna settlement, nationalism as a doctrine, New Imperialism, the alliance system and outbreak of 1914, the Habsburg nationality crisis, interwar Italy, the Lateran settlement of 1929, the Austro-German union question and the Indian reception of the Risorgimento are all routed to their owners; each is named where it arises and none is narrated here.
- Remaining open uncertainty, recorded rather than filled: the distribution of popular national consciousness in either country at any date; the conduct and the actual results of the plebiscites; the internal decision-making of the Piedmontese government and the negotiations preceding Teano; the content of the Plombieres agreement; the internal German debate over the annexation of Alsace and Lorraine; the practical effectiveness of the Reichstag as against its formal position; the relative weight of economic against military-diplomatic factors in German unification; and the relative weight of the structural legacy of 1871 against the specific decisions of the following decades.
